CHAPTER 1




Proofs for “What it takes to be
certified”

Proof of the theorem “Certified is Thiele-complete plus certification” (book
Theorem 22.3).
Proof. (1) Clause (a) is (A) together with “loaded states are clean”. Clause (c) is
(C), with the three record kinds as the marks. Clause (b) says clean states have the
record down, which with clean loads gives (B), and it gives the earned chain. Clause
(d) gives (D): the claim’s three moves end with the record up from the loaded state
where it holds and down from the one where it fails. Going back, the same pieces
put the four clauses together again. (2) is (1) read left to right. (3) From any state
with the record down, counting A to zero costs nothing and the first mark then
raises the record: a raise for one mark from every clean state, where a certified raise
from a clean state takes three (Theorem 22.5(3)).

Proof of the theorem “CHECK is a unit-cost test” (book Theorem 22.4).
Proof. At a start the latch is down and the table is empty, so the check passes exactly
when eval(Ge(m), a) says yes, that is when m ≤ a; a failing check sets the latch.
The price table charges a CHECK 1.

Proof of the theorem “What every certified Thiele-complete machine has” (book
Theorem 22.5).
Proof. (1) A raise of the record isn’t made by a base move, by (a), so it’s a record
move, which costs one by (c). Running a program on the base is induction on its
steps, each matched by (a). (2) is (c), added up along the run. (3) Clause (b) finds
a CHECK, a COMMIT and a CERTIFY at three different places, and (2) counts them.
(4) is (d): the three moves certify exactly where the claim holds, the claim fails at
some loaded state, and the check can’t pass there because (b) says a passing check
means the claim holds; at the loaded state where it holds, the three moves raise the
record. (5) Cut the run just after the move where the record rises. That shorter run


                                                   7
8   1 Proofs for “What it takes to be certified”


                starts clean and ends with the record up, so clause (b) puts the record down right
                before a CERTIFY in it and up right after. The record never comes down, so it rises
                only once, and that CERTIFY is the move. (6) Every move is a base move or a record
                move, and (a) supplies base moves.

                Proof of the theorem “The language first, and what a certificate then means”
                (book Theorem 22.6).
                Proof. (1) Take the plain interface the language-first one becomes. (2) The checker’s
                soundness and the keeping proof are the ones clause (b) already supplies. (3) Clause
                (b) gives the chain; the checker’s soundness proof against L gives c’s meaning at the
                state checked; L’s keeping proof carries it, through states where the thing c is about
                is unchanged, to the state committed.

                Proof of the observation “A clock is a Thiele machine and isn’t certified Thiele–
                complete” (book Observation 22.7).
                Proof. Every move costs one, so any raise costs at least one: the toll holds for any
                record. The machine’s own moves are the two-counter instructions, which gives the
                universal base. But a certified Thiele-complete machine has a move priced 0, by
                Theorem 22.5(6), and this one has none.

                Proof of the theorem “Each clause does work” (book Theorem 22.8).
                Proof. (1) The base moves leave the flag alone, TICK never lowers it, the base costs
                0 and TICK costs 1, which gives (a) and (c). Through any interface, (a) gives a
                loaded state, which is clean, so its record is down by (b), and a move that puts
                the record up from every state raises it alone. Clause (b) would need a CHECK, a
                COMMIT and a CERTIFY inside that one-move run, and one move can’t be three.
                (2) The base gives (a) and costs 0, which with no record moves is (c). No run ends
                with the record up, so (b) asks nothing of a run, and a checker that always says no
                is sound for any meaning. Clause (d) needs a run from some loaded state that raises
                the record, and none does.

                Proof of the theorem “The four clauses are independent” (book Theorem 22.9).
                Proof. (a) Nothing moves a trapped state’s flag, so a run that uses DROP ends with
                the flag down. A run from a clean state that ends with the flag up never used DROP;
                it’s a run of this chapter’s machine, and Theorem 15.11 gives its chain. DROP is
                priced 0 and read as a base move, so (c) holds, and (d) holds as it does for the
                plain machine. A state with the flag up has it down after DROP. That breaks the
                permanence in (a), and permanence is about the machine alone, so no interface
                                                                                          9


meets (a). (b) FORGE never lowers the flag, costs one and is read as a record
move, so (a), (c) and (d) hold as before. From a clean start FORGE alone raises the
flag, and a run of one move has no room for a CHECK, a COMMIT and a CERTIFY.
Through any interface: (d) gives a loaded state from which the record rises, so its
trap is down; (a) makes that state clean, so its record is down by (b); and FORGE
alone raises it, which (b) forbids. (c) Clauses (a), (b) and (d) never mention a price,
and they hold as for the plain machine. Base moves cost one, so (c) fails, and every
certified Thiele-complete machine has a move priced 0 (Theorem 22.5(6)). (d) is
Theorem 22.8(2).

Proof of the theorem “Any unit” (book Theorem 22.10).
Proof. (1) At unit one every listed price is unchanged.
   (2) Changing the size of the unit changes no state transition, claim or record.
Thus the computing, earned-chain and distinguishing-claim requirements stay as
they were. To multiply the prices by c, read the old ledger multiplied by c too. Every
addition is then multiplied by the same amount.
   In the other direction, count complete groups of c units in the ledger. There may
be a remainder smaller than c at the start. A step adds either nothing or exactly one
complete group, so it leaves that remainder alone and increases the group count by
the divided price. The equation for that count is
                                          
                                  ℓ + cr       ℓ
                                           =      + r.
                                    c          c
It gives the required whole-number ledger on the same states and runs.
    (3) The move for an ordinary counter instruction is a base move and costs zero
at every unit. A machine charging for every move has no such move.
    (4) Find the passing CHECK, matching COMMIT and raising CERTIFY in the
earned chain. They occupy three different places in the run. Each costs c, and all
other charges are nonnegative, giving at least 3c. The claim was true at its check
and stays true up to its commitment because what it concerns is unchanged. The
record rises at that chain’s CERTIFY and never comes down; changing the price unit
changes none of these events.
    (5) Doubling the small machine’s prices gives unit two by (2). Its CHECK then
costs two. At unit one every move must cost zero or one, so this same priced machine
cannot qualify there through another choice of interface.

Proof of the theorem “The small machine, the generic one and the sorted one
are certified Thiele-complete” (book Theorem 22.11).
10   1 Proofs for “What it takes to be certified”


                Proof. (1) Clause (a) is Theorem 15.3 together with the step line: INC, DEC and
                HALT never touch the flag, the flag never comes down, and a start is clean with its
                trap down. Clause (b) is Theorem 15.11 with checker soundness (Lemma 15.2),
                and a meaning about c keeps holding while c’s value doesn’t move. Clause (c) is the
                price table. Clause (d) is Theorem 15.20 with the claim “A is zero,” which holds
                at (0, b) and fails at (5, b). (2) and (3) are the same argument with the property
                language left open. For (3), read a number as a list in the list code: 0 is the empty
                list, and 2x (2y + 1) is x followed by the list numbered y (every positive number
                splits that way in exactly one way, and y is smaller than the number, so the reading
                ends). The meaning of sorted is that each entry is at most the one after it. The
                checker reads the list from the front: yes on the empty list and on a list of one entry,
                and on x followed by a list that starts with y, yes exactly when x ≤ y and it says yes
                on that rest. Induction on the length of the list shows it says yes exactly when the
                meaning holds, and a language with one property has an exact equality test. The
                sorted claim holds of the list numbered 18, which is [1, 2], and fails of 20, which
                is [2, 1]. (4) is (1) of Theorem 22.5 and Section 2’s floor. (5) The interface over
                the language is the one of (1), (2) and (3) with the meanings handed over by the
                language, and the checker’s soundness is Lemma 15.2 and its generic form.

                Proof of the observation “Every certified Thiele-complete machine hides its
                record and its ledger from its window” (book Observation 22.12).
                Proof. Theorem 22.5(4) gives a loaded state and a run from it that raises the record,
                and (3) says that run paid at least 3. Say it ends with window (line ℓ, x, y). Two-
                counter instructions can steer any configuration to any other: empty A, fill it to
                x + 1, empty B, fill it to y, then one DEC of A that jumps to line ℓ. Those are base
                moves, so from the same loaded state they reach the same window, cost nothing,
                and leave the record down, by (a), (b) and (c). That’s two runs, one window, two
                records and two ledgers, which is (2). A function of the window would have to give
                both answers on that one window, which is (3). For (1), load ( x + 1, y) and take just
                the last DEC. (4) is (3) read as a Thiele machine, whose runs are the machine’s.

Proofs and worked runs for “Parts
I and II, run on the example
machine”

Proof of the theorem “The small machine runs the eight-state machine” (book
Theorem 22.13).
Proof. (1) By cases on the move. On a live state CERTIFY runs, because a committed
claim is on the channel; it raises the flag, moves the line on by one and charges
one. INC B moves the line on by one and charges nothing, and it touches only B
and B’s version, so the fact about A and the commitment stand and the state stays
live. In a jump, each COMMIT finds its fact in the table and succeeds, charging
one, and the INC and the DEC charge nothing and end at line a, whose slot is a;
the flag isn’t touched. (2) After each compiled move, the state is live again, so the
next compiled move starts with exactly the condition just used in (1). The window
agrees after each move and each move adds its own charge. At the end of the list
the windows agree and the ledger has added the sum of those charges. (3) The
eight-state machine’s own toll (Section 3) says a run of its moves that takes the flag
from down to up is charged at least one, and (2) carries that charge across. (4)
CHECK (A ≥ 0) passes at every value of A and the COMMIT after it passes, which
takes line 1 to line 3, slot 3, with two units of cost paid; then (2) applies. A raised
flag was raised by a CERTIFY, and Theorem 15.11 gives the chain.

Proof of the theorem “Structural entitlement represented” (book Theo-
rem 22.14).
Proof. (1) is Theorem 9.1(1). (2) Clause (b) applied to a run from a clean state
that ends with the record up gives the chain, and a passing check means the claim’s
meaning holds, which keeps holding while the thing it’s about is unchanged. (3)
The covering (M6) and Theorem 9.1’s count finish it, with the record moves, which



                                                  11
12   2 Proofs and worked runs for “Parts I and II, run on the example machine”


               are the paid steps, in place of the charged steps. (4) is clause (c) added up. (5) is
               Theorem 22.5(3).

                Proof of the theorem “A floor with no supplied tree” (book Theorem 22.15).
               Proof. Write each rival’s answers on a strip, one box per CHECK in the run. One yes-
               or-no question gives two possible strips; a second gives four; each further question
               doubles the possibilities. With c questions there are at most 2c strips. Put rivals with
               the same strip in the same pile. The premise limits each pile to m rivals. Hence at
               most 2c m rivals can be on the original list.
                  Now ⌈log2 m⌉ bits suffice to number m places inside a pile, and c bits suffice to
               name its strip. Together they number every rival using at most c + ⌈log2 m⌉ bits.
               The least number needed for |Ω| rivals cannot be larger. Subtract the inside-pile
               allowance, clipping a negative result to zero, to get the claimed bound of c.
                  Each CHECK is a record move. Counting all record moves can only increase that
               number, and each contributes exactly one to the ledger while base moves contribute
               zero. This gives the two remaining comparisons.

                Proof of the theorem “Structural entitlement, with the narrowing made by the
                run” (book Theorem 22.16).
               Proof. (1)–(2) Start with the prior list. At each CHECK the actual run passed, run
               the preceding instructions from each surviving rival and ask that same question
               there. Keep the rivals that pass. Nothing adds a rival, and the actual start s0 passes
               every one of its own tests, so it stays throughout. A missing rival has a first test that
               removed it. That is the required failed CHECK, at the stated place in the run.
                   (3) Name the successive list sizes q0 , q1 , . . . , qn , with q0 = |Ω| and qn = |Ω′ |.
               The before-and-after sizes for test i are consecutive members of that list. Multiply
               the after-sizes by q0 , and multiply the before-sizes by qn : each side contains exactly
               q0 q1 · · · qn . This is the displayed product identity.
                   (4) The share premise says qi−1 ≤ 2k qi at every test. Apply it first to q0 , then to q1
               in the resulting bound, and keep going. After n tests it gives q0 ≤ 2kn qn . Numbering
               qn final places takes ⌈log2 qn ⌉ bits; another kn bits can number up to 2kn times as
               many. Thus the difference in rounded index lengths is at most kn. Each of these
               n tests is a CHECK the run actually passed, so it is among the paid record moves.
               Exact prices make the number of those moves the ledger increase. Multiplying that
               larger count by k preserves the bound.
                   (5) The run starts clean and ends with its record up. The earned-record require-
               ment therefore finds its CHECK, COMMIT and raising CERTIFY. Those three different
               acts each cost one, so the ledger increased by at least three.
                                                                                              13


   (6) The actual start remained on every list, so every qi is positive. The ratios
q0 /q1 , q1 /q2 , . . . , qn−1 /qn multiply to q0 /qn : each intermediate size cancels once
above and once below. Taking base-two logarithms turns this product into the sum
of the per-test logarithms.
    To account for rounding, set u = log2 q0 and w = log2 qn . The integer ⌈u − w⌉ +
⌈w⌉ is at least (u − w) + w = u, so it is at least the least integer above u, namely
⌈u⌉. Rearranging gives the claimed bound by ⌈log2 (q0 /qn )⌉. Finally, rounding
each per-test logarithm up and adding gives an integer at least as large as their
sum. It cannot be smaller than rounding the sum up just once. This proves the last
comparison.

Proof of the proposition “One CHECK can take four bits” (book Proposi-
tion 22.17).
Proof. A ≥ 15 holds at exactly one of the sixteen starts. From (15, 0) the CHECK
passes and files the claim, the COMMIT finds it with A’s version unchanged, and
the CERTIFY raises the record. The posterior is the one rival that passes the run’s
only CHECK, so the index bits are ⌈log2 16⌉ − ⌈log2 1⌉ = 4, and the run has three
record moves.

Proof of the theorem “What the record adds” (book Theorem 22.18).
Proof. (1) One direction is clause (b): a run from a clean start that ends with the
record up contains the chain. For the other, the chain’s CERTIFY leaves the record
up, which is part of what the chain is, and by the last words of clause (a) a record
that’s up stays up under every move, so whatever the run does after that CERTIFY
it ends with the record up. The floor of 3 is Theorem 22.5(3). (2) Read the proof
of Theorem 22.14 again and mark what each conclusion used. Its (1) used (M1)
and (M2), through Theorem 9.1(1), and nothing about the machine. Its (3) used
(M5) and the count |Ω| ≤ L( T ) · |Ω′ | of (M6), and (M4) to put the record moves
in place of the tree’s depth; the clean start and the raised record never entered. Its
(4) is clause (c) added up along the run, from whatever state the run starts at. The
decoded run being shown by some survivor is in the hypotheses and, like the second
half of (M3) above, feeds none of the three. The conclusions dropped, (2) and (5),
are exactly the two that used the clean start and the record.

Proof of the proposition “The eight-start member” (book Proposition 22.19).
Proof. From any loaded start, the first CHECK passes exactly when a is even. If it
passes, the second has room in the table and passes exactly when a ≥ 2. Nothing has
changed A, so their matching COMMIT and CERTIFY then succeed. If either check
14   2 Proofs and worked runs for “Parts I and II, run on the example machine”


               fails, the trap prevents certification. This proves the claim for every a, b. Within the
               eight rivals, a can only be 0, 1, 2, 3; of those, only 2 is even and at least 2, whatever
               b. From (2, 0) and (2, 1) the COMMIT then finds its fact with A’s version unchanged,
               nothing having written A, and the CERTIFY raises the flag; from every other start
               the first failing CHECK traps the machine, and a trapped machine raises nothing. So
               the posterior is those two. The hypotheses: the posterior narrows the eight strictly
               (M1); a dropped rival whose run fails a CHECK shows receipts no survivor shows
               (M2); the run ends with the record up and its receipts, two passes, are the survivors’
               (M3); the tree has depth 2 and the run 4 record moves (M4); two survivors (M5);
               and the fibres, the four rivals with B at 0 for the survivor (2, 0) and the four with B
               at 1 for (2, 1), cover all eight, add up to eight, and have four members each, the
               leaves of the tree, so 8 ≤ 4 · 2 (M6). Then ⌈log2 8⌉ − ⌈log2 2⌉ = 3 − 1 = 2; the four
               record moves are two CHECKs, a COMMIT and a CERTIFY, and the ledger rises by
               exactly those four by clause (c); and the chain is in the run. For Theorem 22.15,
               the run’s questions are its two CHECKs, and two rivals answer them alike exactly
               when their a’s agree on being even and on reaching 2, which puts the eight starts in
               four classes of two, so m = 2 and ⌈log2 8⌉ − ⌈log2 2⌉ = 2 is the number of CHECKs.
               With the two CHECKs alone there’s no CERTIFY, so the record stays down, the ledger
               rises by 2, and the index bits are still 2.

                Proof of the theorem “A search through n bits” (book Theorem 22.20).
               Proof. Each question is a CHECK of whether one register holds a given bit, which
               passes exactly when it does; a failing CHECK traps. So the program certifies exactly
               when every answer is right, which is when v begins with the answers. The counting
               is the count of bit strings: the 2m fibres of 2k values each add up to the 2k+m of
               the prior, and none is longer than the 2k leaves of the tree, so the representation
               theorem applies with every hypothesis a theorem. The cap is the fact table: it holds
               sixteen facts, never evicts, and a CHECK on a full table traps (Theorem 22.2), so a
               seventeenth CHECK before the CERTIFY ends the run in a trap.

                Proof of the theorem “The time tax” (book Theorem 22.21).
               Proof. (1) Take the starts (m − 1, b) and (m, b). At the jth move, j ≤ n < m, at
               most j − 1 pebbles have left A, so A is at least m − j ≥ 1 in both runs, and no DEC
               finds A empty in either. The two runs take the same branches and differ only by
               the one pebble in A. They are at the same line with the same B, and they stop at
               the same moment if they stop at all, though A ≥ m holds of one start and not the
               other. (2) Run the ladder from A = a. Each of the first min( a, m) moves takes a
               pebble off A and jumps two lines on. If a ≥ m that’s all m moves, ending on line
               2m + 1. If a < m, the run reaches line 2a + 1 after a moves and from there goes on
                                                                                               15


one line per move (a DEC falling through, then an INC), so after m moves it’s on
line a + m + 1, below 2m + 1. It’s all DEC and INC, so it costs 0. (3) is the price
table: CHECK (A ≥ m) passes exactly when A ≥ m, a failing CHECK traps and the
flag stays down, and the three moves cost three. (4) is the same price table with one
instruction: a passing CHECK goes on to line 2 and a failing one traps and leaves
the line at 1. (5) is arithmetic on (1) to (4).

The worked run for the time tax, with its table. Try m = 3 with A = 2. The
ladder takes a pebble on line 1 and jumps to line 3, takes the second and jumps to
line 5, then finds A empty and falls through to line 6, the INC: after three moves it
sits on line 6, not 7. With A = 3 the third DEC succeeds and the run is on line 7.
Two moves in, both runs are on line 5 with B untouched, which is (1).
         Moves taken      Start with two pebbles       Start with three pebbles
         0                      line 1, A = 2                line 1, A = 3
         1                      line 3, A = 1                line 3, A = 2
         2                      line 5, A = 0                line 5, A = 1
         3                      line 6, A = 0                line 7, A = 0
The reader in the theorem sees the line and B, which stays unchanged through these three
moves. The A entries are shown here to explain the run; that reader is not allowed to use
 them. Line 6 still has an instruction: the decision time is not a claim that both runs have
                                            halted.
CHAPTER 3




Proofs, the program and the
worked tables for “The universal
machines”

Proof of the theorem “No host fact on an exact copy of the guest’s counter”
(book Theorem C.1).
Proof. A fixed program mentions finitely many properties, so Q is finite, and the
translation sends the infinitely many Ge(0), Ge(1), . . . into it: two of them land on
the same member. The guest’s COMMIT asks for Ge(m), and its table can only hold
Ge(n), so it traps (or its CHECK already trapped). The host sees one property twice:
its CHECK passes, since the guest’s would, and its COMMIT finds exactly what that
CHECK wrote.

Proof of the theorem “U keeps pace with its guest” (book Theorem C.2).
Proof. The one-step part is a case for each thing the guest can do at its line: stop;
INC; DEC, taken or not; CHECK, passing, failing, or with a full table; COMMIT,
found or not; CERTIFY, passing or not. The block invariants and the case analysis
in Appendix 7 show where each matching stretch lands. The relation carries the
bookkeeping that makes the next case go through: each count register equals the
number of slots in use for its counter, each slot in use holds (property number,
checked value), a slot’s fact is current exactly when the matching guest fact is, a live
slot’s mirror is the property’s number plus one and a stale one’s is 0, and unused
slots, unused mirrors and the dead end are 0. At loading, the counters and guest
line have their specified starting values, and both tables and channels are empty,
so the matching conditions hold before any step. Now take one guest instruction.
The matching stretch just described ends either at the next matching point or with
both machines stopped. In the first case its slot and mirror conditions are exactly
what the following instruction needs, so you can do the next stretch. Repeat this for
the first m guest steps. Each running stretch takes at least one host step; if neither


                                  16
                                                                                           17


machine has stopped, the accumulated host count is therefore at least m. If they stop
earlier, both remain stopped with the matching final fields. That gives the whole-run
statement for every m.

Proof of the theorem “Halting and answers match” (book Theorem C.3).
Proof. Theorem C.2: a halted guest is matched by a stopped host, and a host that
has stopped can’t be back at line 1 with the guest still running, because line 1 isn’t
a stop. A halted machine stays halted, so the two halting points carry the same
values.

Proof of the theorem “The host’s flag is the guest’s, and its ledger is exact”
(book Theorem C.4).
Proof. (1) Flags are equal at matching points and never come down. (2) Ledgers
are equal at matching points; inside the stretch of U that runs one guest instruction,
at most the one paid site of that instruction runs, so the host’s ledger sits between
the guest’s before and after it.

Proof of the theorem “The host earns its own flag” (book Theorem C.5).
Proof. By Theorem C.4 the guest’s flag rises too; find the guest step where it rises, a
passing CERTIFY, and the related host point just before it. The guest’s channel there
names a fact, and the relation says the host’s channel names the matching slot fact.
The host’s own flag rises at its own first passing CERTIFY, and nothing paid runs
between the related point and that CERTIFY, so the host’s channel hasn’t moved: it
names that slot fact. The host machine’s version of Theorem 15.11 gives the host’s
CHECK and COMMIT on that slot. The record kept with the slot says what pair it
held and when, and the checker’s meaning gives holds( p, v). The same theorem
on the guest’s run, up to the step where its flag rose, gives the guest’s chain on the
same p and c, and the record says c held v at its CHECK.

Proof of the theorem “The host is certified Thiele-complete, and U’s halting is
undecidable” (book Theorem C.6).
Proof. (1) The base is registers 0 and 1, run by INC and DEC; the claims are “Slot
holds of register r”; the rest is the host machine’s own versions of the small machine’s
theorems, and for non-vacuity, register 0 loaded with 1, which is ⟨0, 0⟩, the pair
of Zero and 0, against register 0 loaded with 0, which is no pair. Over the claim
language the interface is the same one, with the meanings handed over by the
language. (2) is Theorem 22.11(4) and Theorem C.5. (3) is Theorem C.3, and
18   3 Proofs, the program and the worked tables for “The universal machines”


               the loading map reduces the small machine’s halting question, undecidable by
               Theorem 15.5, to this one.

               Proof of the theorem “One host runs every computably presented Thiele ma-
               chine” (book Theorem C.7).
               Proof. Two layers, each proved on its own. The bottom layer is the compiler: for
               every presented machine, its guest program’s run passes through points that code
               M’s states in order, with the guest’s ledger equal to M’s ledger plus the surcharge
               and the guest’s flag equal to M’s latch, and the guest’s own flag stands on an earned
               chain whose claim is M’s reading program applied to the coded state where the
               reading first said yes. Here is why. The guest keeps a few registers packed into its
               counters the way Minsky packed them: the coded state, a latch bit, the price still
               owed, and a register that counts the reading’s steps. It starts at the reading with
               nothing owed, and goes round one loop. Run the compiled reading on the coded
               state, counting its steps. If it says yes and the latch bit is 0, run CHECK, COMMIT
               and CERTIFY on the claim “reading program r says yes of v,” v being the packed
               registers with the step count as fuel, set the latch bit, and take three off what’s owed,
               stopping at zero. Pay what’s owed in PAYs. Run the compiled driver, and halt if it
               says stop. Otherwise run the compiled step and price for the move it picked, put
               the new coded state and that price in place, and go round again. Everything but
               the PAYs and the one chain is INC and DEC, at no cost. So at the point just after
               the PAYs and before the driver runs, once n moves have been made, the coded state
               is M’s state after n moves, the latch bit is M’s latch, and the ledger is M’s ledger,
               except that the move where the latch bit was set was paid with the chain’s three
               and the larger of c − 3 and 0 in PAYs instead of its price c (or, if the reading is yes at
               s0 , the start was charged the chain’s three). The CHECK passes, because the fuel is
               the reading’s own step count, and at such a value the fixed checker says what the
               reading says; the COMMIT finds that fact, since nothing wrote B in between; and
               the latch bit lets the chain run once. The top layer is UP , proved the way U was in
               the previous theorems, with the PAY case added: it keeps pace with any guest of
               this kind, halting and flag matched, ledger equal at matching points, and its flag
               earned on a slot. Compose the two. The earned part takes one more step: the host’s
               chain gives a guest CHECK that passed, and a guest run that ends with its flag up
               has exactly one passing CHECK, the compiler’s CHECK of the reading on counter
               B. So the guest CHECK the host’s chain points to is that one, and its claim is M’s
               reading at the first state where it said yes.
                                                                                           19


Proof of the theorem “Exact, within two, and no better” (book Theorem C.8).
Proof. (1) and (2): the guest pays M’s prices in PAYs, a unit at a time, except at the
move where M’s reading first says yes. There it has to run its CHECK, COMMIT
and CERTIFY, three units, and it makes up the rest of the move’s price in PAYs, so it
pays the larger of the price c and three. The price is at least one, because the move
raised a reading (the toll), so the excess is at most two, and none when c ≥ 3. (3):
a raised flag on the host machine costs at least three from a clean start, whatever
the program, and M’s ledger is less. (4): the guest finds the reading yes before M
has made a move, runs its CHECK, COMMIT and CERTIFY there, three units on top
of a ledger of nothing, and from then on its flag is up, so the reading never first says
yes again and every move’s price is paid in PAYs, exactly.

Proof of the theorem “The host runs any guest” (book Theorem 29.1).
Proof. (1) Before any move, the guest is at g(h). One untrapped guest move has
budget 1, enough for every guest instruction, so it takes exactly the guest’s next step.
It leaves the host untrapped, which lets the same argument apply to the next move.
After the first move the guest has taken one step; after the second, two; and after
n, exactly n. No such move changes the guest’s program. (2) One pass of U0 is a
guest step, then INC A and DEC A back to line 1, so it is (1) once per pass. (3) A
host program made only of OWN moves is the small machine, core for core, and
Theorem 15.4 does the rest.

Proof of the observation “The copy is the record” (book Observation 29.2).
Proof. One move at a time. An OWN move touches neither the mirror nor the guest.
A trapped GSTEP touches neither. An untrapped one sets the mirror to the old
mirror or the crossing, and the guest’s flag is a latch (Theorem 15.6), so if the old
mirror equalled the old flag, the new mirror equals the new flag. The last sentence
is this together with Theorem 29.1.

Proof of the theorem “The host’s step collects the guest’s toll” (book Theo-
rem 29.3).
Proof. (1) The guest’s flag rises only on a passing CERTIFY (Theorem 15.6), which
costs 1, and the budget let it run, so b ≥ 1; the mirror was the guest’s flag, so it was
down, and the move sets it to the crossing. (2) is (1) at the matching move. (3)
Write the host’s charge and the guest’s ledger increase beside every move. When
a guest step runs, its price is at most the budget the host paid. When none runs,
20   3 Proofs, the program and the worked tables for “The universal machines”


               the guest’s increase is zero and the host’s charge is nonnegative. Adding these
               comparisons along the trace gives
                                   µ( g(run(t, h))) − µ( g(h)) ≤ µ(run(t, h)) − µ(h).

               Move the two starting readings to the other sides to get the stated form.

               Proof of the theorem “No unpaid host certification” (book Theorem 29.4).
               Proof. An OWN move copies the mirror unchanged, and a trapped GSTEP does too,
               so the rise is an untrapped GSTEP b whose guest step crossed; a crossing is a passing
               CERTIFY, which costs 1 and needed the budget. The own flag moves by the small
               machine’s step line.

               Proof of the theorem “The host is a Thiele machine” (book Theorem 29.5).
               Proof. The mirror’s half is Theorem 29.4; the own flag’s half is the small machine’s
               toll on the host’s core. The trace form is Section 2’s universal floor.

               Proof of the theorem “Rice’s theorem on the host machine” (book Theo-
               rem 29.6).
               Proof. The reduction above. The prefix before y leaves every register but the two
               fresh ones as it found them, and empties those, so when H halts the built program
               ends in whatever state y ends in from the same input, with the same ledger, table,
               channel, trap and flag. When H doesn’t halt, the built program stops on no input.
               You don’t even need to know whether the looping program has Π: ask the decider
               about it, and its answer says which of y and n to put at the end.

               Proof of the theorem “Rice’s theorem for the small machine, from the clean
               start” (book Theorem 29.7).
               Proof. The road of Theorem 29.6, with the input dropped. Take a two-counter
               program H with a start ( a, b), and let y have Π and n lack it; as before, if the
               program that loops has Π, swap the roles of y and n. Build the program that does
               INC A a times and INC B b times, runs a copy of H with its jumps moved down
               (a jump to a line where H has no instruction, line 0 or past its end, goes to the
               line just after the copy), empties A and then B with a one-line DEC loop each, and
               then runs y with its jumps moved down. From the clean start the copy of H begins
               at ( a, b), which is H’s own start, so it stops exactly when H does. If it does, both
               counters are emptied and y runs from (0, 0). Every instruction before y is an INC
               or a DEC: it adds nothing to the ledger and touches no fact, channel, trap latch or
               flag. It does move the versions of A and B up, each by the number of writes that
                                                                                            21


counter took before y started, and the facts y records carry versions. But a CHECK
or a COMMIT reads versions only to ask whether two versions of the same counter
are equal, and adding one fixed amount to both keeps the answer. So y takes the
same steps inside the built program as it takes from the clean start, with the same
counters, trap latch, ledger and flag at every point, and with facts that differ only
by that fixed shift in their versions. That shift is why the fact table and the channel
are left out of “behaves the same from the clean start.” If H doesn’t stop, neither
does the built program: the copy of H never reaches a HALT, and nothing before
y can trap. So the built program behaves the same as y when H stops from ( a, b),
and the same as the two-line program that loops, INC A then DEC A back to line
1, when it doesn’t. A decider for Π would then decide whether H stops from ( a, b),
with the consequence stated in Theorem 15.5; which of y and n goes at the end is
the decider’s own answer about the looping program. “Halts from the clean start” is
carried by behaving the same from the clean start, held by the empty program and
lacked by the looping one, so it’s covered.

Proof of the theorem “s-m-n, and a universal program for host programs” (book
Theorem 29.8).
Proof. (1) The prefix writes register 2 c times and nothing else, so it moves that
register’s version from 0 to c. A fact records a version, so after the prefix every later
version of register 2, and the version in every fact the run records about register 2,
is c more than in a clean run. A CHECK or a COMMIT reads versions only to ask
whether two versions of the same register are equal, and adding c to both keeps the
answer, so the two runs take the same steps. The run is V’s run from the stated start,
moved down by c lines. (2) is the evaluator. A finite algorithm decodes a number
into a program, runs it step by step on given registers, record layer included, and,
searching over the number of steps, returns the output of the first halting run. It
is a finite tape algorithm, and the translation of Appendix 6 turns any finite tape
algorithm into a finite program of INC and DEC on finitely many counters. The host
runs those instructions on registers of its own, with HALT marking the returned
output. That’s W.

Proof of the theorem “Kleene’s recursion theorem on the host machine” (book
Theorem 29.9).
Proof. (1) Let E be the following program of INC and DEC, built the same way as
W from one finite evaluator, with T’s number t written in. On inputs x and c it
computes the number of spec(c, Pc ), where Pc is the program numbered c, runs T
on that number to get d, and then runs the program numbered d on x. Let c0 be the
number of E, and let e = spec(c0 , E). By (1) of Theorem 29.8, e on input x does
22   3 Proofs, the program and the worked tables for “The universal machines”


               what E does on x and c0 . And E on c0 builds the number of spec(c0 , Pc0 ), which is
               the number of e, since Pc0 is E. So e hands its own number to T, gets d, and runs d
               on x. (2) and (3) are (1) with T carrying out F or f .

               Proof of the theorem “The recursion theorem, up to the record” (book Theo-
               rem 29.10).
               Proof. The construction is Theorem 29.9’s, with a different evaluator. The first step
               is to see that a record is small. From a clean start, a host program that stops has a
               record I can read off five numbers, in this order: what it left in register 0; how many
               facts the table holds; what is left of the ledger once the facts, the trap and the flag are
               paid for, which I’ll call the replay allowance; 1 if the flag is up and 0 if not; and 1 if
               the trap latch is up and 0 if not. The record obeys a short rulebook, and you can read
               each rule off the price table. A run stops at its first trap, so its ledger is its passing
               CHECKs, plus its passing COMMITs, plus its passing CERTIFYs, plus one if it trapped
               (only those three can fail, and the failing one is charged). Its passing CHECKs are
               its facts, at most sixteen. Its flag is up exactly when some CERTIFY passed. So the
               allowance is the passing COMMITs plus the passing CERTIFYs less one for the flag,
               which is never negative, and the ledger is the facts, plus that allowance, plus the
               flag, plus the trap. A COMMIT needs a fact behind it, so a positive allowance means
               at least one fact. A CERTIFY needs a commitment, so a raised flag means a passing
               COMMIT, and so a positive allowance. And the channel is empty exactly when no
               COMMIT passed, which is exactly when the allowance is zero, since a CERTIFY can’t
               pass before a COMMIT has. The allowance counts repeated CERTIFYs as well as
               COMMITs; it counts the cost the replay can spend on commitments, not the original
               commitment events. Those are the only properties of a record the replay will use.
                   The second step is five blocks, one for each number. Each block reads the code of
               T, runs T on the number of the specialised program, runs the program that comes
               out on x, and returns its number. All of that happens on plain data: the blocks are
               programs of INC and DEC, built the way W was, from a finite evaluator translated
               into INC and DEC. So if T has marks of its own, they are simulated and never paid.
                   The third step is the payment. The evaluator puts 1 in a fresh register, which is
               ⟨0, 0⟩, the pair of Zero and 0, so it satisfies the host’s one property, and CHECKs it
               once for every fact; there are at most sixteen, so the table has room. It COMMITs to
               that claim once for every unit in the replay allowance. It CERTIFYs once if the flag is
               up. It copies the first number into register 0. And if the trap latch is up, it CHECKs a
               register that holds 0, which fails, since 0 is no pair, and the host traps. Everything
               before the payments is INC and DEC and costs nothing, and the payments cost what
               the record costs, so the ledger is exact. Specialise the evaluator to its own number,
               as before.
                                                                                             23


Proof of the theorem “No fixed point of full behaviour” (book Theorem 29.11).
Proof. Let F ( p) be the one-line program INC r, with r one above the number of
p. To carry this out on numbers, add one to the input code, encode INC with that
register, and encode the one-element instruction list. These are finite arithmetic
operations of the counter construction in Appendix 6. A program’s number is at
least every register it names: the number of an instruction is above its register, and
the code of a list is above every instruction in it. So e can’t name r, the register F (e)
writes. On input 0 the run of F (e) ends with 1 in register r, and if e stops at all, it
ends with 0 there. If e doesn’t stop, then it already differs from F (e), which does.
Either way they don’t behave the same.

Proof of the theorem “The diagonal on the host machine” (book Theo-
rem 29.12).
Proof. (1) The flip, send programs that d calls good to the program lacking Π and
the others to the one having it, is carried out by a host program by hypothesis.
Theorem 29.9(2) gives a program e that computes what its image computes; Π
holds of e exactly when it holds of the image, and the image lacks it exactly when d
calls e good, so d is wrong at e. (2) From a deciding program D build a host program
that computes D’s flip on numbers, by the finite-simulation search of Appendix 6,
reading the Boolean from the first halting run of D; then (1) applies. This one is a
plain no: the flip (1) needs is built from D itself, so no premise about what can be
represented is left over.

Proof of the theorem “Rice’s theorem for the two-counter machine, on any
inputs” (book Theorem 29.13).
Proof. For every input: given a two-counter instance H starting from counters a and
b, build a program that turns the input of n pebbles in bin A into (6n + 5) · 2a · 3b ,
runs H inside the exponents of 2 and 3, divides out every 2 and every 3 when H
stops, takes away 5, divides by 6, and then runs the program that has the property.
Because 6n + 5 shares no factor with 2 or 3, the coding never mistakes it for an
exponent, and the input comes back whole. If H halts, the built program behaves
the same as the one with the property on every input; if not, it never stops on
any input, like the program that loops. As before, if the looping program has the
property, put a program that lacks it at the end instead, and the decider’s own
answer about the looping program says which. So a decider for the property would
decide two-counter halting.
24   3 Proofs, the program and the worked tables for “The universal machines”


               Proof of the theorem “Kleene for the two-counter machine, inputs written as
               powers of two” (book Theorem 29.14).
               Proof. (1) The host’s three pieces, with two counters in place of registers. First, the
               universal program. Load a program’s number e and its input x together as 2x · 3e in
               bin A, with bin B empty. The evaluator of Appendix 6, the one that searches budgets
               and runs the program numbered e on x, is a finite counter program on finitely many
               counters, and packing those counters into two by the prime-exponent construction
               of Appendix 6.3 gives U2 : started on 2x · 3e it stops with 2y in bin A exactly when
               the program numbered e, started on 2x , stops with 2y . Second, specialising. For a
               number c and a program V, let spec(c, V ) be c copies of a block that multiplies bin
               A by three, followed by V with its jumps moved down. The block is INC and DEC
               only, so it adds nothing to the ledger and records nothing, and started on 2x the c
               copies leave 2x · 3c ; so spec(c, V ) on 2x does what V does on 2x · 3c . Third, the fixed
               point. Let T be a program that carries out F: on 2n , n the number of a program
               p, it stops with 2m , m the number of F ( p). Build E from the same finite evaluator,
               with T’s number written in, and pack it into two counters as before: on 2x · 3c it
               computes the number of spec(c, Pc ), where Pc is the program numbered c, runs T
               on that number to get d, and runs the program numbered d on 2x . Let c0 be the
               number of E and e = spec(c0 , E). On 2x , e does what E does on 2x · 3c0 : it builds
               the number of spec(c0 , Pc0 ), which is the number of e itself since Pc0 is E, hands it
               to T, gets the number of F (e), and runs F (e) on 2x . The machine is deterministic,
               so a packed output is unique, and for every x and y, e started on 2x stops with 2y
               exactly when F (e) does. (2) is the counterexample that follows.

               Proof of the theorem “No plain recursion on two counters” (book Theo-
               rem 29.15).
               Proof. (2) from (1): let F ( p) be a program that multiplies bin A by the constant c
               determined by the length of p. Multiplication by a fixed constant is the transfer loop
               of Appendix 6. Decoding a finite program, counting its lines, finding the least prime
               above a number and writing that loop are computable operations, so a program
               carries out F on encoded program numbers. A fixed point would multiply every
               plain input by the constant forbidden by (1).
                  For (1), suppose an n-line program did it. Split each state into the two bin counts
               and a finite control: the line (one of the n lines, or “stopped” for any line with no
               instruction), the trap, whether the channel is empty, and the table read entry by
               entry as a fact’s property, its counter, and whether it is current (its version is its
               counter’s version now) or stale. Only properties the program names can enter the
               table, at most n of them, so an entry is one of at most 4n kinds, and a table of at
               most sixteen entries is one of at most ∑k≤16 (4n)k ≤ (4n + 1)16 lists. So there are at
                                                                                            25


most Q = 4(n + 1)(4n + 1)16 controls. The control and the two counts decide the
next control and counts: a CHECK writes a current fact, a write to a counter makes
that counter’s facts stale, a COMMIT passes exactly when a current fact with its
property and counter is in the table, a CERTIFY passes exactly when the channel isn’t
empty, and the ledger, the flag, the channel’s contents and the versions themselves
never decide a branch. Call the control with the two counts a configuration.
    Pick a threshold h ≥ 1 above every number k in a property Ge(k) the program
names. At h or above, a count answers every question the program can put to it (is
it zero, is it even, is it at least k, can a DEC take one off) the same way as any other
count of the same parity at h or above. Call a control together with the parities of
both counts a pattern; there are at most 4Q = N of them.
    The forced run. A stage start is a configuration with one count below h, in the
exact bin, and the other at h or above, in the large bin, which holds v. From it, run
the program while pretending the large bin can never fall below h: follow the exact
bin exactly, the large bin only by its parity, and keep a tally of the large bin’s net
change. This forced run depends on the control, the exact count and the parity of v,
and not on v itself. The real run does exactly what the forced run does, with the
large bin at v plus the tally, up to the first step where v plus the tally drops below h,
the crossing. A move changes a count by at most one, so at the crossing the large
bin holds exactly h − 1.
    What a forced run does. One of three things, fixed by the stage start. (a) It stops.
(b) It comes back to a step with the same control, the same exact count below h and
the same parity of the large bin as an earlier step; it’s deterministic in those, so it
repeats that stretch forever, and the exact count stays bounded. (c) Neither. Then
it has at most 2Qh steps with the exact count below h, one per combination, so
from some step on the exact count stays at h or above for good. Among any N + 1
consecutive steps of that tail, two have the same pattern, at steps t1 < t2 ≤ t1 + N,
and between them the exact count changes by some δ, even because the parities
match. If δ < 0, the run from t2 repeats the run from t1 with the exact count
lowered by |δ| each time round, so after finitely many rounds it goes below h, which
the tail never does. So δ ≥ 0, and the stretch repeats forever, lifted by δ each time
round, never dipping below h. In every case but (a), then, the forced run from some
step on repeats one stretch forever, and each time round the exact bin changes by a
fixed g and the large bin by a fixed amount; in (b) g = 0, and in (c) the stretch is at
most N moves long.
    What the real run does, once v is large enough for that stage start, meaning the
large bin can’t reach the crossing before the forced run stops or its repeating stretch
has gone round once in full. If the forced run stops, the real run stops the same
way, with the exact bin at a count fixed by the stage start and the large one at v
plus a fixed amount. If the large bin doesn’t lose each time round, the real run
26   3 Proofs, the program and the worked tables for “The universal machines”


               never crosses and never stops. If it loses and g = 0, the real run crosses with both
               bins below a bound fixed by the stage start. The remaining case is a transfer: each
               time round the large bin loses m > 0 and the exact one gains g > 0. That’s case
               (c), so the stretch has at most N moves, each moving one bin by at most one, and
               m, g ≤ N; both are even. Now the stage rule. Start the same stage with v + m
               in place of v. The parity is the same, so the forced run is the same, and since the
               crossing comes after the stretch has gone round once, the bin needs exactly one
               more time round to get down to h − 1. So the real run crosses one time round later,
               with the same control, the formerly large bin at h − 1, and the other bin larger by g.
               With v + km it crosses k times round later, the other bin larger by kg. After a transfer,
               the crossing is a new stage start with the bins’ jobs swapped. For example, a loop
               that takes two pebbles from the full bin and puts four in the other has m = 2 and
               g = 4: two more pebbles going in buy one more trip round the loop, and four more
               coming out.
                   Choosing the input. Apart from v, a stage start is one of finitely many things:
               a control, an exact count below h, a parity, and which bin is which. Take one
               bound V ≥ h that’s large enough, in the sense above, for every one of them, and
               above every count that was called fixed or bounded above: the exact bin of a stage
               that stops, and both bins at a crossing with g = 0. The configurations with one
               count below h and the other below V are finitely many. If the program multiplied
               every input by c, no two different inputs could pass through the same configuration,
               because everything after it, the output included, would be the same, while cx ̸= cx ′ .
               So only finitely many inputs pass through that finite set. Pick an input x that passes
               through none of it and has cx above V. Since ( x, 0) is itself a configuration, x ≥ V.
                   Following that input. The run from ( x, 0) starts a stage: bin B exact at 0, bin A
               large at x. Every later stage start along the run has its large bin at V or above, or the
               run would pass through the finite set. The run stops, since the program multiplies,
               so no stage runs forever. No stage crosses with g = 0, since that crossing is in the
               finite set. So each stage before the last is a transfer, with its own pair (mi , gi ), and
               the last one stops. It stops with its exact bin below V and its large bin at its v plus
               a fixed amount, and the output cx is above V, so the output bin A is the large one.
               There are finitely many stages, because each takes at least one step of a run that
               stops.
                   The comparison. Put D = 2 ∏i mi and run the input x + D. It is x plus a multiple
               of m1 , so by the stage rule the first stage crosses at the same configuration except
               that the other bin is larger by ( D/m1 ) g1 = 2g1 ∏i≥2 mi , which is a multiple of m2 .
               So the second stage crosses the same way, with the next bin larger by 2g1 g2 ∏i≥3 mi ,
               and so on through every stage. The last stage starts with its large bin larger by
               2 ∏i gi , an even amount, so it stops the same way with bin A larger by exactly that.
               So the outputs from x + D and from x differ by 2 ∏i gi . But multiplication by c
                                                                                              27


makes them differ by cD = 2c ∏i mi . Cancel two:
                                     c ∏ m i = ∏ gi .
                                          i           i

Empty products are 1. Every factor on the right is between 1 and N, and c is a prime
above N, so it divides none of them, and a prime that divides a product divides one
of its factors. So c can’t divide the right side. That’s the contradiction.

One slot, done by hand. Do one slot by hand. The property “even” has number
1. Checking it on value 4 puts ⟨1, 4⟩ = (2 · 4 + 1)21 = 18 in a separate slot. To
read that pair, halve 18 until what’s left is odd: one halving gives 9, so the property
number is 1; subtract one from 9 and halve to recover value 4. The host checks that
property of that value. A second register, the mirror, holds 2: the property number
plus one. COMMIT can read this second register while leaving the checked slot
untouched.
   When the guest changes the counter that fact concerned, nudge the slot 18 →
19 → 18 and set the mirror to zero. The number is back where it was, but the
version went up twice, so the host’s old fact about that slot is stale. Putting a number
back doesn’t put an old check back. The mirror is what tells the search to leave that
slot alone, and if the search tried it anyway, the version rule would refuse it.
     Act                                      slot value   slot edition   searchable mirror
     Check evenness of encoded 4                  18             v                2
     Guest writes its counter: INC slot           19          v+1
     DEC slot; clear mirror                       18          v+2                0

The program, register by register and job by job. The program. I’ll describe U
the way Turing would, one register at a time and then one job at a time. Registers
0 and 1 hold the guest’s A and B. Register 2 holds the guest’s whole program as
one number: each instruction gets a number (an opcode paired with its operands,
INC as 0, DEC as 1, HALT as 2, CHECK as 3, COMMIT as 4, CERTIFY as 5), and the
list of them is packed with ⟨·, ·⟩, the empty list as 0. Register 3 is the guest’s line.
Registers 4 to 13 are scratch. Registers 14 and 15 count how many CHECKs on A
and on B have passed. Registers 16 to 47 are mirrors, sixteen for each guest counter,
and registers 48 to 79 are the slots, sixteen for each guest counter. The guest’s table
holds sixteen facts in total, across both counters, and all sixteen can be about one
counter, so the host gives each counter room for all of them. The reservation is the
host’s, and the guest’s own capacity stays sixteen in all. Register 80 is a dead end
that’s never written.
    Each guest step goes like this. At line 1 the program copies the program number
and the guest’s line into scratch, putting the originals back as it goes, because
copying a counter on this machine means emptying it into two others and refilling
28   3 Proofs, the program and the worked tables for “The universal machines”


               it. If the guest’s line is 0, or past the end of the program, it stops. Otherwise it peels
               instructions off the front of the copy, one per line, until it holds the one at the guest’s
               line, and splits that into opcode and operands. Then a chain of DECs on the opcode
               sends it to one of six handlers.
                • INC c: INC the guest’s counter c, nudge every slot about c and zero its mirrors,
                  add one to the guest’s line, back to line 1.
                • DEC c j: DEC the guest’s counter c. If it was 0, add one to the guest’s line. If it
                  wasn’t, nudge every slot about c, zero its mirrors, and set the guest’s line to j.
                  Back to line 1.
                • HALT: stop.
                • CHECK p c: let k be the count of passed CHECKs on c. If k is below 16, build
                  ⟨number of p, value of c⟩ in slot k of c, run CHECK Slot on that slot, set its mirror
                  to the number of p plus one, add one to the count and to the guest’s line, and go
                  back to line 1. If k is 16, run CHECK Slot on the dead end, which holds 0, which
                  is no pair, so it traps, just as the guest’s seventeenth CHECK does.
                • COMMIT p c: look through the sixteen mirrors of c for one equal to the number
                  of p plus one. At the first match, run COMMIT Slot on that slot. With no match,
                  run COMMIT Slot on the dead end, which traps.
                • CERTIFY: run CERTIFY, add one to the guest’s line, back to line 1.
                  Why the mirrors? COMMIT has to find the slot holding the claim, and it can’t
               read the slot to find out, because reading would make the fact stale. So beside
               every slot sits a mirror the program is allowed to read: the property’s number plus
               one while the slot’s fact is live, 0 once the guest has written the counter. If a guest
               CHECK fails its property, the host’s CHECK on the slot fails too, since the slot holds
               exactly the pair the guest checked. If the guest table is full across both counters, the
               host’s own sixteen-entry table is full as well, even when this counter’s slot count is
               below sixteen; its CHECK then traps for lack of room. So both kinds of refusal come
               through.

               The two tally lines for the surcharge. Keep two tally lines for a guest whose
               first three moves each cost one and whose reading first says yes after the third. The
               guest’s totals are 1, 2, 3. This compiler pays one PAY on each of the first two moves,
               then its three-act chain: the host totals are 1, 2, 5. The excess is two, added once. If
               the third move instead costs four, the host runs the chain and one PAY there; both
               totals then read 6. These are counts of charged instructions, not counts of all the
               work needed to decode the guest. Stopping at zero throws away what the chain
               paid beyond the move’s price. A compiler that kept it as credit against the moves
               after the raise would bring the surcharge back to zero once the guest had paid three
                                                                                              29


from the raising move on, and a guest whose reading starts at yes would be matched
exactly from its third unit of cost on. I keep the simpler rule, and every surcharge
stated below is this compiler’s.

A worked replay, with its table. Try CHECK, COMMIT, CERTIFY, CERTIFY on a
true claim. The ledger is four, the table holds one fact, the flag is up and the trap is
down, so the replay allowance is two. The replay pays CHECK, COMMIT, COMMIT,
CERTIFY: the same ledger of four and the same summary, in a different order. The
theorem claims that agreement and no more.
            Run        checks    commits    certificates   total paid   final flag
            Original     1          1            2             4           up
            Replay       1          2            1             4           up
    Each table has one fact and each channel is nonempty. The counts of the
individual acts differ; the selected final summary agrees. In the replay, the trap latch
is up exactly when the fifth number is 1, and the flag exactly when the fourth is
positive.

A worked packing, with its table. Try input 2 while the simulated counters hold
1 and 2. The pile is 17 · 2 · 9 = 306. Raising the first simulated counter doubles it to
612; lowering the second divides it by three, to 204. Strip off every factor of two
and three and 17 remains; subtract five and divide by six, and the original input 2 is
back.
 Act                              packed pile        simulated counters       input factor
 Load input 2; counters 1, 2    306 = 17 · 2 · 32          (1, 2)                  17
 Increment first counter        612 = 17 · 22 · 32         (2, 2)                  17
 Decrement second counter       204 = 17 · 22 · 3          (2, 1)                  17
 Strip factors 2 and 3                17                 discarded                 17
 Subtract 5; divide by 6               2                                    input recovered
CHAPTER 4




Running it

Everything so far is definitions and proofs about them. Does any of it run? This
appendix is me finding out. I built the small machine twice, once straight out of the
definitions and once by hand from the same description, ran both, and compared
the whole state after every step. Then I ran the universal programs on guests small
enough to finish, and real programs through the part of the compiler that executes.
    One rule holds for the whole appendix: the runs test the builds, and the proofs
carry the theorems. A run shows what a build did on the inputs it was given, and no
theorem in the book leans on one: every theorem in it stands on its argument, on
the page.
    There are two counts to keep apart as you read. The ledger counts what the price
list charges. The number of interpreter steps counts the work this particular build
does to carry the run out. One guest step can take millions of host steps and add
one to the ledger.
  Stage                     What the next stage must preserve
  Source orders             Variable values and completed procedure calls
  Assignments and jumps     The same branches, loops and final answer
  Counter blocks            Values at block boundaries; spare counters restored
  Host instructions         The same result, with the opposite DEC convention trans-
                            lated
  Packed      two-counter   The result as an exponent in the packed counter
  guest
  Fixed interpreter UP      Guest counters at matching points; decode the exponent
                            to read the answer
This is a path through the compiler, not a claim that one step at each stage takes
the same time. The source compiler inserts no certification chain. Running the
executable uses physical hardware and energy. These runs did not measure heat per
host step, and the ledger is not a heat measurement. The times only tell you which
runs are practical on this build. The table shows what ran and what didn’t.
