4.1 Taking the coat off

The first executable is generated straight from the formal definitions, the way you’d
strip the coat off a proof and keep the program underneath. The second was written
separately, by hand, from the same description. Both implement the small machine,
the host with a register for every whole number, the host with PAY and the loaders,
and both run the universal programs; their instruction lists the second build reads as
data printed from the definitions, so it checks them by running them, not by writing
them again. The compiler below has an executable runner too.
    Whole numbers matter here. A machine word in one build is 63 bits wide, and the
guest codes U reads go up past 2100 , so ordinary fixed-width arithmetic would wrap
around and lie. The mathematical machine never overflows, so its executable uses
integers of any size, with the arithmetic chosen to match the definitions: subtraction
stops at zero and division by zero gives zero. What sits between the definitions and
the running code, the mappings, the compilers and the routines that read input and
write output, is code I had to trust.


4.2 A second machine, written by hand, to check the first
Comparing two builds step by step catches one of them disagreeing with the descrip-
tion, on the runs I tried. They’re compared on the whole state after every step: the
program line, every counter and register and its version, the fact table in order, the
channel, the trap latch, the ledger and the flag. The comparison covers exhaustive
sets of short programs, random programs and both hosts, and they agree on every
32   4 Running it


              tested step. U and UP on tiny guests are compared at fifteen checkpoints two
              hundred steps apart, over the first three thousand host steps of each run, instead of
              after every step, and the longer runs below against a separate guest implementation,
              once per pass of the loop and at the stop. I also broke the machine on purpose,
              giving it a fact table one too small, a CHECK, COMMIT and CERTIFY that cost
              nothing, a commitment that ignores versions, and a write that doesn’t bump the
              version, and each break gets caught. A second set pins the boundaries this book
              draws in words, and those runs come out as the words say: two runs that end in the
              same state with different first certifications, a check that can’t fail certifying from
              every start, a CHECK and six commitments costing seven without a flag, a write
              after the commitment and a flag over a claim that’s now false, sixteen copies of one
              CHECK filling the table, a CERTIFY costing one at a ledger already at two, and a
              merge between two yes-states that leaves the entering state alone.
                 A mistake both builds share gets past the comparison, and so does a mistake in
              the definitions themselves, since both builds follow them.


              4.3 U on a real guest
              Now the universal program, which is where I expected trouble. The guest I wanted
              to run first was the obvious one, a guest that goes the whole way: CHECK, COMMIT,
              CERTIFY, flag up, run inside U on the built machine. That isn’t what ran, and the
              reason is the way the guest is stored. U keeps the whole guest program as one
              number, a number that grows like two raised to the sum of the codes for the guest’s
              instructions (for a guest of one instruction it’s exactly a power of two), and it reads
              that number the only way a counter can, one unit at a time. So its speed depends
              on the code, and the code grows fast: a guest with one CHECK in it has code 224 ,
              and a guest with the whole chain has a code near 2106 .
                  So I came down to what could finish. The table below is what ran, on the
              executable host, compared against a separate guest implementation at every pass of
              the loop and at the stop: the host stopped exactly when the guest did, and at the
              stop showed the guest’s counters, trap latch, ledger and flag.
                               guest                    host       host steps   time
                               count A down from 2 U             25,953,317
                               one passing CHECK        U       402,654,059 618 s
                               one failing CHECK        U       201,327,155 305 s
                               one passing CHECK        UP      402,654,059 617 s
                               one failing CHECK        UP      201,327,155 313 s
                  The first row’s count is pinned exactly: if the build changed so that it moved by
              one step, the comparison would fail. The times are from one ordinary computer
              and are only there to give you the scale. The counts work out to about twelve host
                                                                           4.3 U on a real guest   33


steps for every unit of the guest’s code each time U goes round its loop, which it
does once for every guest step and once more to see that the guest has stopped:
the failing CHECK traps on its first pass, 12 × 224 and a little, and the passing one
takes two passes, 24 × 224 and a little. The growth is exponential in the size of
the guest. A guest with one CERTIFY in it would have code 232 , about fifty billion
host steps, and at the measured rate of about 650,000 steps a second it would take
something like twenty-two hours, nearly a day. The guest I wanted, with the whole
chain, has a code near 2106 , which at the same twelve steps a unit is about 1033
host steps for each pass; at the measured rate, with this encoding on that computer,
a run of its three steps comes to around 1020 years. So U on a guest that certifies is
proved, in Appendix C.1, and has never been run. Runs this long only fit in memory
at all because the build can repack the host’s register store in the middle of a run;
repacking changes how the registers are stored, not what they hold.
   What a run that long can be is counted. Four loop shapes do most of U’s work.
Count from the DEC instruction that tests the loop counter; any instructions before
that test are still counted separately. You can derive each shortcut from the arrows:
• In the emptying loop, a positive counter v takes one DEC back to its own test and
  becomes v − 1. At zero, one DEC exits. Thus E(0) = 1 and E(v + 1) = 1 + E(v),
  giving E(v) = v + 1.
• In the copying loop, a positive DEC jumps back one line to INC on a distinct
  destination, which returns to the test. Each unit moved therefore takes two steps,
  and the zero test takes one: C (0) = 1, C (v + 1) = 2 + C (v), so C (v) = 2v + 1.
• With two destination INCs before the test, each positive pass takes three steps.
  Thus D (0) = 1, D (v + 1) = 3 + D (v) and D (v) = 3v + 1. If the two destinations
  are the same register, it receives two increments per pass; the step count stays
  three.
• In the halving loop, two successful DECs on the source and one INC on the
  quotient return to the same test. After k = ⌊v/2⌋ such passes, 3k steps have
  removed 2k from the source and added k to the quotient. The remaining source
  is zero or one. Follow that final branch instruction by instruction.
   Each successful decrement or increment also raises its register’s version once.
The zero test does not write. So the same counts determine the version changes
as well as the counter values and next line. These loops neither charge nor change
the table, channel, trap or flag. Replacing one completed loop stretch by its derived
effect and count therefore preserves the whole endpoint and the number of steps.
Repeating that replacement throughout a run preserves both by addition.
   The numerical tally below applies those four rules and steps the other instructions
individually. It agrees with the three runs above that were also stepped all the way.
For the guest CHECK, COMMIT, CERTIFY, using the zero predicate on A, the passing
34   4 Running it


              start (0, 0) ends with the flag up and ledger three. From (5, 0) the first check fails
              and the guest stops with ledger one. Appendix 7 supplies the program blocks and
              their guest correspondence; the displayed totals count that particular encoding.
                             start                                        host steps
                             (0, 0)   3,894,222,629,847,358,066,688,933,266,198,944
                             (5, 0)     973,555,646,581,507,138,873,939,791,446,643
                  What that table shows is a count, not a run. Nothing stepped those 1033 instruc-
              tions, and the row in the table at the top of this section says so.
                  Those big counts come from this encoding and from reading it one pebble at
              a time. They measure the work this interpreter does, and that’s all they measure.
              Whether some other interpreter, or some other base, could do the same job fast is a
              separate construction with its own running-time analysis. Nothing measured here
              puts a floor under every way of doing it.


              4.4 From source programs to the host

              Running guests one CHECK at a time doesn’t tell you whether the built machine
              can do anything you’d recognise as a program. You’d want to hand it gcd, or a
              sort, written the way people write them, and get the answer out, and writing gcd
              by hand in INC and DEC is no way to find out. So the last piece of the build
              is a compiler. The source is a small language you could write by hand: whole-
              number variables, addition, subtraction that stops at zero, comparisons, if, while,
              and procedures that return several results, with the rule that a procedure can only
              call ones defined before it, so there’s no recursion. Each translation stage has a
              proof that it preserves the program’s result: the procedures are inlined, the result is
              flattened to assignments and jumps, that is compiled to Minsky’s counter machine
              through the counter-machine compiler, that to a program for the host machine, that
              to a two-counter guest, and the guest to a run of UP .


              Theorem 4.1 (The compiler is correct). For every source program with no recursion,
              and every input: the program computes y exactly when the runner (the bounded
              evaluator of the compiled host program) halts with y for some amount of fuel;
              exactly when the compiled host program halts with y; exactly when the two-counter
              guest halts with y; and exactly when UP running that guest halts with y, with its first
              counter at 0, its ledger at 0 and its flag down. So a terminating compiled program
              delivers its answer and certifies nothing. To certify a claim about that answer, a
              target program must add a CHECK, a COMMIT and a CERTIFY; this compiler does
              not insert that chain. The runner also takes exactly as many steps as the host
                                                               4.4 From source programs to the host   35


program does, with the rest of its fuel left over, and a run that doesn’t halt has used
all its fuel.

Proof. Six arrows and a runner, each argued below, and the statement is their
composition. Four of the arrows are between machines that take steps, and for those
one argument does both directions, so I give it first.
   The simulation argument. Take two machines S and T, each deterministic, where
S has a next state from every state inside its program, and a relation ∼ between
their states such that: a state of S inside its program is related only to states of T
inside its program, and a state of S outside it only to states of T outside it; and from
any related pair with S inside its program, one step of S is matched by one or more
steps of T that land related to S’s next state. Then from related starts, S halts exactly
when T does, and they halt related. Forward: start with the related pair. Follow the
stretch of T that matches S’s first step; its endpoint is the related start needed for
the second stretch. Keep joining stretches until S stops. There are only finitely many
steps to match, so this gives a finite run of T whose final state is related to S’s final
state. The latter is outside its program, so the former is outside its own. Backward:
suppose T halts after k steps and S doesn’t halt. Then S takes at least k + 1 steps
without leaving its program, the matched stretches give T a run of at least k + 1
steps all of whose stretch ends are inside its program, and T, being deterministic,
has only one run from its start, which halted at step k. That’s a contradiction, so S
halts, and by the forward direction it halts related to where T did.
   Unfolding the calls. Imagine a program written on cards. A call says “carry
out the orders on that other card, then come back.” Here a card may call only a
lower-numbered card. Card zero therefore calls nothing. Replace every call on card
one by the orders from card zero; then do card two, using the already expanded
lower cards, and continue. There are finitely many cards, and every replacement
has already been built. The process ends with calls replaced by actual orders.
   The variables need their own places too. Give each active procedure a block of
numbered boxes, its frame. If the caller’s block starts at box b and uses n boxes, put
the called procedure’s block immediately after it, starting at b + n. A call becomes
four pieces: copy the arguments into that new block; set its other boxes to zero;
carry out the expanded orders using those box numbers; copy the answers back into
the caller’s named boxes. A local variable numbered x in the called procedure is thus
the global box numbered b + n + x. No assignment reaches below the procedure’s
own block.
   Why does this preserve a terminating run? Begin with the lowest-numbered
procedure. It has no calls, so its expanded orders are its original orders with
the boxes renamed. Assignments change the same values, tests choose the same
branches, and loops repeat the same bodies. Now take a procedure whose lower-
36   4 Running it


              numbered calls have already been settled. Each replacement call builds exactly
              the arguments and zeros that the original call would receive. The expanded lower
              procedure returns the same answers, and the copy-back puts them in the same caller
              boxes. The caller’s surrounding orders then continue from the same values. Working
              upward through the numbered procedures settles the main program.
                  The comparison works backward as well. Whenever an expanded call finishes, its
              four pieces have run in their written order. The middle piece is the expanded lower
              procedure, whose terminating runs I have already matched to the original lower
              procedure. Its preparation and copy-back are just the original call’s input and output
              operations. Replace that finished four-piece execution by the call it implements. The
              surrounding assignments, branches and completed loop repetitions then give the
              same original execution. A finite execution in either description therefore gives a
              finite execution in the other, with the same answer. Neither can terminate when the
              other does not.
                  In symbols, matching a source arrangement e to the global boxes G in a frame
              starting at b means G (b + x ) = e( x ) for each local box x. The comparison above
              preserves that equality at the beginning and end of every translated statement.
                  Flattening. The flat code of a statement placed at address a: an assignment is one
              assignment; a sequence is the first statement’s code followed by the second’s; if b is
              a jump on false to the else part, the then part, a jump past the else part, and the
              else part; and while b with a body of l instructions is a jump on false to a + l + 2,
              the body at a + 1, and a jump back to a. Every jump in the code of a statement
              lands inside that code or at its end, the address just past it. Now follow a completed
              structured execution. An assignment performs the same change; a sequence joins its
              two runs; a branch follows the same test answer. For each loop iteration, the test
              falls through, the body’s code takes the run to a + l + 1, and the jump returns it to
              a. Repeat for the remaining iterations; the final false test jumps to the end. Thus
              an execution from variables e to e1 gives a flat run from address a to the end of the
              code with those same final variables.
                  Read a completed flat run in the other direction. Because no jump escapes
              past the code’s end, a run that leaves it must visit that end first. Assignments and
              branches recover their structured operations immediately; a sequence splits at the
              second statement’s first address. For a loop, the run’s first step is the test. If it is
              false, the run jumps to the end, and the loop’s derivation is the one with a false
              test. If it is true, the run enters the body’s code, which it can leave only through
              its end at a + l + 1 (the body’s own backward statement), where the jump returns
              it to a with a strictly shorter run left. Read the next iteration the same way. This
              cannot continue beyond the finite run I started with: its final test must be false,
              giving the last piece of the structured loop execution. So the flat program at address
                                                                 4.4 From source programs to the host   37


1 halts exactly when the structured statement has a derivation, with the same final
variables.
    Counter machine. Here a DEC jumps when its register is zero and goes on
otherwise. Variable x lives in register x + 1, register 0 is kept at 0, so DEC 0 j is a
plain jump to j, and registers above the variables are working space. The relation:
register 0 is 0, registers 1 to n hold the flat variables, and every register above is
0. Each flat instruction becomes a block built from small loops. Follow one pass
and then count how many pebbles remain to be moved: Emptying a register: each
pass takes one off, and the loop exits at zero. Moving s into d: each pass takes one
off s and adds one to d, so s + d never changes, and at exit s is 0 and d holds the
old sum. Copying: move s into two registers at once, then move one of them back.
Cut-off subtraction t − u: take one off each while both are positive; at exit one of
them is 0, and the other holds the difference, or 0. Comparison: take one off each
in step; whichever reaches zero first, or both together, decides < and =, and the
block empties both. Build a larger expression from these smaller jobs. A constant is
a fixed number of increments into an empty working register. A variable is copied
there with its original restored. For a sum or cut-off subtraction, compute the two
smaller expressions in separate working registers, combine them with the loops just
described, and empty the temporary registers. Starting with the innermost pieces
and working outward, the final value lands in register t, the registers above t return
to zero, and those below t are left alone. A test computes its comparison in the
same spare registers, chooses the true or false exit, and clears the spare registers
on either exit. Combining tests follows the selected exits of their smaller tests, each
of which has already restored the registers. Thus a test leaves the registers as it
found them and changes only which address comes next. So an assignment block
computes the value into the first working register, empties register x + 1, moves
the value in, and lands related; a jump on false runs the test’s code with the true
exit at the next block and the false exit at the block of j; a jump is DEC 0 j. The
linker places the blocks one after another and sends every jump to the start of its
target’s block. That’s the one-step matching. The flat machine has a next state from
every address in its program, and an instruction that names a variable outside the
frame just advances, so the step is total. The counter machine is deterministic. The
simulation argument gives both directions: the counter program from the loaded
inputs leaves its code with register out + 1 equal to y exactly when the flat program
halts with y in out.
    Host. The host’s DEC is the other way round: it jumps when its register is positive,
taking one off, and goes on at zero. An INC x stays INC x. A DEC x j at host address
a becomes three instructions: DEC x ( a + 3), then INC 0, then DEC 0 j′ , where j′ is
the host address of j’s block. If register x is positive, the first takes one off and jumps
to a + 3, the start of the next block, which is what the counter machine does. If it is
38   4 Running it


              0, the run goes on to INC 0, which raises register 0 to one more than it held, and
              DEC 0 j′ finds it positive, takes the one back off and jumps to j′ , which is the counter
              machine’s jump at zero, with register 0 as it was. The relation: the same registers,
              at corresponding addresses. Each counter-machine step is matched by one or three
              host steps. The host program has no CHECK, COMMIT, CERTIFY or PAY, and INC
              and DEC change only a register and the line, at no cost, so every host state in the
              run has the start’s facts, channel, trap latch, ledger and flag. The counter machine’s
              step is total, the host is deterministic, and the simulation argument gives: the host
              program halts with register out + 1 equal to y exactly when the counter program
              leaves its code with that register equal to y.
                   Guest. The host program’s registers r0 , . . . , rk−1 are packed into the guest’s
                                  r
              counter B as ∏i pi i , one prime pi per register, with counter A the spare. That is the
              packing of Appendix 6.3, with the two counters’ jobs swapped. The relation: the
              guest is at the start of the block for the matching instruction, B holds the code of
              the registers, and A is 0. An INC on register i is the stretch that multiplies B by
              pi ; a DEC on register i is the stretch that divides B by pi with remainder and takes
              the positive branch when the remainder is 0, the zero branch otherwise, restoring
              B. The appendix argues each stretch: it ends, because each of its loops empties
              the counter it counts down; it lands with A at 0; and, by unique factorisation, it
              changes the exponent of pi alone, by the right amount. The host’s step is total on
              these programs and the guest is deterministic, so the simulation argument gives:
              the guest halts with B coding registers whose register out + 1 is y, that is, with y
              the exponent of that register’s prime in B, exactly when the host program halts with
              that register at y. The guest has no record instruction, so its ledger stays 0 and its
              flag down.
                   UP . UP keeps pace with its guest the way U does, with the PAY case added (the
              proof of Theorem C.7). This guest has no PAY and no record instruction, so on it UP
              runs only branches U has, and the argument of Theorems C.2 and C.3 goes through:
              from a freshly loaded host UP halts exactly when the guest does, with its registers 0
              and 1 holding the guest’s A and B, and its ledger and flag equal to the guest’s. So it
              halts with register 0 at 0, register 1 the code with y in it, ledger 0 and flag down.
                   The runner. The runner keeps the host program and the registers in binary tries,
              a tree indexed by the bits of a number. Read the address one bit at a time: a zero
              chooses one branch and a one the other. Looking up an address follows that route
              to its stored value, or returns the default zero if no value was stored there. Storing
              at an address follows the same route and changes its final value, creating missing
              branches as needed. Every other address either branches off before that end or
              names a different node, and keeps its value. Thus the tree performs the same lookup
              and single-address update as the register map it represents. So each round of the
              runner’s loop does exactly what one host step does to the same registers, and the
                                                              4.4 From source programs to the host   39


runner stops when the line leaves the program or the fuel is spent. If it halts after k
rounds, the host run of k steps is halted at the end of the program with the runner’s
registers, and the fuel left is the fuel given less k. If the source program has a
derivation, the arrows above give a halting host run, of some length k, and the host,
being deterministic, has only that run; any fuel of at least k makes the runner take
those k steps and stop. If the runner hasn’t halted, it used every unit of fuel on host
steps that stayed inside the program, so the host is still running after that many
steps, and a derivation, which would give a halting host run of some length k, would
need k larger than the fuel.
   Composing: the program computes y exactly when the unfolded program does,
exactly when the flat program halts with y, exactly when the counter program does,
exactly when the host program does, exactly when the guest halts with y coded in B,
exactly when UP halts as stated, and exactly when the runner halts with y on some
fuel.
   The theorem says nothing about how many steps the compiled program takes
next to the source; it’s about answers.
   The runner and the compiler are implemented, and the build has been run on a
set of programs I picked to be annoying: gcd, factorial, a primality program written
with procedures, an Ackermann tower, a sorting network on a list packed into one
number, and a tiny interpreter for another machine written in the source language.
Then come 220 random programs that halt, 160 random programs with loops that
might not, programs that never halt run for a fixed budget, and programs mutated
on purpose to make sure the comparison notices. Answers are compared with
direct arithmetic and separate interpreters of the source and compiled programs.
Ackermann’s function at (3, 5) is 253; the source program takes 64,768 operations
to get there, and the compiled host program takes about 119 million steps. The
executable runner does a few million host steps a second, so that’s under a minute.
   That works out to about 1,800 host steps per source operation for Ackermann,
and up to about a hundred thousand for multiplication by repeated addition, because
every addition copies a running total one unit at a time. On this build the work
grows with the size of the numbers being handled, because every operation is one
pebble at a time. How it grows with the size of the program I haven’t measured.
The last two stages, the two-counter guest and UP running it, are proved and have
never been run, and UP on a compiled guest would be slow for the same reason U
on a certifying guest is. Still, I like that 253. It came out of nothing but INC and
DEC, and a proof that they mean what the source meant.
40   4 Running it


              4.5 What wasn’t built
              There’s no chip. The model is not a circuit, and the lack of one says nothing about it.
              The example was never cut into metal: there’s no circuit for the small machine, no
              simulation of one at the level of gates, and no board ever ran any of this. The small
              machine exists as mathematical definitions and executable implementations that
              agree on every tested step. That’s the whole of what “it runs” means in this book.
                 I’d love to see it in metal some day. But builds that can be held against each other
              on every step are worth a lot to me. A board with nothing to hold it up against isn’t,
              and that’s the trade I made.
CHAPTER 5




Constructing the quoting
operation

The proof in Section 13 uses a program that takes the code of a term and returns
the code of that code. Here’s how to build it.
   Draw the instruction “add one” on a card. Now make a second card describing
the first card’s marks, rather than carrying out the addition. The count has not risen:
you have described an instruction. Describe the second card in turn and you have
a description of a description. Quoting does that second job mechanically. In the
language below, take the variable of index 0. Its code is a tree with three lambda
nodes, then an application of variable 2 to the numeral for 0. Quoting that code
builds a description of those three lambdas and that application; it does not look up
variable 0 or execute the term it describes.
   Nothing in the operation lets a program inspect itself by magic: its input is an
ordinary tree encoded as a closed lambda term.
   The construction follows Forster and Smolka (2017), whose construction for L
has the same three syntax builders on Scott-encoded children, an auxiliary procedure
for numerals, and a quote procedure Q with the same specification, defined by the
same recursion on codes; the self-application that uses Q in Section 13 is their
fixed-point lemma, after Barendregt’s (1984) proof for the full lambda calculus.
What follows is my rebuilding of it, with the reductions written out.


5.1 Numerals and syntax as data

For readability, the formulas below give names to bound variables. Renaming a
bound variable changes nothing; the underlying syntax uses the de Bruijn indices
of Definition 20.1. Applications associate to the left. Write X ⇓ Y here for a finite
sequence of the weak call-by-value steps ending in the value Y.
   Scott numerals are the following closed values:

                        0 = λz.λs.z,      n + 1 = λz.λs.s n.



                                                  41
42   5 Constructing the quoting operation


               So a numeral selects either the supplied zero case or the supplied successor case,
               passing its predecessor to the latter. The cases below are values, so choosing one
               does not prematurely run the other.
                  The three syntax constructors, operating on already encoded children, are
                                            mkvar = λn.λv.λa.λl.(v n),
                                            mkapp = λp.λq.λv.λa.λl.a p q,
                                            mklam = λp.λv.λa.λl.l p.
               Each builder is a term of the language L of Definition 20.1. For a variable of index n,
               an application and an abstraction, respectively, define their codes by the following
               resulting values:
                                             ⌜#n⌝ = λv.λa.λl.(v n),
                                             ⌜ p q⌝ = λv.λa.λl.a ⌜ p⌝ ⌜q⌝,
                                             ⌜λp⌝ = λv.λa.λl.l ⌜ p⌝.
               In particular, mkvar n, mkapp ⌜ p⌝ ⌜q⌝ and mklam ⌜ p⌝ reduce to these three codes.
               A code is closed even when the term it describes has a free variable: the index of
               that variable is stored as a numeral. It is data inside the code, and the code itself
               has no free variable.


               5.2 Recursion without running under a lambda

               The quoting procedure will have to call itself on the smaller pieces of a description.
               There is a catch: this language works out an argument before handing it to a
               function. Hand over the recursive calculation itself and it starts calculating at once,
               before the receiving function has decided whether it needs it. That can send you
               straight back into the same calculation forever.
                   Instead, hand over a recipe waiting for an ingredient. In this language a lambda
               is such a recipe, and it counts as a finished value even though its body has not run.
               The receiving function can keep the recipe and call it when it reaches a smaller
               piece. In the expression below, the little recipe λa.x x f a is the part doing that job.
               The letter a names the argument it is waiting for; f names the work to do once the
               recipe and the current input have been handed over.

                             W = λx.λ f .λv. f (λa.x x f a) v,    rec( F ) = λv.W W F v.
               When F and u are closed values, beta reduction gives

                            rec( F ) u −→ W W F u −→∗ F (λa.W W F a) u = F (rec( F )) u.
                                                                            5.3 Quoting numerals   43


The recursive argument is a lambda, so it’s already a value. It starts work only when
F applies it to an argument. That is the whole reason this construction survives the
call-by-value rule.


5.3 Quoting numerals
Let Ej = ⌜#j⌝ for j = 0, 1, 2, and let Z = ⌜0⌝, obtained directly from the finite
syntax of 0 = λλ.1. Write mklamk ( X ) for k nested applications of the syntax builder
mklam to X; it is an abbreviation for constructing k lambda nodes, and the language
gains no new operation from it.
   Define

        FN = λr.λn. n Z (λk.mklam2 (mkapp E0 (r k))),          Q N = rec( FN ).
Follow the two branches. Zero chooses the already built description Z. A number
one larger than n chooses the other branch and hands it the predecessor n. That
branch first asks the same procedure to describe the predecessor. Then it puts the
description inside an application of variable zero and wraps two lambdas around it.
Those are precisely the marks in the numeral for n + 1.
   Zero therefore works. If the procedure describes n correctly, the wrapping just
described makes it describe n + 1 correctly. Starting at zero and taking one more
step proves the claim for every natural number. Every call on a positive number asks
about a smaller one, so it reaches zero and finishes. In the notation for termination
and returned value, the result is Q N n ⇓ ⌜n⌝.
   Do the smallest case by hand. Call the successor case G. Then Q N 1 steps to
FN Q N 1 and on to 1 Z G; the numeral hands its predecessor 0 to G, the recursive
call Q N 0 comes back as Z, and the builders return mklam(mklam(mkapp E0 Z )).
That is the code of λλ.(#0 0), which is 1.


5.4 Quoting terms

For a proposed recursive call r, define three handlers:
                 HV = λn.mklam3 (mkapp E2 ( Q N n)),
              H A (r ) = λp.λq.mklam3 (mkapp (mkapp E1 (r p)) (r q)),
              HL (r ) = λp.mklam3 (mkapp E0 (r p)).
Now set
               FQ = λr.λt.t HV ( H A (r )) ( HL (r )),    Q = rec( FQ ).

The handlers are lambdas and so are values. The Scott code of the input chooses one
of them and hands it the encoded children. Start at the leaves and build outward.
44   5 Constructing the quoting operation


               At each node the handler builds the syntax of that node’s encoding: Q ⌜t⌝ ⇓ ⌜⌜t⌝⌝.
               There are three kinds of node to consider:
                • For t = #n, the first handler constructs three lambda nodes over an application
                  of variable 2 to the quoted numeral. That is the syntax of λλλ.(#2 n), the code
                  of #n.
                • For t = p q, quote the two children first. The second handler constructs three
                  lambdas over variable 1 applied to those two codes. That is the syntax of the
                  application code.
                • For t = λp, the last handler constructs three lambdas over variable 0 applied to
                  the quoted child code. That is the syntax of the abstraction code.
                  Each recursive call works on a proper child of the finite input tree, or on a
               smaller numeral. No chain of calls can keep descending through a finite tree or
               decreasing a natural number forever. Each builder takes finitely many beta steps, so
               the construction finishes and returns the code just described.
                  The mkapp used in Section 13 is this builder. With Q and that builder written
               out, every piece of the self-referential term there is on the page.
CHAPTER 6




Computing with counters

Put a few beans in one cup and leave a second empty. Write a short list of orders:
add one, take one away if there is one, go to this line, stop. A finger on the page
keeps your place. Carry out the orders literally. The question in this appendix is how
to give the same job to another person with different equipment (a strip of paper,
stacks of cards, or a different set of cups) and get the same answer whenever the
first person stops.
    Agree on how the input is laid out and how to read the output before either
starts. One person must not quietly solve the problem while preparing the other’s
cups. The reductions in this appendix concern functions on natural numbers. To
compute a relation R(⃗x, y) means to halt with output y exactly when R(⃗x, y) holds,
and to produce only numerical outputs on halting runs. The machines below are
deterministic, so any relation they compute is the graph of a partial function. A
translation must preserve both the output and whether the run terminates. Its input
and output encodings are part of the translation.


6.1 Finite programs made from two instructions

A counter holds a natural number. INC adds one and advances to the next line.
DEC subtracts one and jumps to its named line when the counter is positive; at
zero, it leaves the counter alone and advances. A finite program uses finitely many
counters. An unconditional jump to j is INC r followed by DEC r j: it restores r’s
value and always jumps. A zero test is DEC r to a positive branch that first restores
r; its fall-through is the zero branch. These operations preserve values, not versions.
They are used here for computation, where there are no live facts to preserve.
    Try copying a cup with three beans. Put two empty cups beside it and keep a
supply of spare beans. Each time you remove a bean from the first cup, put one in
each of the other two. When the first is empty, move the third cup’s beans back into
it. Now the first and second each have three, and the third is empty. Try zero beans,
then five. At every pass one more of the original beans has been counted into both
other cups; that is why it works for any starting count, not just the three you tried.
These are the moves behind the copy C ( x, y, t) in the next appendix.

                                                  45
46   6 Computing with counters


                  Emptying a counter is a DEC loop back to its own line. Moving a pile decrements
              its source and increments its destination until the source is empty. Copying uses
              two such destinations and then restores the source from one of them. Addition,
              multiplication by a fixed natural number, and bounded loops are finite combina-
              tions of these moves. Division by a fixed positive number b needs only b control
              states: decrement the input, cycle through remainders 0, . . . , b − 1, and increment a
              quotient counter whenever the cycle returns to zero. On exit the quotient is in that
              counter and the remainder is in the control state.


              6.2 A tape in finitely many counters

              Cut a strip into numbered squares and put a finger on one square. Lift the squares
              on its left into a stack, nearest on top, and do the same on its right. Moving your
              finger one place right is now two acts: put the square you were reading on top of
              the left stack, then take the top of the right stack as the new square. Write a new
              mark before moving and the stack keeps the changed square. Move left and the two
              stacks exchange jobs. You can walk the strip either way without losing its order. An
              exhausted stack supplies a fresh blank square.
                  Now replace each stack by a count. Use digit 1 for a blank and digit 2 for a mark,
              with base 3. Push a mark onto an empty stack: its code is 2. Push a blank on top:
              its code is 3 · 2 + 1 = 7. Count seven beans in groups of three. The one left over
              says “blank on top”; the two groups say the remaining stack has code 2. Pop that
              and the marked square comes back. Multiplication pushes and division pops.
                  In general, number a finite tape alphabet by 1, . . . , q, including its blank, and put
              b = q + 1. Encode a finite stack whose top symbol is d and whose remaining stack
              has code n by bn + d; the empty stack is 0. A push is multiplication by b followed
              by d increments. A pop is division by b: the remainder is the top symbol and the
              quotient is the remaining stack. The digits are nonzero, so no nonempty stack has
              code 0. Popping an empty stack gives remainder 0, which is no digit; the control
              reads it as the blank and leaves the stack empty.
                  A tape configuration is a control state, the scanned symbol, and two stacks: the
              symbols to the left of the head, nearest first, and the symbols to its right, nearest first.
              The control state and scanned symbol have finitely many possibilities, so they fit in
              the program’s line number. To write d and move right, push d onto the left stack
              and pop the right stack for the new scanned symbol. A left move does the reverse.
              The arithmetic just described implements each transition in a finite terminating
              stretch of counter instructions. Extra stored blanks do not change the represented
              tape. A finite number of tapes uses two stacks per tape and finitely many scratch
              counters. Halting states translate to halting lines. Induction over tape steps proves
              the simulation; termination of every arithmetic stretch proves its converse.
                                                          6.3 Packing finitely many counters into two   47


    In the other direction a tape can hold the finitely many counter values as unary
strings separated by delimiters. INC appends a mark in the selected string; DEC
erases one when present and otherwise detects the empty string. The current
instruction is finite control. Each scan and rewrite terminates, so halting and the
output are preserved. Multiple tapes may be represented on one tape by a list of
tracks with marked head positions: a complete scan reads the marked symbols, a
second scan writes their changes and shifts their markers. Extend the represented
interval by a blank cell when needed. A fixed-length binary code for the finite
alphabet implements each scan with binary symbols and finitely many intermediate
control states. So this argument also applies to the single-tape binary halting
question.


6.3 Packing finitely many counters into two

Try holding two counts in one pile of eighteen beans. Count how many times you
can halve eighteen exactly: once. Count how many times you can divide it exactly
by three: twice. Reassemble the eighteen beans after each reading. Those are the
two stored counts, 1 and 2. Double the pile to 36 and only the first stored count
rises. Divide 36 by three to get 12 and only the second falls. Divide by three again:
four beans, storing 2 and 0.
    Try taking one more off that second count. Four beans do not divide evenly into
threes. The attempted division must restore the four and take the zero branch;
keeping only the quotient would corrupt the first count. This is why the procedure
below remembers the remainder. Two and three share no factor, so changing the
number of one kind of factor leaves the other alone. Assign another prime to each
extra count. An all-zero collection is represented by one bean: the empty product is
1.
    Let p0 , . . . , pk−1 be distinct primes. At the boundary between simulated instruc-
tions, keep
                                    k −1
                               A = ∏ pi i ,
                                           r
                                                 B = 0,
                                    i =0

where ri are the simulated counter values. The simulated line is finite control. The
representation is unique by prime factorization. There are enough primes for any
finite program: a prime divisor of 1 + ∏i pi is not among the primes already chosen,
so the list can always be extended.
    To simulate INC on ri , empty A into B, adding pi to B per decrement, then move
B back to A. The result is pi Aold and B = 0. To simulate DEC, divide A by pi using
the finite remainder cycle described above. On exit A = 0, B = q and the control
holds d, where Aold = pi q + d and 0 ≤ d < pi . If d = 0, the original number was
48   6 Computing with counters


              divisible by pi , so ri > 0. Move B to A and take the positive branch. Its exponent
              of pi has decreased by one and every other exponent is unchanged. If d > 0, then
              ri = 0. Restore A by moving B back with pi increments per unit, add d, and take
              the zero branch. Both branches restore B = 0. All loops terminate because their
              source counter decreases. Induction gives the represented run at every simulated
              instruction boundary, and no simulated instruction can conceal an infinite internal
              run.
                  This is universality with an encoding. With input n in the first simulated counter
              and the others zero, the packed input is 2n when p0 = 2. It is supplied to the
              two-counter machine as its input; the construction does not claim that two counters
              starting at (n, 0) can first manufacture 2n . That is precisely the distinction Schroep-
              pel’s theorem in Section 15 turns on, and the undecidability used by the one-counter
              theorem in Appendix 30 ranges over packed starts too. For a tuple of inputs, supply
              its product of prime powers. If a numerical output is needed in ordinary notation,
              the external decoding reads the designated prime’s exponent. A host with finitely
              many additional counters can perform this decoding itself by repeated fixed-prime
              division.


              6.4 Why halting has no decider

              Write two responses on a card: “If the predictor says STOP, keep going forever. If it
              says KEEP GOING, stop.” Suppose somebody offers a predictor that always finishes
              and always gets a program’s eventual behaviour right. Give it this very card, with
              these instructions for using its answer. Work through both possible answers. STOP
              makes the card keep going; KEEP GOING makes it stop. Waiting longer for the
              predictor does not create a third correct answer: its promise was to finish with one
              of the two.
                  The card can be a program because each response is a finite instruction, and
              waiting for and branching on the predictor’s answer are computable operations.
              Here is how to include the input explicitly. Finite tape programs can be written
              as finite binary words, and a program can interpret such a word by looking up
              the next transition and maintaining the simulated tape. Invalid descriptions may
              be assigned one fixed halting program. Suppose a terminating algorithm H ( p, x )
              decided whether the program described by p halts on x. Build a program D which,
              on input p, computes H ( p, p), loops forever if the answer is yes, and halts if it is no.
              Let d describe D. Either answer to H (d, d) contradicts what D (d) does. So no such
              deciding algorithm exists.
                  Translate a tape program and its input by the stack construction and then prime
              packing. Both translations are finite algorithms, and the translated two-counter
              run halts exactly when the tape run halts. A decider for two-counter halting would
                                                       6.5 Seven descriptions of the same computation   49


then give H. The same reduction, followed by Theorem 15.4, applies to the small
machine. Halting instances can be enumerated by simulating every description and
input for successively larger finite budgets. If the nonhalting instances could also be
enumerated, interleaving those two enumerations would decide each instance. This
proves the impossibility underlying the reduction formulation of Theorem 15.5.


6.5 Seven descriptions of the same computation
Here are the translations behind Theorem 30.6. Each one keeps the partial function,
including where it runs forever. The constructions are the familiar ones behind the
results of Minsky, Shepherdson and Sturgis, and Forster, Larchey-Wendling and their
coauthors; the encodings and the reason each translation works are given here.
    Binary stacks. A binary stack is a finite word with push and pop operations,
with a distinct empty case. Encode the empty word as 1 and pushing d ∈ {0, 1} as
n 7→ 2n + d. Division by two pops it, with 1 tested before division. Finite counters
implement these operations. Conversely two stacks hold a tape as above; encode
every symbol of a larger alphabet by a fixed-length binary block. A push or pop of
that block takes a fixed finite number of stack operations, and the control stores a
partially read block. A tape simulates counters, so the translations connect binary
stack machines and counter machines in both directions.
    The two decrement conventions. Some counter machines jump on zero and fall
through after a positive decrement; the convention of this book does the reverse. To
translate a conditional decrement, send its two outcomes to separate short blocks
that unconditionally jump to the intended destinations. In this book’s convention
an unconditional jump is the restoring INC/DEC pair above. Under jump-on-zero
that pair falls through, because the DEC finds the counter positive; there the jump
is a DEC on one extra counter that nothing ever increments, so it is always zero.
So either convention implements the other’s instruction, with the same final values
on the original counters and one finite stretch per step, and the translation into
jump-on-zero spends one counter more. Relabel each target to the beginning of
its translated block. This proves the equivalence of the two Minsky instruction
conventions without identifying their individual steps.
    FRACTRAN. A program is an ordered finite list of positive rational numbers. At a
positive integer N, multiply by the first fraction whose product with N is an integer;
halt if none applies. Counter machines simulate this by fixed-denominator division,
multiplication and an ordered scan of the finite list. For the reverse translation,
assign distinct primes pr to counters and qi to control states. Represent a counter
configuration by qi ∏r prnr . Every translated instruction uses a fresh intermediate
state prime, so its state marker never cancels out of a fraction. For INC r going
to j, use ti pr /qi , followed by the transfer q j /ti . Try it with a counter represented
50   6 Computing with counters


              by factors of 2, a starting line marked by 5, a destination marked by 7 and an
              intermediate marker 11. Ten beans, 5 · 2, mean line 5 with counter value 1. The
              fraction 22/5 takes ten to 44; 7/11 takes 44 to 28. Divide the result by its line
              marker 7: four remains, two factors of 2. The line has changed and the counter has
              risen by one. The intermediate 11 keeps the two parts of the instruction separate.
              For DEC r with positive target j and zero target k, use, in this priority order,
                                                    ti        ui
                                                         ,       ,
                                                   qi pr      qi
              with transfers q j /ti and qk /ui . All state and intermediate primes are distinct from
              the counter primes. Exactly one state marker is present. Fractions belonging to other
              states cannot apply. At state i, the first decrement fraction applies exactly when
              nr > 0; otherwise the second applies. The next fraction transfers the intermediate
              marker to the destination marker. A halting state has no outgoing fraction. Each
              counter step then takes two fraction steps, and the prime exponents preserve all
              counter values. Read the output as the exponent of the output counter’s prime.
                  Partial recursive functions. Compare two orders. “Try the first ten tickets” gives
              you a stopping point before you begin. “Try tickets in order until one passes” does
              not. If each test finishes, the second order can still keep you working forever because
              every ticket fails. If one test itself never finishes, you never reach the next ticket.
              Those are the differences between a bounded loop and the search used here. Start
              with zero, successor and projections; close under composition, primitive recursion
              and minimization. In minimization, search 0, 1, 2, . . . for the first argument where
              the tested function returns zero; every earlier test must terminate with a positive
              result. A counter program implements the initial functions directly. Disjoint scratch
              counters preserve arguments across composition. For primitive recursion it keeps
              the recursion index and accumulated result, increments the index, and runs the
              step program until it reaches the supplied bound. For minimization it runs the tests
              successively; an undefined test leaves the run there forever, as the definition requires.
              Induction on these constructions supplies a finite counter program for every partial
              recursive function.
                  Conversely encode a counter configuration by nested pairs ⟨m, n⟩ = (2n + 1)2m ,
              with a separate finite code for the line. Addition, multiplication, powers, bounded
              division and bounded search are primitive recursive: their loops have a supplied
              natural bound and can be written as recursion on that bound. Pair decoding is
              bounded by the encoded positive number. The next configuration and the halt test
              are then primitive recursive for any fixed finite program. The configuration after
              t steps is primitive recursion on t, keeping halted configurations fixed. Minimize
              the total halt test over t, then read the output counter. This is the program’s partial
              function, and it is undefined exactly when the program never halts.
                                                       6.5 Seven descriptions of the same computation   51


    The language L. Fold two instruction cards shut. One says “put three beans
in the output cup and stop”; the other says “keep adding beans forever.” A zero
input is to choose the first card. If your rule makes you carry out both cards before
choosing, you never deliver the three beans. You have to keep the instructions
folded until the choice is made. An abstraction does that job in weak call-by-value:
its body waits until the abstraction is applied. The Scott numeral definitions and the
delayed recursion construction in Appendix 5 give zero, successor, predecessor, case
distinction and guarded recursion as terms. A case on a numeral n can be delayed
explicitly as
                            n (λd.u) (λp.λd.(v p)) (λa.a) :

at zero it evaluates u, and at a successor it evaluates v on the predecessor. Both
branches are values until selected, so call-by-value does not evaluate the unused
branch. Composition is application; primitive recursion repeats a step on the
predecessor; minimization is a guarded search that evaluates each test before
moving to its successor. Build the term in the same order as the function: begin
with its initial functions, then use the corresponding term operation each time
composition, recursion or minimization is used. Each operation consumes the
results of the already built pieces exactly as the function definition requires. On
numerical inputs, a computation that finishes therefore returns the Scott numeral
of its answer. If a required subcomputation never finishes, evaluation waits for it;
if minimization never finds a successful test, its search keeps going. In either case
there is no resulting value.
    In the other direction, encode a finite term as a finite string or as the nested-pair
code of its syntax tree. Reading the outer constructor, finding a variable index,
renaming bound indices and substituting a finite term are terminating traversals
of finite trees. A tape algorithm performs them with an explicit stack of unvisited
children. For weak call-by-value evaluation it evaluates an application’s function and
argument, then substitutes the argument value in the abstraction’s body; it never
reduces inside an unapplied abstraction. Each operation is a finite tape computation.
Induction on a terminating evaluation gives a terminating tape run with its value;
reading a terminating run backwards gives the evaluation tree. Scott numerals are
decoded by counting successor constructors. So an L computation of a numerical
relation is a tape computation of it.
    The tape and counter translations, the two counter conventions, binary stacks,
FRACTRAN, partial recursive functions and L now lie in one connected family of
translations. Composing the translations preserves their input/output relation. None
of them keeps time, space, or a ledger of what was paid just because it keeps a
partial function. Where I need a ledger kept, I build that in on purpose, as the
universal program of Appendix 7 does; time and space I don’t claim at all.
52   6 Computing with counters


              6.6 Searching for a finite computation
              Give yourself one token and run one instruction of the program you want to test.
              If it has not stopped, reset its starting arrangement, give yourself two tokens and
              try again. Then three. A program that stops after seven instructions is found on
              the seven-token attempt. Seeing six attempts fail says only that it has not stopped
              within six instructions; it does not tell you it runs forever.
                  The evaluators used in Appendix 29.2 work by that search. A program and a
              natural budget determine a finite simulation. Enumerate budgets 0, 1, 2, . . ., and
              stop at the first one that exhibits a halting run, returning its output. If no such
              run exists, the search continues forever. Encoding a finite program, decoding an
              instruction, and performing one step use the finite operations above, so this search
              is itself a partial recursive function and has a finite counter program. The host can
              execute that program instruction for instruction, using as many registers as the
              construction requires. Its computation uses only INC, DEC and HALT, so it adds
              nothing to the ledger and leaves the fact table, channel and flag alone. This supplies
              the evaluators and program transformations used in the host’s s-m-n and fixed-point
              arguments.
CHAPTER 7




The universal program in blocks

Put the guest’s instructions on one sheet and the host’s on another, with a finger
marking the current line on each. The guest takes one instruction. The host takes
as many of its smaller instructions as it needs, then returns to the head of its sheet.
Compare the two arrangements there: counts, live facts, commitment, flag and
ledger. The host is allowed to do more work between comparisons. It is not allowed
to lose a fact, revive a dead one, or charge another unit for its bookkeeping. The
lists below let you carry out that comparison instruction by instruction.
    Here is the finite list of instructions that is U. A block is a list, not an extra
instruction: replace every block name by its list, place the lists consecutively, and
resolve each label to its instruction number. The first instruction is at address 1.
Within a block, o is its first address and o + k its offset k. Unless a branch says
otherwise, execution continues into the next block. The only final instructions are
INC, DEC, HALT, CHECK, COMMIT and CERTIFY, with the meanings and prices of
Definition 15.3 on the host’s registers (Definition C.2).


7.1 Arithmetic blocks

Write J ( a, p) for the two-instruction list INC a; DEC a p. It jumps to p and restores
the value of a. The following lists specify the other arithmetic blocks. Semicolons
concatenate lists; a named block in a list is placed at the next available address. A
target such as o + 3 refers to the enclosing block’s start, not the nested block’s start.

   Block             Expansion at address o
   Z(x)              DEC x o
   J0 ( x, p)        DEC x (o + 3); J ( x, p); INC x
   M( x, y)          J ( x, o + 3); INC y; DEC x (o + 2)
   M2 ( x, y, z)     J ( x, o + 4); INC y; INC z; DEC x (o + 2)
   C ( x, y, t)      Z (y); Z (t); M2 ( x, y, t); M (t, x )
   H ( a, x, p)      Z ( a); M( x, a); J ( a, o + 8); INC x; DEC a (o + 11); J ( a, p); DEC a
                     ( o + 7)


                                                       53
54   7 The universal program in blocks


                  Block                  Expansion at address o
                  P( a, x, y)            Z ( a); M2 ( x, a, a); INC a; M ( a, x ); J ( a, o + 22); M2 ( x, a, a);
                                         M( a, x ); DEC y (o + 13)
                  N ( a, x, y)           J0 ( x, 0); Z (y); J ( a, o + 8); INC y; H ( a, x, o + 7)


                  Their lengths, in the order Z, J0 , M, M2 , C, H, P, N, are 1, 4, 4, 5, 11, 12, 23, 20.
               The source and destination of a move are distinct; M2 may have equal destinations,
               as in P. In C the three registers are distinct; in H, a ̸= x; in P and N, all three are
               distinct.
                  Each claim about these blocks follows from a decreasing counter. Z decrements
               to zero. M adds the old source to the destination and empties the source; M2 does it
               to both destinations. So C leaves x unchanged, writes its old value to y, and leaves
               t = 0. J0 tests zero without changing the value: a positive counter is decremented
               and then restored, while zero takes the jump.
                  In H, first move the old x into a. Each complete pass removes two from a and
               adds one to x. So it leaves a = 0 and x = ⌊ xold /2⌋; an even old value jumps to p, an
               odd one falls through. In P, the first four blocks make x = 2xold + 1 and a = 0. Each
               subsequent pass doubles x while decrementing y, so it ends at x = (2xold + 1)2yold ,
               with a = y = 0. In N, zero jumps to address 0, which holds no instruction, so
               the run stops. A positive number is repeatedly halved until odd, counting the even
               divisions in y. So N sends x = ⟨m, n⟩ to x = n, y = m, a = 0. These invariants
               prove both the returned values and termination on the stated inputs.


               7.2 Selection, comparison and fetching
               The selector D ( x, t; p0 , . . . , pk−1 ) has length 3k. For i = 0, . . . , k − 1, place at offset
               3i the three instructions DEC x (o + 3i + 3); J (t, pi ). If the entering value is i < k,
               it takes target pi with x = 0. If it is at least k, it falls through with x reduced by k.
               This follows by counting the decrements before the first zero.
                   The equality block E( x, y; t1 , t2 , u; p) first makes copies C ( x, t1 , u) and C (y, t2 , u),
               occupying offsets 0 to 21. Its remaining list is
                                                      22 : DEC t1 (o + 29),
                                                      23 : J0 (t2 , p),
                                                      27 : J (u, o + 32),
                                                      29 : DEC t2 (o + 22),
                                                      30 : J (u, o + 32),
                                                      32 : Z (t1 ); Z (t2 ).
                                                                  7.3 Registers and instruction codes   55


The listed offsets count expanded instructions, so the block has length 34. The
temporary registers are distinct from each other and from x, y. The two copies count
down together. If they reach zero together, the block jumps to p; if one reaches
zero first, it clears both and falls through. So it preserves x, y, leaves its temporary
counters zero at either exit, and branches exactly on equality.
   For fetching, define F (q, l; w, k, h, a, t; p0 , pe ) by the list
                                  0:    C (q, w, t),
                                 11 :   C (l, k, t),
                                 22 :   J0 (k, p0 ),
                                 26 :   DEC k (o + 27),
                                 27 :   J0 (w, pe ),
                                 31 :   N ( a, w, h),
                                 51 :   J0 (k, o + 56),
                                 55 :   DEC k (o + 27).
Its length is 56. Register q holds a list encoded by 0 for empty and ⟨head, tail⟩ for a
nonempty list, and l is a line number. The scratch registers are distinct and separate
from q, l. A zero line takes p0 . Otherwise decrementing the line copy once makes
k = l − 1. Each pass removes the next list head into h and its tail into w. If k = 0,
that head is the selected instruction and the block exits; otherwise it decrements k
and repeats. An exhausted list takes pe . The original program and line values are
restored by the copies. Every call terminates: each complete pass decreases k, or
exits.


7.3 Registers and instruction codes

Put Ti = i + 4 for 0 ≤ i < 10. For bank c ∈ {0, 1} and slot 0 ≤ k < 16, put
       Rc = c,    Qc = 14 + c,     Vc,k = 16 + 16c + k,    Sc,k = 48 + 16c + k.

Register 2 is the program, register 3 its line, and register 80 is the dead end. V
are the mirrors, S the slots, and Q the counts of successful checks in each bank.
Property codes are 0 for zero, 1 for even, and n + 2 for at least n. The code of an
instruction is the pair of its opcode and operand: INC has opcode 0 and operand c;
DEC has opcode 1 and operand ⟨c, j⟩; HALT has opcode 2 and operand 0; CHECK
has opcode 3 and operand ⟨c, p⟩; COMMIT has opcode 4 and that same operand
form; CERTIFY has opcode 5 and operand 0. A program is their list code. These
rules specify the loaded number in register 2.
    A bump of slot S and mirror V is INC S; DEC S to the following instruction; Z (V ).
It has length 3. It leaves the slot’s value alone, increases its version by two, and
56   7 The universal program in blocks


               empties the mirror. Let Bc concatenate the sixteen bumps of bank c, in increasing
               slot order; its length is 48.


               7.4 The fifteen blocks of U
               The following table fixes the order, start address and expanded length of every major
               block. Labels in the definitions below mean these addresses; H without arguments
               denotes the head at 1 and K the halt block at 98.
