                   The head is F (2, 3; T0 , T1 , T2 , T3 , T4 ; K, K ); Z ( T0 ); N ( T3 , T2 , T5 ); D ( T5 , T4 ; 109,
               221, 98, 369, 2441, 3725); J ( T4 , K ). Fetching supplies an instruction code in T2 .
               Unpacking leaves its operand in T2 and its opcode in T5 . The six-way selector
               chooses its handler. The final jump handles an invalid opcode, though a loaded
               program has none. The halt block is Z ( T0 ); · · · ; Z ( T9 ) followed by HALT.
                   INC’s bank selector is D ( T2 , T4 ; 117, 169); J ( T4 , K ). Each other bank selector first
               runs N ( T3 , T2 , T6 ), leaving the property code or jump target in T2 and the bank in
               T6 , then selects its two bank blocks with D ( T6 , T4 ; bank 0, bank 1); J ( T4 , K ).
                   The INC block for bank c is INC Rc ; Bc ; INC 3; J ( T4 , H ). The DEC block, starting
               at o, is DEC Rc (o + 5); INC 3; Z ( T2 ); J ( T4 , H ); Bc ; Z (3); M ( T2 , 3); J ( T4 , H ). A
               zero counter follows the first return, advancing the guest line without bumping slots.
               A positive counter jumps over it, bumps the bank and installs the target line.
                                                                             7.5 Why the blocks simulate a guest   57


   A CHECK slot block for (c, k ) is the following 60 instructions after expansion:
              C ( Rc , T8 , T4 ); C ( T2 , T9 , T4 ); P( T3 , T8 , T9 ); M( T8 , Sc,k );
              CHECK Slot Sc,k ; Z (Vc,k ); M ( T2 , Vc,k );
              INC Vc,k ; INC Qc ; INC 3; J ( T4 , H ).
The CHECK bank block at o is C ( Qc , T7 , T4 ), then a selector on T7 whose sixteen
targets are o + 62 + 60k, then CHECK Slot 80 and J ( T4 , K ), then the sixteen slot
blocks. Its length is 11 + 48 + 1 + 2 + 16 · 60 = 1022. The selected unused slot
starts at zero, so the move into it installs exactly the pair of property code and guest
value. The host’s own table has capacity sixteen: if it is full, its CHECK traps even
when this bank’s count is below sixteen. So the capacity is shared between the
banks, just as it is in the guest. A count of sixteen in one bank already fills the table,
so the dead-end check and the full-table trap are the same case.
   A COMMIT slot block is COMMIT Slot Sc,k ; Z ( T2 ); INC 3; J ( T4 , H ), of length
5. The COMMIT bank block at o first increments T2 , making the wanted mir-
ror value one more than the property code. It then runs, for k = 0, . . . , 15,
E(Vc,k , T2 ; T7 , T8 , T4 ; o + 548 + 5k ). After those comparisons come COMMIT Slot 80
and J ( T4 , K ), followed by the sixteen slot blocks. Its length is 1 + 16 · 34 + 1 + 2 +
16 · 5 = 628. The comparison reads the mirror, leaving the slot untouched. Its first
match commits that slot’s current fact; absence of a match reaches the dead end and
traps. The CERTIFY block is CERTIFY; INC 3; J ( T4 , H ), of length 4.
   These expansions determine every instruction and target. Adding the fifteen
lengths gives 3728. There are sixteen ordinary CHECK sites and one dead-end
CHECK in each bank, sixteen ordinary COMMIT sites and one dead-end COMMIT in
each bank, and one CERTIFY site: 2 · 17 + 2 · 17 + 1 = 69 paid sites. Every other
instruction costs nothing.


7.5 Why the blocks simulate a guest
Run a case that catches more than a wrong sum. Start the guest with four beans
in A and run CHECK Even A. Put its fact on a card saying “even, version 0.” The
host copies the value into an unused slot as the pair (Even, 4), whose code is
(2 · 4 + 1)21 = 18, and checks that slot. Put one unit on each ledger.
    Now let the guest add one bean and take it away. Its pile is four again, but its
version is 2. The old fact still says version 0. On the host, each of those guest writes
bumps the slot by one and back: 18 to 19 to 18. The slot’s value ends where it
began, while its version rises and its mirror is cleared. Try COMMIT Even A using
the old check. Both machines must refuse, even though four is even. Both pay one
more unit for that failed attempt. A translation that merely compares the piles at
the end would miss the failure this experiment is designed to catch.
58   7 The universal program in blocks


                   Start again and leave out the two bean moves. CHECK, COMMIT, CERTIFY must
               succeed on both sheets, raising both flags for exactly three units. The longer list of
               host instructions adds nothing to the ledger for copying or looking up a line. These
               two runs test the reason for keeping both values and versions. The argument for all
               runs has to keep the following correspondence at every return to the head.
                   At the head, registers 0, 1 and 3 give the guest counters and line. Each successful
               guest CHECK has one host slot fact holding its property and checked value. Its
               mirror is the property code plus one while the guest fact is current, and zero after the
               guest counter has been written. The matching host slot fact is current in exactly the
               first case. Count registers say how many slots have been used in their bank; unused
               slots and mirrors are zero. Tables have corresponding entries in the same order, and
               channels name corresponding entries. The ledgers and flags agree. Loading a guest
               establishes every part of this relation.
                   Fetching and dispatching terminate and preserve that relation’s stored values.
               For INC or a positive DEC, the guest writes its counter; the selected bank’s bumps
               invalidate exactly its former slot facts and clear their mirrors. A zero DEC writes
               nothing and bumps nothing. These cases add nothing to either ledger. For CHECK,
               an unused slot receives the exact pair tested by the guest. Both checkers answer
               the same, and the tables have the same length. A passing check appends the
               corresponding fact and updates its mirror and count. A false check or a full table
               traps both machines, after the same single paid act. For COMMIT, a nonzero mirror
               matches exactly a current guest fact with the requested property and counter. Its
               host slot has not been read or changed, so the corresponding COMMIT succeeds.
               If none matches, both machines trap. Again exactly one unit is paid. For CERTIFY,
               channels are either both empty or both hold corresponding commitments, so the
               instruction either traps both or raises both flags, for one unit.
                   All arithmetic between these paid acts terminates by the block invariants. A
               normal case returns to the head; a halting or trapped case stops. No arithmetic block
               has a paid instruction. This proves the one-step correspondence and bounds every
               intermediate host ledger between the guest’s old and new ledgers. Induction proves
               the run correspondence of Theorems C.2 and C.4. It also proves the reverse halting
               implication: an infinite guest run entails infinitely many nonempty host stretches,
               and a running guest instruction cannot produce a stopped host except in its own
               halting or trapping case.


               7.6 Adding PAY

               For UP , use opcode 6 with operand 0 for PAY, append a PAY handler to the six
               handlers, and append its block after CERTIFY. The block is PAY; INC 3; J ( T4 , H ),
               of length 4. The head’s selector now has seven entries and is three instructions
                                                                                7.6 Adding PAY   59


longer. Every block after the head then starts three addresses later. Resolve targets
to those new addresses; the head itself remains at address 1. The new PAY block
starts at 3732. The total length is 3728 + 3 + 4 = 3735 and there are 69 + 1 = 70
paid sites. The interpretation of Slot uses the priced host’s property language
from Appendix C.2; the arithmetic blocks and their invariant do not depend on
the particular meaning of a property code. A PAY guest step is one PAY host step
surrounded by arithmetic that costs nothing, so the same ledger argument applies.
CHAPTER 8




The CHSH derivation, in full

Appendix 32 tells the story; this appendix does the algebra. Each item gives the
statement with its hypotheses and the steps you need to check it. Nothing here says
that a matrix is a physical state. Where quantum strategies come in, I say exactly
how they stand to the matrices.


8.1 Setting and notation

Start with four piles of tally sheets, one for each pair of settings Alice and Bob can
be given. On each round their answers either agree or disagree. Give agreement a
score of +1 and disagreement a score of −1. In each pile, add the scores and divide
by the number of rounds. That pile’s average is its correlator. It says how strongly
the two answers agree for that pair of settings.
   For example, three agreements and one disagreement give a total of two over
four rounds, hence an average of one half. The four averages make a little square
table: Alice’s setting chooses the row, Bob’s chooses the column. The score I will
study adds three cells of that table and subtracts the bottom-right one. Here are the
names for those quantities.

Definition 8.1 (Settings, outcomes, correlators). Alice receives a setting x ∈ {0, 1}
and Bob a setting y ∈ {0, 1}. Each answers a bit, recoded as an outcome a, b ∈
{+1, −1} (bit 0 becomes +1, bit 1 becomes −1). The correlator Exy is the average
of the product ab over the rounds with settings ( x, y). The CHSH quantity is
                             S = E00 + E01 + E10 − E11 .

I collect the four correlators into the 2 × 2 matrix
                                                  !
                                          E00 E01
                                   E=                ,
                                          E10 E11

whose rows are indexed by Alice’s setting and whose columns by Bob’s. The rows of
E are ( E00 , E01 ) and ( E10 , E11 ); the columns are ( E00 , E10 ) and ( E01 , E11 ).


                                  60
                                                                            8.2 The classical bound   61


Definition 8.2 (Counters). There are eight counts: for each setting pair, samexy
counts rounds whose two answer bits matched and diffxy counts rounds whose bits
differed. A round adds one to exactly one of the eight. Write
                                                                           Dxy
        Nxy = samexy + diffxy ,      Dxy = samexy − diffxy ,       Exy =       .
                                                                           Nxy
    A matched round contributes ab = +1 and a differing round ab = −1, so Dxy
is the sum of the products and Dxy /Nxy is their average. Since | Dxy | ≤ Nxy , every
correlator from counters lies in [−1, 1]. When a bucket is empty, its correlator is
defined as 0. The score is E00 + E01 + E10 − E11 .


8.2 The classical bound
Write a fixed plan on a card: Alice’s answer to setting zero, her answer to setting one,
and Bob’s two answers. Each answer is a sign, plus or minus. Before multiplying
anything, look at Bob’s pair. Either the signs agree or they differ. If they agree,
adding them gives plus or minus two and subtracting them gives zero. If they differ,
the sum is zero and the difference is plus or minus two. One of those two quantities
vanishes; the other cannot exceed two in size. The next calculation uses exactly that
split.



Lemma 8.1 (Deterministic strategies). Let a0 , a1 , b0 , b1 ∈ {+1, −1}. Then a0 b0 +
a0 b1 + a1 b0 − a1 b1 is 2 or −2.

Proof. Factor: a0 b0 + a0 b1 + a1 b0 − a1 b1 = a0 (b0 + b1 ) + a1 (b0 − b1 ). If b0 = b1
then b0 + b1 = ±2 and b0 − b1 = 0; if b0 ̸= b1 the other way round. In both cases
exactly one of the two brackets is ±2 and the other is 0, so the expression is ±2
times a sign, which is +2 or −2. There are sixteen sign choices, and every one of
them gives exactly ±2. None gives 0 or ±4.
   You can also just list the sixteen sign choices and add each one up. Nothing in
the argument needs real numbers: the signs are whole numbers, so the bound holds
the same way whether you read the correlators as rationals (what a counter gives)
or as reals.
   A fixed card is only the first case. You can shuffle among several cards, perhaps
using some more often than others. Every card still tells each player an answer using
that player’s setting alone. The shuffle changes how often each card contributes; it
does not change the score that a particular card would produce. Since each card’s
score lies between minus two and two, their average must stay between those same
ends. The following definition writes out that weighted shuffle.
62   8 The CHSH derivation, in full


               Definition 8.3 (Finite factorizable model). Four real correlators E00 , E01 , E10 , E11
               are factorizable when there are a natural number λmax , weights p(λ) and re-
               sponse signs A( x, λ), B(y, λ) for λ = 0, . . . , λmax such that: 0 ≤ p(λ) ≤ 1;
               ∑λmax
                 λ
                   =0 p (λ ) = 1; every A ( x, λ ) and every B (y, λ ) is −1 or +1; and for all x, y,
               Exy = ∑λλmax
                          =0 p (λ ) A ( x, λ ) B ( y, λ ). In words: a hidden card λ is drawn with probabil-
               ity p(λ), and each player reads an answer off the card using only their own setting.
               Only the correlators are modelled.



               Theorem 8.2 (The classical bound for a finite local model). If E is factorizable,
               then −2 ≤ E00 + E01 + E10 − E11 ≤ 2.

               Proof. Substitute the factorization: S = ∑λ p(λ)Sλ , where Sλ is the deterministic
               expression of Lemma 8.1 at the signs A(·, λ), B(·, λ). Each Sλ ∈ [−2, 2], the weights
               are nonnegative and sum to 1, so −2 = ∑λ p(λ)(−2) ≤ S ≤ ∑λ p(λ) · 2 = 2.

               Remark 8.1 (Scope of the classical bound). Fine’s 1982 theorem is an equivalence
               between several descriptions of local models and the Bell inequalities. The preceding
               theorem proves one direction: a finite factorizable model obeys the bound. The
               converse of this single inequality is false: (1, 1, −1, 1) has S = 0, but it breaks
               another of the sign variants (E00 + E01 − E10 + E11 = 4), so no local model gives it.
               The correlation-form equivalence needs each correlator between −1 and 1 and all
               eight inequalities; with those, Section 33.10 builds the local model. The preceding
               theorem is stated for finitely many hidden cards (λ ≤ λmax ), and that loses nothing.
               Whatever the hidden variable is and however it is drawn, the correlators see it only
               through its four answer signs, so lumping together the cards that carry the same
               four signs gives at most sixteen cards and the same four correlators. All the lumping
               needs is a probability for each of the sixteen sign patterns.
                  The finite-tally statement concerns one fixed strategy replayed on every recorded
               round.

               Definition 8.4 (A tally consistent with one local plan). For bits a0 , a1 , b0 , b1 ∈ {0, 1}
               and a counter record w, w is locally consistent with ( a0 , a1 , b0 , b1 ) when, for each
               setting pair ( x, y): if a x = by then diffxy = 0, otherwise samexy = 0; and every
               bucket is sampled, N00 , N01 , N10 , N11 > 0.
                                                                        8.3 Tsirelson’s bound from algebra   63




Theorem 8.3 (A tally above 2 is not one replayed strategy). Let w be a counter
record whose rational score S(w) is greater than 2. Then for all bits a0 , a1 , b0 , b1 ,
w is not locally consistent with ( a0 , a1 , b0 , b1 ). Every locally consistent record has
S(w) = 2 or S(w) = −2; the first statement is that fact turned around.

Proof. Under local consistency each sampled bucket is pure: if a x = by every round
in the bucket matched, so Exy = Nxy /Nxy = +1; otherwise every round differed
and Exy = −1. In signs, Exy = α x β y with α x = (−1) ax and β y = (−1)by . The score
is then the deterministic expression of Lemma 8.1, which is 2 or −2. A score above
2 contradicts this.
   The theorem rules out one explanation, a single four-bit plan used on every
round. It says nothing about a local model that redraws its hidden state every round;
Appendix 32 gives a four-round coin-flip tally with score 4. The clause that every
bucket is sampled does work here. An empty bucket reads as 0, and the plan that
answers 0 to everything, never asked the pair 11, would score 1 + 1 + 1 − 0 = 3.


8.3 Tsirelson’s bound from algebra
Now put the cards aside and look just at four numbers in the square. I want to
bound their three-plus-one-minus score from their squared sizes. The useful trick
is to write the proposed allowance minus the squared score as a pile of squares.
A square cannot be negative. If the difference really is that pile, the score cannot
outrun the allowance.
    The identity below is the receipt for that trick. The letters a, b, c, d are the four
entries read across the first row, then across the second. It holds for any four real
numbers. After proving it, I will put in the two bounds on row length that this part
of the checker supplies.


Lemma 8.4 (The six-square identity). For all real a, b, c, d,
       4( a2 + b2 + c2 + d2 ) − ( a + b + c − d )2
          = ( a − b )2 + ( a − c )2 + ( a + d )2 + ( b − c )2 + ( b + d )2 + ( c + d )2 .

Proof. Expand the square on the left: ( a + b + c − d)2 = a2 + b2 + c2 + d2 + 2ab +
2ac − 2ad + 2bc − 2bd − 2cd. So the left side is
             3( a2 + b2 + c2 + d2 ) − 2ab − 2ac + 2ad − 2bc + 2bd + 2cd.
64   8 The CHSH derivation, in full


               On the right, each variable appears in exactly three of the six brackets (a in a − b,
               a − c, a + d; b in a − b, b − c, b + d; c in a − c, b − c, c + d; d in a + d, b + d, c + d),
               so the squared terms add to 3( a2 + b2 + c2 + d2 ). The cross terms are −2ab from
               ( a − b)2 , −2ac from ( a − c)2 , +2ad from ( a + d)2 , −2bc from (b − c)2 , +2bd from
               (b + d)2 and +2cd from (c + d)2 . Both sides agree term by term.
                                                                          2 for any four reals.
                   Since the right side is a sum of squares, S2 ≤ 4 ∑ xy Exy

                                                                                                 E11
                     2     2           2     2        2
               with E00 + E01 ≤ 1 and E10 + E11 ≤ 1, S ≤ 8. The absolute form |S| ≤ 8 = 2 2
               follows.

                                                            2 ≤ 2. By Lemma 33.3, S2 ≤
               Proof. Adding the two row bounds gives ∑ xy Exy
                       2 ≤ 8.

               Theorem 8.6 (Tightness). The point E √00 = E01 = E10 = 1/   2, E11 = − 1/   2
               satisfies both row bounds and has S = 8.
                                                                            √       √
               Proof.
               √      Each row has squared length 1/2 + 1/2 = 1, and S = 4/   2 = 2   2 =
                 8.

               Remark 8.2 (Where equality can happen). Equality S2 = 8 under the row bounds
               forces equality in both steps of the proof above: the six squares must all vanish,
               which gives a = b = c = −d, and the rows must be tight, which gives 2a2 = 1.
               So the point of Theorem 33.5 and its negative are the only places the bound is
               reached. A tally’s correlators are ratios of whole numbers and 2a2 = 1 has no
               such solution, so no tally reaches the bound; the integer check’s passing tallies stay
               strictly below it and come as near as you like. (Take rational a, b with a2 + b2 = 1,
               from a Pythagorean triple, and the correlators ( a, b, b, − a): they pass
                                                                                       √the check of
               Section 33.5 with no slack, and S = 2( a + b), which gets as close to 2 2 as you like
               as a/b approaches 1.)


                                                                         √
               Lemma 8.7 (A rational constant above the bound).              8 < 5657/2000.

               Proof. Both numbers are positive, so it suffices to compare squares.
               56572 = 32,001,649 and 8 · 20002 = 32,000,000, so (5657/2000)2 =
                                                          8.4 The zero-marginal 5 × 5 moment matrix   65

                                   √         √
32,001,649/4,000,000 > 8 = ( 8)2 . If 8 ≥ 5657/2000 held, squaring both
nonnegative sides would give 8 ≥ (5657/2000)2 , a contradiction.
                                                     √
   The constant is the four-decimal round-up of 2 2 = 2.8284271 . . .; the gap is
about 7.3 × 10−5 . It lets rational code test “above the Tsirelson bound” without a
square root. It plays no part in the integer check of Section 33.5.


8.4 The zero-marginal 5 × 5 moment matrix
The four averages fill only the Alice–Bob part of a larger table. Give that table five
labels: a constant reading 1, Alice’s two possible readings, and Bob’s two. Each cell
records an average product of its row and column readings. A reading multiplied by
itself is 1, so the diagonal is all ones. The four averages measured fill the cross-player
cells and their reflected partners.
    Other cells are still empty. This particular check fills them with zero: each player’s
average answer against the constant reading, and the cells between that player’s
two settings. These are choices of the check; the four cross-player averages alone
did not supply them. I will later let the two within-player cells vary and ask whether
any filling works. For now, write down this one fixed filling and test it.

Definition 8.5 (The pinned level-1 matrix). Take rows and columns
I, A0 , A1 , B0 , B1 . Put 1 on the diagonal and the four correlators in the Alice–
Bob cells, and set the four one-party entries (each player’s own average answer) and
the two within-party entries (A0 against A1 , B0 against B1 ) to zero. The resulting
matrix is:


                                        I A0 A1 B0 B1 
                                 I       1 0    0   0   0
                                A0     0 1     0  E   E   
                                                    00  01 
                   M( E) =             
                                A1     0 0     1 E10 E11 
                                                          
                                       0 E00 E10 1     0 
                                                          
                                B0
                                B1       0 E01 E11 0    1
   What do I ask of this table? Choose five numbers, one beside each row and one
beside the matching column. For each cell, multiply its entry by its row’s number
and its column’s number, then add all twenty-five results. Do this with any five
numbers you like, positive, negative or zero. I require the total never to be negative.
That requirement is called positive semidefiniteness, abbreviated PSD.
   Write the five chosen numbers as a column v. The superscript T turns a column
into a row, so ordinary matrix multiplication writes that same weighted total as
66   8 The CHSH derivation, in full


               vT Mv. The condition is therefore vT Mv ≥ 0 for every choice of v. The table is
               symmetric too: reflect it across its diagonal and the entries agree.
                  The first row and column have only one nonzero entry, the corner 1. Their
               contribution is the square of the first chosen number. The remaining four rows and
               columns can be tested on their own. Grouping them gives
                                                      !                         !
                                                1 0                   I2   E
                                      M( E) =             ,   G =                   .
                                                0 G                   ET   I2

                   Here I2 is the two-by-two identity table: ones on its diagonal and zeros elsewhere.
               The superscript on E again swaps rows and columns. The weighted total for M ( E)
               is the first number’s square plus the weighted total for G. If every total for G is
               nonnegative, adding a square keeps it nonnegative. If some total for G is negative,
               choose the first number to be zero and the same negative total appears for M ( E).
               Thus the two tables pass or fail together.
                   The next operation is completing a square, with several numbers at once. For
               one number, u2 + 2uw + rw2 becomes (u + w)2 + (r − 1)w2 . The first square
               cannot hurt, and choosing u = −w makes it vanish. So the whole expression stays
               nonnegative for every choice exactly when the remaining part does. The matrix
               formula below does the same job for two groups of numbers.
                   Here positive definite means that the weighted total for P is strictly positive
               for every nonzero choice of weights. In particular, P has an inverse, written P−1 :
               applying it undoes multiplication by P. The matrix left after completing the square
               is called the Schur complement. Its name is less useful here than the operation that
               produces it.


               Lemma 8.8 (Schur complement, the standard!form). Let P be symmetric positive
                                                    P Q
               definite. The symmetric block matrix        is PSD if and only if R − QT P−1 Q
                                                    QT R
               is PSD.

               Proof. For u, w of the right sizes,
                    !T          !      !
                  u      P Q         u
                                         = (u + P−1 Qw)T P (u + P−1 Qw) + wT ( R − QT P−1 Q) w.
                  w     QT R        w

               The first term is never negative because P is positive definite, and it is zero at
               u = − P−1 Qw. So the form is nonnegative for all (u, w) exactly when the second
               term is nonnegative for all w.
                                                              8.4 The zero-marginal 5 × 5 moment matrix   67


    The identity itself holds for any invertible symmetric P. One direction (block PSD
gives the complement PSD) needs only that: put u = − P−1 Qw. The other direction
is where P has to be positive definite. For the pinned matrix I don’t need the lemma
in the abstract: I carry out its one instance with P = I2 , Q = E, R = I2 , where the
complement is
                                                                           !
                                1 − E 2 − E2        −( E   E   + E   E   )
                                                        00  01    10  11
            I2 − ET E =               00    10
                                                             2 − E2          .
                            −( E00 E01 + E10 E11 )     1 − E01     11

The diagonal of ET E holds the squared lengths of the columns of E, which is why
the conditions below are column conditions.

Definition 8.6 (Column contractive). Write p = 1 − E00 2 − E2 , q = 1 − E2 − E2
                                                             10          01   11
and s = E00 E01 + E10 E11 . The correlators are column contractive when

                          p ≥ 0,       q ≥ 0,          p q − s2 ≥ 0.



Lemma 8.9 (The 2 × 2 test). If α ≥ 0, δ ≥ 0 and αδ − β2 ≥ 0, then αu2 + 2βuw +
δw2 ≥ 0 for all real u, w.

Proof. If w = 0 the form is αu2 ≥ 0. Otherwise put t = u/w, so the form is
w2 (αt2 + 2βt + δ). If α = 0, then − β2 ≥ 0 forces β = 0 and the bracket is δ ≥ 0.
If α > 0, αt2 + 2βt + δ = α(t + β/α)2 + (αδ − β2 )/α, a sum of two nonnegative
terms.



Theorem 8.10 (Contractive implies PSD). If p ≥ 0, q ≥ 0 and pq − s2 ≥ 0, then
M ( E) is positive semidefinite.

Proof. Write v = (v0 , x0 , x1 , y0 , y1 ). Reading off the entries of M ( E),

vT M ( E) v = v20 + x02 + x12 + y20 + y21 + 2x0 ( E00 y0 + E01 y1 ) + 2x1 ( E10 y0 + E11 y1 ).
Complete the squares in x0 and x1 :
         vT M ( E) v = v20 + ( x0 + E00 y0 + E01 y1 )2 + ( x1 + E10 y0 + E11 y1 )2
                        + p y20 − 2s y0 y1 + q y21 .
To check the last three terms, expand the two squares: they contribute ( E00 y0 +
E01 y1 )2 + ( E10 y0 + E11 y1 )2 beyond the cross terms, which is ( E002 + E2 ) y2 +
                                                                               10 0
2s y0 y1 + ( E01 + E11 )y1 ; subtracting it from y0 + y1 leaves p y0 − 2s y0 y1 + q y21 .
              2      2   2                        2    2           2
68   8 The CHSH derivation, in full


               The first three terms are squares. The last is the 2 × 2 form with α = p, β = −s,
               δ = q, nonnegative by Lemma 8.9.



               Lemma 8.11 (Discriminant). If a + 2bt + ct2 ≥ 0 for every real t, then b2 ≤ ac.

               Proof. If c < 0 the quadratic tends to −∞, so c ≥ 0. If c = 0, the line a + 2bt is
               nonnegative for every t only if b = 0, and then b2 = 0 = ac. If c > 0, evaluate at
               t = −b/c: a − b2 /c ≥ 0, so b2 ≤ ac.



               Theorem 8.12 (PSD implies contractive). If M ( E) is positive semidefinite, then
               p ≥ 0, q ≥ 0 and pq − s2 ≥ 0.

               Proof. Feed the quadratic form three test vectors, written in the order
               ( I, A0 , A1 , B0 , B1 ). Each is the choice x = − Ey that zeroes both completed
               squares in the proof of Theorem 8.10, so only yT ( I2 − ET E)y is left.
                    First, v = (0, − E00 , − E10 , 1, 0) gives vT Mv = p, so p ≥ 0.
                    Second, v = (0, − E01 , − E11 , 0, 1) gives vT Mv = q, so q ≥ 0.
                    Third, for each real t, v(t) = (0, −( E00 t + E01 ), −( E10 t + E11 ), t, 1) gives
               v(t)T Mv(t) = p t2 − 2s t + q ≥ 0. Apply Lemma 8.11 with a = q, b = −s, c = p:
               s2 ≤ pq.



               Corollary 8.13 (The equivalence). For all real E00 , E01 , E10 , E11 , the symmetric
               matrix M ( E) is positive semidefinite if and only if the correlators are column
               contractive.

               Proof. Theorem 8.10 is one direction and Theorem 8.12 the other.
                  The equivalence above is about the columns. The bound can go through either
               the rows or the columns: the six-square identity only needs the four squares to add
               to at most 2, and the two row bounds give that, and so do p ≥ 0 and q ≥ 0.


               Theorem 8.14 (PSD implies Tsirelson). If the pinned matrix M ( E) √      is symmetric

  Every deterministic plan has all four correlators equal to ±1, so p = 1 − 1 − 1 =

Remark 8.3 (What PSD of the pinned matrix means). M ( E) is the level-1 moment
matrix with the two within-party entries ⟨ A0 A1 ⟩ and ⟨ B0 B1 ⟩ pinned to zero. Its PSD
is sufficient for the correlators to admit some PSD completion (Section 33.7), and it
is not necessary: the all-ones plan has a completion but fails here. So a check built
on M ( E) is sound for the completion set and not complete for it, and it rejects some
honest classical tallies. That correlators with a PSD completion are exactly those
reachable by quantum measurements is Tsirelson’s theorem, and both halves are
proved here in finite dimension. For real and for complex amplitudes, a strategy’s
correlators are inner products of unit vectors (with complex amplitudes, use real
vectors of twice the length) and its level-1 moment matrix is PSD, so they have a
PSD completion. The other half, that every PSD completion comes from a quantum
strategy, comes from this construction: the strategy is built on two four-level systems
from the completion’s Gram vectors (Section 33.8).
70   8 The CHSH derivation, in full


               8.5 The integer check
               The machine has whole-number tallies. The three conditions above were written
               using averages, which are fractions. You can test the same conditions without
               ever dividing: first make sure each denominator is positive, then multiply it away.
               Multiplying by a positive number cannot turn a passing inequality into a failing one
               or a failing one into a passing one.
                  For the first column, the condition starts as one minus two squared averages.
               Multiplying by the product of their squared denominators leaves three whole-
               number products. I call that result A. The second column gives B the same way.
               The cross-column term is a sum of two products of averages; multiplying by all four
               denominators leaves a whole number I call C. The last condition becomes C2 ≤ AB,
               because both sides have had the same positive denominator removed. Four positive
               bucket sizes and these three comparisons make seven checks. Here are the products
               to calculate.

               Definition 8.7 (The seven-integer check). From the counters of Definition 33.2,
               with all arithmetic in Z, let
                                              2   2     2   2     2   2
                                         A = N00 N10 − D00 N10 − D10 N00 ,
                                              2   2     2   2     2   2
                                         B = N01 N11 − D01 N11 − D11 N01 ,
                                         C = D00 D01 N10 N11 + D10 D11 N00 N01 .
               The check is the conjunction of seven facts, in this order: 0 < N00 , 0 < N01 ,
               0 < N10 , 0 < N11 , 0 ≤ A, 0 ≤ B, and C · C ≤ A · B.

                   There is no division, no real number, no square root and no S in it.


               Lemma 8.15 (The seven facts are the three conditions with denominators cleared).
               Assume N00 , N01 , N10 , N11 > 0 and put Exy = Dxy /Nxy . Then, with p, q, s as in
               Definition 33.6,
                                        A                        B                     C
                              p=       2 N2
                                               ,       q=    2 N2
                                                                      ,     s=                   ,
                                      N00 10                N01 11
                                                                                 N00 N01 N10 N11
                                                                     AB − C2
                                                   pq − s2 =                        .
                                                               ( N00 N01 N10 N11 )2
               Because every denominator is positive, 0 ≤ A ⇐⇒ p ≥ 0, 0 ≤ B ⇐⇒ q ≥ 0 and
               C2 ≤ AB ⇐⇒ s2 ≤ pq.
                                                                            8.5 The integer check   71

                    2 /N 2 − D2 /N 2 ; multiply by N 2 N 2 to get N 2 N 2 −
Proof. For p: 1 − D00   00    10  10                00 10          00 10

C /( N00 N01 N10 N11 ) ; subtract. Multiplying an inequality by a positive number
preserves it in both directions.
   The check has the following guarantee:


Theorem 8.16 (Soundness of the integer check). If a tally w passes the seven-
integer check, then its four correlators Exy = Dxy /Nxy are column contractive.
Consequently its pinned matrix is positive semidefinite.

Proof. The first four tests say that every bucket contains a round, so each average
is its signed total divided by a positive count. The next two tests say that A and B
are nonnegative. Dividing each by its positive denominator gives p ≥ 0 and q ≥ 0.
The last test says AB − C2 ≥ 0. Divide that difference by the positive square of the
product of all four counts; the result is pq − s2 ≥ 0, as the preceding calculation
shows. Those are exactly the three column conditions, and Theorem 8.10 shows
that they make the matrix pass the nonnegative-total test.



Corollary 8.17 (A passed check implies the Tsirelson bound). If a tally w passes
the seven-integer check, the correlators of w satisfy S2 ≤ 8. It is the composition of
Theorem 33.10, Corollary 33.7 and Theorem 33.8.

   Two of the seven facts carry the bound by themselves. With every pair sampled,
0 ≤ A and 0 ≤ B are p ≥ 0 and q ≥ 0, so the four squared correlators add to
at most 2 and Lemma 33.3 gives S2 ≤ 8. The fact C · C ≤ A · B is what positive
semidefiniteness adds, and the bound doesn’t use it. For the form this takes on the
small machine, where a raised flag has to stand on a passing check, see Appendix 32.
72   8 The CHSH derivation, in full


                  A worked tally. Take (same, diff) = (4, 1), (4, 1), (4, 1), (1, 4) for the pairs
               00, 01, 10, 11. Then Nxy = 5 for all four, D00 = D01 = D10 = 3, D11 = −3,
               and the correlators are 0.6, 0.6, 0.6, −0.6 with S = 2.4. Compute:
                              A = 25 · 25 − 9 · 25 − 9 · 25 = 625 − 225 − 225 = 175,
                               B = 25 · 25 − 9 · 25 − 9 · 25 = 175,
                              C = 3 · 3 · 5 · 5 + 3 · (−3) · 5 · 5 = 225 − 225 = 0.
               All seven facts hold: four positive N, 0 ≤ 175 twice, and 0 ≤ 175 · 175. Dividing
               back, p = 175/625 = 0.28 and s = 0, the second row of the table in Section 33.4.
                   Two more, for contrast. One round per pair from the all-ones plan, (1, 0) four
               times, gives N = D = 1 everywhere and A = 1 − 1 − 1 = −1 < 0: the check
               fails. The tally (4, 1), (9, 1), (9, 1), (1, 4) gives correlators 3/5, 4/5, 4/5, −3/5,
               with N = 5, 10, 10, 5 and D = 3, 8, 8, −3: A = 25 · 100 − 9 · 100 − 64 · 25 = 0,
               B = 100 · 25 − 64 · 25 − 9 · 100 = 0 and C = 3 · 8 · 10 · 5 + 8 · (−3) · 5 · 10 = 0. It
               passes with no slack, at S = 2.8.


               8.6 Level 1+AB

               The first table had one place for each player’s answer. Enlarge it by giving each
               Alice–Bob product a place too. There are four such products, so the five places
               become nine. A cell still asks for the average product of its row and column labels.
               Some of those products reduce to entries I already have; five more numbers are
               needed for the particular filling below. Call them γ1 through γ5 . The longer labels
               say which products they stand for.
                  The five additional parameters name the moments γ1 = ⟨ A0 A1 B0 ⟩, γ2 =
               ⟨ A0 A1 B1 ⟩, γ3 = ⟨ A0 B0 B1 ⟩, γ4 = ⟨ A1 B0 B1 ⟩ and γ5 = ⟨ A0 A1 B0 B1 ⟩. These are
               labels for the entries; the definition below does not construct operators.

               Definition 8.8 (The level-1+AB matrix). The 9 × 9 real matrix on rows and columns
               I, A0 , A1 , B0 , B1 , A0 B0 , A0 B1 , A1 B0 , A1 B1 , with the following entries:
                                                                                                  8.6 Level 1+AB   73



                         I A0 A1 B0 B1 A0 B0 A0 B1 A1 B0 A1 B1
                  I      1  0   0  0   0  E00 E01   E10   E11
                 A0      0  1   0 E00 E01  0   0    γ1    γ2
                 A1      0  0   1 E10 E11 γ1  γ2     0     0
                 B0      0 E00 E10 1   0   0  γ3     0    γ4
                 B1      0 E01 E11 0   1  γ3   0    γ4     0
                A0 B0   E00 0 γ1 0 γ3      1   0     0    γ5
                A0 B1   E01 0 γ2 γ3 0      0   1   −γ5     0
                A1 B0   E10 γ1 0   0 γ4    0  −γ5    1     0
                A1 B1   E11 γ2 0 γ4 0     γ5   0     0     1
    Two constraints are fixed by this layout. First, the four-body cells carry opposite
signs: +γ5 at ( A0 B0 , A1 B1 ) and −γ5 at ( A0 B1 , A1 B0 ). In a real moment matrix
those two cells are ⟨ A0 A1 B0 B1 ⟩ and ⟨ A0 A1 B1 B0 ⟩, so the tie says ⟨ A0 A1 ( B0 B1 +
B1 B0 )⟩ = 0, which for a real matrix is ⟨{ A0 , A1 } ⊗ { B0 , B1 }⟩ = 0. The level-1
pinnings don’t imply it. Take a classical strategy: Alice’s two answers a0 , a1 are
independent fair signs, and Bob copies them, b0 = a0 and b1 = a1 . Every pinned
cell of its moment matrix is 0 and both four-body cells are 1, so no choice of the
nine numbers gives its matrix. So the matrix here is level 1+AB cut down to the
slice ⟨{ A0 , A1 } ⊗ { B0 , B1 }⟩ = 0, and that is what “level 1+AB” names in this book.
The slice holds whenever A0 and A1 anticommute. The soundness theorems below
are about this matrix, so they stand as stated. Second, the matrix keeps the level-1
pinning: every cell whose label reduces to a marginal or to ⟨ A0 A1 ⟩ or ⟨ B0 B1 ⟩ is zero,
for instance ( A0 , A0 B0 ), ( A0 B0 , A0 B1 ) and ( A0 B0 , A1 B0 ). The displayed matrix is
symmetric.
    I want to test this larger table with the same operation as before: put a weight
beside each row and column and add the weighted cells. The first three weights will
each be collected into one completed square. Everything involving the other six will
be left over. That leftover is what I call the residual; it is the part whose sign I still
need to settle.
    Write a vector as (v I , v A0 , v A1 , v B0 , v B1 , v00 , v01 , v10 , v11 ), where v xy is the coor-
dinate of A x By . Put

                          L1 = E00 v00 + E01 v01 + E10 v10 + E11 v11 ,
                          L2 = E00 v B0 + E01 v B1 + γ1 v10 + γ2 v11 ,
                          L3 = E10 v B0 + E11 v B1 + γ1 v00 + γ2 v01 .
74   8 The CHSH derivation, in full


               Definition 8.9 (The residual).
                                R = v2B0 + v2B1 + v200 + v201 + v210 + v211
                                      + 2v B0 (γ3 v01 + γ4 v11 ) + 2v B1 (γ3 v00 + γ4 v10 )
                                      + 2γ5 (v00 v11 − v01 v10 ) − L21 − L22 − L23 .
               The condition called column contractivity at level 1+AB is R ≥ 0 for all six real
               inputs.



               Lemma 8.18 (Three squares plus the residual). For every vector,
                               vT M9 v = (v I + L1 )2 + (v A0 + L2 )2 + (v A1 + L3 )2 + R.

               Proof. The coordinates v I , v A0 , v A1 appear in the form only through their diagonal
               squares and the cross terms 2v I L1 , 2v A0 L2 , 2v A1 L3 , read off rows I, A0 , A1 of the
               matrix. Completing each square adds L21 + L22 + L23 , which the residual subtracts.
               What is left in R is the diagonal of the remaining six coordinates and the cross terms
               among them, read off the lower right 6 × 6 block.



               Theorem 8.19 (PSD at level 1+AB). For all real Exy and γ1 , . . . , γ5 , the matrix of
               Definition 33.7 is positive semidefinite if and only if the residual R is nonnegative
               for every choice of its six real inputs.

               Proof. If R ≥ 0 everywhere, Lemma 8.18 writes the form as three squares plus R.
               Conversely, given the six residual inputs, choose v I = − L1 , v A0 = − L2 , v A1 = − L3 ;
               the three squares vanish and PSD gives R ≥ 0. This is the level-1 argument of
               Section 33.4 one size up.
                  As a quadratic form in (v B0 , v B1 , v00 , v01 , v10 , v11 ), numbered 1 to 6, the residual
               has the symmetric coefficient matrix H with these upper entries. Each entry can be
               read back off R by collecting the coefficient of vi v j .

   The full check does not decide R ≥ 0 directly. It tests the stronger fact that H is
positive definite, by two Schur steps and a Sylvester test, all fraction-free.


Lemma 8.20 (Fraction-free Schur step). For a symmetric n × n matrix h and any
vector v, with Sij = h11 hij − h1i h1j for i, j ≥ 2 (here S is the Schur matrix; the score
plays no part in this lemma),
                                                 2
                        h11 vT h v = ∑ h1j v j + ∑ Sij vi v j .
                                           j             i,j≥2

So if h11 > 0 and the (n − 1) × (n − 1) matrix S gives a nonnegative form, then h
does too. The cascade uses this identity at sizes six and five.

Proof. Expand the square: h211 v21 + 2h11 v1 ∑ j≥2 h1j v j + (∑ j≥2 h1j v j )2 . The left side
is h211 v21 + 2h11 v1 ∑ j≥2 h1j v j + h11 ∑i,j≥2 hij vi v j . The difference is ∑i,j≥2 (h11 hij −
h1i h1j )vi v j . Dividing by h11 > 0 keeps the sign.

               Definition 8.10 (What the integer check computes). The supplied moments arrive
               as bucket pairs: γk = Ngk /Dgk with Ngk = samegk − diffgk and Dgk = samegk +
               diffgk . Watch the letters: for these five buckets N names the numerator and D
               the denominator: the difference and the count, the reverse of Section 33.1. Let
               K = ( N00 N01 N10 N11 Dg1 Dg2 Dg3 Dg4 Dg5 )2 . The check computes the 21 integers
               KHij , the 15 integers of S(6) at scale K2 and the 10 integers of S(5) at scale K4 , each
               by h11 hij − h1i h1j on integers. The decider is the conjunction of: Nxy > 0 for the
               four pairs; Dgk > 0 for k = 1..5; − Dgk < Ngk < Dgk for k = 1..5; and six strict
                                                                (6)
               positivities, namely KH11 > 0, K2 S22 > 0, and the four leading minors of the
                                                                                     8.6 Level 1+AB   77


integer S(5) . The full check combines the seven-integer check of Definition 8.7 with
this decider.
   Each cleared integer, read as a real, equals the corresponding real quantity times
the stated positive power of K. A k × k leading minor of a matrix scaled by c > 0
scales by ck . So every integer positivity holds exactly when the real one does.
   Two parts of the full check are there to fail early, and no proof below needs
them. A passing decider makes H positive definite (Remark 8.4), and the top left
two-by-two block of H has p and q on its diagonal and −s off it, so p > 0, q > 0
and pq > s2 , and the seven-integer check passes. And with H positive definite, the
nine-by-nine form is zero only at zero: it is three squares plus R (Lemma 8.18), so
where it is zero the six inputs of R are zero, then so are L1 , L2 and L3 , and then so
are v I , v A0 and v A1 . So the nine-by-nine matrix is positive definite; with ones on its
diagonal, that puts every γk strictly between −1 and 1, which is the bucket bound
− Dgk < Ngk < Dgk .


Theorem 8.22 (The full 1+AB check implies PSD). Let the counts make every Nxy
and every Dgk positive, and let the decider just defined pass on them. Then the
matrix of Definition 33.7 at the four correlators and γk = Ngk /Dgk is symmetric and
positive semidefinite.

Proof. The bucket conjuncts make every denominator positive, the scaling remark
just above carries the six integer positivities over to the real cascade, and Lemma 8.20
twice plus Lemma 8.21 give R ≥ 0, and so positive semidefiniteness by Theo-
rem 33.12.

Remark 8.4 (Interior only). Every test in the cascade is strict, so a passing check
certifies that H is positive definite, which is stronger than semidefinite. A point
where H is PSD but singular cannot pass, and neither can a moment with |γk | = 1.
For example, the tally of Section 33.5 with correlators (3/5, 4/5, 4/5, −3/5) has
H11 = 1 − 9/25 − 16/25 = 0, so the full 1+AB check fails on it for every choice
of γ, though the level-1 check of Section 33.5 accepts it. The argument establishes
soundness only; no converse is stated.
   A worked example of the cascade in exact rational arithmetic. Take the correlators
(0.6, 0.6, 0.6, −0.6) and γ = (0, 0, 0, 0, −1/2); as buckets, γ1 to γ4 are (1, 1),
giving 0/2, and γ5 is (1, 3), giving −2/4, both inside the strict bounds. Then
H11 = H22 = 7/25, H12 = 0, and every entry H1j , H2j with j ≥ 3 is zero. So

8.7 The elliptope completion

Go back to the two within-player cells I filled with zero. Leave them blank this time.
Keep all four measured cross-player averages fixed, and ask whether there is any
way to fill those two blanks that makes the table pass. A failure with zeros no longer
settles the question; another filling may work.
     Call the first missing number x and the second y, and copy each into its reflected
cell so the table stays symmetric. The collection of averages that admit a passing
filling has a name, the elliptope completion set. Membership means that at least one
choice of those two numbers works. It does not require every choice to work, or
require the zero choice to work.

Definition 8.11 (Completion and membership). Complete the matrix of Sec-
tion 33.4 by setting the within-party entries to free reals: x in the ( A0 , A1 ) cells and
y in the ( B0 , B1 ) cells, marginals still zero. The correlators are elliptope realizable
when some x, y make the completed matrix symmetric and PSD.
80   8 The CHSH derivation, in full


                                                                                 
                                                       1 0        0      0     0
                                                     0 1         x     E00   E01 
                                                                                 
                                      M ( E; x, y) = 0 x         1     E10   E11 
                                                                                 
                                                     0 E00              1
                                                                                 
                                                                 E10           y 
                                                       0 E01     E11     y     1

                  Its quadratic form at v = (v0 , . . . , v4 ) is ∑k v2k + 2xv1 v2 + 2yv3 v4 + 2E00 v1 v3 +
               2E01 v1 v4 + 2E10 v2 v3 + 2E11 v2 v4 . The pinned matrix is the case x = y = 0, so PSD
               of M ( E) implies membership.


               Theorem 8.24 (Deterministic plans are inside). If a20 = a21 = b02 = b12 = 1, then
               ( a0 b0 , a0 b1 , a1 b0 , a1 b1 ) is elliptope realizable, with x = a0 a1 and y = b0 b1 .

               Proof. With these entries the form equals v20 + ( a0 v1 + a1 v2 + b0 v3 + b1 v4 )2 . Ex-
               panding the second square gives a20 v21 + · · · on the diagonal, which is v21 + · · · by
               the unit squares, and the cross terms 2a0 a1 v1 v2 = 2xv1 v2 , 2a0 b0 v1 v3 = 2E00 v1 v3 ,
               and so on for all six pairs. A sum of two squares is never negative.
                  The all-ones plan is one instance. The classical mixture (1, 0, 1, 0) has a comple-
               tion at x = 1, y = 0, with form v20 + (v1 + v2 + v3 )2 + v24 .


               Theorem 8.25 (Convexity and finite mixtures). For 0 ≤ λ ≤ 1, if E and F are ellip-
               tope realizable, so is λE + (1 − λ) F. For finitely many members with nonnegative
               weights summing to at most 1, the weighted sum is realizable. Every finite mixture
               of deterministic plans is realizable.

               Proof. The form is affine in ( E, x, y) for each fixed v. Take the completion (λx1 +
               (1 − λ) x2 , λy1 + (1 − λ)y2 ); the form at the mixture is the same mixture of two
               nonnegative forms. For several members, weight each one’s completed matrix by its
               assigned share and add them. If the shares total less than one, give the missing share
               to the zero-correlator point with both missing entries zero; its completed matrix is
               the identity. The diagonal of the weighted matrix is then one, its cross entries are
               the desired weighted correlators, and every weighted total is a sum of nonnegative
               totals with nonnegative weights. It therefore supplies the required completion. The
               last statement combines this with Theorem 8.24.
                                                                         8.7 The elliptope completion   81




Theorem 8.26 (Tsirelson’s bound holds on the completion set). If E is elliptope
realizable then S2 ≤ 8.

Proof. For a symmetric PSD matrix the bilinear form obeys Cauchy-Schwarz,
B(u, w)2 ≤ Q(u) Q(w), because Q(u + tw) = Q(u) + 2t B(u, w) + t2 Q(w) ≥ 0
for all t and Lemma 8.11 applies. Take u1 = e A0 and w+ = e B0 + e B1 :
B(u1 , w+ ) = E00 + E01 , Q(u1 ) = 1, Q(w+ ) = 2 + 2y. Take u2 = e A1 and
w− = e B0 − e B1 : B(u2 , w− ) = E10 − E11 , Q(w− ) = 2 − 2y. So c21 ≤ 2 + 2y
and c22 ≤ 2 − 2y with c1 = E00 + E01 , c2 = E10 − E11 , and both budgets are
nonnegative. Then c21 c22 ≤ (2 + 2y)(2 − 2y) = 4 − 4y2 ≤ 4, so c1 c2 ≤ 2, and
S2 = (c1 + c2 )2 = c21 + 2c1 c2 + c22 ≤ (2 + 2y) + 4 + (2 − 2y) = 8.
   The completion can make a row of E longer than 1 (the all-ones plan has rows
of squared length 2), which is why this proof does not use the row bounds of
Section 33.4.


Corollary 8.27 (The PR box is outside). (1, 1, 1, −1) is not elliptope realizable,
since its S2 = 16 > 8.



Theorem 8.28 (Strictly beyond the classical bound). (3/5, 3/5, 3/5, −3/5) is
elliptope realizable and has S = 12/5 > 2.

Proof. At x = y = 0 the form equals v20 + (v1 + 35 v3 + 53 v4 )2 + (v2 + 35 v3 − 35 v4 )2 +
 7 2        7 2                                                 2       9   9    18
25 v3 + 25 v4 . Check: the two middle squares give v3 coefficient 25 + 25 = 25 , plus
 7                                    18           18                               3
25 is 1; the v3 v4 terms are + 25 and − 25 and cancel; the cross terms are 2 · 5 v1 v3 ,
2 · 53 v1 v4 , 2 · 35 v2 v3 and −2 · 35 v2 v4 , as the entries require.
    By Theorem 33.1 no finite factorizable model has S > 2, so this point is not
a finite mixture of plans, while Theorem 8.25 puts every such mixture inside.
The completion set then strictly contains the finite classical set, and the PR box
lies outside it. It sits inside the no-signalling set, for the plain reason that every
correlator in it is between −1 and 1 (the vector that is 1 at A x and ∓1 at By gives
2 ∓ 2Exy ≥ 0), and any four such numbers come from the no-signalling box that
answers ( a, b) at settings ( x, y) with probability (1 + ab Exy )/4, each player’s own
answer alone a fair coin.
82   8 The CHSH derivation, in full


                  The elliptope gate is an integer decider with a soundness proof. Its completion
               entries arrive as bucket pairs, and an empty bucket is read as 0/1, so unlike
               Section 33.5 it does not require every pair to be sampled.

               Definition 8.12 (The two branches). The positive-minor branch: with the comple-
               tion supplied as buckets and all entries scaled to integers by a positive common
               factor K, the four leading minors of the 4 × 4 block on A0 , A1 , B0 , B1 are strictly
               positive. The factorization branch: with the completion and a rational certificate
               supplied (six subdiagonal entries over a common positive denominator, four pivots
               dk ≥ 0 over another), the ten entry equations of M = LDLT hold in cross-multiplied
               integer arithmetic. The full gate is their disjunction.



               Theorem 8.29 (Soundness of the gate). If the full gate returns yes on a counter
               record, any completion buckets and any certificate, then the four correlators of the
               record are elliptope realizable. On the PR box tally the gate returns no for every
               choice of the remaining arguments.

               Proof. In both branches it is enough to show the 4 × 4 block on A0 , A1 , B0 , B1 gives
               a nonnegative form, because the I row and column are 1 on the diagonal and 0
               elsewhere. The first branch: dividing by the positive factor K (a k × k minor scales
               by K k ) gives four positive leading minors of the real block, and Lemma 8.21 applies.
               The second: cross-multiplying by positive denominators changes no equation, so
               M = LDLT holds over the rationals with L unit lower triangular, and the form
               is vT LDLT v = ∑k dk Pk2 with P = LT v and every dk ≥ 0. The PR statement is
               soundness followed by Corollary 8.27.
                   Examples by direct computation: the all-ones plan passes through the fac-
               torization branch at x = y = 1 with pivots (1, 0, 0, 0); the classical mixture
               (1, 0, 1, 0) passes through the same branch at x = 1, y = 0 with pivots (1, 0, 0, 1);
               the 12/5 point passes through the positive-minor branch at x = y = 0; and
               (3/5, 4/5, 4/5, −3/5) passes through the factorization branch at x = y = 0 with
               pivots (1, 1, 0, 0), since B0 = 35 A0 + 54 A1 and B1 = 45 A0 − 35 A1 reproduce every
               entry.
                   That’s all the gate is: no property of the small machine is built from it.


               8.8 The four-vector construction

               Put two equal-length pencils down with their ends together, and draw a line along
               one of them. Keep that pencil still and turn the other. Use the square corner of a
                                                                      8.8 The four-vector construction   83


sheet of paper to bring the moving pencil’s tip straight across to the line, and mark
where it lands. The mark moves from a full pencil-length forward, through the
joined ends when the pencils make a square corner, to a full pencil-length backward.
Call those readings 1, 0 and −1. That is the signed shadow I want. Between
those positions the reading is the cosine of the angle. A dot product of unit vectors
measures that shadow.
   Now ask for four directions, two labelled Alice and two Bob, with a prescribed
shadow for each Alice–Bob pair. Try this request: Alice’s first points exactly along
both of Bob’s; Alice’s second points along Bob’s first and exactly against his second.
Each request alone is easy. Put them together and your hands cannot do it: Bob’s
pencils would have to point both the same way and opposite ways. Four numbers in
range need not describe four directions that can coexist.
   Write those four readings as x = E00 , y = E01 , z = E10 and w = E11 . I haven’t
specified the shadow between Alice’s own two directions, or between Bob’s own
two. Those are the two blanks, t and u, in the following table. Can they be filled so
that the whole table comes from directions? The pencils let you ask it in a plane;
the construction will allow four coordinates. A completion is a positive-semidefinite
matrix                                             
                                      1 t x y
                                     t 1 z w
                                                   
                               G=                  .
                                    x z 1 u 
                                      y w u 1
The extra row and column labelled I in Section 33.7 have diagonal 1 and all other
entries zero, so they do not change this condition.
   Why positivity? Lay arrows end to end, allowing a backward arrow when its
weight is negative. Square the length from the start to the finish. Expanding that
square adds up the arrows’ pairwise shadows, with their weights. It cannot be
negative. A proposed shadow table that gives a negative answer for some choice of
weights cannot come from arrows. That is the PSD test. A Gram matrix is simply
the table of all those pairwise dot products.
   The converse has to build the arrows. Pick the first direction as a baseline. Give
every other arrow the component along it that its shadow demands, then build the
remaining components perpendicular to it. What remains to be fitted is a smaller
shadow table. Here is that construction, including its boundary. If its first diagonal
entry is zero, positivity of h11 a2 + 2h1j ab + h jj b2 for every a, b forces h1j = 0: with
b = 1, a nonzero h1j would let the linear term go negative without bound. Delete
that zero row and column, construct vectors for the smaller matrix, and put back
84   8 The CHSH derivation, in full


               the zero vector. If h11 > 0, set
                                                         h1i h1j
                                           Kij = hij −             (i, j > 1).
                                                          h11
               In the quadratic form of h, take the first coordinate to be − ∑ j>1 h1j v j /h11 . The
               remaining form is vT Kv, so K is PSD. Repeat the construction on this smaller matrix.
               Each repetition removes a row and column, so it ends at a single nonnegative entry,
               represented by its square root, or at the empty table. Working back up supplies
                                                             √                       √
               vectors ri with ri · r j = Kij . Then u1 = ( h11 , 0) and ui = (h1i / h11 , ri ) have Gram
               matrix h. The dimension is at most the number of rows. Applied to G, this gives
               four unit vectors a0 , a1 , b0 , b1 in R4 , with the required cross inner products.
                  Three dimensions are always enough. The table fixes the cross entries and leaves
               two entries of G open, a0 · a1 and b0 · b1 . Hold the first and let the second be t.
               The values of t that keep G PSD form an interval: if two values do, so does every
               value between, because the quadratic form at a mixture is the same mixture of the
               two forms; the set is closed, because a form that is nonnegative at every value of a
               convergent sequence is nonnegative at its limit; and it is bounded, because a PSD
               matrix with ones on its diagonal has every entry between −1 and 1. At an end of
               that interval G is singular: if it were positive definite there, it would stay positive
               definite under every small enough change of t, on both sides. Take G at an end. A
               singular PSD G has a nonzero c with cT Gc = 0, and cT Gc is the squared length of
               ∑i ci vi for the four vectors vi , so that sum is zero and the four vectors lie in a space
               of at most three dimensions. Coordinates in an orthonormal basis of that space keep
               every inner product, so a0 , a1 , b0 , b1 can be taken in R3 .
                  The next question is harder: how do directions become a prescription for a
               quantum measurement? Start with two signed cards in a row. One move swaps
               them; another changes the sign of the card in the right-hand place. Start at (+, +).
               Swap then change the right sign: (+, −). Start again, change the right sign then
               swap: (−, +). Every sign is reversed. Try unequal numbers on the cards: the two
               orders still give negatives of each other. “Anticommute” means exactly that relation
               between the two moves. Do either move twice and everything is back.
                  Write the swap as X, the sign change as Z, and doing nothing as I:
                                               !                      !                  !
                                         0 1                   1 0                  1 0
                                X=               ,     Z=               ,    I=            .
                                         1 0                   0 −1                 0 1

               For the larger construction, label four places by the two-bit words 00 through 11
               and put a signed number at each. An X in one bit-position swaps each pair of places
               differing at that bit; a Z reverses the signs at places whose bit there is 1. The two
               positions give the following three moves, written as real 4 × 4 matrices:

                                      Γ1 = X ⊗ I,   Γ2 = Z ⊗ X,        Γ3 = Z ⊗ Z.
                                                                          8.8 The four-vector construction   85


The tensor product has entries ( A ⊗ B)(i,k),( j,l ) = Aij Bkl . Each Γi is symmetric and
squares to the identity. For i < j, the first position where the two products differ has
X against Z, and XZ = − ZX; all other positions commute. So Γi Γ j = −Γ j Γi . For a
unit vector a, the symmetric matrix A( a) = ∑i ai Γi then satisfies A( a)2 = ∑i a2i I = I.
Its two-outcome measurement has projectors ( I + A( a))/2 and ( I − A( a))/2: they
are symmetric, square to themselves, are orthogonal, and sum to the identity.

    What would that mean at a bench? Prepare the stated shared state, choose one
of the two specified measurements on each side, and write down the two signs
returned. For each pair of settings, count agreements as +1 and disagreements as
−1. Their average is the correlator. The model predicts the four averages x, y, z, w;
a finite tally fluctuates around its expectation. The signed-card moves let you check
the measurement algebra by hand. Realizing that state and those measurements in
a device, and comparing its tallies with the predictions, is the physical experiment.
    Conversely, in a normalized quantum state ψ with self-adjoint measurements
A x = By2 = I on separate systems, put a x = ( A x ⊗ I )ψ and by = ( I ⊗ By )ψ. They
  2

are unit vectors and Re⟨ a x , by ⟩ = Exy . Replace each complex coordinate by its real
and imaginary parts. This preserves those real inner products, and their Gram
matrix is a completion. A mixed state gives the same construction by taking the
direct sum of the vectors of its pure components, weighted by the square roots of
their probabilities. So the completion condition and finite-dimensional quantum
realizability are equivalent.
    The necessary direction does not require finite-dimensional vectors. If a
positive normalized expectation ω on an algebra has self-adjoint A x , By , with
ω ( A2x ), ω ( By2 ) ≤ 1, then the real symmetric matrix with entries Re ω ( Xi X j ) is
86   8 The CHSH derivation, in full


               PSD: its quadratic form is ω (C ∗ C ) ≥ 0 for the real linear combination C of the
               four observables. Increasing each diagonal entry to 1 preserves PSD. When Alice’s
               observables commute with Bob’s, its cross entries are precisely the real correlators.
               This supplies the same G. Including the unit as a fifth observable also gives the
               level-1 moment matrix with its marginals. The sufficient direction already used only
               two four-level systems.


               8.9 The arcsine boundary, in both directions
               Keep Bob’s two directions hinged at one end. Alice’s first direction has to make a
               specified angle with each. How far can Bob’s hinge open while that is possible? Hold
               Alice’s direction upright and sweep each of Bob’s around it at its required angle.
               Point them towards the same side to close the hinge as far as it will go; point them
               towards opposite sides to open it as far as it will go. There is a whole interval of
               openings between those limits. Alice’s second direction asks for another interval.
                  Draw both intervals on one strip marked from 0 to 180 degrees. If they miss, no
               hinge setting satisfies both. If they overlap, choose an opening in the overlap and
               construct each Alice direction at that opening. The algebra below permits each its
               own perpendicular component in four dimensions. The pencils show why the shared
               opening is the question; they need not fit the entire construction on a tabletop. In
               the impossible request above, one interval is just 0 degrees and the other just 180.
               There is nowhere to put the hinge.
                  Assume x, y, z, w ∈ [−1, 1], and put s x = arcsin x, with analogous notation for
               the other three entries. A unit vector making angles α, β ∈ [0, π ] with two unit
               vectors can exist at an angle δ between those two exactly when
                                       |α − β| ≤ δ ≤ min(α + β, 2π − α − β).
               To see this, decompose the two vectors along and perpendicular to the first. Their
               inner product ranges from cos α cos β − sin α sin β to cos α cos β + sin α sin β, by
               Cauchy–Schwarz; every value in that interval is attained by choosing the angle
               between their perpendicular components. Taking arccosines gives the displayed
               interval, including zero perpendicular components.
                  Apply this to Alice’s first vector and Bob’s two. Since α = π/2 − s x and β =
               π/2 − sy , Bob’s angle must lie in
                                           I0 = [ |s x − sy |, π − |s x + sy | ].

               Alice’s second vector gives I1 = [ |sz − sw |, π − |sz + sw | ]. Each interval is nonempty
               because | a − b| + | a + b| = 2 max(| a|, |b|) ≤ π for a, b ∈ [−π/2, π/2]. They
                                                             8.10 The finite local model, in both directions   87


intersect exactly when
               |s x − sy | + |sz + sw | ≤ π,      |sz − sw | + |s x + sy | ≤ π.
Using | a| + |b| = max(| a + b|, | a − b|), these are the four inequalities
                |s x + sy + sz − sw | ≤ π,        |s x + sy − sz + sw | ≤ π,
                |s x − sy + sz + sw | ≤ π, | − s x + sy + sz + sw | ≤ π.
So a completion implies all four.
