8.10 The finite local model, in both directions
Make sixteen cards, each with four boxes labelled A0 , A1 , B0 , B1 , and put every
possible arrangement of plus and minus signs on them, one arrangement per card.
Draw a card before choosing which setting to ask each player about. Alice reads her
chosen box; Bob reads his. Neither needs the other’s setting. How often each card is
drawn is the only choice left in this model.
   Try to get perfect agreement for the first three pairs and perfect disagreement
for the fourth. Any card that ever gets drawn would need A0 = B0 , A0 = B1
and A1 = B0 , which makes A1 = B1 too. Changing how often you draw the
cards cannot fix it. For less extreme requests the question is quantitative: can you
assign nonnegative weights to the cards, adding to one, that give all four requested
averages? The following construction supplies those weights exactly when all eight
CHSH inequalities hold and the correlators are in [−1, 1].
88   8 The CHSH derivation, in full


                  There is a useful way to assemble the deck. First allow only Bob’s first question
               and make a distribution of the three answers A0 , A1 , B0 . Then do the same for his
               second question. Both smaller decks must draw Alice’s answer pairs in the same
               proportions. The adjustable number t is Alice’s agreement-minus-disagreement
               average across her two boxes. Choose it in both intervals
                      J0 = [ −1 + | x + z|, 1 − | x − z| ],          J1 = [ −1 + |y + w|, 1 − |y − w| ].

               Each interval is nonempty. They overlap exactly when | x + z| + |y − w| ≤ 2 and
               |y + w| + | x − z| ≤ 2, which expand to the eight CHSH inequalities. For a0 , a1 , b ∈
               {−1, 1} set
                                                         1 + ta0 a1 + xa0 b + za1 b
                                     P0 ( a0 , a1 , b) =                            ,
                                                                     8
                                                         1 + ta0 a1 + ya0 b + wa1 b
                                     P1 ( a0 , a1 , b) =                             .
                                                                     8
               When a0 = a1 , nonnegativity of P0 is 1 + t ≥ | x + z|; when a0 = − a1 , it is
               1 − t ≥ | x − z|. These are precisely t ∈ J0 . The same check for P1 uses J1 . Summing
               signs shows that both distributions have mass 1 and the desired pair correlators.
               Their common Alice marginal is PA ( a0 , a1 ) = (1 + ta0 a1 )/4.
                  To join the two smaller decks, first draw Alice’s pair with its common probability.
               Among the first deck’s cards bearing that pair, draw Bob’s first answer; among the
               second deck’s cards bearing the same pair, draw his second. Those conditional
               draws give a four-answer card. A pair that never occurs needs no card. Written as
               probabilities, this procedure is
                                           (
                                             P0 ( a0 , a1 , b0 ) P1 ( a0 , a1 , b1 )/PA ( a0 , a1 ), PA ( a0 , a1 ) > 0,
                  P( a0 , a1 , b0 , b1 ) =
                                             0,                                                      PA ( a0 , a1 ) = 0.
               If PA = 0, nonnegativity forces both corresponding triple distributions to be zero.
               Otherwise summing over either Bob answer cancels the denominator. So P has the
               two triple distributions as marginals and total mass 1. Sample one of its sixteen
               answer quadruples and let each party answer with its assigned sign. This is a finite
               mixture of deterministic local plans with exactly the four required correlators.
                   The other direction is one line per card. On a single card, A0 ( B0 + B1 ) + A1 ( B0 −
               B1 ) is ±2, because one bracket is 0 and the other is ±2. Each of the other seven
               CHSH expressions is this one with the boxes relabelled or a sign flipped, so it is ±2
               on every card too, and any weighting of the cards keeps all eight averages between
               −2 and 2 (Theorem 33.1 is the first of them). So a deck exists exactly when the
               correlators are in [−1, 1] and the eight inequalities hold.
                                                                    8.11 Scope of the constructions   89


8.11 Scope of the constructions
The constructions above pin down the correlators. What follows is where they stop:
they aren’t a completeness claim for the integer check, they say nothing about full
behaviours with marginals, and they say nothing about data from a lab.
    The arcsine boundary. For two settings and two outcomes per side, Tsirelson,
Landau (1988) and Masanes (2003) give theorems characterizing the correla-
tors reachable by quantum measurements: | arcsin E00 + arcsin E01 + arcsin E10 −
arcsin E11 | ≤ π and its sign variants. The characterization holds as an if and only if
with the completion set and, through Tsirelson’s representation, with the quantum
set. The point (3/5, 4/5, 4/5, −3/5) is on the boundary: arcsin 35 + arcsin 45 = π2
by the 3-4-5 triangle, so the sum is π; Tsirelson’s point is on it too. The necessary
direction holds in any dimension, for any expectation on an algebra of observables
in which Alice’s commute with Bob’s.
    Equality with the quantum set. That the completion set of Section 33.7 is exactly
the set of quantum correlators is Tsirelson’s representation theorem, and it is proved
in finite dimension: for both real and complex amplitudes, with two four-level
systems enough for every point, and two two-level ones with complex amplitudes.
That PSD of the level-1 moment matrix is necessary for quantum behaviour is
proved for strategies in finite dimension, with real amplitudes and with complex
ones, and in any dimension, marginals included, for any expectation on an algebra
of observables.
    Achievability. Theorem 33.5 is a point of R4 . A state      √ and measurements that
reach it are explicit:
                  √    the two-qubit  state (|
                                            √  00 ⟩ + | 11 ⟩) /  2 with√A0 = √
                                                                             Z, A1 =√X,
B0 =√( Z + X )/ 2√and B1 = ( Z − X )/ 2 has correlators 1/ 2, 1/ 2, 1/ 2,
−1/ 2 and S = 2 2, and every strategy in finite dimension has S2 ≤ 8, with real
amplitudes and with complex ones. Both are arithmetic about the model. How close
a real laboratory comes, and what that costs in loopholes closed, is the story told in
Appendix 32.
    Completeness of the checks. The 1+AB cascade and the elliptope gate are proved
sound. The cascade has no proved converse; the gate’s factorization branch accepts
every rational PSD completion, by the row-peeling of Section 33.8 with each pivot
kept. The level-1 integer check is the exception: on the small machine the same
seven integer facts hold exactly when every pair was sampled and the pinned matrix
is positive semidefinite (Theorem 32.4), so that check is complete for what it tests.
The 1+AB cascade is strict and misses boundary points. The two 1+AB checks
don’t sit inside one another. The full check accepts the 12/5 tally above, which the
zero-moment check refuses, and it refuses the tally with E00 = 1 and the other three
correlators 0, where H11 = 0, which the zero-moment check accepts.
90   8 The CHSH derivation, in full


                  Finite levels. For full behaviours, marginals included, the NPA levels are relax-
               ations. Chaturvedi (arXiv 2607.14569, 2026) argues, in a preprint, that no finite
               NPA level characterizes the complete quantum set even in this simplest Bell scenario.
               I haven’t gone through that proof, and nothing here rests on it.
                  Statistics and physics. Nothing here says how many rounds make a tally infor-
               mative, and nothing says the counters came from honest play; a program can INC
               whatever counts it likes.


               8.12 Prior work
                                                             √
               Tsirelson’s 1980 paper gives the bound 2 2 for quantum correlations, and his
               theorem says that quantum two-outcome correlators are inner products of real unit
               vectors. Landau (1988) and Masanes (2003, arXiv quant-ph/0309137) each give a
               condition that, by their proofs, characterizes the quantum correlators exactly for two
               settings and two outcomes. Wehner (2006, arXiv quant-ph/0510076) gets Tsirelson
               bounds, CHSH among them, from a semidefinite program on the 4 × 4 Gram matrix
               of those vectors. Navascués, Pironio and Acín (2007, arXiv quant-ph/0607119;
               2008, arXiv 0803.4290) built the hierarchy of moment-matrix tests; their 2008
               paper applies a Schur complement to the level-1 matrix of this scenario, recovers
               the arcsine condition, and discusses the 1+AB level. I went back to each of those
               papers for these points, and that is what I found in them. The results are theirs.
               Two of them are proved again here, in finite dimension: Tsirelson’s representation
               and the arcsine characterization; the rest of their proofs are not redone. Echenim
               and Mhalla give a detailed derivation of Tsirelson’s bound (arXiv 2306.12535), and
               exact rational sum-of-squares certificates
                                                  √       go back at least to Peyrl and Parrilo (2008).
               So the moment-matrix route to 2 2 is standard. What is specific here is narrower:
               an integer certificate computed from outcome counts, and the argument from “the
               counts pass” to PSD of the pinned matrix and on to S2 ≤ 8. I did not find that chain
               elsewhere, and I make no claim beyond it. On the host machine, the same chain
               is earned: a raised flag from a clean start stands on a passing check of the packed
               counts, and the check passes exactly when the pinned matrix is positive semidefinite
               and every pair was sampled (Appendix 32, Theorems 32.4 and 32.5). The converse
               direction of the integer check, integers from PSD, is proved there too, and there too
               I make no claim beyond the chain.
CHAPTER 9




From the front matter

That sentence walks straight into one of the oldest fights in mathematics, so I’ll
take a short trip into it first. G. H. Hardy, a Cambridge number theorist who wrote
A Mathematician’s Apology in 1940 to defend doing mathematics for its own sake,
came down on the found side about as hard as anyone ever has: mathematical
reality, he wrote, lies outside the people who study it, and the theorems they like
to call their creations are really just notes on what they saw there. The German
mathematician Leopold Kronecker supposedly split the difference: God made the
whole numbers, and everything else is the work of people. Supposedly, because push
on that line and it gets thin fast. It comes down through Heinrich Weber, another
German mathematician, in a memorial piece written after Kronecker died, pointing
back to a lecture from 1886, and that’s the only source I could find anybody citing.
It’s a great line with one witness. (The record chapter of Part I, Section 2, stops
early on to ask how many witnesses a claim ought to need. The answer is more
than one.) None of this settles whether I found the Thiele Machine or made it up. A
proof doesn’t settle it either: showing what follows from the premises doesn’t decide
whether you found the theorem or made it. That’s why it’s labelled a conviction.
What it changes is how I hold the thing: like something that was already there,
which means I can be wrong about its shape and the shape won’t care. And to be
exact about what “found” covers, it covers the parts the counting forces on you, the
latch shape and the merge or the fresh states. The event to record, the price list,
and putting the count inside the step are choices, mine, and the three lists below
say which is which.


The four steps of the argument

The four steps above are the argument. The proofs establish the counts and con-
structions; the claims section at the back lists them with what each stands on.
   Argued only: that the structure a computation makes is kept distinctions, priced
by the toll, while a reading that undoable moves keep never rises and is never
charged; that’s a choice of what to price, and on a finite machine the second half is
proved.

                                                 91
92   9 From the front matter


                   Argued only: that an account of computation should carry what a computation
               made the case, and what it cost, in how it steps. The proofs show that it hangs
               together, what it follows from, and what it costs; whether it ought to be adopted is
               a claim about what matters, which no theorem decides.
                   My proposed reason for choosing certification is that its records get copied and
               relied on by parties who didn’t produce them. That is the pointer thesis in Part III.
               It connects the machine to how people use records; the counting arguments work
               with whichever record is chosen.
                   The results also have a history. By my own sorting at the back (Appendix 28),
               each piece has its neighbours standing right there. Start from the top. The small
               machine and its universal programs carry the reference monitor’s every-program
               move (Anderson in 1972) to a record that’s priced and earned; the two-player
               game check in the appendices (Appendix 32) is careful engineering on known
               mathematics. The latch has the 1918 flip-flop and write-once memory for neighbours.
               The window fact is an old fact about functions, from Edward Moore’s 1956 thought
               experiments and Rudolf Kalman’s observability, with a price put on it. The toll
               derived from finiteness, permanence and a price on merging is a ledger form of
               the core underneath. And that core, what step one says keeping takes, is old
               mathematics: the pigeonhole principle, Dedekind’s 1888 definition of infinite and
               the finite heart of Ernst Zermelo’s 1896 recurrence argument, with Landauer and
               Bennett on merging and Thomas Ouldridge and David Wolpert’s 2023 price in
               entropy on the merges a finite automaton can’t avoid. For deterministic machines
               the arrangement is old too: Ming Li and Paul Vitányi (1996) wrote that a bounded
               memory forces erasure in the long run, and Demaine and his coauthors (2016)
               that extra space only postpones it. What I’d defend most is there, at the bottom, in
               the form I didn’t find: one law with three columns and no fourth, stated alike for
               deterministic, noisy and quantum steps, with two small exact lemmas, the count of
               fresh states for a bounded memory and every scale paying the same way, and a toll
               on a reading that stays, which charges for keeping whichever column pays for it.
                   The route through the book is the route through the model: follow a step, see
               which distinctions survive it, and count what the next step has to work with. Then
               put the record and its price into the step itself. The example at the end shows those
               choices can all be made at once.
CHAPTER 10




From “A distinction that stays”

The walker is the case proved here. Other finite-time results use other, explicitly
specified dynamics. Drag a bead held in a spring-like trap through water from one
place to another in a set time and the least work you can get away with is the
drag times the distance squared, divided by the time plus twice the drag over the
stiffness, which at long times is the drag times the distance squared over the time
(Schmiedl and Seifert, 2007, who worked out the best protocols for doing it in
finite time). Within their overdamped diffusion or Markov jump models, change a
probability distribution into a different one in a set time and the entropy produced
is at least a fixed fraction of the square of how far apart the two spreads are,
divided by the time and by how mobile the states are: the diffusion, for a bead
that drifts (Aurell, Mejía-Monasterio and Muratore-Ginanneschi, 2011), and how
busy the jumping is, for states that jump (Shiraishi, Funo and Saito, 2018). Those
are their results, and I cite them as physics. These bounds require their stated
dynamics, a nonzero change of distribution, and finite activity or mobility. For the
jump-process bound, local detailed balance identifies the usual thermal entropy
production; without it the cited result uses a different entropy-production quantity. A
thermal environment alone does not supply these assumptions. The bounds concern
entropy produced, not positive heat on every step: a step that leaves the spread
exactly as it was owes nothing on these bounds, and the heat a single step gives
off can be negative when the device’s own entropy goes up. An idealised machine
cut off from everything, frictionless billiard balls bouncing their way through a
computation (Edward Fredkin and Tommaso Toffoli’s, 1982), could in principle
run without dissipating at all; Bennett (2003) wrote that such ballistic machines
compute at constant velocity without dissipating any energy, and that they must be
kept isolated from heat baths and noise. Keeping one on course against noise means
correcting its errors, and whether that brings the dissipation back I don’t settle here;
in 2003 Bennett called the thermodynamics of error correction a subject that appears
to be in its infancy. The bead of Jun, Gavrilov and Bechhoefer (2014) shows the
other half. Put through the same moves as an erasure but with no tilt, so nothing is




                                                  93
94   10 From “A distinction that stays”


               squashed, its work extrapolated to the slow limit came out at 0.05 ± 0.03 kT, within
               two error bars of zero: no floor under it, which is what the premise says.
                   I also built a numerical bead to see the steps, using a quartic double well. It
               follows the experiment’s sequence: lower the barrier for one second, ramp the tilt,
               remove the tilt, then raise the barrier for one second. This is a chosen potential and a
               numerical approximation, not the measured trap. The starting positions are sampled
               from a numerical approximation to the thermal distribution in that potential, with
               equal chances of starting in either well.
                   Keep three accounts as it moves. Changing the trap while holding the position
               fixed does work on the bead. Moving the bead in the fixed trap transfers heat to the
               bath. Whatever is left changes the bead’s potential energy. These are the steps the
               script actually adds up:
                                                        Old trap
                                                 Bead at its old position

                                                               work in

                                                     Changed trap
                                                     Same position

                                                               heat out

                                                   Same changed trap
                                                     New position

                Work in minus heat out equals the change in stored energy. Either transfer can be negative
                                                  on an individual step.
                  The work account includes removing the tilt and restoring the starting trap. The
               heat account uses the potential difference during each displacement. Those two
               accounts balance on every simulated path to rounding precision. That checks the
               accounting; it does not show that the time steps are small enough to reproduce
               continuous motion exactly.
                  Here is what came out, with 3,000 paths at each setting and a time step of half a
               millisecond. Each setting uses a specified tilt, chosen earlier to make roughly nine
               erasures in ten work. Success means ending in the right well. The last two columns
               average only those successes; energies are in units of kT. The time column is the tilt
               ramp, with the two one-second barrier ramps in addition.

         Tilt time (s)   Success rate   Heat out on success   Work in on success
                    2          0.909                 2.517                2.501
                    5          0.916                 1.466                1.490
                   10          0.913                 1.073                1.095
                   20          0.899                 0.830                0.907
                   40          0.893                 0.773                0.782
                   80          0.869                 0.693                0.719
    Heat and work are close in some rows and less close in others. The difference
is measured by the simulation, not supplied as a constant correction. At the last
setting the sampling standard errors are about 0.023 kT for heat and 0.011 kT for
work. They do not include error from the time step or the model.
    Now try to make a floor out of those six points. Fit the successful heat to a
constant plus a number divided by the tilt time: the constant is 0.674 kT, with
sampling error about 0.015 kT. Halve the time step and repeat the six settings: it
becomes 0.732 kT, with sampling error about 0.016 kT. The second run also samples
different paths, so this comparison mixes sampling and numerical error. It is enough
to stop me treating a fitted constant near ln 2 as a confirmation. The barrier ramps
are still one second long, the tilt changes between settings, and this fit does not take
the whole protocol to a slow limit.
    There is a useful theoretical comparison, but it is a comparison with work.
For the continuous thermal model, beginning in equilibrium and ending with the
same symmetric trap, the detailed Jarzynski relation gives the successful paths a
mean-work bound kT ln(2p), where p is the success probability. Averaging the
successful and failed paths gives the bound kT [ln 2 + p ln p + (1 − p) ln(1 − p)]. At
nine successes in ten these are about 0.588 kT and 0.368 kT. Bérut, Petrosyan and
Ciliberto’s 2013 paper, Detailed Jarzynski Equality applied to a Logically Irreversible
Procedure, works through the success-conditioned relation. Neither formula is a
heat bound for this simulation without also accounting for its change in stored
energy. Nor does an exact bookkeeping identity make the discrete simulation obey
every continuum fluctuation relation exactly.
    The script is scripts/bead_erasure_sim.py. It reports heat, work, stored-
energy change, sampling errors and the descriptive fits, and accepts a smaller time
step for checking them. The slower settings here use less mean work and release
less mean heat. That is what this calculation demonstrates. It does not determine a
universal floor or reproduce the laboratory’s fitted constant.

                  The cited erasure experiments test specified memories and protocols, including
               symmetric-memory Landauer bounds and heat versus duration. They are not an
               exhaustive survey of physical computation, nor an experimental proof of the abstract
                                                                                        97


state-space dichotomy. Bekenstein’s bound relates entropy to energy and size
under its physical assumptions; it does not supply a finite enumeration of arbitrary
device states. The finite-state theorems here take their finite set as a mathematical
hypothesis.
CHAPTER 11




From “A record and a toll”

11.1 Clay, seals, and seven witnesses

Start a little over five thousand years ago, in the Near East, with people who counted
sheep with little clay tokens. To send a count across a distance without the courier
changing it, they put the tokens inside a hollow clay ball, pressed the shapes of the
tokens onto the outside while the clay was soft, and sealed it. The archaeologist
Denise Schmandt-Besserat has spent a career on these, and she puts the practice at
around 3300 BC; the ball is called a bulla. The push is one you can do with a cookie
tin, with a paper seal glued across the lid and rim. Let it dry, then try opening the
lid without tearing or disturbing the seal. The bare lid would come straight off; it
is the seal that makes this a test. A substitute seal could fool you, so this little trial
shows a disturbance, not an unforgeable history. In the clay ball, the outside shows
the count inside and opening the ball breaks it. That is the property the tin and seal
are trying to reproduce. The idea is a real one and it will show up again in a form
you won’t expect: a record is worth something only if it can’t be silently undone.
    Now law, which is where people get serious about who’s allowed to say what
happened. The book of Deuteronomy, chapter nineteen: nobody is convicted on the
word of one witness; it takes “two or three.” The Roman jurist Gaius, writing in the
second century, says a will the praetor will honour is one sealed by seven witnesses.
Why seven? Gaius doesn’t say. The legal historian Henry Maine, in 1861, had an
answer: the older Roman will was a mock sale, done in front of five witnesses, a
man holding the scales, and the buyer of the estate, seven people in all, and when
the ceremony fell away the seven seals kept the count. Maine’s is the only account
of it I found, so treat it as his. Gaius’s own text does show they kept those old
formalities around a will, and it isn’t hard to see why anyone would: one person
can lie, and a will moves a fortune, and the dead man can’t object. So the record
isn’t the paper; it’s the number of independent people who have put their own seal,
their own name, their own risk, next to the claim. Try it: write “paid five dollars,
signed A” on a napkin and ask a friend to forge it. It takes them ten seconds. Now
require two other people to initial it at the moment of payment and each keep a
copy. If the readers compare those independently kept copies and trust their holders,


                                   98
                                                                  11.2 Two columns and a stamp   99


changing your napkin alone will be exposed. Stolen copies, forged initials, collusion
or a reader who never compares them can defeat the test. The claim didn’t change,
but what stands behind it did.
    Which brings me to seals, and the medieval historian Michael Clanchy, whose
book is called From Memory to Written Record. England between the Norman
Conquest and the early 1300s was learning to trust writing. Until then it had trusted
people who remembered. The lesson was painful: seals were forged, charters were
forged, and historians today have a whole craft of telling real charters from fake
ones because the fakes are so many. A seal is only as good as whoever holds the
stamp. Keep that one too, because in 2011 a certificate authority called DigiNotar
found out, at the cost of its existence, that a mathematically perfect signature is
worth exactly what the custody of the key is worth, and intruders had taken control
of the machines that signed. It is the same failure nine hundred years apart, and I
find that oddly comforting.
    And then the obvious next question: if seals could be forged, what caught the
forgeries? It wasn’t the seals. The most famous forged record in European history
is the Donation of Constantine, a document in which the emperor Constantine
supposedly handed rule over Rome and the western empire to the Pope. It was most
likely written in the eighth century, some four hundred years after Constantine died,
and it was leaned on for centuries. Around 1440 a scholar named Lorenzo Valla
took it apart by its Latin: the language belonged to a much later age, and it used
words, “satraps” among them, that no Roman of Constantine’s day would have used
for his officials. (Valla wasn’t even first. Nicholas of Cusa, a German churchman and
philosopher who later became a cardinal, had already called it a forgery in 1433, in
De concordantia catholica, a long book on how the Church and the empire ought to
hang together. I know that from the reference works, and I haven’t read the book.
Valla’s is the version people remember, which is a pattern that keeps turning up.)
Look at what kind of check that was. The question wasn’t “is the stamp real.” It was
“could these words have been written when they say they were.” A record can pass
the first and fail the second, which is a hint that what I keep calling one thing, a
record, is doing more than one job.


11.2 Two columns and a stamp

The one I’d never have guessed mattered is bookkeeping. Around 1300, the ledger
of a Florentine company called Farolfi, kept at its branch in Provence, shows the
earliest complete example anyone has found of a trick merchants were working out:
write every transaction twice, once where the money came from and once where
it went, and the two columns have to balance. The treasurers of the city of Genoa
kept their ledger for 1340 that way, and accounting historians still count it among
100   11 From “A record and a toll”


               the most famous government account books of the Middle Ages kept in double entry.
               And in 1494 a friar named Luca Pacioli printed the method in a mathematics book,
               and it spread. The method is double-entry bookkeeping, and it’s easy to take it for
               just a tidy habit.
                   Do it for one day. Keep two columns of every dollar that moves, and total them
               at night. Now have a friend secretly change one entry in one column. The totals
               disagree; you catch it. Now have them change the matching entry in both columns.
               The totals agree, and you’ve caught nothing. Push on that and you’ve found what
               double entry actually is: a check for slips, which a careful liar walks straight through.
               Did Pacioli see that? I can only get at it secondhand. The scholar Forrester, in a paper
               on the University of Strathclyde’s accounting-history pages, reads him as reporting
               that in many places the books were taken to the local consul to be authenticated
               before a single entry went in. The stamp is a witness again, from outside. The
               two columns are the discipline; the stamp is the claim that someone other than
               you will stand behind the fact that these are the books. That works only as far
               as the authentication and custody distinguish this set of books from a substitute.
               Keep both halves separate in your head, because one is a method and the other is a
               commitment.
                   And then I found the place where this got turned into a motto. The Royal Society,
               founded in London in 1660 and still running, picked as its motto Nullius in verba:
               take nobody’s word for it. (Where did they get it? From somebody’s words, naturally.
               It’s half a line of the Roman poet Horace, from a verse letter to his patron, saying
               he isn’t bound to swear by the words of any master. So the society that takes
               nobody’s word for it took its motto from a poet and cut the line to fit. That’s not a
               contradiction, and it’s a nice picture of the method: borrow what’s useful, say whose
               it was, and don’t swear by it.) The historian Steven Shapin wrote a paper about
               what that meant in practice, and the answer is more interesting than the slogan.
               Robert Boyle, pumping the air out of a glass globe to see what happens to a candle
               or a bird, had three problems: the machine, the report, and the witnesses. He solved
               the third by having gentlemen present who’d sign their names, and the second by
               writing the report so richly that a reader who wasn’t there could picture the scene
               and count as a witness anyway. Shapin calls that virtual witnessing, and he notes,
               dryly, that the air pump was very expensive, seventeenth-century big science. So
               there was a cost; it just wasn’t a cost of declaring the fact. It was the cost of the
               instrument and of getting enough people into the room. Hold on to that distinction,
               because the rest of the book hangs on it.
                                                 11.3 What they all agree on, and who they rule out   101


11.3 What they all agree on, and who they rule out
Lay them down, and this time what shows through is not one thing. It’s five different
jobs that the word “record” has been doing, and I’d been letting one word carry all
five. Most of them have a corpse next to them, and the corpses are different too.
    Durability: the record can’t be silently undone. The bulla, the write-once disk,
the hash chain and Bennett’s card that has to be wiped all do this job. The corpse
is the editable log, and the company that kept its debts off the books: Enron hid
its debt in side entities set up to keep it off the balance sheet, which is how the
Justice Department described the scheme in 2002, and its auditor, Arthur Andersen,
shredded the paper. Andersen was convicted for the shredding that year, and
the Supreme Court threw the conviction out in 2005 over how the jury had been
instructed; either way, the books concealed debts they should have shown. But
notice that this job doesn’t need a witness at all. A copy will do: a hash chain
nobody signed is still hard to change quietly, so long as somebody else holds the
latest link, because a chain with no copy anywhere can be rewritten from end to
end and recomputed, and nobody would know.
    Authenticity: the record came from who it says it came from. Seals, the signature
only a key-holder can make, and the consul’s stamp all do this one. The corpse is
the forged charter and DigiNotar. This job is about custody of a stamp, and it’s a
different job from durability; a perfectly authentic entry can still be erased, and a
perfectly durable one can still be a forgery.
    Evidence: enough independent people stand behind the claim that lying is
expensive. Two witnesses, seven seals and Boyle’s gentlemen do this one. It rules
out anonymous assertion, and it rules out “the universe keeps the receipt”, because
information spread into the environment is not a record anyone can read or rely on.
This one is about numbers of people and their risk. Paper has nothing to do with it.
    Accounting: the record checks itself for slips. The two columns do this one. And
one thing has to be kept straight: the second column is not an outside witness. It’s
you, checking you. It catches a dropped digit. A careful liar gets past it, as you saw.
It belongs on this list as its own job, separate from the seven seals.
    Cheap checking: a record whose correctness can be confirmed for much less than
it cost to make. Cook’s certificates, the signature and the hash do this one. This one
isn’t universal, and I thought it was. Whether checking needs to be cheaper than
making depends on who you’re worried about and how much a wrong record costs
you; plenty of records are checked by redoing the whole thing, and that’s fine when
the thing is small.
    Now for the thing none of them says. Not one of these jobs is pricing the
moment a record goes up and stays. Bennett prices merging and erasing, which that
moment might or might not be. Proof-of-work prices a search the designer chose.
102   11 From “A record and a toll”


               Bookkeeping prices a discipline. Witnesses and seals price showing up and guarding
               the stamp. I looked, with everything I had, and that’s where it ran out. If there is a
               cost charged for that moment itself, nobody in this lineage wrote it down, and that’s
               either because it isn’t there or because the one person who did write it down is on a
               shelf I never found. The literal one-bit reset already has a thermodynamic analysis:
               Landauer’s restore to one. The question here is which premises force an instruction
               that raises a record to merge full states, and which further premises put a price on
               that loss. So I’m going to have to try it myself, on the smallest thing I can build, and
               let you watch. And I’ll have to be honest about which of the five jobs the small thing
               even attempts, because the toy has a permanent flag under its three moves, but no
               witnesses, authenticated identity or check of a claim.
                   Take an ordinary computing machine and put a lamp and tally beside it. Let
               computing run as before, leaving both alone. A separate mark leaves the computing
               state fixed, adds one to the tally and turns the lamp on when its named event holds.
               Forget the lamp and tally and the old computation is still there, step for step. Below
               I build that box, compare different ways to implement it, and ask exactly which
               differences its questions require. The old machine may already have prices or a
               reading of its own; this construction supplies the new lamp and its zero/one account.
               With effective rules an ordinary computer can store all three parts.

               What the next move could tell us
               Put two boxes beside each other and read everything the task lets you read. Suppose
               the answers agree. That is not yet enough to put the boxes in the same pile. Press
               the same button on each. Perhaps one lamp now comes on and the other stays dark.
               The difference mattered, even though the first reading missed it.
                   Keep two states together only when every permitted finite continuation gives the
               same required answer. Include doing nothing: the current answer counts too. This
               tells us which differences the task can afford to forget.


               Theorem 11.1 (Keep what a later question can separate). For a fixed deterministic
               machine, permitted instructions and required observation, equality of observations
               after every finite continuation is the unique coarsest equivalence that preserves
               the current observation and is stable under every instruction. A deterministic
               implementation preserving those steps and answers cannot identify two states
               separated by such a continuation.

               Proof. Agreement with oneself, reversal of an agreement, and joining two agree-
               ments all hold one continuation at a time, so this is an equivalence. Doing nothing
               checks the current observation. To check stability after a move, put that move in
                                                 11.3 What they all agree on, and who they rule out   103


front of each continuation. If two states were already grouped by any observation-
preserving, stable relation, apply its stability once for every move in a continuation,
then read the answer. Those states agree on every continuation and belong to our
equivalence. Our grouping therefore contains every permitted grouping. Finally, if
an implementation put two separated states in one full state, the same continuation
from that state would have to produce two different answers. Determinism gives
only one.
   Change the questions and the piles can change. A box that toggles between two
positions needs two distinguishable states if I ask its position. If my only required
answer is always “ready”, and every later answer is also “ready”, those positions can
share one description. The task tells me what has to survive.

The picture, the lamp and the tally
Take an ordinary computing box. Put a lamp beside it and a tally beneath it. A
computing button moves the box by its old rule, costs zero, and leaves the lamp and
tally alone. A mark button leaves the box where it is, adds one to the tally, and turns
the lamp on if its named event holds there. Once on, the lamp stays on. Start with
the lamp down and the tally at zero.
    Those instructions tell us what to do without telling us how to build the box. One
builder may keep every button press on a tape. Another may keep an extra turn
counter. I require each builder to show me the same three things: the computing
picture, the lamp and the exact tally. Its computing moves must follow the base rule
through that picture. Its marks must leave the picture fixed and latch exactly the
named event. Its prices are zero for computing and one for marking; its reading
obeys the toll and permanence; its tally increases by exactly the charge on every
move.


Theorem 11.2 (Every such box has the same three-part description). Fix those
base rules, mark events and accounting requirements. Every implementation has
exactly one map to the product of base state, Boolean reading and natural tally
that preserves the three readings. That map preserves every instruction and every
finite run. From a start with lamp down and tally zero, it maps the reachable
implementation states onto all reachable states of the product lift with the matching
base start.

Proof. Read the picture, lamp and tally, and write them down in that order. There
is no other choice for a map required to preserve those three readings. Press a
computing button. The picture follows the base rule. The lamp stays put by the toll
and permanence, and the tally stays put because the charge is zero. Press a mark
104   11 From “A record and a toll”


               instead. The picture stays put, the lamp takes its old value or the event’s yes, and
               the tally rises by one. These are exactly the two rules of the product lift. Repeat for
               every move in a finite run.
                  The clean starts agree in all three readings. Any reachable product state has some
               finite list of buttons reaching it. Press that same list on the implementation. The
               step argument makes its three readings the desired product state. Every reachable
               product state therefore has a reachable implementation state above it.
                   Here are the three stages. First write a list of buttons. Then run it on the builder’s
               box. Then read the picture, lamp and tally. You get the same result as running the
               list directly on the three-part description. Written as arrows, after we have done it:
                        button lists −→ implementation states −→ picture, lamp, tally.

                   The first arrow has only one possible meaning once the start and buttons are
               fixed. The empty list gives the start; appending a button takes one more step. This
               is the free history construction. The second arrow can forget history. Its direction
               matters.
                   Turn a box halfway around with a free button. Turn it again. It is back where it
               started, with the same lamp and tally. Now give another builder’s box a counter that
               goes up on every turn, without charging for it. After two turns its counter is two
               larger. It meets all the requirements above: the extra counter is separate from the
               tally of charges.



               Proposition 11.3 (Forgetting the turn count goes one way). Even on reachable
               states, the three-part lift need not have a step-preserving map into every implemen-
               tation satisfying its contract.

               Proof. In this example two turns return every product state to itself. A map pre-
               serving turns would have to send it to an implementation state that also returns
               after two turns. No such state exists there: its extra counter has increased by two.
               Forgetting that counter works in the other direction and preserves both turning and
               marking.
                   There is an exact way to tell when the reverse map would work. Whenever two
               button lists reach the same source state, they must also reach the same target state.
               Otherwise the map would have to send that one source to two targets. Supply one
               access list reaching each source state, and this condition is sufficient: run its access
               list on the target. Any other list reaching that source gives the same target by the
               condition. The empty list preserves the start, appending a button preserves a step,
                                                   11.3 What they all agree on, and who they rule out   105


and reachability leaves no other possible map. The turn counter fails exactly this
test: the empty list and two turns agree in the source and disagree in the target.
             Front          turn         Back            turn         Front
          Lamp down,                  Lamp down,                   Lamp down,
            tally 0                     tally 0                      tally 0

                                               forget
                                               turn count

         Front; turns 0               Back; turns 1               Front; turns 2
          Lamp down,                  Lamp down,                   Lamp down,
            tally 0         turn         tally 0         turn        tally 0

 The top row returns to the same full state. The bottom row does not. Forgetting the turn
            count respects each turn; recovering it from the top row cannot.



Theorem 11.4 (The smallest description that answers these questions). Under the
preceding contract, two implementation states give the same three readings after
every common finite continuation exactly when their current three readings agree.
If every two distinct reachable implementation states can be separated by such a
continuation, projection is a bijection onto the reachable product states. It preserves
starts and steps and is the unique start- and step-preserving map there.

Proof. If the current readings differ, read them now. If they agree, the two product
states are equal. Equal states obeying the same rule give equal next states; repeat
for every move. Projection preserves those moves, so the implementation’s readings
agree throughout too.
    Now require every distinct reachable pair to be separable. Two states projecting
to the same triple could never be separated, so they must be one state. Projection
is therefore one-to-one; the previous theorem makes it onto the reachable product
states. Any start- and step-preserving map is fixed on a reachable state by a list
reaching it: map the start, then follow the list. It must agree with projection there.
    The same reasoning explains uniqueness more generally. Take two reachable
machines with the same answers to every button list from their starts, and suppose
each keeps no two states that all future answers fail to separate. Supply an access
list for each state of the first. Run that list on the second. If two lists reach the same
first state, append any continuation: their answers agree on the first machine, hence
on the second. Separation in the second makes their endpoints equal. The map is
well-defined and preserves steps and outputs. Reachability makes it onto; separation
in the first makes it one-to-one. Following an access list forces uniqueness.
106   11 From “A record and a toll”


                   For our box, that smallest description keeps the tally because I asked for the tally.
               Stop asking for it and repeated failed marks may become indistinguishable. Ask for
               every past button and the extra tape starts to matter. With a clean start and an event
               that never holds, no reachable state has its lamp up. Counting unreachable lamp-up
               states would answer a different question. “Smallest” here means no two retained
               states are indistinguishable by the required future readings, including on an infinite
               machine.
                   This way of grouping states is the familiar behavioral equivalence behind minimal
               automata. The initial history construction and final observation construction are
               treated in Rutten’s The Method of Coalgebra, Chapters 2 and 4 and Section 8.6.1
               Here the box tells us exactly which observations and operations the lift preserves.
               Choosing the event, the window and the accounting is where the application en-
               ters. Once they are fixed, the argument above determines which implementation
               differences can be forgotten.

               What can choose the question?
               Take a dial with two faces and one button. Each press turns it to the other face.
               For this question, supply no starting face, no lamp and no names with an outside
               meaning. Draw its two arrows. Now exchange the names on the two faces. The
               arrows describe exactly the same machine.
                  Suppose those arrows alone are meant to tell us which face deserves yes. A rule
               that does not depend on our choice of names must give the exchanged face the
               same reading. So both faces get the same answer. A nonconstant reading cannot be
               selected that way. Give one face a role—the loaded start, a completed job, a passed
               check—and we have supplied more to work with. The role has to be stated and
               defended. Renaming the arrows did not supply it.
                  The argument is two lines: the exchange takes the first face to the second;
               respecting that exchange makes their readings equal. Those are all the faces. It
               does not make every machine’s reading constant. It rules out a nonconstant reading
               selected from the transition structure alone on every machine. 2
                  That fixes what the uniqueness claim can say. The box, lamp and tally have a
               unique observable realization under the contract already proved. The arrows by
               themselves do not choose that contract. Adding the word “intrinsic” cannot do the
               choosing for us.
                  1Jan Rutten, The Method of Coalgebra (2019), https://www.cs.cornell.edu/courses/cs4810/

               2025sp/Handouts/Rutten.pdf.
                  2 Checked in coq/audit/IntrinsicBoundary.v: toggle_symmetry_forces_constant_reading

               and no_nonconstant_renaming_invariant_toggle_reading.
                                                 11.3 What they all agree on, and who they rule out   107


Does the picture keep enough of the device?
Put a lamp on a box and a hidden switch inside it. The button copies the hidden
switch to the lamp. Start with the lamp dark in both boxes, and put the hidden
switch down in one, up in the other.
                Lamp before Hidden switch Lamp after the button
                Dark           down              dark
                Dark           up                lit
    A description that keeps only the lamp has one starting state, dark, and is asked
for two different next answers to the same button. It cannot do it. Keep the hidden
switch too and the problem disappears. This is a reason the additional distinction
is necessary for this task: the next press can expose it. It is not a reason every task
needs that switch. 3
    There is a complete test for this kind of description. First write down the under-
lying configurations, the permitted buttons, every permitted successor, the picture
being retained, and the answers the task requires. This is the operational model of
the device. Then check two things. Configurations with the same picture must give
the same required answer now. And the same button, from any configurations with
that picture, must leave the same next picture, for every permitted successor.
    Both conditions are necessary. An answer decoder has only the picture to read. A
deterministic next-picture rule has only the picture and button. Neither can return
two different results on one input.
    They are sufficient too, with explicit choices. Supply a representative configura-
tion for each picture and a permitted successor for each button at each configuration.
Decode a picture by reading its representative. Compute its next picture by pressing
the button on that representative and taking the chosen successor. The two con-
ditions ensure that every other configuration and every other permitted successor
agrees. Repeat that argument at each step: all finite button lists preserve the re-
quired answers. On a finite listed model this can be checked against its complete
table. For arbitrary state types the choices and their effectiveness are separate data;
the proof does not manufacture a search procedure.
    This test allows several successors of an underlying configuration.4 If two of
them give different required answers, no exact deterministic encoding of that same
configuration and button can represent both. 5 A probability distribution over
outcomes, or a finer state including a chosen input or seed, is a different contract
and must be described as one.
  3 hidden_bit_blocks_visible_only_dynamics, coq/audit/ObservationContract.v.
  4 operational_adequacy_iff, coq/audit/ObservationContract.v.
   5 branching_answers_exclude_exact_deterministic_encoding,

coq/audit/ObservationContract.v.
108   11 From “A record and a toll”


                  The test starts with a model. Establishing that a real apparatus follows that
               model still needs evidence about the apparatus: preparation, controls, readout and
               the range over which the description is claimed. Passing the mathematical test does
               not supply those measurements.

               How long did we watch the lamp?
               Watch two boxes for three ticks. Both start dark and light on the first tick. Both stay
               lit on the second and third. One stays lit forever. The other has a counter inside that
               turns it off on the fourth tick.
                                       Tick Enduring lamp Delayed reset
                                       0       dark              dark
                                       1       lit               lit
                                       2       lit               lit
                                       3       lit               lit
                                       4       lit               dark
                   The first three ticks do not tell the boxes apart. Pick any longer finite watch
               and I can move the reset one tick beyond it. Both boxes can pay one unit per tick
               and satisfy the basic toll. Only the first has permanence. The permanent interface
               excludes the second by its rule, not by pretending that the finite observation already
               excluded it.
                   The construction works for every finite horizon.6 It is not a prediction that every
               real record fails. It shows why a finite run alone cannot establish an unrestricted
               forever claim. A proof from an adequate mechanism, or an explicitly bounded
               reliability claim supported by measurements, has work to do beyond reporting that
               the lamp stayed lit while we watched.




                  6 finite_observations_do_not_establish_permanence, coq/audit/IntrinsicBoundary.v.
CHAPTER 12




From “Why the toll isn’t arbitrary”

There is also a limited statement about how long a noisy record lasts, using a
different quantity: total entropy production during relaxation. Take a device with
no outside hand: a random process hopping among finitely many states at rates that
never change, with a reverse for every hop and local detailed balance, able to get
from any state to any other. Require at least one up-state and one down-state. Left
to run, it settles into a long-run spread π. Measure each up or down stretch from
its entrance, with entrances sampled in the stationary process, and call the mean
lengths Tup and Tdown . Start the device in a known no-state s. The total entropy it
produces over relaxation is at least ln(1 + Tup /Tdown ), in units of k B .
    First compare the starting spread with the spread the device settles into. A state
that is common at the start but rare in the settled spread is a large mismatch. For
each state, take the ratio of those two chances, take its natural logarithm, and
average using the starting chances. With p the starting spread, that number is
                                                                  p( x )
                           D ( p∥π ) =      ∑         p( x ) ln
                                                                  π (x)
                                                                         .
                                         x:p( x )>0

It is called relative entropy. Here every π ( x ) is positive, so the ratios are available.
The number is nonnegative: apply ln y ≤ y − 1 to π ( x )/p( x ), multiply by p( x ), and
add. The right side is at most zero, since the included π chances total at most one
and the p chances total exactly one. Negating that comparison gives D ( p∥π ) ≥ 0.
    For a known start s, all the starting chance is at s. There is only one term:
D = ln(1/π (s)) = − ln π (s). A state with settled chance one tenth therefore starts
with mismatch ln 10. Since s is a no-state, its settled chance cannot exceed the total
chance of all no-states. Its mismatch is at least − ln π (no).
    The physical step in the argument is the fixed-rate entropy-production split of
Massimiliano Esposito and Christian Van den Broeck (2010). In that setting the total
entropy produced is at least the decrease in this relative entropy. Once the spread
has settled to π, the relative entropy left is zero. So the initial mismatch supplies
the lower bound for the whole relaxation.




                                                         109
110   12 From “Why the toll isn’t arbitrary”


                   Now read the same number from the record of up and down stretches. In a long
               settled run, each entry into YES is followed by an exit; apart from the two ends of
               the record, there are equally many up-stretches and down-stretches. Total time up
               is the number of up-stretches times their mean length, and likewise for time down.
               Dividing gives
                                                 π (up)     Tup
                                                         =       .
                                                 π (no)    Tdown
               The two chances add to one. If the ratio on the right is r, the no-share is 1/(1 + r ),
               and its negative logarithm is ln(1 + r ). That gives the stated bound. The settling and
               mean-length arguments below justify passing from a long record to these limiting
               quantities.
                   For a large up-to-down ratio, doubling that ratio raises this lower bound by about
               k B ln 2. Making both stretches longer by the same factor leaves it unchanged. Across
               a family of devices whose ratio tends to infinity, the bound diverges; no single
               finite irreducible device in this class keeps its flag up forever. This is a relaxation
               bound, not a heat charge on each flip. It uses neither a thermodynamic reverse-path
               construction nor time-symmetric control, so it does not identify the quantities in
               those other results. It also leaves the known-input case of the deterministic entropy-
               loss bound where it was: this noisy evolution and its total entropy production are a
               different question. The entropy is added up over one whole relaxation, from the
               known no-state s until the device has settled, and local detailed balance is what
               ties it to heat exchanged with the reservoirs. Total entropy production includes the
               change in the device’s entropy too; it is not heat alone. It also isn’t the permanence
               of the finite-state theorems on a noisy device: on this device the flag comes back
               down, and Tup and Tdown are average stretches.
                   The core of it for a chain that ticks, worked out, runs as follows in the case where
               the long-run spread π gives every state a positive chance and satisfies detailed
               balance: in the long run as much weight flows from x to y in one step as from y
               to x, π ( x ) P( x, y) = π (y) P(y, x ), where P( x, y) is the chance of stepping from x
               to y. Count the entropy produced in one step as the change in the device’s own
               entropy plus what goes out to the surroundings, which for a hop from x to y is
               ln( P( x, y)/P(y, x )). Detailed balance turns that ratio into π (y)/π ( x ), and the sum
               collapses: the entropy produced in one step is exactly the drop in relative entropy to
               π. It’s never negative, because a step that leaves π where it is can’t make any other
               spread harder to tell from π (that’s the data-processing inequality, a standard fact of
               information theory). Add the steps up and the sum telescopes. A known start s has
               relative entropy − ln π (s), so the entropy produced in the first T steps is − ln π (s)
               minus the relative entropy left at step T: it never exceeds − ln π (s), and it reaches it
               in the limit exactly when that remainder goes to zero. Next, − ln π (s) ≥ − ln π (no),
                                                                                            111


because s is one of the no-states and so carries at most their total chance. The flow
from the no-states to the yes-states equals the flow back, because in the long run the
weight on the yes-states doesn’t change, so what flows in equals what flows out. Last,
take each mean stretch as its mass over that flow; then Tup /Tdown = π (up)/π (no),
and since the two chances add to one, − ln π (no) = ln(1 + Tup /Tdown ).
   Try the stretches with a bag of ten counters: nine marked YES and one NO.
Draw, record the answer, replace the counter and mix again. Each draw gives
YES with chance 9/10, whatever the last answer was. A YES stretch ends on the
next draw with chance 1/10, so its mean length is ten draws: the mean L obeys
L = 1 + (9/10) L. A NO stretch similarly averages 10/9 draws. Their ratio is nine,
just like the ratio of YES to NO counters. In a long record, count all the YES entries
and divide by the number of YES stretches; that is the measured mean stretch length.
Divide both counts by the total number of draws and you have mass divided by flow.
   For this rule the long-run flow from NO to YES is (1/10)(9/10) = 9/100 per
draw, and the reverse flow is the same. A start known to be NO has relative entropy
− ln(1/10) = ln 10 against the long-run spread; after one draw the spread is already
1/10, 9/10, so none of that relative entropy remains. This bag shows the probability
accounting. The entropy-production interpretation still needs the stated physical
model; shaking counters in a bag does not measure that heat.
   Two things need establishing for a general device: that it actually settles, and
that “mean stretch length” means what it sounds like. I’ll say what each says, and
why it holds.
   Settling comes first. A chain doesn’t have to settle. It can get stuck in a corner, or
cycle round forever. What rules that out here is a condition named for Wolfgang
Doeblin, a German-born French mathematician, son of the novelist Alfred Döblin,
who took his doctorate at the Sorbonne in 1938 for work on chains like this one and
died in June 1940, at twenty-five. In words: from wherever the device is, its next
step lands on each state with at least some fixed fraction δ of that state’s long-run
chance. No state is ever out of reach in one step, and none is favoured so heavily
that the others starve. Under that condition each step shrinks the distance between
where the device is and where it’s going by the factor 1 − δ (the distance here is the
total of the differences in chance, state by state). To see why, split each step in two:
with chance δ, forget where the device was and draw a fresh state from π; otherwise,
move by whatever is left over, and the condition is exactly what makes the leftover
a genuine set of chances. The fresh draw puts the device’s spread and π in the same
place, so only the other share, 1 − δ, can keep them apart, and no step pushes two
spreads further apart than they were. So the distance dies off geometrically and the
spread converges to π at every state. The payoff for the bound is that the entropy
produced from the known start stays under − ln π (s), and it also gets there: after T
steps it falls short by at most (4/πmin )(1 − δ)2T , where πmin is the smallest long-run
112   12 From “Why the toll isn’t arbitrary”


               chance of any state. The shortfall is the relative entropy left, and ln y ≤ y − 1 once
               more puts that under the sum of ( p( x ) − π ( x ))2 /π ( x ), which is at most the square
               of the distance over πmin ; the distance starts at no more than 2 and shrinks by 1 − δ
               a step. So − ln π (s) is the exact total entropy production of the whole relaxation, in
               units of k B , and the bound − ln π (s) is reached.
                   For rates satisfying detailed balance with π, the same equality holds when the
               device hops at random moments, with no clock ticking. Write out how fast the
               relative entropy to π changes and pair each hop with its reverse. Detailed balance
               makes each pair’s share minus (the flow one way minus the flow back) times the
               logarithm of their ratio, and that is exactly the pair’s share of the rate entropy is
               produced; a difference times the logarithm of the same ratio is never negative. So at
               every instant the relative entropy falls at exactly the rate entropy is being produced,
               and that rate is never negative; add the rate up between two times and you get
               exactly the drop in relative entropy between them. For settling, Doeblin’s condition
               becomes a condition on rates: the rate of hopping from any state x to any state y
               is at least c times π (y), for some fixed c > 0. Split the rates in two again: a part
               that, at rate c, forgets where the device was and redraws it from π, and the rest,
               which still satisfies detailed balance. The production rate adds over the two parts,
               because with detailed balance it’s the rates times quantities that don’t depend on
               the rates. The rest produces nothing negative, and the redraw part alone produces
               at least c times the relative entropy still left. So relative entropy falls at least as fast
               as e−ct . One wrinkle needs its own line. A known start puts all its weight on one
               state and none anywhere else, and the logarithms in the relative entropy go bad at
               zero. The relative entropy is still continuous at the starting instant, because each of
               its terms has the form u ln(u/π ), and u ln u goes to zero as u does. So adding up
               the production from the start out to infinity gives exactly − ln π (s).
                   I should say what Doeblin’s condition costs. It asks for every hop to be possible
               directly, in one step or at a positive rate. A device whose states reach each other
               only through other states can still settle, and the standard theory of these chains
               says when: always, for a device that hops at random moments and can get from any
               state to any other; and for a chain that ticks, as long as it also doesn’t march round a
               cycle. Those devices meet the condition for some fixed stretch of time or number of
               steps instead of one, and the same argument runs on blocks of that length, because
               the relative entropy never goes up in between.
                   Then come the stretches. The bound used the fact that the long-run chances of
               up and down stand in the ratio of the mean stretch lengths, and I took the mean
               length of a stretch with the flag up to be the long-run weight of the up-states divided
               by the flow into them. For a chain that ticks, that’s right for the following reason.
               Start each stretch the way the flow starts them, count the steps until the chain leaves
               the up-states, and the average is exactly π (up) over the flow. Look at the long-run
                                                                                               113


weight that has been up at each of the last k + 1 ticks. It splits in two: the part
whose stretch began exactly k ticks ago, which is the flow times the chance that a
fresh stretch lasts more than k ticks, and the part that was up one tick earlier still.
Add those splits from k = 0 up to some K and the sum telescopes: π (up) is the flow
times the sum of those chances, plus the weight that has been up for more than K
ticks. The sum of the chances that a stretch lasts more than k is its mean length.
Doeblin’s condition makes the leftover die: from any up-state the next step lands in
the down-states with chance at least δ π (down), so the weight that has stayed up
for K ticks is at most (1 − δ π (down))K .
    With the stretches measured in seconds, when the device hops at random mo-
ments, it comes out the same, by a different route. Ask, for each up-state, how
long on average the device takes to leave the up-states from there. Call that its
leaving time. The leaving times are finite: from every up-state the device hops
into the down-states at a total rate of at least c π (down), so the chance a stretch
is still running at time t falls at least as fast as e−c π (down) t , and no leaving time is
more than one over c π (down). And they obey one plain rule, which you get by
asking what happens in the first instant: from any up-state, the rate out of it times
its leaving time, minus the rates into the other up-states times their leaving times,
comes to exactly one (per second times seconds is a pure number). Average the
leaving times over the way the flow starts stretches, multiply by the flow, and the
rule plus the balance of the steady spread (as much weight flows into each state as
out of it) rearrange the sum into exactly π (up), so in seconds too the mean stretch
is its mass over the flow. Both of the bound’s premises are proved now, in steps and
in seconds, under Doeblin’s condition.
CHAPTER 13




From “Learning borrows,
committing pays”

13.1 Somebody got there first, goddamn it

I’d been sitting on this idea that committing, making a result stay so it can’t be
handed back, is what pays, and learning, writing on a blank page you could hand
back, only borrows, and I thought: this one’s mine, I finally had an original thought.
Then I found Carlo Rovelli’s 2024 paper, “The Thermodynamic Cost of Choosing.”
I hadn’t. Somebody got there first, goddamn it: a real physicist, with a real paper,
saying that making a choice that sticks dissipates at least the energy Landauer
charges for erasing a bit. He even quotes a German saying, Wahl ist Leiden, to choose
is to suffer, and at that point what are you going to do but laugh.
    And then, taking him apart properly, it got good, because on its face he contradicts
Bennett. Bennett says measuring owes no floor; Rovelli says any measurement costs.
So which is it? Look at what Rovelli calls a choice. A coin in the air hasn’t chosen
anything; it has to hit the ground and stop. And then comes the sentence I’d put
on my wall: “stabilization must be irreversible, otherwise it is not stabilization.”
Bennett’s floorless measurement is one that could still be undone; that’s why it owes
no floor. It only borrows the page. Rovelli’s choice is one that’s been made to stay;
that’s why it costs. The way I read them, they’re standing on opposite sides of the
same line: learning, which borrows, on Bennett’s side; committing, which pays, on
Rovelli’s. (Bennett’s demon still pays in the end, when it wipes the page.) Neither of
them puts it that way, so push on it. But if it holds, it’s the best sign I’ve got that the
line I’m drawing between finding something out and making it stay isn’t something
I made up.
    Push on it with his paper open and it gives in four places, which I go through at
the end of this chapter, once the demon has been built. So the two-sides-of-one-line
picture is my reading, not his, and the piece of it that’s really his is the one sentence
about stabilizing. That sentence I’ll still put on my wall.
    So he’s my closest neighbour, and the difference between us is exact. His is
physics: a dissipation bound, at least kT ln 2 at the temperature of the recording

                                   114
                                                                13.2 A small being at a small door   115


parts, with no outside heat bath, for a choice that sticks. Mine is a rule in a model
of computation: a charge in the machine’s own count, on the step that flips a
permanent reading on, with no temperature anywhere in it. It’s the same instinct in
a different place, and I’ll take it, because it’s annoying to be second but worse to
be alone and wrong. And he’s the closest only on choosing. On copying there’s an
older neighbour, in the chemistry of copying molecules, and the end of this chapter
gets to it.


13.2 A small being at a small door

You met Maxwell’s demon briefly in Section 2. It gets a whole section here, because
the step it seemed to pay for and the step it actually pays for are what this section is
about.
    In December 1867 James Clerk Maxwell, who had published his equations for
electricity and magnetism two years before, in 1865, and to my mind had as good a
claim as anyone to being the most important physicist alive, wrote a letter to his
friend Peter Tait describing a problem he couldn’t get rid of. Take a box of gas at one
temperature, with a wall down the middle and a tiny door in the wall. Now imagine,
he said, a “very observant and neat-fingered being” sitting at the door, who can see
individual molecules coming. When a fast one approaches from the left, it opens
the door and lets it through. When a slow one approaches from the right, same.
Otherwise it keeps the door shut. It never pushes anything; it only opens and closes
a frictionless door at the right moments. After a while the right side is hot and the
left side is cold, and you’ve got a temperature difference you could run an engine
off, made out of nothing but looking. That’s supposed to be impossible; it’s the thing
the second law of thermodynamics says can’t happen, and the second law is the law
physicists are least willing to give up. Arthur Eddington, the English astronomer,
wrote in a 1928 book of popular lectures that a theory found to contradict it has
nothing for it but to collapse in deepest humiliation, and nobody has had to test that
yet.
    Maxwell put it in his textbook in 1871, and it got named Maxwell’s demon,
though not by Maxwell. William Thomson, later Lord Kelvin, called it a demon
in Nature in 1874, and Maxwell, in an undated note to Tait headed “Concerning
Demons,” tried to take the name back: “Call him no more a demon but a valve . . . ”
Nobody listened, which is probably for the best, because “Maxwell’s valve” would
not have lasted a century. But keep his correction, because it’s exactly right and it’s
the whole problem: the being does no work. It opens and shuts a door. So physicists
spent the next century trying to find where the being was cheating. I want you to
feel the pull of the obvious answer, because I felt it and so did most of them: the
being has to see the molecules, seeing costs, and the case is closed. In 1929 Leo
116   13 From “Learning borrows, committing pays”


              Szilard built a one-molecule version of the box and did the arithmetic, and said
              the second law survives if finding out which side the molecule is on costs at least
              as much entropy as the being gains. In 1951 the French physicist Léon Brillouin
              made it concrete: to see a molecule you have to bounce light off it, and the way the
              philosopher of physics Jeffrey Bub sums up the result, finding out that way costs
              at least k ln 2 of entropy for every bit, which is kT ln 2 of heat, Landauer’s number
              before Landauer, and there was your charge on looking. For thirty years that was
              the answer. Information costs because getting it costs.
                  That’s a price on one way of looking, and it doesn’t show that every possible way
              of making a record has to pay it. A device can burn energy without that being a law.
                  Szilard is worth one more minute, for a reason that has nothing to do with the
              demon. Four years after the one-molecule engine, in September 1933, by his own
              later telling, he was waiting at a traffic light on Southampton Row in London, having
              just read a newspaper report in which Ernest Rutherford, the physicist who had
              announced the atomic nucleus in 1911, called the hope of getting power out of
              atoms moonshine, and as the light changed it occurred to him that an element which
              gave off two neutrons when it swallowed one could keep a reaction going by itself.
              That’s the nuclear chain reaction, thought up crossing a street. It’s his memory of
              himself, and Richard Rhodes’s history of the bomb opens with it, in a first chapter
              called “Moonshine,” which is where most people meet it, so hold it as the man’s own
