              story, not a record made at the time. Does it change anything about the demon? No.
              What it adds is a picture of the man: the same mind looked at one molecule in 1929
              and one neutron in 1933, and both times the question was what a single small step
              forces downstream.
                  This is also where I settle up with Rovelli, my closest neighbour from the start
              of this chapter, because I put him and Bennett on opposite sides of one line: a
              measurement that can still be undone borrows and owes no floor, and a choice that
              sticks pays. I owe you how much of that is mine. All of the reconciliation is mine.
              Rovelli’s paper doesn’t make it, and in four places his own framing points somewhere
              else. He reserves “choice” for an outcome that is stable and not predictable, and he
              rules out a thermostat because it runs on a predictable mechanism; the commit in this
              book is a step a rule fixes in advance, so by his own definition his result doesn’t cover
              it. His choice is a branching, one possibility splitting into two, and he calls choosing
              in a sense the inverse of erasing, because branching is the inverse of merging; the
              toll comes from a merge, the opposite shape. His stability is for a while: he says
              stabilization means something only on times that aren’t too long next to the time
              it takes things to thermalize, and past that nothing stays chosen; my permanence
              is forever (and the last chapter owned up that forever is an idealization). And he
              doesn’t stand beside Bennett at all: he holds that any measurement is dissipative,
              credits the point to the philosopher of science Hans Reichenbach, and doesn’t cite
                                                               13.2 A small being at a small door   117


Bennett. So what Rovelli and I share is a sentence, that a choice that sticks costs
something like Landauer’s heat. There’s no shared mechanism. Push on it and his
coin hitting the ground, a fact gained from something nobody could predict, sits
closer to what this section calls learning than to committing. I still think it’s the
same instinct. This is my reading, and he doesn’t make it; you now have everything
you need to disagree with it.
    And on one point Rovelli isn’t the nearest physics. The nearest I’ve found is in
biology. Thomas Ouldridge and Pieter Rein ten Wolde (2017) derived what they call
the fundamental costs of making a persistent copy of a polymer, one whose sequence
stays correlated with its template after the two have physically come apart, and of
destroying one. On their account a copy like that is a state out of equilibrium, and
making it has a thermodynamic cost they measure efficiency against. A companion
paper the same year, with Christopher Govern, does the same kind of accounting
for the molecules a cell uses to read out the state of its receptors, which is a
measurement in all but name. The way I read them, the copy that has to stay is
the one that pays, and that’s the closest thing in physics I’ve found to the line this
section draws between finding out and committing. The differences, so it doesn’t get
borrowed for more than it is: theirs is work and stored free energy, in chemistry, for
a correlation between two molecules, and their title has the destruction of copies in
it too, so their copies don’t last forever; mine is a count in a model of computation,
for a reading that never turns off, with no temperature anywhere. It’s the same
instinct again, from a different direction.
    The chemistry has the sharp form of it, too, and it came two years later. Jenny
Poulton, Pieter Rein ten Wolde and Thomas Ouldridge (2019) studied minimal mod-
els of polymer copying, and in those models, when the copy grows at equilibrium,
with the weakest chemical driving, a persistent copy’s accuracy advantage over
random copying and the efficiency of storing that information both vanish. This
does not mean every copied site is wrong. Driving growth out of equilibrium can
produce correlations that remain after the two polymers let go, with chemical work
supplied to the process. This is finding out against committing, in chemistry, with
the dissipation attached, and I’d rather say plainly that biology had the distinction
before I did. What stays different is the kind of thing the record is: theirs is a
correlation in a chemical ensemble, mine is a reading enforced by a step in a model
of computation.
    Two more neighbours have “learning” and “commit” in their titles, so the slogan
shouldn’t be read against them by accident. Sebastian Goldt and Udo Seifert (2017)
did the stochastic thermodynamics of a neural network learning a rule, and argued
from it that learning has a thermodynamic cost: on their account, the entropy the
network produces bounds how much it can learn. That doesn’t contradict this section,
and the reason is that their learner isn’t copying onto a blank page. Its weights are a
118   13 From “Learning borrows, committing pays”


              driven thermal system, jostled by noise and dragged toward what the data push them
              to, and what they price is that physical process. This section’s claim is narrower: one
              way of finding out, a copy onto a blank, is allowed to owe no floor and no cost. It can
              dissipate under the stated thermal-model assumptions, and it still spends the blank.
              Nothing here says every way of learning is cheap, and theirs isn’t. From statistics,
              Aurélien Garivier, Tor Lattimore and Emilie Kaufmann (2016) compare two arms
              with unit-variance Gaussian rewards and different unknown means. Their explore-
              then-commit strategies sample the arms equally during exploration, then choose one
              for the remaining horizon. Within the uniformly efficient classes they compare, the
              best leading logarithmic regret is twice that of fully sequential strategies. Whether
              the gap between the means is known changes both constants; fixing the exploration
              length in advance changes the comparison again. The factor two belongs to that
              model and strategy class, not every act called commitment. That’s the closest
              published sentence I’ve found to “committing costs something” with a number on it.
              Their price is reward lost, which is a different unit from a unit of cost on a ledger, so
              it’s a cousin. A twin would have to count cost.
CHAPTER 14




From “What structure is”

14.1 A string, a square, and a drowning nobody can place

Start with the oldest one, and start by doing it. Take a string. A guitar string, a
rubber band over an open box, anything that twangs. Pluck it. Keep its tension
as nearly unchanged as you can when you stop it at a new point; stretching it
tighter changes the pitch for a second reason. Now stop it exactly at the middle
and pluck half. With the tension held steady, that’s the same note an octave up, the
one that sounds like the same note sung higher. Press at a third of the way along
and pluck the long side: that’s the fifth, one of the handful of intervals a bugle can
play at all, which is why you hear it all through bugle calls. Half, two thirds: small
whole numbers, and your ear says yes before your brain does. About twenty-five
hundred years ago, in the south of Italy, the followers of a man named Pythagoras
are credited with finding exactly that, and it’s as true in your kitchen tonight as it
was on their hillside.
   I’ll tell you what they did with it, as best anyone can tell. Nothing survives that’s
surely from Pythagoras’s own hand. The earliest fragments are from a later member,
Philolaus, and scholars argue over those. Mostly what’s left is Aristotle, one to two
centuries later, writing in his Metaphysics about them the way you’d write about
the neighbours’ religion, and he says they took the elements of numbers to be the
elements of all things: everything is number. That doesn’t mean everything can be
counted; it means the parts of numbers are the parts of all things.
   Stop and ask what I asked. What does that even mean? Is a chair a number?
Does a chair have a number tucked inside it somewhere? Aristotle doesn’t say, and
he’s reporting a belief he doesn’t hold. So don’t take it on his word, or theirs, or
mine. But before you push on it, know what it was holding up, because you can’t
test a load-bearing wall without knowing what sits on it. These weren’t people
doing math for fun on weekends. They were a brotherhood, with rules about what
you could eat and when you had to keep quiet, and that plucked string was their
scripture. Halve the string, hear the octave: to them that was proof that under the
mess of the world there were whole numbers in clean ratios, that the cosmos was
a chord and they’d heard it. “Everything is number” wasn’t a slogan; it was what


                                                  119
120   14 From “What structure is”


               they’d organized their lives around. And that’s exactly the kind of sentence you
               should be suspicious of, mine included.
                   Now push, and push where they loved most: the square. Draw one, with a side
               one unit long, any unit you like, and draw the line from one corner to the opposite
               corner. Call that the diagonal. If everything is number, then the diagonal is some
               whole number of parts long when the side is some whole number of parts, so the
               diagonal divided by the side is a fraction, a whole number over a whole number.
               Cancel anything the top and bottom share, the way four-sixths becomes two-thirds,
               until they have no common factor left. I’ll call the top p and the bottom q, and the
               letters are purely so I don’t have to keep writing “the top” and “the bottom.”
                   You need one fact about squares, and it’s the one Pythagoras is famous for. The
               square built on the diagonal has exactly twice the area of the square built on the side.
               If you want to see it for yourself, cut out two identical squares, cut the second one
               along both diagonals into four triangles, and lay one triangle against each side of the
               first, its long edge on that side. The outline is the square on the first one’s diagonal,
               and it’s made of exactly two of the small squares; it’s a five-minute job with scissors.
               So p times p is twice q times q. People write “times itself” as a small raised two, so
               that’s p2 = 2q2 , and that is the only piece of notation in this argument.
                   Twice anything is even. So p times p is even. An odd number times itself is
               always odd: draw that many rows of that many dots, pair off the columns, then pair
               the dots in the one column left over, and one dot remains. The same pairing works
               for any odd side, so if p were odd, p times p would be odd too. It isn’t, so p itself
               must be even: it’s two times something, and I’ll call that something r. Put it back
               in. Two r times two r is four times r times r, and that equals twice q times q. Halve
               both sides: two times r times r equals q times q. So q times q is even, so q is even
               too, by the same step as before. But you already cancelled everything the top and
               bottom had in common, so they can’t both be even. The argument has eaten itself,
               and when an argument eats itself the thing you supposed at the start is false. There
               is no such fraction. The diagonal of a square can’t be named by any ratio of whole
               numbers, ever. Aristotle reaches for this very argument in his Prior Analytics as his
               example of proof by contradiction (suppose the diagonal can be measured, and odd
               numbers come out equal to even ones), and you just did it with a pencil.
                   There’s a story that a member of the brotherhood named Hippasus let this
               secret out, and his brothers took him to sea and drowned him for it. I went and
               checked, because it’s a good story and good stories are the ones to check. The oldest
               drownings I could find are in Iamblichus, writing around AD 300, seven or eight
               hundred years after Hippasus. In Iamblichus the man drowned by name is Hippasus,
               and what he gave away was how to fit twelve pentagons together into a ball, the
               dodecahedron. A few pages on, Iamblichus tells it again about whoever first let out
               the incommensurable, and that time he names nobody. Carl Huffman, who wrote
                                                                    14.2 Shapes poured into nothing   121


the Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy’s entry on Pythagoreanism, follows the
historian Walter Burkert there and finds no ancient writer who connects Hippasus
to this discovery at all. So the version with Hippasus drowned for the diagonal
looks like two old stories stitched together later. It’s a legend, but notice that the
legend exists. Somebody, long after, felt that a man should have been drowned for
this. That’s how much was riding on it. To whoever stitched the story together, the
diagonal wasn’t a math problem; it was the end of the world.
    So the claim is dead, and you killed it. I only handed you the pencil. Now the
question that matters: what’s left? Look at your square again. The diagonal is
still there. It still stands in a perfectly definite relation to the side; you drew it,
and nobody drowned. That relation was real before anyone tried to express it as a
fraction, and it stayed real after that attempt failed. The relation came first. The
number, when there is one, is a name for the relation, and sometimes there isn’t a
name. I’m going to keep that, and I want you to keep it too, because you’re going to
watch the same move get made again and again as this book goes on: somebody
says “everything is X,” and it’s true enough to do real work with, and it sounds right
when you pluck it, and then somebody draws the diagonal. Whether the thing I
built survives its own diagonal is a real question, and Section 13 answers it.


14.2 Shapes poured into nothing

About a century and a half after the brotherhood, Plato, in a dialogue called the
Timaeus, tried to build the whole physical world out of geometry. Fire, air, water and
earth were four regular solids (the pyramid, the eight-faced one, the twenty-faced
one, and the cube), and the faces of those solids were built from exactly two kinds
of right triangle. You can make one of each in a minute: cut a square from corner to
corner for the first, and cut an equilateral triangle from a corner to the middle of
the opposite side for the second. Everything else, in Plato’s story, was a receptacle,
a featureless medium that takes on shape the way wax takes a seal. Structure, for
Plato, is shape laid into something that has no shape of its own.
    Why did he care? He wanted a world made on purpose, by a craftsman working
from a plan, and triangles were the plan. Is it true? No, but not for the cheap
reason. You can’t refute a claim about what things are made of at the smallest scale
by picking one up; the smallest scale is exactly where your hands don’t reach, and
Plato knew that. What sank it is that it made no prediction anyone could check,
and the people who eventually did reach that scale, two thousand years on, found
atoms, and atoms aren’t triangles. The idea was a guess with no way to lose, until it
lost. But push on it and two things survive. One: structure is something laid in, and
the stuff it’s laid into doesn’t carry it by itself. That’s a real idea, and I’d ask you to
keep it loosely for now; when the machine arrives in Part III, you can ask whether
122   14 From “What structure is”


               the state it holds is the wax or the seal. Two: Plato put a crack in his own forms
               himself, in another dialogue, the Parmenides, with an objection that Aristotle later
               nicknamed the third man, and it’s the most useful mistake in this section. Take all
               the large things. Say they share one thing, Largeness. But Largeness is itself large,
               so now there’s a further thing that all the large things and Largeness share, and
               that one is large too, and it never stops. The extra move is treating Largeness itself
               as one more large thing. Grant that again at each stage and it never stops; refuse
               it and this particular regress never starts. The lesson, and I think it’s right though
               Plato never spells it out: the structure of a collection is not one more member of the
               collection. The plan isn’t a brick. Hold on to that one.


               14.3 Prove it from what you and I both accept

              Around 300 BC, in Alexandria, Euclid wrote the Elements, and the thing to take
              from it is not the geometry. It’s the method, and the method is the whole reason I
              trust anything in this book, so give me a paragraph on it. He starts with definitions
              (“a point is that which has no part”), five postulates (things he asks you to grant,
              like “to draw a straight line from any point to any point”), and five common notions
              (things nobody argues with, like “things which are equal to the same thing are also
              equal to one another”). Then he derives everything else, four hundred and sixty-five
              propositions, from only those, in order, each meant to use nothing but what came
              before (he sometimes leans on things he never stated, and later geometers filled
              those in). He never says “trust me” or “it’s well known”; he says, in effect, here are
              the rules you and I both accept, now watch. If you’ve ever been in an argument that
              ended with “well, that’s just your opinion,” Euclid is the man who found a way out
              of that room, and I’m not embarrassed to say I learned the way out from someone
              dead for two thousand three hundred years. Four hundred and sixty-five times he
              does the same trick, and the trick is everything.
                  And then there’s the one rule Euclid seems almost embarrassed by, and it’s a
              hole worth falling into. His fifth postulate, the one about parallel lines, is long and
              awkward next to the others, and for two thousand years people tried to prove it from
              the rest so it wouldn’t have to be assumed. Proclus, the fifth-century philosopher
              who wrote a commentary on Euclid’s first book, tried. Mathematicians in Persia and
              the Arab world tried. In 1733 an Italian priest named Girolamo Saccheri spent a
              whole book assuming it was false so he could watch the contradiction arrive, and he
              got pages deep into what is now a perfectly good geometry before declaring that it
              was repugnant and stopping. A century later three people, separately, took the step
              he wouldn’t: drop the postulate, and what you get is other geometries that hold up
              just as well, and nonsense never arrives. Farkas Bolyai begged his son János to stay
              away from the problem, warning that it had swallowed years of his own life; the
                                                                 14.4 The statue and the bronze   123


son went in anyway and came out with one of those geometries. The part I keep
is that they found something real by refusing to believe one of the rules everyone
accepted, and checking what happened without it. Refusing a rule and checking
what happens without it is the push, and this one went on for two thousand years.
    What is structure, to Euclid? Whatever the starting rules force. And when are
two shapes the same? You can do this one yourself: cut out two paper triangles
and lay one on the other. If they coincide, they’re equal. That’s his fourth common
notion, and it’s how he proves his fourth proposition, by picking up one triangle and
setting it down on another. Sameness is coinciding under a move. Keep that; it’s
about to get very general, and I didn’t see that coming either.


14.4 The statue and the bronze

Aristotle, Plato’s student, pulled the forms down out of heaven and put them inside
things, which was a bit rude to his teacher and also, I think, correct. His example,
from the Metaphysics: a bronze statue is bronze plus a shape. And a circle can be
made in bronze or stone or wood, so, he says, the bronze is no part of what it is to
be a circle. The same shape in marble makes a different statue with the same form.
The bronze is the matter and the shape is the form, and the form is what the thing
is.
    Push on it with two things from the kitchen. A cup can be ceramic or metal and
still hold your coffee. A sandwich carved in wood can keep the outline and fail to be
lunch. The two swaps preserve different things. If the question is shape, the carving
may pass; if the question is whether you can eat it, it does not.
    Aristotle already makes a distinction of this kind. In Book VII, chapter 11, he
warns against treating a living animal just like the circle whose bronze can be
changed: its parts must be in a condition to do their work. The sandwich is a way to
make that difficulty visible, not a correction he never considered.
    That is the bet I need for the record. Change the material, then check the
operations and the answers again. If they still agree, the record has survived the
change in the respects I promised. Section 11 states those promises over a chosen
base; Part III builds an instance. Neither theorem says that every material can do
every job, or that the price in one material is the price in another.


14.5 Where things are, without numbers

In 1679 Gottfried Leibniz, who co-invented calculus and had opinions about every-
thing, wrote to the physicist Christiaan Huygens asking for something that didn’t
exist: a way to reason about where things are directly, the way algebra reasons
about how much, without ever laying down coordinates. He called it the analysis of
124   14 From “What structure is”


               situation. Try it yourself. Describe where the furniture is in the room you’re sitting in
               without using a single number. “The couch is against the wall. The table is between
               the couch and the window. The lamp is next to the chair.” You just did it, and what
               you used was relations between places and nothing else. Huygens wrote back that
               November and was not impressed. It took roughly two hundred years for Leibniz’s
               idea to become a field, and the field is called topology, and Huygens is remembered
               for other things.
                   What survives the push is the same thing that survived the square, coming at you
               from the other side. Position is a relation among places. The numbers you lay on
               top are a convenience, and they’re a convenience you can take away and still have
               the room. I’ll be honest about why this one landed for me: the thing I built keeps
               track of which things belong together, and I spent a long time thinking that was a
               list of numbers before I understood it was a relation that the numbers were merely
               naming. Leibniz had that in 1679. Huygens told him no. I’d like to think I’d have
               sided with Leibniz. I probably would have sided with the famous guy.


               14.6 Naming a thing by saying when two are the same
              In 1884, in a short book called The Foundations of Arithmetic, Gottlob Frege noticed
              something you can check in your head right now. “Line a is parallel to line b” and
              “the direction of a is the direction of b” say exactly the same thing. There is no fact in
              one that isn’t in the other. But the second sentence has a new object in it, a direction,
              that the first one doesn’t. Where did it come from? From the sameness. Once you
              can say when two things count as the same, you’ve earned the right to name the
              thing they share. Frege used exactly this move to say what a number is: the number
              of things in a pile is whatever every pile you can match up with it, one to one, has
              in common. The residue has teeth: a sameness relation lets you name a new object.
                  Now push, because Frege pushed too hard, and it cost him. In his formal system,
              Basic Law V treated value-ranges as objects and identified them exactly when
              the corresponding functions agreed everywhere. Together with his unrestricted
              comprehension principles, that let the self-membership construction in. The familiar
              set version is easier to tell: In June of 1902, a young British philosopher and logician,
              Bertrand Russell, wrote him a short letter. Consider the collection of all collections
              that are not members of themselves. Is it a member of itself? If it is, then by its own
              rule it isn’t. If it isn’t, then it qualifies, so it is. Do it in your head; it flips every time
              you look at it, like one of those drawings that’s a vase and then two faces and then a
              vase. Frege’s floor had a hole in it, and he knew it the day he opened the letter, and
              his second volume was already at the printer. One repair was Zermelo’s 1908 axiom
              of separation: a property selects a subset of a set already given. His system also had
              other existence axioms, including power set and infinity; separation was not its only
                                                       14.7 Choose the changes, keep what survives   125


way to obtain a set. What it removed was unrestricted sorting of “everything.” You
sort a pile. I’ll tell you now that this rule shows up again, later, in a place where I
had to obey it or fall through the same hole, and I’ll point at it when the book gets
there.
    Frege’s reply is worth hearing, because it’s the best example I know of how to
lose. He wrote back within days. He didn’t hide it or argue around it. He said, more
or less, that arithmetic was tottering, and in an appendix to the volume that was
about to come out he wrote that hardly anything worse can happen to a scientific
writer than having a foundation give way right after the work is finished. Then he
tried to fix it. If I’m wrong about something in this book, and I’d bet I am somewhere,
that’s how I’d like to take it.


14.7 Choose the changes, keep what survives

By 1872 there were too many geometries. Euclid’s, and ones where parallel lines
meet, and ones with no such thing as distance, and nobody could say what made
them all geometry and kept them from being a pile of unrelated hobbies. Felix Klein,
twenty-three years old and taking up a professorship at Erlangen, published an
answer in a program paper that people still call the Erlangen program. Pick a set
of allowed changes: rotations, stretches, whatever you decide. Then a geometry is
the study of the properties that stay the same under those changes. Pick a different
set of changes and you get a different geometry on the very same page. Euclid’s, in
Klein’s reading, is the one where you may slide, turn and flip a shape and scale it
evenly, and stretching it more one way than another is off limits. And I’d underline,
if this were my book, which it is, that in Klein’s scheme any group of changes can
define a geometry. The choice is yours. Nobody tells you which changes to allow.
    You can do this one at your desk. Take any drawing and ask what’s still true after
you turn it upside down (lengths, angles), after you stretch it (lengths are gone by
then; which lines cross which is still there), after you bend and crumple the sheet
without tearing it (which drawn paths remain connected survives). Two distant ink
marks may touch across a fold; that new physical contact is not a new connection in
the drawing. You have to say which relation you are following. The three changes
give three answers and three structures, all from one drawing. The residue is that
structure is what survives a chosen set of changes, and “chosen” is doing real work
in that sentence. There’s no structure of a thing from nowhere. You have to say
what you’re allowed to do to it first. I didn’t know that when I started, and I’d been
saying “the structure of” things for years.
    Klein’s move has a descendant in logic, and it goes all the way. In a lecture in
London in 1966, published only in 1986, three years after his death, the Polish-
American logician Alfred Tarski asked what makes a notion logical, and answered
126   14 From “What structure is”


               with Klein’s recipe at its far end: allow every one-to-one transformation of the world
               onto itself, and call logical the notions that survive all of them. The more changes
               you allow, the fewer notions are left standing, and with every change allowed what’s
               left is logic. Tarski’s answer is the sentence above, “structure is what survives a
               chosen set of changes,” with the choice made as large as it can be.


               14.8 An invariance comes with a conserved quantity
               In 1918 Emmy Noether, who at the time wasn’t allowed to qualify for a university
               teaching post because she was a woman, and for a while taught courses advertised
               under David Hilbert’s name, proved something that physicists still treat as close to
               holy. Take a physical system whose laws can be written the way most of physics
               is written, as “of all the paths the system could take, it takes one that makes a
               certain total stationary” (the total is called the action, and stationary means a small
               change to the path doesn’t change the total to first order; the path needn’t make it
               smallest, and that’s all I’ll say about it here). For such a system, every continuous
               symmetry that leaves the action unchanged comes with a quantity that is conserved.
               If the action doesn’t change when you shift everything in space, momentum is
               conserved. If it doesn’t change when you shift everything in time, energy is. A
               symmetry isn’t decoration. It has a consequence downstream that you can measure
               with instruments. I can’t hand you this one to do at the table; the proof needs
               calculus I’d have to build first and I’d lose you and probably me, and the “written
               as an action” condition is doing real work that I’m not going to unpack here. What
               I can hand you is the shape. Structure, once it’s really there and the setting is the
               right kind, has consequences you can measure. That’s the sentence that made me
               think a machine could carry a cost for structure in the first place. Noether makes
               it reasonable to ask. She doesn’t make it true, and nothing I build in this book is a
               system with an action.


               14.9 All of mathematics as structures
               Starting in the 1930s, a group of young French mathematicians began publishing
               a vast treatise under one made-up name, Nicolas Bourbaki, as if he were a single
               person. He wasn’t. In 1948 he also applied for membership in the American
               Mathematical Society, and the application was quietly turned down, which I find
               comforting. (The historian Michael Barany has gone through that application, a
               second one a year later, and the letters they set off.) Their goal was to rebuild all
               of mathematics on one idea: a structure is a set of elements “whose nature has not
               been specified,” together with one or more relations on them that satisfy stated
                                                              14.10 The structure is in the arrows   127


axioms. They believed three kinds of structure were the mothers of all the rest:
algebraic, order, and topological.
    Push on it. The historian Leo Corry went through the entire treatise looking for
the place where this formal definition of structure actually does any work, and what
he found is that it “plays no actual role in the presentation of theories within the
treatise.” Outside the chapter that defines it, it does work almost nowhere. And I
found no argument in their writing for why the mother structures number three. And
the chapter on category theory, which they promised their readers, never appeared;
Corry is where I found that too, and Ralf Krömer’s 2006 history of Bourbaki and
categories in the 1950s follows the attempt. That does not mean Bourbaki left
maps out. Their Theory of Sets has sections on morphisms, derived structures and
universal mappings (Chapter IV, sections 2 and 3). Corry’s criticism concerns how
much their general formal apparatus was actually used. The lesson I can take for my
own work is smaller: naming a structure is not enough; show which constructions
and comparisons it supports. I learned that one the slow way, and the origin story at
the back of the book (Section 24) tells it.
    Philosophers of mathematics took the same idea further and gave it a name,
structuralism. In 1965 the philosopher Paul Benacerraf pointed out that the whole
numbers can be built out of sets in two equally good ways that disagree about
which set the number three is, and concluded that numbers aren’t objects at all:
what arithmetic is about is the pattern, and anything standing in the right relations
to everything else can play the part of three. The philosophers Michael Resnik
(Mathematics as a Science of Patterns) and Stewart Shapiro (Philosophy of Mathe-
matics: Structure and Ontology), both in 1997, made the patterns themselves what
mathematics is about. The philosopher Geoffrey Hellman (Mathematics without
Numbers, 1989) kept the talk of patterns and did without the patterns, reading each
statement as a claim about what would hold in any system with the right structure,
if there were one. The logician Steve Awodey (1996) argued that category theory
says it best, a structure being what its maps keep, which is where this section goes
next. And the philosopher of science James Ladyman (1998) carried the idea into
physics: what a successful theory gets right about the world is its structure. This
section’s thesis, that structure is the relations and the things in them don’t matter,
is theirs. None of the formal results takes their arguments as a premise, and the
bibliography lists them under the same heading as Bourbaki.


14.10 The structure is in the arrows

I met this one first, and backwards. I’d built it by feel, in a rendering engine, as
“digital DNA,” and only went looking for its name after the thing already ran. In
1945 two mathematicians, Samuel Eilenberg and Saunders Mac Lane, published a
128   14 From “What structure is”


               paper that founded a field now called category theory, and they said in plain words
               that it was a continuation of Klein, which I’d have loved to know at the time. The
               move they made was to stop asking what a thing is, and ask how it’s carried. A
               category is some objects and some arrows between them, where arrows can be
               followed one after another and every object has a do-nothing arrow to itself, plus
               two rules that make the following dependable: following three arrows in a row gives
               the same result whether you pair up the first two or the last two, and the do-nothing
               arrow really does nothing when you tack it onto either end, and that’s the whole
               definition. On the page the two rules look like nothing, and they’re the part people
               skip, so slow down on them for a second, because each one is there for a reason you
               can see.
                   Picture three dots in a row, A, B and C, an arrow f hopping from the first to
               the second and an arrow g from the second to the third. Composition says there’s
               an arrow taking the whole stride from A to C in one, g after f . Without it, every
               multi-hop path has to be named on its own, and the structure falls apart into a pile
               of unconnected pairs, a phone book without a search function. Associativity says
               that with a third arrow h on the end, pairing up the first two and then adding h gives
               the same arrow as adding h to the last two first. Both spell the same three-hop path.
               With your paper triangle, bracket “slide, then turn” before “flip,” or bracket “turn,
               then flip” after “slide”: you still carry out the same three moves in the same order.
               Swapping the turn and the flip would be a different question. If the two bracketings
               came out different, two people who built the same chain in different orders would
               hold two different arrows, which is a bug, because the chain is the thing and how
               you assembled it isn’t. The identity laws say that tacking the do-nothing loop onto
               either end of an arrow gives back the arrow. If the loop changed things, it wouldn’t
               be the loop. Those six terms, objects, arrows, composition, identities, associativity
               and identity laws, are all of Eilenberg and Mac Lane’s definition.
                   Composing relations is older than the definition, too. Who put a name on that
               move first? I went looking. Augustus De Morgan, writing on the logic of relations
               around 1860, took the “is” out of the old syllogism, put a general relation in its place,
               and composed two relations to get the conclusion. The example that gets quoted
               from that tradition, Peirce’s a decade later, is “x is the lover of a servant of y,” which
               is relational composition with better gossip. When exactly De Morgan read that
               paper to the Cambridge Philosophical Society, and on which pages it was printed, the
               sources disagree, which is a small reminder that history is where you start digging.
               Then in 1870 Charles Sanders Peirce, an American scientist and philosopher who
               turns up again in Section 14, carried George Boole’s algebra of logic (the arithmetic
               of true and false that Part III comes back to in Section 15, along with the story of
               Boole himself, a largely self-taught schoolmaster) over to relations, with composition
               as one of its basic operations, and he’s usually counted as the real founder of the
                                                          14.11 Structure is the part that compresses   129


theory. So following one arrow after another is older than computers, older than
categories, older than Turing’s machine: it is a nineteenth-century logician’s product
of relations.
    And where did they get the word? They didn’t get it from mathematics. They
borrowed category from the philosophers, from Aristotle and Kant, and functor (a
map from one whole category to another) from the philosopher Rudolf Carnap,
and then gave both words exact meanings none of those philosophers had in mind.
So Aristotle walks back into this chapter without being asked: the man who put
the form inside the bronze lent his word to the field that keeps track of forms by
their arrows. That doesn’t change the laws. It’s a reminder that a word can start in
philosophy and arrive in mathematics with a precise meaning bolted on, which is
the thing I’ve been trying to do with established since the start of the book.
    What a thing is is how it maps. For now the residue is the emphasis I needed:
structure lives in the maps. Nothing later in this book needs more category theory
than that sentence, and the latch of Section 11, where a record is pinned down by
the maps between a machine and the base it extends, is where the sentence does its
work.


14.11 Structure is the part that compresses

Play this one with me. I write “th” and you guess the next letter. You said “e,” and
you were right. Now “the q”: you said “u.” Congratulations, you just measured
the structure of English. In 1948 Claude Shannon measured it properly, in a paper
that founded information theory, and found that about half of written English is
“determined by the structure of the language.” It’s the reason a zip file of a long
letter is so much smaller than the letter. Be careful what that says. It does not say
that every other letter is fixed by the ones before it; you just guessed “e” after “th”
and you could have been wrong (think “than”). It says that, on average and over
long stretches, about half of what’s written could be rebuilt from the rest, so that
the text is roughly twice as long as the choices in it. And he said why that matters in
one line that I’d frame if I framed things: the structure “allows one to make a saving
in time” (he added “or channel capacity”). The more you can predict, the less you
have to read.
    Thirty years later Jorma Rissanen, a Finnish information theorist at IBM’s research
lab in San Jose, turned that saving into a price. His idea is called the minimum
description length principle, and it says that the best model of some data is the one
that makes the total description shortest, where the total is the length of the model
plus the length of the data once the model has explained what it can. A model is paid
for in bits, and it’s worth stating only if it saves more than it costs. Peter Grünwald, a
statistician who leads a machine learning group at CWI, the Dutch national institute
130   14 From “What structure is”


               for mathematics and computer science in Amsterdam, in a tutorial you can find
               online, adds the footnote that keeps this honest: the price of a model depends on
               the code you chose to write it in, so the price itself needs a principle behind it, and
               that principle is something you have to argue for, not something you’re handed.
                  That’s a lot of “length of” to swallow in one paragraph, so do one with me, with
               numbers small enough to check on the back of an envelope. Every number in it
               comes from a code I’m about to make up on the spot, and I’ll say every rule of the
               code out loud, because Grünwald’s footnote is the whole point: change the rules
               and every number moves.
                  The data is a row of thirty-two digits, each a 0 or a 1, and you and I agree in
               advance that the row is thirty-two long, so nobody has to spend anything saying so:

                                     0101010101010101 0101010101010101

                 (The space is only there to rest your eyes.) Every description in the code starts
              with one digit that says which kind of description follows: 0 means “here’s the row,
              written out,” 1 means “here’s a pattern, then the exceptions to it.” Each digit is
              one yes-or-no answer, one bit, which is what Shannon’s unit is. Written out, the
              row costs that first digit plus the thirty-two digits themselves, thirty-three bits, and
              there’s no model in it at all. A pattern description goes like this. First come five
              digits giving the block length minus one: 00000 means one digit, and 11111 means
              thirty-two. That covers every allowed length. Then comes the block itself. The
              rule is “repeat the block, then stop at exactly thirty-two digits, cutting off the last
              repetition if necessary.” Then come five digits for how many places the row disagrees
              with the pattern (up to thirty-one, which is plenty here), and five more digits for
              each of those places, numbered zero through thirty-one and listed in increasing
              order without repeats. At every listed place, flip the predicted digit.
                 Do it for the row above. The block is 01, two digits long. So the model costs 1
              (which kind) plus 5 (the block’s length) plus 2 (the block), which makes eight bits.
              Then comes what’s left to say about the data once the model has said its piece: the
              row disagrees with “repeat 01” nowhere, so that’s five digits saying “zero exceptions”
              and nothing else, five bits. The total is thirteen, against thirty-three for writing it
              out. The pattern pays for itself, with twenty bits to spare, and Rissanen’s sentence
              has just happened on paper: a model is worth stating when it saves more than it
              costs.
                 Now spoil the row. Flip two of its digits anywhere you like. The model doesn’t
              change, eight bits, but now the leftover says “two exceptions, here and here”: five
              plus five plus five, fifteen, for a total of twenty-three. It’s still cheaper than thirty-
              three; the pattern still pays, it just pays less, because the data has more to say that
              the model can’t. Flip four, and the total is 8 + 5 + 4 × 5 = 33, a dead tie, and a
                                                            14.11 Structure is the part that compresses   131


pattern that saves nothing isn’t worth stating. Flip five and it’s thirty-eight, and
now writing the row out is cheaper than explaining it, and this particular pattern
has lost to writing the row out. Another pattern might still win. Flip places 6, 12,
18, 24 and 30 of the original row: now the block 010100, repeated and cut off at
thirty-two digits, fits exactly. Its description costs 1 + 5 + 6 + 5 = 17 bits. Five
changes defeated “repeat 01” and produced a better description of their own. The
comparison is between descriptions in the stated code, not a count of spoiled digits
alone.
   Message                            Original row Two flips Five specified flips
  Write all 32 digits                           33           33                        33
  Repeat 01, list exceptions                    13           23                        38
  Repeat 010100, no exceptions          does not fit    depends                        17
For the last column, flip positions 6, 12, 18, 24 and 30. That defeats one short description
      and supplies another. Pattern descriptions include the type bit, length field and
  exception-count field. The raw row uses one type bit followed by its thirty-two digits.
So “is there structure here?” has turned into a question with a number on each side,
and a break-even point you can find yourself: four spoiled digits, in this code.
    And now change the code, because I promised you’d see the numbers move.
Say I’d used seven digits for each exception’s place (wasteful, but nobody stopped
me). Then three spoiled digits cost 8 + 5 + 3 × 7 = 34, one more than writing the
row out, where the five-digit code charged twenty-eight and the pattern paid. The
pattern stopped paying one spoiled digit sooner. The row is the same, the pattern is
the same, the verdict is different, and the only thing that changed was a convention
I chose. That’s Grünwald’s footnote, and it’s why I won’t let you take any of those
numbers anywhere else. They’re the price of a description in one made-up code, a
length of a written-down message, and nothing more. They are not the charge I
opened this book with. That charge, as Section 2 states it, is on one step a machine
takes. How long anybody’s description is doesn’t enter it, and if the two ever turn
out to be related, that has to be shown. Both of them being counted in whole
numbers doesn’t get to stand in for the showing.
    Now push, because this is where “structure means information” gets born, and
it’s wrong, and it’s an easy thing to believe. Flip a fair coin two hundred times and
write down the string. Usually there is no substantially shorter way to say that string
than to say the whole thing. You can watch it happen in the little code above, cut to
thirty-two digits: a coin-flip row disagrees with “repeat 01” in about sixteen places,
which would cost eighty-odd bits to list, and even the one block guaranteed to fit
with no exceptions, the whole row used as its own block, costs 1 + 5 + 32 + 5 = 43.
The written-out row at thirty-three beats those two descriptions. Another block
could do better; a lucky run of coins can make a pattern, so check it. The general
reason long random rows usually resist substantial compression is a count: put
132   14 From “What structure is”


               each short description beside the one row it prints, and there are too few short
               descriptions to stand beside more than a small fraction of all the long rows. Nikolai
               Vereshchagin, a mathematician at Moscow State University, and Paul Vitányi, a
               computer scientist at CWI in Amsterdam, in a 2004 paper on exactly this question,
               put it without mercy: every bit of a string like that is “meaningless information,
               random noise.” Such a string carries maximum information and zero structure. So
               in this sense structure is the part that compresses, the part where the “th” tells you
               about the “e,” and most information and most structure are different things. Hold
               that one tightly, because if you built something that charged for information, you’d
               be charging for the wrong thing, and for a while I wasn’t sure which one I’d built.
                   And now notice that the word just changed meaning on you, and on me. I’m
               pointing at it so it doesn’t slide past. Shannon and Rissanen and Vereshchagin mean
               regularity: a pattern that lets you say something shorter. Tarski, a few pages back,
               and Paul Halmos, a Hungarian-American mathematician whose short textbook on
               sets is coming up, mean a set with named relations on it, and those two are not the
               same thing. You can run the test yourself: number a thousand objects, flip a coin
               for each, heads goes in group one, tails in group two. The result is a perfectly good
               sorting in the set theorist’s sense, every object in exactly one group. And the list of
               who went where is a thousand coin flips, which is to say it compresses about as well
               as a thousand coin flips, which is to say not at all. Feed it to a code built like the little
               one, with the fields widened to fit a thousand, and you get the coin-flip row again,
               only longer: typically most of the assignment still has to be written down. A lucky
               pattern can save some of it; the coin tosses alone do not guarantee there is none. By
               the set theorist’s rule, the sorting is the structure, and it’s every bit as much a sorting
               as “evens in one group, odds in the other,” which compresses to almost nothing. The
               two are the same kind of object with wildly different description lengths. So the two
               meanings don’t even rank things the same way: one asks “is there a sorting?” and
               the answer is yes for both; the other asks “does a short rule say who went where?”
               and the answer is yes for evens and odds and no for the coins. The coin sorting has
               structure in the set theorist’s sense and none in Rissanen’s. I’ll keep both meanings,
               and I’ll try to say which one I’m using, and when I slip you’ll know what to look for.


               14.12 The four parts of every computer

               In 1936 Alan Turing described the machine that every computer you’ve ever touched
               is a version of, and he did it before any of them existed. It’s a clerk with a pencil,
               following a rulebook, written down as a machine (that clerk comes back in Sec-
               tion 14). Write down its parts; there are four. There’s a tape, divided into squares;
               a head that reads one square at a time and can write on it and move left or right;
               a finite list of states the head can be in; and a table of rules: in this state, reading
                                                           14.12 The four parts of every computer   133


this symbol, do this. Nothing else is in it, and two different things are true about it
that get run together. The standard effective models compared with it, including
register machines and lambda calculus, compute the same partial functions; those
are theorems, proved one pairing at a time, and Turing himself sketched one of
them, the pairing with the logician Alonzo Church’s lambda calculus, in an appendix
to the 1936 paper. That it also captures everything a person could ever carry out
by rote, everything “computable” in the plain-English sense, is a claim, called the
Church-Turing thesis, and nobody has proved it, because the plain-English side isn’t
a mathematical object. Turing himself kept those two apart in the 1936 paper, and
so will I. There’s a third claim that gets stacked on top, and it’s a different kind
again: the physical Church-Turing thesis, that every device you could build out of
real stuff computes no more than this machine does. That one is a claim about
physics. No mathematical proof can settle it. Nobody has built a device that breaks
it. Your laptop, your phone, every server in every data center compute nothing a
Turing machine can’t; what differs between them is speed, space, energy, and cost.
Hold on to that last word. Cost is where this book lives.
    Now do the thing I did, and do it honestly. Look through those four parts for
a place that says “these squares belong together.” There isn’t one, and that’s not
me failing to look. Everything the machine is at any moment is which state it’s in,
where the head is, and what’s on the tape, and the table says what happens next,
and that’s the full list. So any grouping has to live in the states and the table, as a
rule, or on the tape, as symbols, which is to say as bytes like any other bytes. The
machine can hold a grouping. It just has no place where holding one is different
from holding anything else.
    In 1972 David Parnas, a software engineer, wrote a short paper that every
programmer should be made to read and most haven’t, on how to cut a program
into modules. He took one small program and cut it two ways. Both worked. But
one was easy to change later and the other wasn’t, and the easy one could be “much
less efficient” if talking between modules cost anything. His point was that a module
is “a responsibility assignment,” a decision about who answers for what, and how
you cut decides what every later change costs. If you’ve ever maintained someone
else’s code, you’ve paid for their cut the way Parnas said you would, probably at
two in the morning. What survives the push is that a division is a commitment: it
changes what the work costs, and it is no caption, no label stuck on afterwards. I’d
like to say the thing I built understood that from the beginning. It didn’t. I did,
eventually.
CHAPTER 15




From “Windows and shadows”

15.1 People who found this on their own

The fact is so plain that it got discovered separately in every field that has to deal
with seeing part of something, and I want to lay them beside each other, because
that’s the kind of evidence I trust: people who’d never met kept walking into the
same wall.
    In 1956 Edward Moore, working on the small switching machines that became
computer hardware, asked when an experimenter who can only feed a machine
inputs and watch its outputs can tell two of its internal states apart. His answer gave
a precise meaning to “indistinguishable from outside,” and a theorem with a sting
in it: even when every pair of states can be told apart by some experiment, there
are machines where no single experiment tells you which state you started in. What
you can always do is run an experiment after which you know which state you’re
in now. In 1960 Rudolf Kalman, whose filter went on to aerospace and industry,
asked the same question for continuous systems, called it observability, and gave
a test (a matrix rank, which you can compute) for whether watching the output
over time pins down the internal state. His unobservable states are exactly a fibre:
states whose difference never reaches the output, and never will, however long
you watch. That second half took me longest to take in. More samples help only
when the states eventually show different outputs, and states in the same fibre of
the whole observation history remain indistinguishable. Try it with two toy cars on
identical roads, a mile apart. Give them the same speed at every tick and let neither
one’s motion depend on its mile marker. Their speedometers then tell the same
story throughout. No amount of rereading that story supplies the missing starting
position. Those assumptions do work: a landmark sensor, or motion affected by a
known hill, could expose the position.
    Read the mile markers at two known times instead. Their difference divided
by the elapsed time gives the average speed between the readings. To recover an
instantaneous speed, you need a model of the intervening motion or a limiting
sequence of exact readings. Real noisy instruments give estimates with uncertainty.



                                 134
                                                            15.1 People who found this on their own   135


   Where did Kalman’s filter go first, before aerospace and industry at large? It went
toward the Moon. In the autumn of 1960 Kalman called an acquaintance, Stanley
Schmidt, at NASA’s Ames Research Center, and came to visit, not knowing what
Schmidt’s group was working on. The group, a branch with eight people doing the
analysis, was stuck on how a spacecraft going around the Moon and back could
work out where it was from what its crew could measure through an optical sight: a
few angles to the Earth and the Moon. That question is a window problem with a
capsule in it. The state is position and velocity, the view is a handful of angles, and
the job is to keep a running best guess at the state from a sequence of views. Kalman
presented his new filtering paper to them, and with real work (their problem wasn’t
linear and his theory was) it fit, and it went into the feasibility studies for navigating
Apollo. Leonard McGee, of Schmidt’s group at Ames, and Schmidt, telling the story
in 1985, call it a fortunate sequence of events and say the solution might have gone
undiscovered but for that meeting. The story shows the limit from the useful side.
The filter didn’t make the angles show more. It used the dynamics and a sequence
of noisy views to estimate the state. Observability asks whether ideal observations
can distinguish the states at all; filtering also has to handle noise and uncertainty.
More computation cannot recover a distinction that the entire specified observation
history loses.
   In 1977 Patrick and Radhia Cousot built a whole discipline of program analysis,
called abstract interpretation, on throwing information away on purpose. You run
the program over coarse descriptions of its values, and the exact values never enter.
And you accept that the answer you get has to be true of every real value the coarse
description could stand for. For example, an input known only to lie between −1
and 1 has a square between 0 and 1; the interval does not tell you its exact square.
That is a sound bound over the hidden possibilities. Exact recovery would require
the stronger condition of fibre constancy. And in 1970 Edgar Codd, inventing the
relational database, made programs independent of how the data is stored, and the
relational systems that followed gave users views, deliberately partial windows onto
the stored data. A decade after Codd, in 1981, two database researchers, François
Bancilhon and Nicolas Spyratos, gave the view-update problem an exact semantics.
The problem asks, if a user changes what they see through a view, which of the
many underlying states that cast that view should the database move to? Several
are legitimate, and that’s the fibre again, seen from the writing side.
   Two more fields have the fact as a founding definition. In the theory of knowledge,
what an agent knows is modelled by a partition of the possible states into cells, the
states it can’t tell apart, and it knows a fact at a state exactly when the fact holds
at every state in that state’s cell. The economist and game theorist Robert Aumann
used that model in 1976 to prove that two people who start from the same prior,
and whose estimates of an event’s chance are common knowledge, can’t agree to
136   15 From “Windows and shadows”


              disagree; the computer scientists Ronald Fagin, Joseph Halpern, Yoram Moses and
              Moshe Vardi built a whole logic of knowledge on the same partitions in Reasoning
              About Knowledge (1995). Theorem 8.1 is that model with the cells written as fibres:
              a window knows the answer to a question exactly when the question is constant on
              its cell. And in computer security, the computer scientists Joseph Goguen and José
              Meseguer defined noninterference in 1982: one group of users doesn’t interfere with
              another when nothing the first group does changes what the second can see. Hiding
              the counter in today’s view is only the first step toward that property. If tomorrow’s
              move reads the counter and writes it onto the display, the secret comes out. To keep
              it hidden through runs, the permitted moves must preserve the agreement of the
              views too. The adaptive-observer argument earlier in the book supplies that extra
              condition for its horizontal moves.
                  And Parnas, from Section 6, hid things on purpose: a module is characterised
              by the design decision it hides from its callers. Hiding is a window. The caller can’t
              later read back what was hidden, including the things it later wishes it could check,
              and that’s the price side of the coin whose benefit side Parnas was selling. None of
              these people were doing philosophy. They were trying to build filters, databases,
              compilers, and modules, and the wall is the same wall in each.
                  There’s one more from information theory. Oliver Kosut, at Arizona State, and
              Jörg Kliewer, at the New Jersey Institute of Technology, two information theorists,
              asked in 2018 when a receiver on a channel with a possible attacker, and no shared
              key, can always either get the message right or notice the attacker. The answer is
              all or nothing: the rate is zero, or it’s everything the channel would carry with no
              attacker at all, and which one depends on a condition they call overwritability, that
              the attacker can make what the receiver sees look exactly like an honest transmission
              of some other message. Overwritability is a printer, a way to put the same thing
              in the window without the event behind it. The fibre fact is their condition in its
              plainest form, with the probabilities taken out.
                  And there’s one from machine learning, where the two computers of The point,
              same bits and different histories, turn up with a lawyer attached. Four machine-
              learning security researchers at the University of Toronto and the Vector Institute,
              Anvith Thudi, Hengrui Jia, Ilia Shumailov and Nicolas Papernot, asked in 2022 how
              a company could prove a trained model had forgotten someone’s data, and found
              that the same model can come out of different training sets, so the finished model
              can’t show whether a point was used or removed. The only claim they found you
              can audit is about the procedure that was run. It’s the same wall: the finished state
              is a window, and what happened is in the run.
                  Philosophers who study testimony, which is the trade of being told things, have
              the same wall from the other side. Read one after another they blurred into a list
                                                          15.1 People who found this on their own   137


of opinions for me, and the list didn’t tell me anything until I built a picture that
showed where each one sits, so I’ll give the picture first.
     Somebody passes you a note across a room. It says the answer to question seven
is 42. What crossed the room? Ink. Whatever made the writer believe 42 stayed on
their side: the working-out, how careful they were, whether they knew or guessed.
Pull that apart and there are five things in play: the claim on the paper, the writer’s
head, whatever process got the writer there, the trip across the room, and your head
at the other end. Every position I found puts its weight on one of those five. Once I
saw that, they stopped being a list and became a map, and the map has connections
the list hid.
     The two ends of it are Locke and Burge, and they’re the same dial turned
opposite ways. The philosopher John Locke, in the first book of his Essay in 1690,
says believing something because someone with authority said it isn’t knowing it. If
the reason didn’t cross the room, you don’t have it. Tyler Burge, a philosopher of
mind and knowledge at UCLA, argued in 1993 that you’re entitled to accept what
you’re told, if it’s presented as true and it makes sense to you, unless you have a
reason not to. Locke says only ink crossed, so you’ve got nothing. Burge says the ink
comes with a default, and the default holds until something knocks it over. I get the
pull of Burge. Nobody could get through a day checking every note. But a default
anybody can trigger by writing something sensible is a record nobody paid for, the
thing this book keeps refusing, so on that dial the book sits at Locke’s end. And
sitting there has a price: staying there means carrying the check across the room
every time.
     Jennifer Lackey, a philosopher at Northwestern who has spent much of her work
on testimony, cuts the wire between the writer’s head and what arrives. Her case,
from 2008, is a teacher who doesn’t believe the theory she’s required to teach, and
her students come away knowing it anyway. I went back and forth on whether those
students really know it, and I haven’t settled it. But look at where the case cuts. If
it’s right, what arrives at your end never had to be believed at the other end, and the
writer’s head drops out of the map. Sanford Goldberg, also at Northwestern, moves
the weight one more place over, onto the process. In 2010 he argued that whether
your word justifies me can depend on how reliable the processes behind your word
were, which sit outside my head. That one took me a few reads. What I think he’s
saying is that part of my reason for believing you lives in you, in machinery I can’t
see, and he’s fine with that. I’m not, quite, and why I’m not is why this book exists:
machinery I can’t see is machinery I can’t check. Christoph Jäger and Nicholas
Shackel, philosophers at Innsbruck and Cardiff, built a model of degrees of belief in
2025 in which the writer knowing is neither necessary nor sufficient for the reader
to know. That lands where Lackey’s teacher landed, from a completely different
direction, a story on one side and a calculation on the other. Agreement like that I
138   15 From “Windows and shadows”


              trust more than either piece alone, as long as I remember a model only shows what
              can happen inside its own rules.
                   Then come two sets of people who drew arrows. Wesley Holliday, a logician then
              at Stanford, put testimony into a logic of belief change in 2009, with an authority
              graph recording whose word each agent takes and rules for what hearing a speaker
              does to a belief. I’ve only read a summary of Holliday’s paper, so I’m taking that
              on somebody’s word, which is a funny thing to admit in this paragraph. Security
              people had drawn the same kind of arrow two decades earlier. In the logic of
              authentication of Michael Burrows, Martin Abadi and Roger Needham (1990), two
              computer scientists at Digital’s research lab in Palo Alto and one at Cambridge, if
              I believe an authority has jurisdiction over a statement and I believe the authority
              holds it, I believe it. Set that beside Burge and it’s his default with one change: the
              authority is named before the note arrives. That one change is the whole difference.
              Burge’s default trusts whoever is writing. Theirs trusts only a writer you picked in
              advance.
                   Kenny Easwaran is the one who goes after what crosses the room itself. He’s a
              philosopher who works on probability and on how mathematicians know things,
              and in 2009 he took up a question I’d never have thought to ask. The philosopher
              Don Fallis had pressed it in a series of papers: why will mathematicians accept a
              proof that’s long and complicated, or partly carried out by a computer, and turn
              down a probabilistic one that can be far more certain? That looked backwards to
              me at first, because more certain should win. Easwaran’s answer is a property he
              calls transferability. A deductive proof puts the reason on the paper. Every step
              crosses the room, so you can learn from the writer without trusting the writer. A
              probabilistic proof leaves the reason behind and sends only the writer’s word about
              what the dice did. Once I saw it that way it stopped looking backwards, and it’s
              the cleanest statement I found of what the machine of Part III does with a check:
              make the reason cross the room, carry the thing, and run the check on your own
              side. That reading is mine. His subject is proofs passed between mathematicians, so
              if it’s wrong, it’s wrong on me.
                   The machine carries the check across the room and runs it on a specified checker.
              Its correctness proof says that YES means the claim holds. That proof is supplied
              once, outside the individual runs. In Burrows, Abadi and Needham’s terms, the
              checker is the named authority. Here you can examine its rules and the proof of
              what they mean. That is the connection between the event the machine records and
              the claim the event supports.
                   Only Burrows, Abadi and Needham write about machines passing messages, and
              even they write about the computers as if the computers held beliefs. The rest are
              about what survives the trip from one head to another. Either way it’s the copier’s
                                                           15.1 People who found this on their own   139


problem (the computer in The point that was handed the sorted list and copied it
in), with philosophers’ names on it and, in one case, computer scientists’.
    Distributed-systems people hit the wall too, and one pair of them measured how
far it goes. Their papers were a pile of acronyms to me until I put them all in one
room. People at desks work from one rulebook and pass slips to each other. Nobody
sees into anybody’s head; all anyone gets is the slips handed to them. Somebody
may be cheating. What can the honest ones do about it with nothing but slips?
    Andreas Haeberlen, then at the Max Planck Institute for Software Systems in
Saarbrücken, and Petr Kuznetsov, then in Berlin, asked in 2009 which faults an
outside observer can detect at all. As I read their answer, it sorts cheating by where
it sits. A cheat can break the rulebook without changing any slip. A cheat can
write a slip an honest player could have written too, for all anyone holding it can
tell. A cheat can fail to send a slip that’s due, an omission fault, or send one the
rulebook would never produce, a commission fault. The first two, they argue, no
algorithm can detect. The last two can be caught, and they put a floor under how
many messages the catching takes, which I liked for obvious reasons. My first guess
was that the classes ranked how bad a fault is. They don’t. They rank how far the
fault gets toward a slip, and the first two are a fibre: an honest room and a crooked
one that hand out the same slips. That pair of classes is the fibre fact with an auditor
attached.
    Their group’s PeerReview system (Haeberlen, Kouznetsov, the same Kuznetsov
spelled another way, and Peter Druschel, the institute’s founding director, 2007) is
the ladder up to that wall: every node keeps a secure record of the messages it sent
and received, and a checker replays the record against a reference implementation,
a copy of the rulebook it can run, which exposes the faults a correct node got to see.
It comes close to the whole-state escape from the three escapes below, built: ship
the log, and the checker re-runs it. I say close because a log of slips stands in for a
whole state only if the rulebook can rebuild what a desk did from them, and only if
the checker’s copy of the rulebook is the right one.
    The next three ask what you can say once you’re at the wall. Ralf Küsters, Tomasz
Truderung and Andreas Vogt, three security researchers at Trier, asked what a verdict
should hold. They gave in 2010 a general definition of accountability, a verdict
naming who misbehaved, and argued, with a proof inside their own definitions, that
verifiability, the property that an outsider can check a result, is a restricted form
of it. The way I read the restriction: verifiability says something went wrong, and
accountability points at a desk. Radha Jagadeesan, Alan Jeffrey, Corin Pitcher and
James Riely, programming-language people at DePaul in Chicago and, for Jeffrey,
Bell Labs, asked when the verdict comes. They built in 2009 an operational model of
systems that enforce a policy after the fact by audit, and studied the trade between
the power of the auditor, the cost of the audit and what’s asked of the agents.
140   15 From “Windows and shadows”


              Robert Künnemann, Ilkan Esiyok and Michael Backes, at the CISPA security center
              in Saarbrücken, in 2019 implemented a check for it, with Certificate Transparency
              (CT), the public logging of the certificates web browsers trust, among their examples.
              As I understand it, they did it by turning accountability into a property of the trace,
              the whole run. It’s Thudi and his coauthors’ wall again: what happened is in the
              run.
                  In September 2026 Timo Treitz, a student at Saarland University, and Künnemann
              gave CT the full treatment (Section 17 has my fragment of it) and found, in their
              model, that plain CT gives accountability only if the log is assumed honest, an
              assumption the SCT auditing extension (SCT is the signed certificate timestamp a
              log hands out) can drop and the gossiping extension can’t. In my room, CT hands
              PeerReview’s log of slips to a third party to keep. An honest log is the commitment
              bit from the three escapes below, with a name on the contract: somebody’s promise
              that the bit is set when, and only when, the claim holds, trusted at the step.
                  Turn the room on me and it gives twice. A raise of the flag that costs nothing and
              never reaches the window is Haeberlen and Kuznetsov’s first class, and no auditor
              outside catches it; that’s the clerk’s trouble, next. My answer to that is PeerReview’s,
              and it trusts a copy of the rulebook too: the checker, proved honest once, outside
              every run. And every verdict in this book is accept or reject on one claim. It never
              points at a desk. In Küsters, Truderung and Vogt’s terms that’s the restricted form,
              and the rest of accountability isn’t here.
CHAPTER 16




From “Structural entitlement”

16.1 The count, exactly

I’ll set out the words and then the count, for any system with Section 7’s counter. A
list is a finite list of candidates, repeats allowed, and |Ω| is the length of the list Ω,
repeats counted. ⌈log2 x ⌉ is the base-two logarithm of x rounded up: the smallest
k ∈ N with 2k ≥ x. At x = 0 it is 0, since 20 ≥ 0. And a −̇ b is subtraction on whole
numbers that stops at zero, so 3 −̇ 5 = 0.

Definition 16.1 (Decision tree). A decision tree is a leaf, or a branch with two
subtrees. Its depth is 0 for a leaf and one more than the deeper subtree for a branch;
its leaf count L( T ) is 1 for a leaf and the sum of the two counts for a branch. Nothing
is written on a tree: no question, no answer, no candidate.



Lemma 16.1 (A tree is no wider than two to its depth). For every decision tree T,
L( T ) ≤ 2depth(T ) , and so ⌈log2 L( T )⌉ ≤ depth( T ).

Proof. Start at the root. At the first level there can be at most two places, at the
second at most four, and each further level at most doubles the places available. To
count leaves that stop early as well, extend each such leaf with branches until all
tips reach the tree’s greatest depth. This only adds leaves. At depth d the filled-out
tree has 2d tips, so the original tree has at most that many leaves. For depth zero
there is just the root, which is one leaf.
   Thus d bits are enough to number all the leaves. The least number of bits that
will do, ⌈log2 L( T )⌉, cannot be greater than d.
    Nothing about any machine is in the definition or the lemma. They’re about trees,
a leaf or a branch, and nothing else.




                                                   141
142   16 From “Structural entitlement”




               Theorem 16.2 (Paid narrowing, counted). Take a system with a counter µ and
               a price list c that obey conservation (Theorem 7.2): each step raises µ by exactly
               its charge. Let Ω and Ω′ be lists with |Ω′ | > 0, let T be a decision tree with
