               |Ω| ≤ L( T ) · |Ω′ | (the tree covers the narrowing), and let t be a run from s ending
               at s′ in which at least depth( T ) steps are charged at least one each. Then
                               ′
                  |Ω| ≤ 2µ(s )−µ(s) · |Ω′ |,        so      ⌈log2 |Ω|⌉ −̇ ⌈log2 |Ω′ |⌉ ≤ µ(s′ ) − µ(s).

               Proof. Count the bill first. There are at least as many paid steps as levels in the tree.
               Each paid step adds at least one and every other charge is nonnegative. The total
               bill is therefore at least the tree’s depth. Conservation says that bill is exactly the
               counter’s rise, µ(s′ ) − µ(s).
                   Now count the room the tree supplies. It has at most two to its depth leaves. The
               covering premise allows at most |Ω′ | prior entries per leaf, so
                                                                               ′
                                   |Ω| ≤ L( T )|Ω′ | ≤ 2depth(T ) |Ω′ | ≤ 2µ(s )−µ(s) |Ω′ |.
               That is the first bound.
                  For the bit version, let k = ⌈log2 |Ω′ |⌉. The posterior has at most 2k entries.
                                                                                ′
               Insert that count in the first bound: the prior has at most 2µ(s )−µ(s)+k entries. So
               numbering its entries needs no more than the counter’s rise plus k bits. Subtracting
               the posterior’s k bits gives the claimed bound; if the subtraction would go negative,
               the stipulated subtraction stops at zero. An empty prior already has bit count zero
               and satisfies the same conclusion.
                   Two arguments are stuck together in that proof, and they come apart. The tree
               half (the covering and the lemma) bounds the index bits by the sum of the charges
               of the run, on any price list at all. Conservation is only what turns that sum into the
               counter’s rise. So the form in terms of cost, with the sum of the charges in place of
               the counter, holds in any Thiele machine, and it’s part (3) of Theorem 9.1 below.
               The shape of the bound shows in what’s missing from the hypotheses: anything
               tying the lists to the run.
                   There are three honest pieces of small print, all of them visible in the statement.
               Both logarithms round up separately, and the subtraction stops at zero, so when the
               prior and the posterior round up to the same number of index bits (four candidates
               narrowed to three, say) the rounded bound says nothing, though the first form of the
               theorem charges the run one unit: one leaf can’t cover four candidates with three.
               The lists are plain lists, repeats allowed, so “index bits” is a difference of rounded list
               lengths with repeats counted; a list can get shorter by dropping a duplicate without
               losing a single candidate, and “a candidate was ruled out” and “the list got shorter”
                                                                         16.1 The count, exactly   143


are two different claims. And the difference isn’t the number of questions anyone
asked.
    The covering condition, |Ω| ≤ L( T ) · |Ω′ |, is the nearest thing the count has to
an anti-stopped-clock clause, and I have to be exact about how near. Picture each
survivor standing for a little crowd of the prior’s candidates, a crowd no bigger than
the tree has leaves. The condition says the crowds can hold the whole prior between
them: share the prior’s candidates out among them and none overflows. That limits
how much can be deleted per leaf you paid for, but it doesn’t say why anything was
deleted. So the condition rules out deleting too much for the tree you’ve paid for,
and it doesn’t rule out deleting for no reason. A run can satisfy every hypothesis
of the count and have ruled nothing out on purpose; the count prices the size of
the drop and says nothing about the honesty of it. And the covering can’t just be
dropped. On the small machine of Part III (Section 15), whose record moves CHECK,
COMMIT and CERTIFY cost one each, take the sixteen starts with A at 0 through 15,
and the run CHECK “A ≥ 15,” COMMIT, CERTIFY from the start with A at 15. One
yes-or-no question with a big constant in it narrows sixteen candidates to one, four
index bits, and the run has three paid steps, a ledger of three. A tree no deeper than
three has at most eight leaves, and eight times one survivor is less than sixteen, so
the covering fails, and without it the bound would say 4 ≤ 3. More candidates only
widen the gap: 2n starts and the constant 2n − 1 give n index bits for the same cost
of three (Section 22.5, Proposition 22.17).
    A side trip is worth taking here, because it looks like it breaks the count and
doesn’t. Two information theorists, Rudolf Ahlswede and Gunter Dueck (1989),
noticed that asking “is it this one?” is a different job from asking “which one is it?”
If each receiver only wants to know whether the message is theirs, a noisy channel
used n times can serve a number of receivers that grows like two raised to two to
the nC, with C the channel’s capacity, where naming the message only gets you
two to the nC. The task is called identification, and on its face it says checking
can be exponentially cheaper than naming. But their tests are allowed to say yes
wrongly once in a while. Make the yes sound, a test that never says yes to the wrong
message, which is the only kind of check this book lets raise a record, and the double
exponential collapses. The argument is two lines of pigeonhole. Each receiver’s test,
to be any use, says yes on some output its own message can produce, and soundness
means no other message can produce that output; so those outputs are all different,
one per receiver, and there can’t be more receivers than outputs, |Y |n for an output
alphabet Y. That bound is single exponential, the same order as naming. So for a
check that never lies, the card count is the right scale after all, and the cheap trick
needs a check that can be fooled.
144   16 From “Structural entitlement”


               16.2 When the run does the narrowing
               Theorem 9.1 takes the lists in. In the version below, the run makes the posterior,
               and the count stops being an item on the list and becomes a consequence. That
               version needs one thing a bare Thiele machine doesn’t have: which steps are tests,
               and what each asks. So a reading names them. For each instruction it gives either
               nothing or a yes-or-no question about the state the run is in when it gets there, and
               a test step passes when its question says yes there. The candidates are states the run
               could have started from. A passed test is asked of a candidate by running the same
               moves from the candidate up to that step and asking the same question there. The
               posterior is the candidates that pass every test the run passed, asked that way, in the
               order the run asked them.


               Theorem 16.3 (Structural entitlement, with the narrowing made by the run). Take
               a Thiele machine and a reading that names its test steps, every test step charged
               at least one. Take a state s that reads no, a run t from s that ends in a state that
               reads yes, and a list Ω of candidate starts that contains s. Let Ω′ be the posterior t
               makes from Ω, and let bi and ai be the numbers of candidates standing just before
               and just after the ith test t passed. Then (1) Ω′ sits inside Ω and contains s; (2)
               every candidate of Ω that isn’t in Ω′ fails, asked that way, a test t passed; (3)
               |Ω| · ∏i ai = |Ω′ | · ∏i bi ; (4) if every test t passed keeps at least a 2−k share of the
               candidates standing before it, bi ≤ 2k ai , then ⌈log2 |Ω|⌉ −̇ ⌈log2 |Ω′ |⌉ is at most
               k times the number of tests t passed, that number is at most the number of steps
               charged at least one, and the index bits are at most k times the sum of the charges;
               and (5) some step of t took the reading from no to yes and was charged at least one.

               Proof. (1) Start with the prior list. Each passed test only removes candidates; it
               never inserts one. The actual start s passes every test used to filter the list, because
               these are precisely the tests the actual run passed. So s remains throughout, and the
               final list is a nonempty part of the original.
                   (2) Follow any candidate that is absent at the end. It was present at first, so there
               is a first test at which it disappears. The filter removes it only when its version of
               that test says no while the actual run’s says yes. That test is the required witness.
                   (3) Write the list lengths on the bench: q0 before any passed test, q1 after the
               first, through qn after the last. Here q0 = |Ω| and qn = |Ω′ |. Every before-count bi
               is qi−1 and every after-count ai is qi . The left product in the statement is therefore
               q0 q1 · · · qn ; the right product has the same factors with qn placed first. They are
               equal, including when there are no tests and both products are empty products,
               equal to one.
                                                               16.2 When the run does the narrowing   145


   (4) At the first test the share premise gives q0 ≤ 2k q1 . At the second it gives
q1 ≤ 2k q2 . Substitute one into the other, and continue. After n tests, q0 ≤ 2kn qn . If
j bits number the final candidates, then qn ≤ 2 j , so kn + j bits suffice to number the
original ones. The rounded bit drop is at most kn. Each of the n passed tests is a
separate paid step, and each adds at least one to the nonnegative bill. Thus n is
at most the number of paid steps and at most the total charge, giving the two cost
comparisons.
   (5) Read the actual run from its initial no until its first yes. The preceding reading
was no, so that step raised it. The toll charges that step at least one.
    Part (3) is the exact count: take base-two logarithms and log2 |Ω| − log2 |Ω′ | is
the sum, over the tests the run passed, of log2 (bi /ai ), the bits each one took. Part (2)
answers the complaint I made about the covering, that it doesn’t say why anything
was deleted: here everything deleted was deleted by a test the run passed and paid
for. Two premises stay, the prior list and the share, a bound k fixed before the run on
what one test can take. Every run meets some bound, its own biggest test’s, so what
the share adds is fixing it in advance, and that is needed. On the small machine
one CHECK of “A ≥ 15,” asked of sixteen candidates, keeps one, a 2−4 share, and
narrows by 4 index bits on a run of 3 record moves (Proposition 22.17); with k = 1
part (4) would say 4 ≤ 3. The same statement on every certified Thiele-complete
machine, where the tests are the CHECK moves and the charges are counted by the
ledger, is Theorem 22.16.
CHAPTER 17




From “Records over any base: the
latch”

17.1 Bases from outside, and a relation I have to be honest about

The theorem is stated for any base, so let me put it on three models of computation
that other people built: a small Turing machine (any program); a register machine
of the kind the computer scientists Stephen Cook (of the certificates) and Robert
Reckhow described in 1973, with unit-cost indirect addressing (any program); and
a lambda calculus (call-by-value, meaning an argument is worked out before it’s
plugged in, and made deterministic by letting a term with no step left to take step
to itself). On each, the latch form holds, by the one generic theorem applied to
that base (the bases are defined below). So a register machine with a base-driven
record is a register machine plus a cup, and so is a lambda calculus. (There’s one
more instance, the machine I build later, where a particular flag is shown to be a
base-driven record and the same theorem makes it a latch, in Section 15.)
   I’ll give the three bases explicitly. A base needs a state, a next-state function, a
set of starting states and a halting predicate; the latch theorem asks for nothing else
about its instruction vocabulary.

Definition 17.1 (Three concrete bases). The tape base has a finite Boolean list t, a
head index h, a program counter q and a natural-number cost field. Reading beyond
the list returns false. Writing at h extends the list with false cells if needed, then
changes that cell. Moving right increments h and extends the list through the new
head position. Moving left decrements a positive h; at h = 0, it prepends a false cell
and keeps the head at zero. A fixed finite program contains these instructions:

Instruction                                 Next state
Write a bit                                 Perform the write and advance q by one.
Move left or right                          Perform the move and advance q by one.




                                 146
                                    17.1 Bases from outside, and a relation I have to be honest about   147


Instruction                                Next state
Branch to j                                Set q := j if the current cell is true;
                                           otherwise advance q by one.
Halt                                       Leave the entire state unchanged.
ClaimTapeIsZero with a label ℓ             Advance q by one and do nothing else.


   An out-of-range program fetch is Halt. Every state is allowed as a starting state,
and halting means the fetched instruction is Halt. The cost field is preserved by
every instruction in this base. In particular, its ClaimTapeIsZero instruction neither
erases the tape nor charges the cost field; neither the name nor the label supplies
any semantics.
   The random-access base has a register function X : N → N and a program
counter q. For a fixed finite program its instructions are:

Instruction                                Next state
Constant (i, c)                            Set X (i ) := c.
Add (i, j, k)                              Set X (i ) := X ( j) + X (k).
Subtract (i, j, k)                         Set X (i ) := max(0, X ( j) − X (k)).
Indirect load (i, j)                       Set X (i ) := X ( X ( j)).
Indirect store (i, j)                      Set X ( X (i )) := X ( j).
Positive branch ( j, a)                    Set q := a if X ( j) > 0; otherwise advance q
                                           by one.
Jump to a                                  Set q := a.
Halt                                       Leave the entire state unchanged.


    Each register-writing instruction advances q by one; its operands are evaluated
in the old state, and all other registers are preserved. A fetch outside the program
also leaves the state unchanged. Starting states have q = 0 and arbitrary register
contents. A state is halted when it fetches Halt or is outside the program. Unit cost
here counts instructions. It has nothing to do with the record’s ledger.
    For the indirect instructions, draw boxes 1, 2 and 3 and put 2, 7 and 0 in them.
Indirect load (3, 1) first reads the 2 in box 1, uses it as an address, and copies box
2’s 7 into box 3. Now put 9 in box 3 and execute indirect store (1, 3): the 2 in box 1
tells it where to put the 9, so box 2 changes and box 1 stays 2. An address is being
read from a box; it is not necessarily the box’s own number.
    The lambda base uses the terms and weak call-by-value rule defined in Defi-
nition 20.1. To compute its next state, first try to reduce the function part of an
application. If that part cannot step and is an abstraction, either substitute an
148   17 From “Records over any base: the latch”


               argument that is already an abstraction or take a step in the argument. If neither
               applies, the term cannot step. Variables and abstractions cannot step by themselves,
               and there is no reduction under an abstraction. The base leaves a term unchanged
               when it cannot step and calls precisely those terms halted. Every term is allowed as
               a starting state, including open terms.
                   So all three are bases in the sense of Definition 11.1, and the latch theorem
               applies to each. It says nothing else about them, nothing about what they can
               compute or how fast.
                   There’s also a relation defined between bases, weak base equivalence: two bases
               are equivalent when some correspondence covers their start states both ways, related
               states agree on an observation and on halting, and one step on one side is matched
               by some number of steps, possibly zero, on the other. That “some number of steps”
               is the stuttering idea Leslie Lamport (the same Lamport as Section 7’s message
               counters) put forward in 1983: a fine-grained machine and a coarse one shouldn’t
               count as different because one takes three ticks to do what the other does in one.
               The relation carries no cost; a machine that multiplies huge numbers in one step
               and a Turing machine differ in price. The relation never looks at price, so on what it
               does look at they can match. It’s proved to be an equivalence relation, and the latch
               form is preserved across it.
                   Draw a coarse machine 0 → 1, with 1 halted, and a finer one a → b → c, with
               c halted. Let a and b both show 0 and c show 1; halted states stay where they are.
               Relate 0 to both a and b, and 1 to c. The fine move a → b changes nothing visible,
               so the coarse machine matches it by taking no step. Its move b → c matches 0 → 1.
               Starting from a, the coarse move is matched by two fine steps. “Possibly zero” allows
               this; the relation is about matching observations and halting, not matching the
               number of ticks or their cost.
                   There is a limit to that statement. The “preserved across it” proof throws away its
               equivalence hypothesis; the latch holds on every base regardless, so the preservation
               is true for a boring reason. And nowhere in this book are two of the bases actually
               related by this relation. The relation is defined, it’s an equivalence, and it is not
               used to connect the Turing machine to the register machine to the lambda calculus,
               or any of them to the machine of Section 15. It would be easy to read that list as if
               it did. The honest sentence is the one that’s proved: each base, separately, carries a
               latch.

               Definition 17.2 (Weak base equivalence). Bases B1 and B2 , with observations o1
               and o2 into one common set, are weakly equivalent when some relation R relates
               every starting state of each to some starting state of the other, and any related x,
                                                                  17.2 What it takes to be complete   149


y have o1 ( x ) = o2 (y), the same halting, some n ≥ 0 with R(nextB1 ( x ), nextnB2 (y)),
and some n ≥ 0 with R(nextnB1 ( x ), nextB2 (y)).



Theorem 17.1 (Outside bases, and the relation). (1) Weak base equivalence is
reflexive, symmetric and transitive. (2) If two bases are weakly equivalent, Observa-
tion 11.1 holds over one exactly when it holds over the other. (3) Observation 11.1
holds over each of the three bases in Definition 17.1, for every fixed program where
the base has one.

Proof. (1) A base matches itself by pairing each state with itself and matching one
step with one step. Reverse any matching between two bases and it gives a matching
in the other direction.
    For three bases, pair a state of the first with a state of the third whenever some
middle state matches both. A start on either outside has a middle start beside it, and
that middle start has a start on the far side. Observations and halting agree across
the first pair and then across the second, so they agree across the outside pair too.
    Follow one step on the first base. Its match supplies a finite sequence of middle
steps. Match the first of those steps on the third base, then the second from where
that match ended, and continue. Joining the finite sequences gives a match for the
original step. If the middle sequence is empty, take no step on the third base. Do the
same in the other direction for a step on the third base. This checks both stepping
requirements and proves transitivity.
   (2) The latch argument uses only a base step and the stated record rules. It
therefore holds over both bases, whether or not they are matched. (3) Each of the
three displayed constructions supplies that step, its starts and its halting reading.
The same latch argument applies separately to each.


17.2 What it takes to be complete
The latch left two choices open, the price list and the event. A machine I can hold up
against everything else has to make both, and a name for “this machine computes,
and its record keeps something” has to ask for more than the toll does. The toll
alone is a low bar. A machine that charges one for every step it takes is a clock bolted
to a computer, and it obeys the toll for any record at all: every raise costs at least
one because every move does. And a machine whose record never goes up obeys it
too, because a toll on a raise that never happens is never broken. The clock-priced
machine may keep useful records; its prices simply do not distinguish computing
150   17 From “Records over any base: the latch”


               from recording. The constant reading can keep no new yes/no distinction. The toll
               permits both.
                  I’ll give the names now, all of them, once, with the reason for each, and they work
               the way Turing’s do. The Thiele Machine, with capitals, is the model: a computer
               with a reading that can go from no to yes, natural instruction prices and the toll.
               The reading may also be constant or revocable. An internal ledger is extra structure.
               A Thiele machine, lower case, is any particular system that fits the model, the way
               one rule table is a Turing machine. That bar is low on purpose, and a clock clears it.
               Thiele-complete, defined next, is the bar worth meeting, the way Turing-complete is:
               a machine that computes anything, with its record kept apart from its computing
               and able to tell starts apart. Part III adds one stricter bar, certified Thiele-complete,
               for a record that, from a clean start, goes up only on a claim that was checked,
               committed and certified. And Appendix C names a universal Thiele machine: one
               machine that runs the others, the way Turing’s universal machine runs every Turing
               machine. Those are all of them: the model, the systems that obey it, the ones that
               compute and keep, the ones whose keeping is earned, and the one that runs them.
                  The bar comes in four clauses, and nothing in it mentions what the record is
               about.

               Definition 17.3 (Thiele-complete). A machine with the five parts is Thiele-complete
               when it can be read through an interface that hands over three things: a universal
               base, a split of its moves into computing moves and marks, and a ledger, a whole num-
               ber for each state. A universal base is a way of reading each state as a configuration
               of a machine that runs every program (I use Minsky’s two counters, Section 22.1), a
               class of live states, a computing move for each of that machine’s instructions that
               does to the configuration exactly what the instruction does from every live state
               and keeps it live, and, for each start, a live loaded state showing that start. The
               interface has to meet four clauses. (A) Computing can’t reach the record, and the
               record stays. No computing move changes the record, and a record that’s up stays
               up under every move. (B) Starts are blank. Every loaded state has the record down.
               (C) Computing costs nothing, marks cost one. Computing moves cost 0, every other
               move costs exactly 1, and each move adds its cost to the ledger. (D) The record tells
               starts apart. Some run, the same list of moves, ends with the record up from one
               loaded state and down from another.
                  Read the clauses as four plain sentences, with the universal base underneath
               them: the machine computes anything a Turing machine can. (A) The computing
               never touches what’s kept, and what’s kept stays. (B) Nothing is kept before the
               run starts. (C) The ledger counts the marks, and only the marks. (D) What’s kept
                                                                   17.2 What it takes to be complete   151


depends on where you started: there’s a run whose record says something about its
start.
    (D) is the clause where distinction comes in, and it’s a low bar: a mark that reads
one bit of the input meets it. What it excludes is a record that ignores the start. A
record that goes up on every run from every start keeps a before and an after, and
nothing about where the run began. (D) doesn’t ask that every raise tell starts apart;
it asks that the machine can. A machine may have a mark that raises the record
from any state at all; it pays one for it, like every raise, and (D) is still met by some
other run. Which runs a machine wants its record to tell apart is its own choice, and
Part III makes one.
    The name echoes Turing-complete, but this is a specification a machine meets,
not a class closed under simulation: a machine is held up against it through an
interface, and changing the unit of price drops a machine out.


Theorem 17.2 (What every Thiele-complete machine has). Let a machine be Thiele-
complete through an interface. (1) It obeys the toll, and the move that raises its
record is a mark, never a computing move, and costs exactly one. (2) Along any run,
the ledger rises by exactly the number of marks in it. (3) From any state with the
record down, every run that ends with the record up raises the ledger by at least
1. (4) Some run from some loaded state raises the record. (5) Every move costs 0
or 1, and some move costs 0. (6) Its base runs every two-counter program step for
step from every live state, and halting from a loaded state matches halting from the
configuration it shows.

Proof. (1) If a computing move raised the record it would change it, against (A);
so the raising move is a mark, and a mark costs one by (C). (2) is (C) added up
along the run. (3) Somewhere along the run the record goes from down to up,
and that move is a mark, by (1), so it adds one. (4) is (D), and by (B) the record
started down. (5) Every move is a computing move or a mark, and the universal
base supplies computing moves. (6) is the universal base, one instruction at a time,
from a loaded state that is live and shows its start.
    The floor is one, and one is reached: the lift below gives a machine whose record
goes up after a single mark.
Observation 17.3 (A clock is a Thiele machine and isn’t Thiele-complete). Take the
two-counter machine, let every move cost 1, and take as the record any yes/no question
about its configuration; or a flag that switches on, and stays on, the first time a chosen
test of the configuration says yes. It obeys the toll and has a two-counter machine inside
it, and it isn’t Thiele-complete. No machine whose every move costs at least 1 is.
152   17 From “Records over any base: the latch”


               Proof. A Thiele-complete machine has a move priced 0, by Theorem 17.2(5), and
               this one has none.
                   A two-counter base priced 0 with a record that’s down at every state obeys the
               toll and meets (A), (B) and (C), and isn’t Thiele-complete either: (D) asks for a run
               that raises the record.


               Theorem 17.4 (Each of the four clauses is needed). For each clause there’s a
               machine that meets the other three through some interface and is Thiele-complete
               through none. Two of them are built on the two counters with two marks, one that
               raises the record when A is 0 and one that always raises it, and a ledger beside them
               that each move adds its cost to. (A) fails: that machine, and one more move, priced
               0, that puts the record back down. (B) fails: the two counters, every move priced 0,
               with a record that no move ever changes, up at the starts where A is 0 and down
               elsewhere. (C) fails: the two counters with two marks, every move priced 1. (D)
               fails: the two counters, every move priced 0, with a record that’s always down.

               Proof. Each machine meets the three by construction. The first fails (A) through
               every interface, because a move lowers the record, so the record doesn’t stay. The
               second: every one of its moves costs 0, so (C) makes every move a computing move
               under any interface; then by (A) no run changes the record, and with every start
               blank by (B) no run raises it, so (D) fails. The third has no move priced 0, so it fails
               by Observation 17.3. The fourth never raises its record.



               Theorem 17.5 (The record can’t be read off the window). Let a machine be Thiele-
               complete through an interface. (1) Every configuration is shown, at no cost, with
               the record down, by one computing move from some loaded state. (2) Some loaded
               state has two runs that end on the same window, one with the record up and one
               with it down, and their ledgers differ by at least 1. (3) So no rule that reads only the
               window says, at the end of every run from every loaded state, whether the record is
               up, and none says what the ledger reads.

               Proof. (1) From the loaded state (1, x + 1, y), the computing move “DEC A to line
               p” shows ( p, x, y), costs nothing by (C), and leaves the record down by (A) and (B).
               (2) Take the run (D) gives, from the loaded state where it raises the record. Its end
               shows some configuration. From the same loaded state, computing moves alone
               reach any configuration at all: count each counter down to zero, count it up to the
               value wanted, and jump to the line wanted. That run ends on the same window,
                                                                 17.2 What it takes to be complete   153


and it’s all computing, so by (A) and (B) its record is still down and by (C) its ledger
hasn’t moved, while the first run’s ledger went up by at least one, at its raise. (3)
A rule that reads the window gives one answer for one window, and (2) has one
window with two answers.
   Part III proves the same blindness for its certified machines (Observation 22.12),
with a gap of three, because a certified raise costs three. Here the gap is one, and
one is reached.


Theorem 17.6 (Every universal base becomes Thiele-complete). Take any machine
with a universal base and any events on its states, and add a record, a ledger and,
for each event, a mark that costs one and raises the record exactly when the event
holds; computing moves cost nothing and leave the record alone, and every move
adds its cost to the ledger. If some run of computing moves makes some event
hold from one loaded state and not from another, the result is Thiele-complete, its
computing is the original machine’s step for step, and its record goes up after a
single mark from any state where the event already holds.

Proof. (A): computing moves are the original’s and don’t touch the record, and a
mark only adds to it. (B): the record starts down. (C): by construction. (D): run the
moves that make the event hold from one loaded state and not the other, then the
event’s mark. Step for step: a computing move acts on the original part and nothing
else. And a mark from a state where its event holds puts the record up.
    One mark on “A is at least 1” always works: the starts with A at 0 and at
1 already differ on it, so that mark alone, run from each, tells them apart. So
Thiele-complete isn’t a property only a special machine has. Any model with a
universal base becomes Thiele-complete by adding marks, and needs no claims and
no checker to do it; a model that simulates the two counters some other way does
so once its moves are grouped into ones that carry out a counter instruction each
(Theorem 30.6). Two kinds of base don’t lift, whatever the events: one whose moves
don’t depend on the state it’s in, and one whose moves lead from each state to only
finitely many next states. A universal base needs, from one state, a move at no cost
to every line of a program, and that’s infinitely many places to go.
    Some Thiele-complete machine runs every Thiele machine a program can de-
scribe. Appendix C defines a universal Thiele machine and builds hosts that run
any machine a program can describe, in order, carrying its reading as a latch and
matching its ledger up to a surcharge of at most three, with every computing step
done by counter moves on the host and every rise of the host’s record placed by its
program. None of that needs certification: the presented host’s own program, read
154   17 From “Records over any base: the latch”


               on a host with no claims, no checker and no table of facts, and one paid mark that
               raises the record when a register is at least 1, meets the definition too.
                  The rest of the book holds machines to this bar. Part III gives one example that
               clears it, and then asks for the stricter certified bar: that from a clean start its record
               go up only on a claim that was checked, by a checker proved to mean it, and then
               committed, and that some claim’s check can come out either way. Asking that is a
               choice of what the record answers to, the second of the latch’s two open choices,
               made one way, with clean starts and three priced acts added (Theorem 22.3).
CHAPTER 18




From “The whole direction”

People got here before me, and from a direction I didn’t expect, which was program-
ming for many processors at once. Lindsey Kuper and Ryan Newton’s LVars (2013),
“lattice-based data structures for deterministic parallelism” in their own title, are
memory cells whose value only climbs in an order, where a read can’t ask for the
value and can only ask whether it has climbed past a threshold. That’s a cup. Writers
running side by side can push the value up in different orders, and a threshold once
passed stays passed. Their deterministic behaviour also depends on which reads
and updates the language permits. Permanence alone would not do it: a program
allowed to ask whether the cup is up yet could still see another writer’s timing. Amin
Timany and Lars Birkedal (2021), coming at it from proofs about programs, show,
as I read their paper, that any preorder at all can be carried as monotone ghost state
in a program logic. That’s the closest thing I’ve found to somebody else arriving at
the decomposition into lines.
    The two rest on the same thing, a value that only climbs and a question that
only asks whether it’s past a point, and they use it for opposite jobs. In an LVar the
threshold read steers the program. Ghost state, as far as I understand the term,
lives in the proof, and the running program never reads it. Here the record is never
allowed to steer the base computation, so that particular restriction sits closer to the
ghost state. Push on that and it gives a little: Section 6’s glance, the whole reason to
keep a record, is a read. What keeps me on that side is that a glance worth trusting
gives the answer the long way would have given. Their purposes aren’t mine, but
the shape is theirs first. What I can’t find in either is a price on crossing a line.




                                                  155
CHAPTER 19




From “The shadow, exactly”

19.1 How much gets destroyed, on average

The halving price is a worst case, because it prices the largest landing pile a move
can have. The next question is the average one: if I don’t know which state the
machine was in, cups and all, but I have some spread of chances over the states,
how much do I lose, on average, about which state it was, once I know where it
landed? Shannon’s conditional entropy measures exactly that (Section 3 built the
entropy from scratch, so I won’t do it again here), and I’ll write it in bits.


Theorem 19.1 (The halving price in bits bounds the average loss). Take any spread
of non-negative weights over the states, uniform or not, with finite total weight, on a
machine of any size. (1) If every landing pile has at most N states, with N ≥ 1, the
weighted conditional entropy of the state given its landing spot is at most the total
weight times log2 N bits. (2) So on a machine with the halving price, that weighted
information loss is at most its cost in bits times the total weight, even when the
spread’s own entropy is infinite. For a probability distribution, the total weight is
one and the average loss is at most the cost. (3) If a landing pile has 2c states, the
uniform probability distribution on it loses exactly c bits and attains that bound.

Proof. Look at one landing spot. Suppose the sources that land there carry total
weight w > 0. Divide each source’s weight by w. Those shares now add to one:
they describe the uncertainty about the source once you know this landing spot.
There are at most N possible sources. The logarithm argument in Section 3 says
the uncertainty is greatest when those possibilities have equal shares, and is then
at most log2 N bits. Multiply by w to put this pile back into the whole spread. Its
contribution is at most w log2 N. A pile with zero weight contributes nothing.
   Add the contributions. The pile weights add to the original total weight, so
the same log2 N multiplies that total. Under the halving price, N = 2cost , whose
base-two logarithm is exactly the cost.



                                 156
                                                            19.1 How much gets destroyed, on average   157


    Infinitely many piles cause no subtraction of one infinite entropy from another.
I am adding the nonnegative uncertainties inside the piles. Every finite collection
contributes at most its weight times the bound, and its weight is at most the total.
Taking the largest value approached by those finite sums keeps the same bound.
    Finally put all probability on one pile of 2c sources, equally shared. Before landing
there are 2c equally possible origins; afterwards the landing spot distinguishes none
of them. The uncertainty left about the origin is log2 (2c ) = c bits. That attains the
bound.
    Where does it stop? My first guess was one place, a landing pile with infinitely
many states, and that the average would blow up there right along with the worst
case. That’s half right, and the half that’s wrong surprised me. No finite charge
satisfying the halving price can price a move that sends infinitely many states to one
spot. The weaker merge price could still charge one, since it does not measure the
pile’s size. But the average depends on the spread. Put weight 12 on the first state, 14
on the next, 18 on the next, and so on, and the information lost by crushing the pile
never reaches 2 bits, however many of its states you count. Under that spread, a
two-bit information budget covers the average, though no finite budget covers the
worst case. So I went looking for a spread that does blow up, and it took a nastier
                                           k                          1
one: blocks of states, block k holding 22 states and a share (k+1)(     k +2)
                                                                              of the weight,
which adds up to one. Under that spread the loss grows past every bound, and no
finite information budget covers that average loss.
    You can generate the first spread by tossing a fair coin until the first head. Zero
tails names state 0, one tail names state 1, and so on. State j has chance 2−( j+1) , and
identifying that outcome takes j + 1 toss-results, so its entropy term is ( j + 1)/2 j+1 .
Add these by counting each possible toss position: the first is reached with chance
1, the second with chance 1/2, the third with chance 1/4, and so on. The total
is 1 + 1/2 + 1/4 + · · · = 2; every finite partial sum of the state terms is smaller.
Resetting all those outcomes to one box can lose only those two bits on average,
despite having infinitely many possible starts.
    For the block spread, split each block’s weight equally among its states. The block
weights sum to one because 1/((k + 1)(k + 2)) = 1/(k + 1) − 1/(k + 2). Within
                       k
block k, there are 22 equally likely choices, so just identifying a state within that
block contributes 2k /((k + 1)(k + 2)) to the average bits. Those contributions grow
without bound as k grows, and forgetting which block it was only adds to the loss.
And no finite worst-case price can handle an infinite pile: for any proposed cost c,
select 2c + 1 different states in it. They all land together, violating the halving rule’s
maximum of 2c .
158   19 From “The shadow, exactly”




              Theorem 19.2 (An infinite pile). (1) A move that sends infinitely many states to
              one spot isn’t priced by the halving price at any finite cost. (2) Under the halving
              spread 2−( j+1) , crushing the pile loses exactly 2 bits, and counted over its first n
              states, less than 2 for every n. (3) Under the block spread, the information lost
              grows past every bound, so no finite bit budget covers it.

               Proof. The three computations above.
                   And heat? Part I already said where the line is, and it’s the same line here.
               Landauer’s principle, that crushing c bits’ worth of possibilities gives off at least
               c kT ln 2 of heat on average, is a premise from physics, and a contested one (Sec-
               tion 3). Nothing in this section proves anything about heat. What the counting does
               give is that, under that premise, on a machine with the halving price, a move’s cost
               priced at kT ln 2 per unit, times the weight of the piles, is always enough to cover
               the minimum the premise would demand, for every spread of finite total weight.
               If the premise falls, that sentence falls with it, and every theorem above it stands,
               because none of them used it.
CHAPTER 20




From “The diagonal”

20.1 Five names and a table

Do this first, with a pencil, because the whole family of arguments is this one trick
and you should own it. Write five friends’ names down the left of a page. Next
to each name, write a five-digit string of zeros and ones, anything you like. Now
build a new string: its first digit is the opposite of the first digit of the first row; its
second digit is the opposite of the second digit of the second row; and so on down
the diagonal. Look at what you’ve made. It differs from row one in position one,
from row two in position two, from every row at that row’s own position. So it’s
nobody’s string, and your table of five missed something.
    With five rows and five digits that’s a cute fact. Georg Cantor, a mathematician
working out how to compare infinite sets, made the point in 1891 that it works for
any list, however long, even infinite: hand me a complete list of all infinite strings
of two symbols, I’ll walk the diagonal flipping as I go, and I’ll hand you back a string
that’s on no row. So there is no complete list. There are more such strings than
there are places on any list, and that’s where “some infinities are bigger than others”
comes from. (He wrote the two symbols as m and w, where I’d write 0 and 1, and
that paper wasn’t even his first proof of the fact. The first was in 1874, by a harder
road through the real numbers, and in 1891 he called the new one much simpler.
The diagonal was him finding the easy way.)
    Now look at what the argument did, because it’s going to do the same thing
again and again, in new clothes, and I want you to recognise it each time. (Not
every name below is a flipped diagonal in the table sense. Kleene’s theorem is the
tool that makes the diagonal buildable, Rice’s is the general form, the one from Ken
Thompson, who built the original Unix operating system at Bell Labs, is an attack
that uses the same trick, and the last, Lawvere’s, is the theorem that makes them all
one.) There was a table: rows are the things being listed, columns are questions,
each cell says “does row i do column j.” There was the diagonal: the cells where
a thing is asked about itself. There was a flip: something with no fixed point, like
turning yes into no, applied along the diagonal. And the thing that got refuted was



                                                    159
160   20 From “The diagonal”


              the assumed object, the complete list. Nothing outside the assumption was touched.
              Hold on to those four: a table, a diagonal, a flip, and the assumption eating itself.


              20.2 The same move, again and again
              Russell, 1902, from Section 6: the collection of all collections that don’t contain
              themselves. It’s Cantor’s flipped diagonal with “contains” as the table, and Russell
              said as much, in The Principles of Mathematics in 1903. He got there, he wrote, by
              running Cantor’s argument on a supposed collection of everything, which ought to
              be the biggest collection there is. What got refuted was the assumed object, the
              collection of everything, and Zermelo’s fix was to stop assuming it. I promised in
              Section 6 that I’d come back to this here. Every reading in this book is a yes or no
              question on the states of one machine, a collection cut out of a collection I was
              already given, and never a sort of everything. When Part I asked for one state that
              codes every question about the states (Section 1), the same flip refuted it.
                   Jules Richard, a French mathematician who taught at the lycées of Tours, Dijon
              and Châteauroux, took his doctorate in Paris in 1901 and wrote mostly on geometry.
              In 1905, in a short note, a letter to the editor of a French science review, he
              observed: some phrases (his were in French) define real numbers (“the ratio of a
              circle’s circumference to its diameter”); there are only countably many phrases, so
              list the numbers they define in order; run Cantor’s diagonal down their decimal
              expansions and you get a real number no phrase defines, except that you just defined
              it, in the phrase that describes the diagonal. The lesson I take from that one is the
              lesson this whole section turns on: keep apart the language a system talks in and
              the language you use to talk about it. Mix them and the diagonal bites you.
                   Richard’s paradox has a cousin small enough to do at the table, and it comes
              with a footnote I can’t resist. Russell, the first to put it in print, in 1906, credited
              it to G. G. Berry, a junior librarian at the Bodleian Library in Oxford. The paradox
              has outlived empires, and it came from the library desk. In the version in Principia
              Mathematica it’s about syllables. Take the phrase “the least integer not nameable
              in fewer than nineteen syllables.” There are only so many phrases shorter than
              nineteen syllables, so they name only so many numbers, so some least number is
              left over, and the phrase picks it out. Now count the phrase: the, least, in-te-ger,
              not, name-a-ble, in, few-er, than, nine-teen, syl-la-bles, and it comes to eighteen.
              In eighteen syllables it has named the number that can’t be named in fewer than
              nineteen. It’s the same lesson as Richard’s, in a smaller coat: “nameable” is a word
              about the language, used inside the language, and that’s where the bite is.
                   Kurt Gödel, 1931, with an improvement in 1936 by Barkley Rosser, a logician
              who had studied under Alonzo Church at Princeton and whose trick lets the theorem
              assume only that the system is consistent: the big one, and I’ll say exactly what it
                                                              20.2 The same move, again and again   161


says, because every word of it is carrying weight. Take any consistent formal system
with a mechanically checkable list of axioms that can do a bit of arithmetic. Then
there are sentences it can neither prove nor refute. The engine is a lemma that lets
you build, for any property you can write down, a sentence that the system proves
equivalent to that property holding of the sentence’s own code number. Apply it
to “not provable” and you get a sentence equivalent, inside the system, to its own
unprovability. The table is the sentences listed by code, the diagonal is a sentence
about its own code, and the flip turns provable into not-provable. The Stanford
Encyclopedia stresses two cautions, and I’ll repeat them. The sentence doesn’t
literally “say” anything about itself. It’s provably equivalent to a claim about its code,
which is less mystical and more exact. And the theorem doesn’t say the sentence is
unknowable. It says this one system can’t settle it.
    And how did the world take it? Mostly, at first, it didn’t. Gödel, twenty-four years
old, announced the first theorem during a round-table discussion on the last day
of a conference in Königsberg in September 1930, and by the account of Solomon
Feferman, one of the editors of Gödel’s collected works, the room met it with general
incomprehension, except for one man. John von Neumann pulled Gödel aside to
talk about it, kept thinking about it, and a couple of months later saw that the
argument gave something more: a consistent system of this kind can’t prove its own
consistency. He wrote to Gödel with it in a letter dated 20 November 1930. Gödel
had already got there. His paper had reached the journal three days earlier, with
that second theorem announced in it, and von Neumann left the credit with Gödel.
It’s the “somebody got there first” shape from Section 5, with one difference: here
the man who got there second was von Neumann.
    Alan Turing, 1936. I’d always taken it that Turing proved the halting problem
undecidable, and here I had to go and look. The set theorist Joel David Hamkins and
his coauthor Theodor Nenu went back to the 1936 paper and report that the word
“halting,” the modern statement, and the self-referential proof everyone teaches are
all, strictly speaking, absent from it. What Turing proved undecidable were two
other questions about his machines (whether a machine prints infinitely many digits;
whether it ever prints a given symbol). The second is equivalent to halting and the
first is strictly harder. So their answer cuts both ways, and they say so: Turing never
stated the halting problem, and he essentially proved it, because his second question
is a short translation away from it. The name came later. Martin Davis seems to have
been the first to write “halting problem,” in a book of 1958, and Stephen Kleene had
already stated the problem, without that name, in his 1952 textbook Introduction to
Metamathematics. The textbook version is still right as mathematics, and it fits on
two index cards. Card A is the Checker: hand it any program card and it announces
“halts” or “runs forever.” Card B is the Contrarian: “Hand me to the Checker. If
it says I halt, run forever. If it says I run forever, stop.” Hand B to A, and either
162   20 From “The diagonal”


              answer is wrong, so there’s no Checker. The table is programs against programs, the
              diagonal is a program run on itself, and the flip turns halt into loop.
                  Stephen Kleene, 1938, the logician whose textbook I just mentioned, and this is
              the one that turns the paradox into a tool, so slow down. Everything above needs
              the self-referring object to exist: Gödel’s sentence has to be buildable, the Contrarian
              has to be able to “hand me to the Checker.” Kleene’s second recursion theorem
              says: for any transformation of programs you can compute, there’s a program that
              behaves exactly as if it had been handed its own source code. Any program may
              be written as though it already had a copy of itself in hand. He stated it almost in
              passing, in a paper on something else, and proved it in the last two lines of one
              of its sections. I have that count from Yiannis Moschovakis, a logician at UCLA
              and Athens who wrote a 2009 paper on it, expanded in the Bulletin of Symbolic
              Logic in 2010, called “Kleene’s Amazing Second Recursion Theorem,” and he calls
              it a little gem. It’s the reason “programs that print their own source” exist in every
              ordinary programming language: one that can compute whatever a Turing machine
              can and can build, from a program and an input, a program that runs the first on
              that input (they’re called quines, after a philosopher I’ll come to in a moment; try
              “Print the following twice, the second time in quotes: ‘Print the following twice,
              the second time in quotes:’ ” and obey it). Self-reference in computation isn’t a
              paradox but a construction. The paradoxes happened only when someone assumed
              an unrestricted collection or language; the theorem happens when you build the
              self-referrer honestly.
                  And where does the word quine come from? The philosopher didn’t coin it
              himself. Douglas Hofstadter coined it in 1979, in Gödel, Escher, Bach, in honour of
              Willard Van Orman Quine, who had made a long study of self-reference that works
              indirectly. Quine left a sentence you can take apart at the table, from a 1962 essay
              on paradox, and in full it reads: “yields falsehood when preceded by its quotation”
              yields falsehood when preceded by its quotation. Do what it says. Take the part in
              quotation marks, put a quoted copy of it in front of itself, and look at what you’ve
              built: it’s the whole sentence, word for word. So the sentence says that it itself is
              false. It’s the liar paradox, but look at how it got there: no “this sentence” anywhere,
              no pointing at itself, just an instruction to quote and copy, the same move as the
              print-twice instruction above. Quoting and copying is Kleene’s trick in plain English,
              and it’s why the paradox and the tool look so alike. The paradox is what you get
              when the copy asserts something about its own truth. The tool is what you get when
              the copy just does something.
                  Henry Rice, 1953, whom you met briefly in Section 8: the general form. Any
              property that depends only on what a program does, whatever its text, and that’s
              true of some programs and false of others, has no algorithm that decides it for all
              programs. “Does this program halt” is one such property. “Does this program ever
                                                            20.2 The same move, again and again   163


output 7” is another. “Does this program contain an if-statement” isn’t, because
that’s about the text. One standard proof goes straight through Kleene.
    And the one that’s actually an attack: Ken Thompson, in his Turing Award lecture,
published in 1984. Build a compiler that, when it compiles the login program, plants
a back door, and when it compiles a compiler, plants both habits into that one too.
Then delete the trick from the source. The compiled compiler still carries it, and
recompiling a clean source with the poisoned binary re-plants it, forever. Reading
the source never reveals it. A locksmith teaches an apprentice to cut a spare for
every town-hall key and to teach that to every apprentice they train, and never to
write it in the manual; years later the manual is spotless and every locksmith in
town cuts the spare. Thompson’s compiler is the recursion theorem with a knife in
it, and his moral was that you can’t trust code you didn’t write entirely yourself.
    And Thompson wasn’t first either, and he said so. Pull on the lecture’s acknowl-
edgment the way Section 14 pulls on Bitcoin’s footnotes: he writes that he first read
of the possibility in an Air Force critique of the security of an early implementation
of Multics, a time-sharing system from the 1960s, and that he couldn’t find a more
specific reference to it, and he asks anyone who can to send him one. The critique is
Paul Karger and Roger Schell’s 1974 security evaluation of Multics, which already
described a trap door planted in a compiler that, because the compiler is written in
its own language, keeps itself alive. So the knife was in the drawer ten years before
the lecture was printed. Then comes the obvious next question, whether there’s
any way out or whether “trust no binary you didn’t build” is the bottom. There’s
a partial answer, and it took a while. David A. Wheeler, in a 2005 paper and a
2009 dissertation at George Mason University, worked out diverse double-compiling:
compile the suspect compiler’s source with a second, independently made compiler,
use the result to compile that source again, and compare the output bit for bit
with the suspect binary. If they match under Wheeler’s assumptions, that gives
a source-to-binary correspondence. The second compiler must correctly translate
the relevant source, the execution and comparison environments must be trusted
for these runs, and the build’s language and environmental effects must match
the conditions of the argument. Two compilers having different authors is useful
evidence for trust, not a substitute for those premises. The question has moved to a
boundary where we can name what we trust.
    Bill Lawvere, in 1969, proved a fixed-point theorem that, as I read it, makes all
of these one theorem in disguise: if a set can be mapped onto all the functions from
itself to some target, then every function on the target has a fixed point; so if the
target has a function with no fixed point (a flip), no such covering exists. Cantor,
Russell, Gödel and halting all come out as instances of one shape. Noson Yanofsky
retold it in 2003 without the category theory. I mention it because “it’s the same
wall” is a claim I make below, and I want you to know it’s fair at the level of proof
164   20 From “The diagonal”


              shape, and that every instance has its own boundary of who counts as a decider, and
              that boundary is exactly where most overclaims live.


              20.3 Applying the diagonal
              Before applying the diagonal to a system with a record, identify who the proposed
              decider is and what it must decide. Four familiar applications show why those
              details matter.
                  “Gödel shows minds aren’t machines.” John Lucas, a philosopher who spent
              thirty-six years as a fellow of Merton College, Oxford, argued it in 1961, in a paper
              called “Minds, Machines and Gödel.” Roger Penrose, the Oxford mathematical
              physicist who later shared a Nobel prize for his work on how black holes form,
              revived it twice, in two books, The Emperor’s New Mind in 1989 and Shadows of
              the Mind in 1994. I’m sure of who argued it and when. Whether they’re right is
              the question, and the standard replies, as far as I can tell, have held up: the Gödel
              sentence is true only if the system is consistent, so a human who “sees” its truth has
              to know the system is consistent, which is the hard part; and nobody can build the
              Gödel sentence of a system they can’t specify, so humans might be machines too
              complicated to diagonalise. The theorem has a conditional in it and the argument
              drops the conditional.
                  And the part that’s easy to miss is that Gödel himself didn’t go as far as Lucas.
              In a lecture on the day after Christmas in 1951, at a meeting of the American
              Mathematical Society at Brown University, he drew from his own theorems only an
              either-or. Either the human mind, even inside pure mathematics, infinitely surpasses
              the powers of any finite machine, or there are problems about whole numbers, of
              a certain simple kind, that no proof the human mind can conceive will ever settle.
              Feferman, writing about that lecture, says Gödel privately believed the first half and
              still stated only the disjunction, because he didn’t have a knock-down argument
              against the machine side. Gödel wrote soon afterward that he meant to publish the
              lecture, and never did; the text sat among his papers until after his death. So the
              man whose theorem gets quoted for “minds aren’t machines” stated an either-or and
              left the deciding to whoever came next. The conditional is there again, kept by the
              person with the most right to drop it.
                  “Undecidable means unknowable.” Two senses share the word: a problem with
              no single algorithm correct on every instance, and a sentence independent of one
              particular system. Neither means nobody can know a given answer. Any single
              instance is decidable, trivially: for any one program, one of “say yes” and “say no” is
              right. What’s ruled out is one inside procedure that’s right on every case.
                  “Everything interesting is undecidable.” Rice is about behavioural properties over
              all programs. It says nothing against checking the text, nothing against deciding for
                                                                         20.3 Applying the diagonal   165


specific programs, nothing against analyses allowed to say “I don’t know,” nothing
against finite-state model checking or type checking. Real compilers and analysers
live with Rice by being sound but incomplete, and what limits them in practice is
mostly cost. The diagonal shapes them; it doesn’t stop them.
    “Self-reference is a paradox.” It’s a theorem with a construction, as Kleene, the
quines and Thompson show.
    Two forms of the wall live in this section, and they’re honest in different ways.
    The first is the nat family, a tiny substrate built so that program two is the
Contrarian for a given candidate decider d, and for every d, d is wrong about
program two. It’s a family, one instance per d, and its recursion theorem holds by
construction. It’s the index cards made formal and nothing more.


Theorem 20.1 (The nat family). Fix any Boolean function d on N. Let the substrate
Nd have programs and states N; program 0 returns 1, program 1 returns 0, program
2 returns 0 if d(2) is true and 1 if it is false, whatever the input state, and every
other program gives nothing; each program is encoded as itself; µ is constantly 0, so
any step relation will do; Rep( f ) holds when f ( p) = 1 for every p with d( p) true
and f ( p) = 0 for every other p; and A( p) holds when program p returns 1 on input
0. Then it is not the case that d( p) = true ⇐⇒ A( p) for every p.

Proof. The recursion theorem of Nd holds with the fixed witness 2: for a repre-
sentable f , f (2) is 1 or 0 according to d(2), and program 2 returns exactly what
that program returns. The witnesses for A are 0 and 1, and A is extensional because
it reads only what a program returns. The flip of d is representable by the definition
of Rep, so Theorem 13.1 applies to d itself. Directly: A(2) holds exactly when d(2)
is false.
    Now push on it once more, because the nat family shows the outside decider in
three lines, and you can do them yourself. Stand outside Nd , knowing d. Write down
a new decider e: say yes on program 0, say “not d(2)” on program 2, and say no on
everything else. Check it against A. Program 0 returns 1, so A(0) holds and e says
yes. Program 1 returns 0, so A(1) fails and e says no. Program 2 returns 1 exactly
when d(2) is false, and that’s what e says. Every other program gives nothing, so A
fails and e says no. So e is right about every program. Does that break the wall? It
doesn’t, and the reason is the part that matters: in Nd a flip is representable only if it
says 1 exactly where d says yes, so the flip of e is representable only if e and d agree
everywhere, and they disagree at program 2. The correct decider exists; its flip just
isn’t one of the substrate’s own moves. (It’s three lines and a pencil, and you just did
it.) One and the same property is undecidable from inside and decided cleanly from
166   20 From “The diagonal”


              outside, at the same time. That’s not a paradox, because it’s two different people
              asking.
                  The second form is the one where the recursion theorem is proved from the
              reduction rules rather than assumed. It’s on L, the weak call-by-value lambda
              calculus that Yannick Forster and Gert Smolka presented in 2017 as a model of
              computation, and which a group at Saarland has since used as the seed of a
              whole library of undecidable problems. Here L is built from its reduction rules.
              The recursion theorem is proved, Rice’s theorem follows, halting follows, and the
              diagonal is reproved in the shape of the substrate theorem, for any property of
              programs that depends only on what they compute and that holds of one program
              and fails of another. The statement mentions no shortcut. So on L the result is the
              diagonal for one fixed model, and I don’t plug L into the substrate theorem, for this
              reason: a substrate’s run map has to answer, with a state or with nothing, and for L
              that map is the halting function itself, which no term of L computes. Using it would
              take something from outside L, and L is in this book as the model where nothing
              is taken from outside. So on L I run the argument again from scratch instead of
              applying the general one. Both are true; they’re different sentences.
                  I’ll say what that string of words means, because L is the strangest thing in this
              book that computes, and the smallest. The lambda calculus is Alonzo Church’s, from
              the 1930s, and it’s a programming language with one idea in it: functions. A term is
              one of three things: a variable; a function, written λ followed by its body, the body
              saying what to do with whatever you hand it; or one term applied to another, written
              side by side. Running a program is one move, over and over: when a function meets
              an argument, put the argument into the body where the variable was. The lambda
              calculus has no numbers, no memory, no instructions, and in 1937 Turing published
              a proof that it computes exactly what his machines compute. “Weak call-by-value” is
              two house rules about the order of moves: an argument is run down to a finished
              function (a value) before it’s put in, and nothing runs inside a function that hasn’t
              been handed anything yet. Variables are written as numbers counting how many
              λs back they were bound (a trick of Nicolaas de Bruijn; it means two variables can
              never get mixed up by sharing a name), and a term is closed when every variable
              in it is bound inside it, so it leans on nothing outside. Last, to hand a program to a
              program, L needs a way to write any term as data, the way a program’s source is
              a string you can pass around. The one used here is the Scott encoding, named for
              Dana Scott, a student of Church’s who went on to build mathematical models of the
              lambda calculus itself. As far as I can trace it, the encoding first appeared in lecture
              notes of his from Berkeley in 1962 that he never published, as a way to write data;
              Torben Mogensen carried it over to lambda terms themselves in 1992, and that’s the
              form used here. All you need about it is that every term t gets one code, written ⌜t⌝,
              and the code is itself a closed value. Two tools get built on top of that in the proof:
                                                                       20.3 Applying the diagonal   167


a quoting term Q that takes the code of a term to the code of that code (a program
that prints its own source needs exactly this move), and a fixed-point combinator, a
term that lets a function call itself, which is how Q works its way through a code of
any size.
    Do two of these on paper before I give you the general rule. Write I = λx.x: its
instruction is “return whatever you were handed.” Hand I its own card and you
get I I → I, and it stops. Now write D = λx.x x: “make two copies of what you
were handed and apply the first to the second.” Hand it its own card. Putting D in
for both copies of x gives D D → D D. You’re back at exactly the same instruction
and argument, so the rule never finishes. Both arguments were already functions,
so neither example sneaks any work under a function that hasn’t been handed
anything.
    The variable numbers are what keep the copying from getting confused. In
λx.λy.x, the last x points past the nearest λ (the one for y) to the one before it, so
its number is 1; in λx.λy.y the last variable has number 0. Rename x and y however
you like and neither recipe changes. The codes below are a way of putting a whole
recipe on a card that another recipe can read.

Definition 20.1 (L, as built here). A term of L is a variable (a de Bruijn number),
an application s t, or an abstraction λs; the values are the abstractions. One step
is weak call-by-value, left to right: (λb) v steps to b with v substituted when v is a
value, a step inside the left part of an application is a step of the application, and
so is a step inside the right part once the left part is a value. A term s evaluates to
v when s reaches v in some number of steps and v is a value; p ≡ q when p and q
evaluate to the same values. Every term t has a code ⌜t⌝, its Scott encoding, which
is a closed value. A map f on terms is L-computable when some closed value s has
s ⌜ p⌝ ≡ f ( p) for every p. A term D decides a property P of terms when, for every p,
D ⌜ p⌝ reaches λλ.1 (true) if P( p) holds and λλ.0 (false) if it fails. P is extensional
when p ≡ q and P( p) give P(q).



Theorem 20.2 (Recursion, Rice and halting for L). (1) For every closed value s
there is a closed term t that reaches s ⌜t⌝; so every L-computable f has a term p
with p ≡ f ( p). (2) Let P be extensional, with P(y) and not P(n) for closed terms
y and n. Then no closed term decides P. (3) No closed term decides halting, the
property of evaluating to some value.

Proof. (1) First build a program that can assemble the code of the very application I
am about to run. The quoting program Q takes a term’s code and returns the code
168   20 From “The diagonal”


              of that code. Appendix 5 gives the idea of its construction. The program mkapp
              takes two codes and joins them into the code of an application.
                 Make A do this when handed a code x: quote x, put that quoted code beside x
              using mkapp, and hand the result to s. Written out,

                                           A = λx. s (mkapp x ( Q x )).
              Now run A on its own code, and call that whole application t: t = A ⌜ A⌝. The
              first step puts ⌜ A⌝ where x stood. Quoting it supplies the code of ⌜ A⌝; joining the
              two codes supplies the code of A ⌜ A⌝. That is the code of t itself. The computation
              therefore reaches s ⌜t⌝, as promised. All the pieces were closed, so t is closed too. If
              s computes f , the resulting behaviour is exactly f (t).
                  (2) Suppose a closed program D decides P. Build another program that asks D
              about the code it receives, then runs the no-example n after yes and the yes-example
              y after no. In this language you must keep the two branches wrapped as functions
              until the answer chooses one; otherwise call-by-value would try to run them before
              choosing. The following term does that:

                                          λx. D x (λ_. n) (λ_. y) (λz.z).
              The final identity function is the harmless argument that opens the selected wrapper.
              Thus a true answer runs n, and a false answer runs y.
                 Apply part (1) to this program, obtaining its self-matching t. If P(t) holds, the
              judge says true and t behaves like n, where P fails. If P(t) fails, the judge says false
              and t behaves like y, where P holds. Either answer contradicts the requirement that
              alike behaviour has the same verdict. No such D exists.
                 (3) Halting depends only on which values a term reaches. The identity λz.z is
              already a value. The term (λx. x x )(λx. x x ) takes a step back to itself each time,
              and is never a value. These supply the yes-example and no-example for part (2), so
              halting has no closed decider either.
                 The construction in that proof isn’t mine, and the credit belongs right here.
              Forster and Smolka’s L development of 2017 has the Scott encoding of terms,
              a closed quoting term with exactly this specification, the code of a term to the
              code of its code, and a fixed point built the same way: their t is C ⌜C⌝ with
              C = λx. s ( A x ( Q x )), so their C is my A, and their A, which builds the code of an
              application, is my mkapp. They say they follow the proof in the Dutch logician Henk
              Barendregt’s textbook on the lambda calculus (1984), where statement (1) is the
              full lambda calculus’s second fixed-point theorem, and part (2) is what they call
              Scott’s theorem. The idea of the term construction is in Appendix 5; the route is
              theirs, and Barendregt’s before them.
                                                                    20.4 Where the diagonal applies   169


    So I’ve given the wall on paper, on a family built to fit it, and on one outside
model. The machine built in Part III (Section 15) fills in this same shape: on its
host machine, the one the universal program of Appendix C.1 runs on, which has a
counter for every whole number, programs are numbers, Kleene’s recursion theorem
is a theorem, and so is Rice’s, and the fixed point can be taken to agree on what the
program computes and also on the ledger and the flag at the end of every run where
they stop (Appendix 29.2).
    The table below lines up the picture all of that fills in against Turing’s, one column
per axis.

Time axis                                    Structural axis
counts: steps                               counts: µ
asks: does it halt?                         asks: does it admit the shortcut?
the wall: no Turing machine decides halting the wall: no decider whose flip is
                                            representable, under a recursion theorem


   Read the table as two uses of one diagonal, each under its own premises, and
as saying nothing more than that. The structural wall never uses µ; the µ entry
only says what that axis counts. The nat result is a family indexed by the candidate
decider, and L’s results are about one fixed model and properties that mention
no shortcut. Neither hands you a second fixed undecidable problem, and neither
separates Turing degrees: a property can sit behind the wall from inside and be
decided cleanly from outside, as the pencil argument on Nd showed. And “admits
the shortcut” in the right-hand column is the theorem’s sense, any behavioural
property with a yes and a no. “Has a cheaper way to the same answer” is a second
question, and the column has no wall for it.


20.4 Where the diagonal applies

The three versions put the candidate procedure in different places. In the general
substrate argument, the substrate must represent the candidate’s flip. In the natural-
number family, each candidate d supplies the substrate whose predicate defeats
it. In L, the candidate is a closed term and the question is a nontrivial extensional
property of closed terms. The reduction rules and recursion argument give Rice’s
theorem there.
    The pencil calculation on Nd is useful precisely because it is outside that substrate.
To run the diagonal inside, I need the representability and recursion conditions
stated in the theorem.
CHAPTER 21




From “What established means”

Open the gauge wider
Go back to the Sorter. It checked the order of the list. Now ask it another question:
did it also check that none of the original numbers went missing? The list on the
screen can be the same whether that second check happened or not. One lamp
saying “checked” will not tell me which work was done.
    Put a sheet beside the scratchpad, with a named box for each check. Order gets
a box. Membership gets a box. Each other check you want to remember gets its
own. When I come back, I want to know which boxes were ticked. If two runs tick
different boxes and leave the same working value, the full machine has to leave me
something that tells them apart. That is more of the fibre above the same tabletop
picture, brought within the question.
    Here are the moves. WRITE puts a value b on the scratchpad and starts a fresh
