sheet, even if I write the same value again. CHECK i tests its named predicate Pi
and records its name when it passes. A failed check changes nothing in this monitor.
IDLE leaves both scratchpad and sheet alone. The reader can see the working value
and ask about every check name: did it pass since the last WRITE? The sheet must
answer yes when it did and no when it didn’t.
    Take a finite list of named checks, and let m(b) count the ones whose predicates
are true at b. Each of those can be run or left unrun. The truths themselves may
overlap; it is my choice to perform each check that is independent. Four true
predicates give sixteen possible ticked sheets above the same value. The general
count follows in the same way.


Theorem 21.1 (Exactly how many current sheets). For a finite nonempty working
set B and this WRITE/CHECK/IDLE interface, an exact host needs at least 2m(b)
reachable full states above each b. The minimum total reachable state count is

                                      ∑ 2m ( b ) .
                                      b∈ B




                                170
                                                                                         171


Proof. Hold the working value fixed and start with two named checks that would
pass there. Run neither, the first alone, the second alone, or both. The working
value is the same in all four cases. The required answers are not. In particular, the
first-only case and the second-only case cannot finish at the same full state: ask
whether the first check ran, and that one state would have to answer both yes and
no.
     Each further check that would pass doubles the choices. For every sheet you
already had, you can leave its new box blank or run that check and tick it. Starting
from one empty sheet, m(b) such choices give 2m(b) sheets. Every sheet is reachable:
WRITE the value b, then perform exactly the checks whose boxes it ticks. Any
two different sheets disagree at some named box, so they need different full states.
States showing different working values are also different. Add the required states
for each value to get the displayed sum.
     Now build a machine with exactly that many states. A state consists of the
working value and its ticked sheet. Include every sheet that ticks only checks true at
that value. WRITE replaces the value and clears the sheet. CHECK ticks its own box
if its predicate is true, and otherwise leaves the sheet alone. IDLE changes nothing.
These rules never produce a forbidden sheet.
     Start with the empty sheet. After a WRITE it is empty again, exactly as the task
requires. After a successful CHECK it has precisely that success added; a repeated
success changes nothing further. A failed CHECK adds nothing, and IDLE adds
nothing. If the sheet described the history correctly before a command, it therefore
describes it correctly afterward. Following any finite list of commands proves the
promised answers. The WRITE-and-check runs above reach every state I included.
The lower count is attained.
   Try it with just one box. The workspace holds false or true, and CHECK asks
whether it is true. The whole monitor fits in three states: false, true-unchecked,
true-checked. WRITE false goes to the first. WRITE true goes to the second. CHECK
moves the second to the third and leaves the other two alone. IDLE leaves all three
alone. Start at false. WRITE true reaches true-unchecked, and then CHECK reaches
true-checked; all three states are reachable. Now take any word of instructions. A
WRITE makes the verdict false and starts a new sheet, even if it writes the same
value. A CHECK on false leaves the verdict false; a CHECK on either true state
makes or keeps the verdict true. IDLE preserves it. These are all the cases, and each
matches exactly what happened since the last WRITE. Applying that argument after
each instruction proves the promise for every finite word. In particular, the first
arrival at true-checked must use CHECK: neither WRITE nor IDLE can enter it from
another state.
172   21 From “What established means”


                 The two true states also show you the merger. CHECK sends true-unchecked
              and true-checked to the same next state, right above the same working value. A
              larger implementation can move that merger later: let CHECK enter a finite region
              of accepting states in the fibre and keep it there, and some two distinct states must
              share a CHECK successor. WRITE can still clear the current sheet. Permanence under
              that one check and permanence under every instruction are different promises.
              Call that finite accepting region H and the entering state u. The move sends all of
              H ∪ {u} into H. There are | H | + 1 distinct sources and only | H | possible targets,
              so two sources share a successor. The move preserves the working picture, so both
              sources and their common successor lie in that same fibre.

              Keep the old receipts when the page is wiped
              Now wipe the scratchpad and use it again. I still want the receipt for the first page.
              Then use it a third time. I want that receipt too. Think of carbon copies filed before
              each sheet goes in the bin. On the tabletop, you can be back where you started. In
              the file, you have a past you didn’t have before.
                 On each of n editions, give me the choice to certify it or leave it uncertified.
              Bring every choice back to the same final working picture b. Then ask the machine
              about any edition I name. It has to answer whether that edition was certified. Two
              histories that disagree on that answer need two different full states, however alike
              their tabletops look. There are 2n possible yes-or-no lists to distinguish.


              Theorem 21.2 (Room for independently retained editions). Under those realization
              and exact-query requirements, the fibre above b contains at least 2n reachable states.
              If one fixed host meets the requirements for every finite n at that same b, no finite
              set covers its reachable fibre.

              Proof. Two different lists of length n differ at an edition. Equal endpoints could
              not answer that edition’s query differently, so the endpoints are distinct. A putative
              finite cover of size K fails once 2n > K. Every endpoint being counted has the same
              working view b.
                 You can build the witness directly. Keep a Boolean workspace, the answers for
              the completed editions, and the current stage of CHECK, COMMIT and CERTIFY.
              On a true workspace, run the chain for an edition you choose to certify, or skip it for
              one you don’t. Then WRITE true to start the next edition. Every choice brings you
              back to the same true workspace and a fresh current stage. Open the file and the
              choices are still there, in exactly the places you made them.
                 Put the whole machine on the table. There is the current page, holding false
              or true. There is a marker beside it, with four positions: raw, checked, committed,
                                                                    21.1 The guy who got closest   173


published. And there is a numbered file of yes-or-no answers for pages already put
away. Start with a true page, the marker at raw, and an empty file.
    CHECK can move the marker from raw to checked, but only if the page says
true. COMMIT can move it from checked to committed. CERTIFY can move it from
committed to published. Press any of those buttons at another stage and it leaves the
arrangement alone. IDLE always leaves it alone. Those are all the ways to move the
marker without replacing the page, so there is no shortcut from raw to published.
    WRITE does three things, in this order. It files yes if the marker is at published
and no otherwise. It puts the requested value on the page. Then it returns the
marker to raw. The answer goes into the file before the marker is reset, and no
command alters an answer already filed.
    Check what that means after any command. CHECK, COMMIT and CERTIFY
move the current page through exactly the named stages; they do not touch earlier
pages’ answers. IDLE leaves both alone. WRITE closes the current page with the
answer its stage warrants, then opens a raw page. Start with no earlier pages and
follow those rules one command at a time: the file always answers the completed-
page questions correctly, and the marker gives the current page’s stage.
    To file a yes, run CHECK, COMMIT, CERTIFY, then WRITE true. To file a no, use
WRITE true alone. Either choice leaves a true page with the marker at raw, ready
for the next choice. After n choices the page and marker are identical in every run,
while the file holds exactly the n answers chosen. Every yes-or-no list is possible,
and different lists are different full states.
    Keep asking for another edition, with the same freedom of choice and the same
right to read back every answer. Whatever finite number of full states you offer
above b, a long enough history outruns it. The tabletop can keep coming back to
one picture. The machine that keeps all those choices cannot fit its reachable fibre
into a finite set. That is where the infinity comes from.
    The old yes-or-no gauge still has a job. Ask whether the file contains any
certification at all and it becomes a permanent lamp: once yes, always yes. Ask
whether today’s sheet is certified and wiping that sheet starts the answer over. The
small native machine in the next chapter has the permanent lamp. I will look behind
it at the whole native state, then count which publication histories even that larger
view leaves out. That camera sees more than the working value here; the next count
will name its fibre just as explicitly.


21.1 The guy who got closest

Somebody had that clue before me, and on that clue he’s the closest neighbour I’ve
found, so he gets the most space and the hardest push.
174   21 From “What established means”


                 Bennett again, the one from Part I’s counting of states and the demon, wrote a
              paper in 1988 that starts where I just ended up: information isn’t the same as value.
              His example is an almanac of where the moon and planets will be every day for
              a hundred years. It has no more information than the equations it was calculated
              from, but it saves you doing the calculating. So the value is the work. He called the
              amount of work a thing carries its depth.
                 Was this my idea, already done, by a guy at IBM? The only way to find out was
              to find where it parts from mine, because if I couldn’t find that place I’d just be
              renaming his idea. And it’s one word: plausibly. Depth is the amount of work a
              thing was plausibly made with. Bennett says straight out that you can’t know how a
              message actually came to be; for all you know it came from flipping coins. Depth is
              read off the finished thing, the way a fossil’s age is read off the fossil.
                 Bring the two computers back. The sorted list in the first one has some depth.
              The identical list in the second has exactly the same depth, because depth looks at
              the list and nothing else. A photocopy of a deep thing is just as deep. Depth tells
              you what work a plausible way of making the thing would take. It’s not a record
              that this machine did it, and it’s not a receipt for what doing it cost.
                 So Bennett’s standing right next to the line, on the other side of it, and as far as I
              can tell he got his own question right. He asked how much work a thing embodies.
              I’m asking who has the right to lean on it and what they paid to get there. His
              answer lives in the object. Mine, if I’ve got one, has to live in the machine.


              21.2 What putting it in the step buys

              The hardest objection is one I’d rather make myself. You defined a flag and a charge,
              then proved the charge gets paid when the flag goes up. That’s your definitions
              agreeing with each other.
                  On the bare model, that’s fair, and the copier’s receipt for nothing is the proof.
              What makes the record mean established is the small machine’s step, which won’t
              let a commit through unless a check of that very claim passed, meeting the first
              three of the four conditions from Gettier’s pocket, with the fourth, an honest checker,
              proved about the checker separately. And what it proves isn’t about one program:
              on every list of instructions anybody can write, starting clean, if the record is up,
              then somewhere earlier a check of some claim, about some edition, passed; then
              a commit of that same claim; then the certificate; with nothing touching the thing
              between the check and the commit.
                  So a raised flag, from a clean start, tells you some claim went through a passing
              check, a commitment to it about the same edition, and a certificate. That piece
              of history is what the flag keeps, and only that. The channel names the latest
              commitment that got through, which needn’t be the one the flag first went up on,
                                                          21.3 The record, the checker and the host   175


and in general you can’t read off the end state which claim was certified at that first
rise, or when. And the flag doesn’t say the claim still holds, at the certificate or at
the end, because the counter can change after the commitment.
    That “every” is what the operation-by-operation proof buys. A log written by one
program inherits that program’s correctness conditions. A database or reference
monitor can instead enforce rules over every operation its interface permits. The
small machine does that too: its invariant covers every string of its instructions from
a clean start, including hostile strings. The coverage comes from those restrictions
and their proof; it does not follow just from calling the record part of the step. And
the tie between a check and the thing it checked gets a job: the step reads it, and
refuses a commitment when it no longer fits, so the structure is doing something in
the machine instead of sitting in a note beside it.
    There are two limits on that “every,” and I’ll say them now so you don’t have
to find them later. First, it’s every program, not every everything. The guarantee
covers any list of instructions, run from a clean start, one step at a time, on a step
that does what its definition says. Hostile programs are inside it. A hostile start (a
table of facts nobody checked, a reset), hostile hardware (a soldering iron on the
flag’s bit, a second writer the step can’t see) or a hostile reader isn’t; Section 22
lists the whole fence. The guarantee has the same shape as a database or reference
monitor’s: it covers operations mediated by a faithful enforcing layer. How large that
class is depends on each interface; being a database does not make it a one-program
guarantee.
    Second, the flag and the ledger on their own are what people who verify programs
call ghost state: erase them and every program computes exactly what it did before
(Section 7 has where that idea comes from). What isn’t ghost on the small machine
is the table of checked facts, which the step reads and refuses on, at run time, for
every program. And the move of putting a rule under every program isn’t mine
either. It’s the reference monitor’s, and its history is a few sections back.


21.3 The record, the checker and the host
Take the flag and ledger off the small machine’s drawing. The core follows the same
steps: neither field tells it what to do next. Put them back and you can read whether
certification occurred and what the instruction schedule charged. That is the passive
record’s job. The latch argument in Section 11 gives its form over any fixed base
when the record is driven by that base and never comes down.
   The checker has another job. CHECK of “A ≥ m” tests the counter in one
instruction; a program using only the two counter moves would have to work
through their operations. A step comparison therefore starts with the instructions
each machine is given. Every checker built in this book is a program. The abstract
176   21 From “What established means”


              certified interface also admits a supplied checker, so its computational power includes
              whatever that checker can do: supply a halting oracle and CHECK can answer halting
              questions. The effective implementations here use effective checks.
                  Now put a guest program inside the host. A guest that can write any value into
              its own “checked” variable can write YES without checking. The host I build gives
              the guest three entry points instead: CHECK, COMMIT and CERTIFY. Its step rule
              controls those entry points for every allowed guest program. The fact table and
              channel determine whether the next act is permitted; the flag and ledger record the
              result. An ordinary universal machine can implement these rules. The work is in
              preserving them through every guest instruction.
                  The universal program in Appendix C.1 does that work with the small machine’s
              six instruction kinds on a host with more counters. It fetches a guest instruction,
              decodes it, and carries it out. At matching points the guest and host ledgers agree.
              The host halts exactly when the guest halts, keeps the guest’s answer in its counters,
              and raises its own flag exactly when the guest raises its flag, through its own earned
              chain. Its guests are the small machine’s programs from clean starts, with Minsky’s
              encoding supplying their universal computation. Appendix 29.1 gives a simpler
              picture with a guest-step operation supplied directly; the instruction-by-instruction
              construction shows how to build it.
                  Add a one-unit PAY instruction and the host can also follow a computably pre-
              sented Thiele machine: its selected moves, transitions, prices and total yes-or-no
              reading are all given by programs. Appendix C.2 constructs that interpreter. Starting
              with the guest’s reading at no, the host follows its ledger with at most two extra units
              at the raise. Starting with the guest already at yes, the clean host must establish
              its own flag immediately and spends three. The same clean-start three-act count
              explains the surcharge when the guest certifies with a whole ledger below three.
CHAPTER 22




From “The earned-chain counter
example”

22.1 Where the counters come from

The base first, and the base isn’t mine. It’s Marvin Minsky’s. In a 1961 paper in the
Annals of Mathematics, and then in his 1967 book Computation: Finite and Infinite
Machines, Minsky studied a machine so bare it’s almost a joke. Picture a few bins of
pebbles and a numbered list of instructions. There are only two kinds of instruction:
put a pebble in bin c and go to the next line; or take a pebble out of bin c if there’s
one to take, and go one way if there was and another way if the bin was empty.
There’s nothing else: no memory but the bins, no tape, no arithmetic beyond plus
one and minus one. When the line you’re on has no instruction, the machine stops.
    What Minsky proved is that two bins are enough to compute anything a Turing
machine can compute, as long as you’re willing to pack the input in a strange way.
The trick is to keep many numbers inside one number, as the powers of different
primes (2x 3y 5z holds three numbers in one), and to do arithmetic on the exponents
by multiplying and dividing with the second bin as scratch space. Packing numbers
that way can turn a modest input into an enormous pile of pebbles, handled one at
a time, so it’s no way to build a fast machine. It’s a way to settle a question: with
that packing, the two bins can do every Turing computation. What the bins give me
here is a base whose ordinary moves you can see all of. Each of the earlier results
says which properties of a base it needs, and Appendix 30 carries the earned layer
over to other bases once you’ve named what they have to supply.
    Who cares? Minsky did, because the question then was where the edge of
computation sits and how little machinery reaches it. You should, because it means
the base of this chapter’s machine hides nothing: if it can compute everything with
plus one, minus one and a jump, then nothing about the record can be smuggling
computing power in through some instruction I made fancy. For this construction
I use Minsky’s two-counter halting result and the counter constructions of Forster,
Larchey-Wendling and their coauthors. Appendix 6 sets out the reduction; the
step-for-step connection to this machine is the part I have to establish.

                                                 177
178   22 From “The earned-chain counter example”


                  And the pebbles aren’t only my picture. They were somebody’s actual machine,
              the same year. In one issue of the Canadian Mathematical Bulletin, September 1961,
              Z. A. Melzak, a mathematician at the University of British Columbia, described a
              computer made of distinguishable holes and an unlimited supply of pebbles, with
              one three-place operation, and a few pages later Joachim Lambek, a mathematician
              at McGill University, trimmed it to two moves: put a pebble in hole X; take one
              out of hole X if there’s one to take. He called it an infinite abacus, and early on
              he writes “Our method is probably not new,” which is a hard sentence to beat. He
              also tells anybody who distrusts infinity not to worry about all those holes, since
              any one computation only ever uses finitely many of them. Is that two inventions
              or one? Lambek says his was a modification of Melzak’s, so call it one and a half,
              with Minsky’s as a separate find the same year. And then Lambek went somewhere
              you wouldn’t guess: in 1986, with Philip Scott, a logician who had come to McGill
              to work with him and later taught at the University of Ottawa, he wrote a book
              whose first part, as the publisher describes it, shows that typed lambda calculi and
              a certain kind of category are essentially the same thing. He went from pebbles to
              category theory in one career, twenty-five years end to end. Does any of it change
              the argument? No. The machine uses Minsky’s version and Minsky’s name. But a
              bare machine that several people reach for in the same year looks like a shape that
              was sitting there. It doesn’t look much like anybody’s invention.
                  The title of Minsky’s 1961 paper is the clue I can’t leave alone. It’s about “Post’s
              problem of tag.” Who’s Post, and what’s tag? Emil Post was a logician, a fresh PhD
              from Columbia, who in 1921, on a fellowship at Princeton, was playing with about
              the simplest rewriting game there is. Take a word of 0s and 1s. Look at its first
              letter, chop the first three letters off the front, and stick something on the end: 00
              if the letter you looked at was 0, 1101 if it was 1. Repeat, and stop when fewer
              than three letters are left. Try it at the table. Start from 1000: you get 01101, then
              0100, then 000, then 00, and it stops. Start from 100: you get 1101, 11101, 011101,
              10100, 001101, and then 10100 again, and it goes round forever. Post wanted a rule
              that tells you, for every starting word, which of those happens. He worked at it for
              months and later called it hopeless, and as far as I can tell it still is: a hundred years
              on, nobody knows whether that one little game, with that one rule, can be decided.
              Stephen Wolfram, the physicist who built Mathematica, wrote a 2021 article on
              it that tells the Princeton story, and it’s where I’d start. In his 1961 paper Minsky
              proved that tag games in general can compute anything, which is why Post’s name is
              on it. It didn’t settle Post’s own game. And the chase goes one step further. In 2008
              Liesbeth De Mol, a historian and philosopher of computing who wrote her doctoral
              work on tag systems, turned the famous 3n + 1 problem (halve an even number,
              triple an odd one and add one, and ask whether you always get down to 1) into a
              small tag game of the same kind. So is Post’s game secretly as hard as everything, or
                                                                        22.2 Nothing gets forged   179


just stubborn? I can’t tell you, and as far as the published work goes, nobody can
yet.
   None of that changes the machine below. It changes the picture. The two-counter
machine came out of a hunt for the smallest thing that can’t be predicted, and the
smallest things are exactly where the open questions are still sitting.
   There’s one word clash to settle before it bites. Minsky’s machine is usually called
a counter machine, and its bins are counters. Section 7 also had a counter: the ledger
µ, the number that only climbs. It’s the same word for two completely different
things, and I’d love to say I set that up on purpose. In this chapter, counter means
one of Minsky’s two bins, called A and B, and the climbing total is always the ledger
µ.


22.2 Nothing gets forged

The theorem and its proof are in the book; this is what stood beside them.
   The closest earlier result is in capability hardware, and it’s much bigger. The
computer scientists Jack Dennis and Earl Van Horn, at MIT, introduced capabilities in
1966: tokens a process has to hold to touch an object, which it can pass on and can’t
forge. CHERI, a capability extension of real instruction sets built at the University
of Cambridge and SRI International, rests on that idea, and in 2020 Kyndylan
Nienhuis and twelve coauthors proved, for the whole CHERI-MIPS instruction set,
that arbitrary code given some capabilities can’t increase them as it runs (up to any
change of protection domain). Their result says nothing gets forged across a full
instruction set. Theorem 15.14 is the same shape, one instruction at a time, for six
instruction kinds and a table of checked facts.

The actual counter covers every added fact
Put the table beside the bill. Every passing CHECK writes one more entry and adds
one to the ledger. A failed CHECK writes no entry. COMMIT and CERTIFY can add
to the bill without adding a table entry. Walk down the run one instruction at a time:
the table can never grow faster than the bill.


Theorem 22.1 (The native bill bounds the table). For every clean run, whether
driven by a finite instruction word or by the stored-program step for a finite number
of ticks,
                                   len(facts) ≤ µ.

More generally, table growth is exactly the number of passing CHECKs, and is at
most the increase in the ledger from any starting state.
180   22 From “The earned-chain counter example”


              Proof. A passing CHECK adds one entry and one unit to the bill. A false CHECK,
              a full-table CHECK or a trapped CHECK adds no entry but still adds one unit.
              COMMIT and CERTIFY add one unit and no entry. INC, DEC and HALT add neither.
              No instruction removes an entry.
                  Place a mark beside each passing CHECK in the run. The final table has exactly
              one more entry for every mark. Each mark also accounts for one unit on the bill;
              unmarked instructions can add further units, never take them away. So table growth
              is exactly the number of marks and at most the ledger’s increase. This works from
              any starting state.
                  At a clean start the table is empty. The initial ledger is a nonnegative whole
              number, so the table length is at most the final ledger. At the specified loaded starts
              the ledger begins at zero as well. For a stored program, every running tick executes
              one of the six instructions just counted, and every stopped tick leaves both table and
              bill alone. The same count therefore applies to any finite number of ticks.
                   One passing CHECK from a clean start gives one entry and a bill of one. Repeat
               a passing check and its duplicate entry counts as another line in the table. The
               tally follows what was written there. COMMIT and CERTIFY can then make the bill
               larger while the number of lines stays put.


               22.3 A full table traps
               The table has room for sixteen facts and evicts none of them, so there are two halves
               to prove: nothing ever leaves, and nothing ever overflows.


               Theorem 22.2 (Bounded table, no eviction). Every instruction keeps every fact
               already in the table. If the table holds 16 or more facts, CHECK p c puts the trap
               up and leaves the table as it was. If the table holds at most 16 facts, it still holds at
               most 16 after any instruction.

              Proof. With a raised trap no instruction changes the table. With the trap down, only
              CHECK can change it, and a successful CHECK adds one fact without removing an
              entry. Success requires a previous length strictly below 16, so the new length is at
              most 16. At length 16 or greater that branch is unavailable: CHECK raises the trap
              and preserves the table. Every other instruction preserves it as well.
                 Why trap? Why not make room? Because “making room” means deciding which
              fact dies, and a fact that can die is a fact whose absence you have to explain later.
              A table that only grows keeps every checked fact available to read back, and the
              shared-memory result above leans on exactly that, since its oldest fact can never
                                                                 22.4 What it takes to be certified   181


move. Provenance doesn’t: a table that dropped its oldest fact to make room would
still hold only facts a CHECK wrote. What the choice gives up is real, though, and
it’s in the subsection near the end of this chapter on what the machine doesn’t do.


22.4 What it takes to be certified
Section 17.2 set the bar worth meeting, Thiele-complete, and it says nothing about
what the record is about. A clock fails it, and so does a machine whose record never
goes up. The small machine clears it (Theorems 22.11 and 22.3(2) below), and it
does more: from a clean start, its flag goes up only on top of a check of a claim, by a
checker proved to mean what the claim says, then a commitment to the same claim
with nothing changed underneath, then a certificate. A Thiele-complete machine
can raise its record with one mark that goes up from any state at all, a receipt for
nothing (Section 14). A certified one can’t. The second definition is the first one
with three things added, and it’s written so that any machine can be held up against
it. You name a language of claims first, then hand over a reading of the machine in
that language, a list of things, and then the reading has to pass four clauses.

Definition 22.1 (Certified Thiele-complete). A machine has states, moves, a step, a
cost for each move and a yes/no reading of each state, the record. A claim language
for it is fixed first: a type of claims with an exact equality test; a meaning for
each claim, a statement about a state; for each claim, a relation “the thing this
claim is about is unchanged” between two states; and a proof that a meaning that
holds keeps holding while the thing it’s about is unchanged. An interface over that
language hands over the rest: a universal base, meaning a two-counter configuration
shown by each state (its window), a class of live states, a move for each two-counter
instruction, and a loaded state for each start (1, a, b); a checker that says yes or no
to a claim at a state, with a proof that a yes means the claim’s meaning, as the
language gives it, holds of that state, with a CHECK of a claim passing at a state only
when the checker says yes to it there; a set of clean states; a ledger, a whole number
for each state; and a kind for each move: a base move, CHECK of a claim, COMMIT
of a claim, or CERTIFY. The machine is certified Thiele-complete over the language
when some interface over it meets four clauses, and certified Thiele-complete when
some interface, with claims, meanings and “unchanged” of its own, meets them. (a)
Universal base. Each two-counter instruction’s move is a base move; from every live
state it acts on the window exactly as the instruction acts on a configuration, and the
state stays live; each loaded state shows (1, a, b), is live and is clean; no base move
changes the record; and a record that’s up stays up under every move. (b) Earned
record. Clean states have the record down. Every run from a clean state that ends
with the record up contains a CHECK of some claim c that passes, then a COMMIT
182   22 From “The earned-chain counter example”


              of c with the thing c is about unchanged at every state in between, then a CERTIFY,
              with the record down just before that CERTIFY and up just after it. A passing check
              means its claim’s meaning holds of the state it checked, and a meaning that holds
              keeps holding while the thing it’s about is unchanged. (c) Exact prices. Base moves
              cost 0, each CHECK, COMMIT and CERTIFY costs exactly 1, and each move adds its
              cost to the ledger. (d) Non-vacuity. Some claim c has a CHECK, a COMMIT and a
              CERTIFY such that those three moves alone, from a loaded state, raise the record
              exactly when c’s meaning holds there; and c holds at some loaded state and fails
              at another. A Thiele machine has a universal base when some interface gives it one
              and nothing more is asked: each two-counter instruction’s move acts on the window
              exactly as the instruction does from every live state and keeps it live, and each start
              (1, a, b) has a live loaded state showing it. Nothing is asked about the record, the
              prices or the checker.

                  An interface over a language becomes an interface of the plain kind by taking
               the language’s claims, meanings and “unchanged” as its own, and the four clauses
               are asked of that.


               Theorem 22.3 (Certified is Thiele-complete plus certification). (1) A machine is
               certified Thiele-complete through an interface exactly when it is Thiele-complete
               through the same interface read without its claims (its base moves as the computing
               moves, CHECK, COMMIT and CERTIFY as the marks, the same universal base and
               ledger), and three more things hold: its loaded states are clean; clause (b) holds, so
               clean states have the record down, every run from a clean state that ends with the
               record up contains the earned chain, a passing check means its claim holds, and a
               claim that holds keeps holding while what it’s about is unchanged; and clause (d)
               holds, a claim whose three moves raise the record from a loaded state exactly when
               it holds there, true at one loaded state and false at another. (2) Every certified Thiele-
               complete machine is Thiele-complete. (3) Not the other way: the two counters with
               two marks, one that raises the record when A is 0 and one that always raises it,
               and a ledger beside them that each move adds its cost to, is Thiele-complete, and
               certified Thiele-complete through no interface.

                   So certification is exactly three things on top of the bar: clean starts, an earned
               record (the chain, a checker proved sound, claims that stay true while what they’re
               about is unchanged), and a claim that could have come out either way. Everything
               Section 17.2 proved holds of every certified machine. What this section adds is what
               those three things buy, from a clean start: a floor of three instead of one, a raise that
               is always a CERTIFY, and no receipt without a check; and, from the loaded states, a
               check that can fail.
                                                                 22.4 What it takes to be certified   183


    The language comes first for a reason. If the interface could name the meanings,
it could take “the checker says yes” as the meaning of every claim, and checker
soundness would hold for nothing. In the language-first form the meanings are
written down before any checker is handed over, so “a checker proved to mean what
it says” has the content it has on the small machine: what the claim says is the
language’s, and the proof is that a yes from the checker makes it true. The theorems
that build certified Thiele-complete machines ask the checker for one thing more,
that it say yes exactly when the claim holds, because clause (d) needs a check that
can come out yes. The plain form stays because the theorems that follow hold for
any interface at all, so they hold over every language too.
    Read the four clauses as four plain sentences. (a) It computes anything a Turing
machine can, under Minsky’s 2n encoding, done outside the machine, and the
computing can’t reach the record: the base moves are the two-counter machine, they
leave the record exactly where it was, and a record that goes up stays up. (b) From
a clean start, the record goes up only on top of the small machine’s chain: a check
that passed, a commitment to the same claim with nothing changed underneath,
then a certificate. (c) Computing costs nothing on the ledger and each of the three
acts costs one unit, so the ledger counts the acts. The steps in between add nothing
to it. (The unit belongs to the ledger; it isn’t a unit of heat.) (d) Somewhere there’s
a claim whose check could have said no. A machine doesn’t qualify over a language
whose every claim is “at least 0”, and neither does one with nothing to certify.
    And look where the checker’s honesty sits. In (b), “a passing check means its
claim holds” is a condition on the interface, proved once, outside every run, exactly
where it sat for the small machine (Lemma 15.2), and over a claim language it’s
proved against meanings the interface didn’t write. The definition doesn’t ask the
machine to vouch for its own checker. The nearest reasons not to ask are Gödel’s
second theorem, about a system proving its own consistency, and the diagonal of
Section 13, about deciding a property of every program. Neither forbids a machine
checking a proof about a checker, so keeping that proof outside is a choice. Nor does
it ask the checker to be something a machine could run. Clause (b) wants a yes or no
for every claim at every state, sound for the claim’s meaning, and nothing in it says
that answer has to be computable. Every instance in this book has a checker that’s an
actual program, so anything those machines certify could be listed by a program that
runs them. For example, take a function that answers whether an arbitrary program
runs forever. No program computes it (that’s Turing’s halting theorem). Hand the
definition a checker built on it and it’s still met, and the machine certifies claims
no program could ever confirm. Nothing in this book builds one. So “a certified
Thiele-complete machine certifies exactly the claims a program could list” is true
only with “and its step and its checker are computable” added, and the definition
doesn’t require either.
184   22 From “The earned-chain counter example”


                  The same scope goes on Section 14’s “it isn’t more computing power.” The record
              (the flag and the ledger) is never read by the step that computes the next core,
              so erasing it changes no run, and it makes nothing faster. The checker is part of
              the machine under the record. With an oracle checker that machine decides what
              the oracle decides, since whether a CHECK traps is there for anyone to see; with a
              checker that’s a program, it does what programs do. And a checker that’s a program
              still makes CHECK a test at unit cost.


              Theorem 22.4 (CHECK is a unit-cost test). From any start ( a, b), the single move
              CHECK Ge(m) A costs 1, and the trap latch is down after it exactly when a ≥ m.
                  The two counters alone look at A only through the zero test of DEC, and started
              one pebble below a threshold or right at it, that test can’t come out differently until
              the pile has been counted down to zero, so they need about as many steps as the
              threshold for what CHECK does in one. Theorem 22.21 gives the precise lower
              bound and the matching ladder. So the record makes nothing faster, and CHECK, a
              primitive test, can.
                  Three kinds of exact. The word exact does three jobs in this book, and each job
              keeps one name. Rise-only pricing charges at least one unit on every step that raises
              the reading and nothing on any other step; its tightest form, one unit per raise and
              nothing else, is the exact schedule. Instruction-exact pricing charges every instruction
              a fixed listed price. On this machine that’s one unit each for CHECK, COMMIT and
              CERTIFY and nothing for the rest, and the definition of certified Thiele-complete
              calls it exact prices, not a toll, because it charges acts that raise nothing. CHECK
              and COMMIT raise no flag, so the small machine is instruction-exact without being
              rise-only, and no rule is broken by that. What the three units pay for isn’t one thing
              three times. A passing CHECK adds a fact to a table that holds sixteen, so it takes
              a fresh state and fills one slot of a bounded store; a passing COMMIT overwrites
              the channel and a passing CERTIFY raises the flag, each sending two states with
              the same base to one (Theorem 3.19). And each of the three, when it fails, sends
              the failed state and its twin with the trap already up to one state. So a passing
              CHECK pays by taking a fresh state, and every one of the three units is also a price
              on a merge. Simulation-exact accounting is a host whose ledger equals its guest’s
              ledger at every point where the two runs match, as the universal program U’s does
              (Appendix C.1). A machine can meet any one of the three and fail the others.
                  One word needs pinning before the theorems, using those three names. In clause
              (c), exact prices means instruction-exact pricing: each of the three acts, CHECK and
              COMMIT included, costs exactly one, so the ledger counts the moves filed as record
              moves. Those are the acts that make a claim, and also a check that fails, a COMMIT
                                                                  22.4 What it takes to be certified   185


of a fact already committed, and a CERTIFY with the flag already up: each is filed as
a record move and costs one. Back in Section 7, one result is about rise-only pricing
in its tightest form, the exact schedule: one unit on the step that raises the flag and
nothing on any other step. Measured against the flag alone, the small machine isn’t
rise-only, since CHECK and COMMIT raise no flag and cost one each; measured
the way (c) asks, it is instruction-exact. Neither is wrong. They answer different
questions: does the ledger count the acts, or does it charge the flag and only the
flag? Simulation-exact accounting, the third name, comes up with the universal
program U in Appendix C.1.



Theorem 22.5 (What every certified Thiele-complete machine has). Let a machine
be certified Thiele-complete through an interface. (1) It obeys the toll and has a
universal base; its base runs every two-counter program step for step from every
live state, and halting from a loaded state matches halting from the configuration
it shows. (2) Along any run, the ledger rises by exactly the number of CHECK,
COMMIT and CERTIFY moves in it. (3) A run from a clean state that ends with the
record up contains at least three of those moves and raises the ledger by at least 3,
and the three moves of clause (d), from a loaded state where its claim holds, raise it
by exactly 3. (4) Some CHECK fails at some loaded state, on a claim that is false
there, and some run from some loaded state raises the record. (5) On a run from a
clean state, the move that raises the record is a CERTIFY. (6) Every move costs 0 or
1, and some move costs 0.



Theorem 22.6 (The language first, and what a certificate then means). (1) A
machine that is certified Thiele-complete over some claim language is certified
Thiele-complete, so every part of Theorem 22.5 holds of it. (2) If a machine is
certified Thiele-complete through an interface whose claims have an exact equality
test, it is certified Thiele-complete over the language made of that interface’s claims,
meanings and “unchanged,” with clause (b)’s proof that a meaning keeps holding
as the language’s own. (3) If a machine is certified Thiele-complete over a claim
language L, some interface over L meets the four clauses and has this property:
every run from one of its clean states that ends with the record up contains a CHECK
of a claim c of L that passed, then a COMMIT of c, and L’s meaning of c holds at the
state the CHECK saw and at the state the COMMIT saw.

   Part (2) says where the strength of the language-first form lives: in the naming.
Any reading can be turned into a language when its claims come with an exact
equality test, so for those “certified Thiele-complete over some language” asks no
186   22 From “The earned-chain counter example”


              more than certified Thiele-complete does. “Certified Thiele-complete over L,” for an
              L written down without looking at the checker, says that what the machine certifies
              is what L says. Every instance below is stated that way.
                 Each item in the proof names the clause it comes from, and that’s all the definition
              gives. A certified Thiele-complete machine needn’t have a fact table, a CHECK that
              traps when it fails, a COMMIT that looks anything up, or claims about counters.
              The definition asks for an earned chain on the runs that raise the record and leaves
              the rest to the machine. So the small machine’s table provenance, its trap, and its
              channel are facts about that machine, and they don’t carry over to every machine
              that meets the definition. Through a plain interface its claims can be any labels
              the interface names; over a claim language they are the language’s. Either way its
              checker can be an oracle, as the halting-function checker above shows.
                 The second half of (6) in Theorem 22.5, that some move costs 0, is the one
              that rules out the clock: a machine that charges every move. It doesn’t rule out a
              program that makes the ledger tick. On the host of Appendix C.1, a program that
              checks one fact once and then commits it again before every instruction runs any
              two-counter program with the flag down and pays one unit a step, plus one for the
              check: the checked fact is about a counter the program never writes, so its edition
              never changes, and every COMMIT finds it.
              Observation 22.7 (A clock is a Thiele machine and isn’t certified Thiele-complete).
              Take the two-counter machine, let every move cost 1, and take as the record any yes/no
              question about its configuration whatsoever; or take a flag that switches on, and stays
              on, the first time any chosen test of the configuration says yes. Either way the machine
              obeys the toll and has a two-counter machine running inside it, and it is not certified
              Thiele-complete. More generally, no machine whose every move costs at least 1 is
              certified Thiele-complete.
                 (It also follows from Observation 17.3 and Theorem 22.3(2).) The proof is short,
              and it’s short because clause (c) does exactly the job it was written for: it says
              computing costs nothing, and a clock charges for computing. The other clauses do
              work too, and that’s worth checking, because a definition with a clause nothing
              needs is a definition hiding something.


              Theorem 22.8 (Each clause does work). (1) Take a two-counter base priced 0
              with a ledger beside it, and one extra move TICK that costs 1 and raises a flag. It
              obeys the toll, and read with TICK as CERTIFY it meets the universal base clause
              and the exact prices clause; it is not certified Thiele-complete, and more generally
              no machine with a move that puts the record up from every state is. (2) Take a
              two-counter base priced 0 and a record that is down at every state, so it never rises.
                                                                   22.4 What it takes to be certified   187


It obeys the toll and meets clauses (a), (b) and (c); it is not certified Thiele-complete,
and more generally no machine whose record never rises is.
    The paid latch, the TICK machine of (1), is the receipt for nothing from Section 14,
wearing a universal base: honest about the price, silent about what was bought. The
definition throws it out.
    The silent machine of (2) meets three clauses and fails only (d). The paid latch
fails (d) as well as (b), so it doesn’t show (b) doing work alone, and nothing above
says whether a clock meets (a), (b) and (d). The sharper check, the usual evidence
that a definition isn’t written around one machine, is a machine for each clause that
meets the other three and fails it.


Theorem 22.9 (The four clauses are independent). For each clause of Defini-
tion 22.1 there is a machine with an interface that meets the other three clauses
and fails that one. Each of the four machines obeys the toll and has a two-counter
machine running inside it, and none of them is certified Thiele-complete through
any interface at all. (a) fails for this chapter’s machine with one more move, DROP,
priced 0 and read as a base move, which lowers the flag and trips the trap latch.
(b) fails for this chapter’s machine with one more move, FORGE, costing one and
read as a CERTIFY, which raises the flag on any state whose trap is down, with no
commitment behind it. (c) fails for this chapter’s machine with every move costing
one. (d) fails for the silent machine of Theorem 22.8(2).

   Three of the four are this chapter’s machine with one thing changed, and in each
case the one clause it fails is the only thing keeping it out. Clause (c) also fixes
something it has no business fixing: the size of the unit. Double every price on the
small machine and it leaves the class, because its CHECK now costs two and (c) asks
for one. A definition that a change of currency breaks would be a strange one, so
the next theorem states (c) with the unit left open.


Theorem 22.10 (Any unit). Call a machine certified Thiele-complete at unit c, for
a whole number c ≥ 1, when some interface meets clauses (a), (b) and (d) and
this form of (c): base moves cost 0, each CHECK, COMMIT and CERTIFY costs
exactly c, and each move adds its cost to the ledger. (1) At unit 1 that is certified
Thiele-complete. (2) Multiply every price of a certified Thiele-complete machine by
c ≥ 1 and the machine is certified Thiele-complete at unit c; divide every price of
a machine certified Thiele-complete at unit c by c and the machine, with the same
states, moves, step and record, is certified Thiele-complete, with the ledger read
as ⌊ℓ/c⌋. (3) At every unit some move costs 0, so the clock of Observation 22.7,
188   22 From “The earned-chain counter example”


              with any reading or any latch as its record, is certified Thiele-complete at no unit,
              and neither is any machine whose every move costs at least 1. (4) At unit c, a run
              from a clean state that ends with the record up contains a CHECK, a COMMIT and
              a CERTIFY and raises the ledger by at least 3c; the claim it stands on held when it
              was checked and still held when it was committed; and the move that raises the
              record is a CERTIFY. (5) The small machine with every price doubled is certified
              Thiele-complete at unit 2 and isn’t certified Thiele-complete.

                  So the class doesn’t care what a unit is worth. It cares that computing costs
               nothing and that the three acts cost the same. The clock fails at every unit for the
               reason it failed at one: it charges for computing. I keep unit 1 in the definition
               because the small machine is built that way and every count in this chapter is in its
               units.
                  Now to the other direction, because a definition nobody meets is as useless as
               one everybody meets.


               Theorem 22.11 (The small machine, the generic one and the sorted one are certified
               Thiele-complete). (1) This chapter’s machine is certified Thiele-complete, read with
               INC, DEC and HALT as base moves, CHECK p c and COMMIT p c as record moves on
               the claim “p holds of c,” CERTIFY as CERTIFY, the checker as eval on the value of c,
               the window as (line, A, B), live states as those with the trap down, start( a, b) as the
               loaded state for (1, a, b), “unchanged” as “same value and same version of c,” clean
               states as in Definition 15.2 and the ledger as µ. (2) So is the same machine over
               any property language with an exact equality test and a checker proved to say yes
               exactly when the claim holds, as long as some property is true of one number and
               false of another. (3) In particular the machine whose property is “this counter, read
               as a list, is sorted” is certified Thiele-complete. (4) Every certified Thiele-complete
               machine is a Thiele machine (Definition 2.2) and obeys the universal floor; for this
               chapter’s machine, that system agrees step for step and cost for cost with the one
               of Theorem 15.7, and for the generic machine with the generic machine’s own. (5)
               Each of the three is certified Thiele-complete over its own claim language, fixed
               before the checker. For this chapter’s machine the language’s claims are pairs of
               a property and a counter, equal when both parts are, the meaning of “p holds of
               c” is the meaning holds of Definition 15.1 applied to c’s value, written without the
               checker, and “unchanged” is “same value and same version of c.” For the generic
               machine the language is built from the properties, their equality test and what they
               mean, and the checker isn’t part of it; the sorted machine is that case.
                  Appendix 30 carries the same fixed-language form to other bases. The claims
               keep their stated meanings; the new interfaces supply the checkers.
                                                     22.5 Parts I and II, run on the example machine   189


    There’s one thing every machine that meets the definition can’t help doing. It
has Section 8’s shape again, only this time it isn’t about one machine but about all
of them.
Observation 22.12 (Every certified Thiele-complete machine hides its record and
its ledger from its window). Let a machine be certified Thiele-complete through an
interface, and let its window be the two-counter configuration its universal base shows.
(1) For every state s there’s a loaded state and one base move priced 0 from it that lands
on a state with s’s window, with the record down and the ledger unchanged. (2) There’s
a loaded state and two runs from it that end with the same window, one with the record
up and one with it down; and likewise two runs from one loaded state that end with the
same window and ledgers at least 3 apart. (3) So no function of the window gives the
record at the end of every run from every loaded state, and no function of the window
gives the ledger. (4) The same holds of the Thiele machine built from the machine.
    The observer in that theorem sees one thing, the final window. An observer who
sees the window before and after each step, or the whole trace of windows, or who
is also handed the program and the start, is a different observer, and the collision
says nothing about them; given the program and the start, the run is fixed and so is
its record. The theorem’s two runs also start from the same loaded state, so they’re
reachable witnesses, which is stronger than two arbitrary states that happen to look
alike. And “no function of the window gives the record” is all it says. It doesn’t say
the window tells you nothing about it.
    Notice how little that took, and say it out loud so nobody mistakes it for more.
It’s the fibre fact from Section 8 plus one observation: a universal base can print
any window at no cost, and that is the point. The window here is everything a
Turing machine would call the machine, the line and the counters. On a certified
Thiele-complete machine, that’s never enough to tell you what was established or
what it cost, and it can’t be, because computing costs nothing and can’t touch the
record. A richer window, one that shows the flag, is a different window, and nothing
above says it fails.


22.5 Parts I and II, run on the example machine
Parts I and II proved their counts and their collisions for any system with the named
pieces, and they left a promise that the machine of this part would be put through
them, and this section is where it’s kept. The eight-state machine of Section 3 gets
run by the small machine, price for price. Structural entitlement, the count of what
narrowing a list of rivals costs, is proved for every certified Thiele-complete machine,
with the small machine as a worked member, a larger search on the host that has a
counter for every number, and a run that shows the count’s one awkward hypothesis,
the covering, can’t be dropped. And the time a check saves gets an exact exchange
190   22 From “The earned-chain counter example”


              rate, for the part of the machine that has no record. (The no-bare-verifier result is in
              Section 8, next to the collision it comes from.)
                   The eight-state machine, run by the small machine. Look at the small machine
              through a window that shows two things: its line number read modulo four, which
              I’ll call its slot (the eight-state machine’s word from Section 3, unrelated to the slot
              registers of the universal program U in Appendix C.1), and its flag. Call a state live
              here, a narrower use of the word than in the definition of certified Thiele-complete,
              when its trap latch is down, the fact “A ≥ 0” about A’s current version is in its table,
              and its channel holds a commitment, which is what CERTIFY needs in order to run.

              Definition 22.2 (The eight-state machine, compiled). Stamp is the single instruction
              CERTIFY. Next-slot is the single instruction INC B. Jump to slot a, for a one of
              0, 1, 2, 3, is the four instructions COMMIT (A ≥ 0) A; COMMIT (A ≥ 0) A; INC B;
              DEC B a. The setup is the two instructions CHECK (A ≥ 0) A; COMMIT (A ≥ 0) A.

                 The two COMMITs in a jump change nothing but the line and the channel, and
              each costs one, which is where the jump’s price of two comes from. The INC makes
              B positive, so the DEC takes one off and jumps to line a at no cost, and B is back
              where it was.


              Theorem 22.13 (The small machine runs the eight-state machine). (1) For every
              live state s and every move m of the eight-state machine, running m’s instructions
              from s ends in a live state whose window is the eight-state machine’s step of s’s
              window by m, and the ledger has risen by exactly m’s charge: one for stamp, two
              for jump, zero for next-slot. (2) The same holds for any list of moves: the window
              after the compiled list is the eight-state machine’s run of the list, the state is live,
              and the ledger has risen by the sum of the charges. (3) So a run of compiled moves
              from a live state that takes the flag from down to up has raised the ledger by at least
              one. (4) From any start ( a, b), the setup ends in a live state with window (slot 3,
              flag down) and the ledger at 2; after it, any compiled list runs as on the eight-state
              machine from (slot 3, flag down), with the ledger at 2 plus the sum of the charges;
              and if the flag is up at the end, the whole run, setup included, contains the earned
              chain of Theorem 15.11.

                 There are three reservations, and I’ll say them now. The setup’s claim is true
              of every value, so its CHECK can’t fail; it’s there because the small machine won’t
              CERTIFY without a checked, committed claim, and not as a test. And the jump’s
              price of two is two paid COMMITs, a choice a program makes that the machine
              allows and doesn’t force. The window is a projection, too: the small machine’s full
              state keeps far more than eight states’ worth.
                                                     22.5 Parts I and II, run on the example machine   191


   Structural entitlement, for every certified Thiele-complete machine. The-
orem 9.1 was about any Thiele machine, and what the run paid went in as a
hypothesis. For a certified Thiele-complete machine (Definition 22.1) what the run
pays is known. Call the CHECK, COMMIT and CERTIFY moves of a run its record
moves; each costs exactly one, so the ledger rises by the number of record moves,
and clause (b) says what a raised record must contain.


Theorem 22.14 (Structural entitlement represented). Let a machine be certified
Thiele-complete through an interface. Take a clean start s0 and a run t from it; a type
of rivals and a type of observations with an exact equality test; an observation obs of
each rival; a decoder d from runs to observations; lists Ω, Ω′ of rivals and a decision
tree T. Suppose (M1) Ω′ strictly narrows Ω, as in Theorem 9.1, and (M2) some
dropped rival shows an observation different from every survivor’s; (M3) the run
ends with the record up and the decoded run d(t) is shown by some survivor; (M4)
depth( T ) is at most the number of record moves in t; (M5) |Ω′ | > 0; and (M6) the
covering: |Ω| ≤ L( T ) · |Ω′ |. Then (1) the membership test of Ω′ is strictly stronger
than that of Ω; (2) t contains the earned chain of clause (b), with the meaning of
the claim true at the CHECK and at the COMMIT; (3) ⌈log2 |Ω|⌉ −̇ ⌈log2 |Ω′ |⌉ is
at most the number of record moves of t; (4) the number of record moves of t is
exactly the ledger’s rise; and (5) the ledger rose by at least 3.
   It’s a checklist, as I said in Section 9, and the small print is the same kind. (M3)’s
second half feeds no conclusion: only the record’s being up is used. (M1) is used only
for the inclusion; its gap feeds nothing, since (M2) already hands over a dropped
rival. And (M5) is used but can go: (M2) puts a rival in the prior, and the covering
then forces a survivor. What the machine adds to Theorem 9.1 is conclusions (2),
(4) and (5): the payment is the machine’s own three acts, each at exactly one, and a
raised record carries the earned chain.
   There are two more, one where the tree comes from the run’s own questions and
one about what the record adds.


Theorem 22.15 (A floor with no supplied tree). Take a machine and an interface
that meets the exact-prices clause (c) of Definition 22.1, so each record act costs
exactly one. Call the questions of a run the claims of its CHECK moves, and say
two rival states answer alike when every one of those claims gets the same verdict
at both (each verdict taken at the rival itself, as it starts; the states the run passed
through don’t enter). If, for a list Ω of rival states and a number m > 0, the rivals of
Ω that answer alike with any one rival, itself included, are never more than m, then
192   22 From “The earned-chain counter example”


               ⌈log2 |Ω|⌉ −̇ ⌈log2 m⌉ is at most the number of CHECK moves of the run, which is
               at most its number of record moves, which is the ledger’s rise.
                 The tree has gone, and the run’s own questions stand in its place. There are
              limits, and they’re the ones in the statement. The verdicts are taken at the rivals
              as they start, and the states the run had reached when it asked don’t come into it.
              A branch that costs nothing, DEC on zero, can still narrow a list without adding
              anything to the ledger. So the theorem prices a narrowing that the paid checks
              explain, and no more.
                 The next statement takes the verdicts where the run took them. A rival is a state
              the run could have started from; a CHECK the run passed is asked of a rival by
              running the moves before it from the rival and checking the same claim at the state
              that reaches. Nothing is supplied but the prior.



              Theorem 22.16 (Structural entitlement, with the narrowing made by the run). Let
              a machine be certified Thiele-complete through an interface. Take a clean start s0 , a
              run t from it that ends with the record up, and a list Ω of rival starts that contains
              s0 . Let the posterior Ω′ be the rivals that pass every CHECK t passed, each asked as
              above, in the order t made them, and let bi and ai be the numbers of rivals standing
              just before and just after the ith. Then (1) Ω′ sits inside Ω and contains s0 ; (2) every
              rival of Ω that isn’t in Ω′ fails, asked that way, a CHECK that t made and passed;
              (3) |Ω| · ∏i ai = |Ω′ | · ∏i bi ; (4) if every CHECK t passed keeps at least a 2−k share
              of the rivals standing before it, bi ≤ 2k ai , then ⌈log2 |Ω|⌉ −̇ ⌈log2 |Ω′ |⌉ is at most k
              times the number of CHECKs t passed, that number is at most the record moves of t,
              the record moves are the ledger’s rise, and so the index bits are at most k times the
              ledger’s rise; (5) t contains the earned chain and the ledger rose by at least 3; and
              (6) with no share assumed, the index bits are at most ⌈log2 (|Ω|/|Ω′ |)⌉, which is
              ⌈∑i log2 (bi /ai )⌉ and at most the sum over the CHECKs t passed of ⌈log2 (bi /ai )⌉.
                 Part (3) is the exact form of “index bits are the sum of what each test took”:
              take base-two logarithms and log2 |Ω| − log2 |Ω′ | is the sum of log2 (bi /ai ). Parts
              (4) and (6) are what survives rounding to whole bits, and (6) rounds once, at the
              end, so it can be a bit lower for every test than rounding test by test. Rivals ( a, 0)
              for a = 0, . . . , 26 with CHECKs A ≥ 9, A ≥ 15, A ≥ 19 stand at 27, 18, 12 and 8:
              the index bits are 5 − 3 = 2, rounding once gives 2, and rounding each test gives 3.
              Here the run does the narrowing, every candidate dropped was dropped by a check
              the run paid for, and what stays a premise is the prior and the share, a bound fixed
              in advance; part (6) shows what the run pays without one.
                                                      22.5 Parts I and II, run on the example machine   193




Proposition 22.17 (One CHECK can take four bits). Take the sixteen starts ( a, 0)
with a = 0, . . . , 15, and the run CHECK (A ≥ 15) A; COMMIT (A ≥ 15) A;
CERTIFY from (15, 0). It raises the record. Its one passing CHECK keeps one rival
of sixteen, so the posterior is (15, 0) alone and the index bits are 4, while the
run has 3 record moves. So index bits at most the record moves, the bound of
Theorem 22.16(4) read with k = 1, fails here.
   The share here is 2−4 , and with k = 4 part (4) reads 4 ≤ 4. It’s the example of
Section 9 that showed the covering can’t be dropped, seen from the run’s side: one
test can take many bits, and the bound of part (4) covers the bits per test times the
tests.



Theorem 22.18 (What the record adds). Let a machine be certified Thiele-complete
through an interface. (1) From a clean start, the record is up at the end of a run
exactly when the run contains the earned chain, and a run with the record up raised
the ledger by at least 3. (2) Conclusions (1), (3) and (4) of Theorem 22.14 hold
without a clean start and without the record being up, for a machine that only meets
the exact-prices clause (c): the hypotheses (M1), (M2), (M4), (M5) and (M6), and
the decoded run being shown by some survivor, are all it needs.
    So the record doesn’t make narrowing cost more. It adds the earned chain and
a floor of 3, and that’s all it adds: a run meeting the same counting and payment
hypotheses is still bound by that index-bit floor even if it never raises the record,
and the floor of 3 no longer applies to it.
    Weights, fibres and partitions. Three more pieces of the same count. First, suppose
the rivals aren’t equally many: give each a whole-number weight, a multiplicity, and
call the sum of the weights of a list its mass. The count survives with masses in place
of lengths. If the prior’s mass is at most L( T ) times the posterior’s, the rounded
index bits of the two masses differ by at most depth( T ); so for any Thiele machine a
run that paid at least that many charged steps has a cost at least the drop, and for a
certified Thiele-complete machine the representation theorem goes through with
masses and the same five conclusions. On such a machine the ledger rises by the
same number from every start, the number of record moves, so the weighted sum of
ledger rises is the prior’s mass times the record moves, and the drop in rounded bits
times that mass is at most the weighted sum. Divide by the total mass and you’d
have an average rise; I leave the weights as whole numbers, cross-multiplied, so no
probability comes into it. Second, a way to meet the covering (M6) that you can see.
Give each survivor a little crowd of rivals, its fibre, no bigger than the tree has leaves,
194   22 From “The earned-chain counter example”


              and let the fibres’ sizes add up to at least the prior. Then |Ω| is at most the sum of
              the fibres, which is at most L( T ) times the number of survivors, and that’s (M6).
              Third, a plainer way to get fibres. Pick a representative observation of each rival, and
              say the rivals are partitioned by it when every rival looks like some survivor under it
              and no observation class, counted inside the prior, has more than L( T ) members.
              Take each survivor’s class as its fibre: every rival is in the class of some survivor, so
              the fibres add up to at least the prior, and (M6) holds. The converse is false: take
              four rivals that all look alike, two survivors and a tree with two leaves, and give
              each survivor two of the four as its fibre. That covers, but the one class has four
              members, more than two.
                 A worked member on the small machine. Eight rivals: the starts ( a, b) with
              a ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3} and b ∈ {0, 1}, counter A at a and counter B at b. The run is
              CHECK (Even) A; CHECK (A ≥ 2) A; COMMIT (A ≥ 2) A; CERTIFY, from (2, 0).
              The program raises the flag from a rival exactly when a is even and at least 2, so
              exactly from (2, 0) and (2, 1), and the posterior is those two. The decoder reads
              one “passed” for each CHECK in the run’s receipts; the tree is the complete tree of
              depth 2; and the covering comes from fibres by a representative observation, “B is
              0,” which isn’t constant: each survivor’s fibre is the four rivals that share its B.


              Proposition 22.19 (The eight-start member). For every a and b, the run raises
              the flag from ( a, b) exactly when a is even and at least 2. All hypotheses of Theo-
              rem 22.14 hold, with eight rivals narrowed to two: the test of the posterior is strictly
              stronger; the index bits are 3 − 1 = 2; the run has 4 record moves and the ledger
              rises by exactly 4; and the earned chain is in the run. The run’s own questions
              (Theorem 22.15) are its 2 CHECKs, so the floor is met with equality. With the two
              CHECKs alone, the record stays down, the ledger rises by 2, and the bound 2 ≤ 2
              still holds.
                   It’s a real narrowing, and not the cups of Section 9 sitting under it in a costume.
               The posterior is exactly the set of rivals the run certifies, so here a proof ties the
               lists to the run, and nobody supplies them. Read with Theorem 22.16, the run’s two
               passing CHECKs, replayed from the eight rivals, leave four and then two, the same
               two, so the posterior is the one the run makes; each keeps half, so k = 1, and the
               index bits, 2, are at most 1 · 2 and at most the ledger’s rise of 4.
                   A search through n bits. The same shape comes back at a size you can’t enumerate,
               on the machine of Definition C.2, the host in Appendix C.1 with a counter for every
               number, with the small machine’s property language of Zero, Even and At-least-n,
               read at registers, in place of Slot. That machine is certified Thiele-complete too,
               by the argument of Theorem C.6 with these three properties in place of Slot. Hide
                                                      22.5 Parts I and II, run on the example machine   195


n = k + m bits, one in each of registers 0 to n − 1. The program asks k questions,
one per bit of the first k: CHECK “at least 1” of register i for a 1, CHECK “is 0” for a
0. Then it COMMITs the first claim and CERTIFYs. The prior is every n-bit value;
the posterior is the 2m values that begin with the answers; the tree is the complete
tree of depth k; and a survivor’s fibre is the 2k values that agree with it on the m bits
nobody asked about.


Theorem 22.20 (A search through n bits). For 1 ≤ k ≤ 16 and any m: from a
world holding a value v of at least k bits, the program certifies exactly when v begins
with its answers, so the posterior is exactly the set of rivals it certifies from; the test
of the posterior is strictly stronger than the prior’s; the earned chain is in the run;
the prior has 2k+m members and the posterior 2m , so the index bits are exactly k;
the run has exactly k + 2 record moves and the ledger rises by exactly k + 2; and the
run’s own questions are k CHECKs, so Theorem 22.15 is met with equality. With the
COMMIT and the CERTIFY left off, the questions alone narrow 2k+m values to 2m ,
the record stays down, and the ledger rises by exactly k. And no run from a clean
start can execute more than 16 CHECKs before its first successful CERTIFY: a failed
CHECK would trap, and the table holds only 16 passing ones. Thus this program
with 17 questions followed by its first COMMIT and CERTIFY certifies from no start.
A trace that certified earlier can keep its flag up even after later failed instructions.
   The sixteen belongs to the machine, and the program has no say in it: it’s the
same ceiling as the item “Sixteen checks per run, ever” in the list of what the machine
doesn’t do, near the end of this chapter, and it’s why this search never asks more
than sixteen questions however many bits are hidden. Unasked bits can be any
number. The lists, the tree and the fibres are supplied here, as in the representation
theorem, and tied to the run only for this member. The covering still prices only the
size of the drop, and its reason goes unpriced.
   The covering can’t be dropped. It’s a hypothesis, and you’d hope it’s a nuisance
you could remove. It isn’t, and the small machine shows it. Fix n ≥ 4, take the 2n
starts ( a, 0) with a below 2n as the rivals, and run the three-move chain on the claim
“A ≥ 2n − 1”: CHECK, COMMIT, CERTIFY. It certifies exactly from A = 2n − 1, so
the posterior is one start, n index bits are gone, and the ledger rose by 3. So for
n ≥ 4 the claim “the index-bit drop is at most the ledger’s rise” is false for this run,
and no tree of depth at most 3 covers the narrowing: (M6) would need 2n ≤ 8 · 1.
One property with a big constant in it carries n bits in a single yes-or-no question;
what the bound prices is a narrowing whose classes are balanced, a tree with enough
leaves. A narrowing of any other shape is outside it.
196   22 From “The earned-chain counter example”


                  The time a check saves. The question is whether counter A is at least m. There
              are two ways to find out. The unpaid way uses base moves only, INC and DEC,
              which add nothing to the ledger. But a base move reads A one pebble at a time: a
              DEC takes a pebble off A or finds it empty. After n moves, a run that started with A
              at least n has never seen A empty, so it can’t have behaved differently for different
              such A except by a constant shift in A itself. The paid way is one CHECK (A ≥ m):
              one move, a ledger of one, and the line it ends on says which way it went. Certifying
