              the answer takes the whole chain, CHECK, COMMIT and CERTIFY.


              Theorem 22.21 (The time tax). (1) For every program of two-counter instructions
              and every n < m, the line number and B after n moves (or where the run stopped,
              if it stopped sooner) are the same from A = m − 1 as from A = m, with the same
              B. So no such program, read off its line number and B after fewer than m moves,
              tells whether A ≥ m at every start; in particular none that stops within fewer than
              m moves does. (2) A program of m DEC/INC pairs (a ladder: pair i is DEC A on
              line 2i − 1, jumping on success to the next pair at line 2i + 1, and INC B on line
              2i, where a DEC that finds A empty falls through) decides it when observed after
              m moves: after m moves its line is 2m + 1 exactly when A ≥ m; and it costs 0. (3)
              Executed as a three-move trace from start( a, b), the paid chain raises the flag exactly
              when a ≥ m and has a ledger of exactly 3, whatever m is. As a stored program it
              pays 3 on success and stops after its first failed CHECK with a ledger of 1. (4) From
              any loaded start start( a, b), a single CHECK (A ≥ m) decides it in one move from
              the line number, with a ledger of exactly 1, whatever m is. (5) So if a unit on the
              ledger is worth λ moves, the paid route to deciding costs 1 + λ and the unpaid route
              costs at least m, and the paid route is cheaper exactly when 1 + λ < m; the paid
              route to certifying costs 3 + 3λ.

                   When 3 + 3λ < m, the paid chain both answers and certifies within a combined
               budget smaller than the unpaid lower bound for answering alone. That compares
               different guarantees; the unpaid route never certifies. For a bare answer on both
               sides, the comparison is 1 + λ < m. The exchange rate is sharper than a comparison
               of two programs I happened to write would give: the unpaid side is a floor under
               every program of INC and DEC there is, so no choice of program comes into it, and
               the paid side doesn’t move with m at all. The bound has limits. It’s for programs that
               make no record move, INC and DEC with HALT or falling off the end as stopping. It
               doesn’t cover every certified Thiele-complete machine, because the definition doesn’t
               forbid a base move that changes a counter by more than one pebble, and such a
               machine is a different machine, one the bound doesn’t speak for. And the two routes
               put their answers in different places, both bare decisions in the line number, with
                                                   22.5 Parts I and II, run on the example machine   197


the paid chain also putting a historical record in the flag; this compares two ways to
answer one question, not two certifications. Zero cost isn’t zero heat, either: every
move on either route is an event, and the moves are what λ prices.
CHAPTER 23




From “Five small models”

The fragment is a three-instruction memory-bounds language with the policy “every
address is below the limit”: the checker accepts exactly when the condition holds,
and a certificate implies the condition. One example sits on top of that, a computa-
tion you can do in your head: a program that reads address two under a limit of
two is rejected. The fragment isn’t the paper’s real case study, and nothing connects
it to anything else in this book. And it has a limit: a program that writes garbage to
address zero passes if zero is below the limit. The proof is of the stated policy and
nothing else.
    And what was the paper’s real case study, the one my fragment isn’t? It was
network packet filters, the little programs an operating system runs on incoming
network traffic to decide which program gets to see which packet. They run inside
the kernel, the most trusted part of the system, and they have to be fast, so they’re
exactly the code you’d most like to take from a stranger and least like to trust. In
the 1996 paper that first set out the idea, Necula and Peter Lee wrote several filters
by hand in the assembly language of a DEC Alpha, shipped each with its proof, and
reported a one-time cost of 1 to 3 milliseconds to check the proof, after which the
filter ran with no checks at all. Those milliseconds put a number on “checking is
cheap by design”. What the paper reports changes nothing about my fragment,
which still isn’t that case study, and it doesn’t add a price: the milliseconds measure
the checker’s work in that experiment. They are not a per-crossing toll theorem,
whether or not an implementation saves the result.

Definition 23.1 (The bounds fragment). Instructions are READ a, WRITE a v, and
HALT. For a memory limit L, the policy allows READ a and WRITE a v when a < L,
and always allows HALT. The verification condition of a program is that the policy
allows every instruction in it. A certificate is a list of per-instruction policy facts, one
for each instruction in order. The checker tests the policy on each instruction and
returns the conjunction.




                                   198
                                                                     23.1 One example, three levels   199




Theorem 23.1 (The checker is the verification condition). For every limit L and
program P: the checker accepts P exactly when the verification condition holds; any
certificate for P gives the verification condition; and with L = 2 the checker rejects
READ 2 followed by HALT.

Proof. Lay the instructions in a row. For each READ or WRITE, compare its address
with L; put yes beneath it exactly when the address is smaller. Put yes beneath HALT
without asking about an address. The checker accepts exactly when every mark in
that row is yes. The verification condition asks for exactly those same per-instruction
facts. An empty row has no failing instruction, and extending the row preserves
acceptance precisely when the new instruction also passes. This covers programs of
every finite length.
   A certificate supplies one policy fact at each place in the same row. Reading
them in order gives every fact the verification condition asks for, so it implies that
condition too.
   With limit two, the first instruction READ 2 asks whether 2 < 2. It does not.
The later HALT is allowed, but cannot undo that failed comparison, so the checker
rejects the two-instruction program.


23.1 One example, three levels

Before I lay all five side by side, let me walk one of them up and down the three
levels from the start of the section, because every claim in here lives at exactly one
level and it’s easy to let a claim slide up a floor without noticing. I’ll take gas, since
it’s the one place all three line up. Think of a restaurant’s real recipe, my photocopy
of one page of it, and a cookbook chapter on what any recipe has to contain. I can
check my photocopy line by line. That tells you nothing about what the kitchen
cooks tonight.
    Start with the real specification. Ethereum’s execution specification says, in a
document that runs real money, what a storage write costs, what clearing it refunds,
and how the refund is capped at the end of the transaction. None of what I proved
is at this level. What the network actually charges is whatever the clients people run
actually compute, and the evidence for that is the clients and the chain. This book
has none of it.
    Next comes the transcription. I copied one mechanism out of that document,
one slot in one transaction, gas and refund arithmetic only, at a named commit
(Definition 17.2, Theorem 17.3). What the result tells you: if the storage rule is
what I copied, then leaving a fresh slot set costs at least twenty thousand, and
200   23 From “Five small models”


              set-then-clear nets twenty-three hundred at the counter. What it can’t tell you:
              anything about the refund cap (not copied, which is why what a real set-then-clear
              pays came out so different from the counter earlier), the stack, calls, other slots, or
              whether any client implements the rule as written. The boundary of the claim is the
              edge of the copy.
                  Last comes the wrapper. The gas schedule of Definition 17.3 isn’t Ethereum. It’s
              any system with steps, a cost per step and a yes/no commitment flag, plus one thing
              stipulated: a charge predicate, with the premise that a charged step costs at least one
              and an uncharged one costs zero. That premise is the whole of what the wrapper
              adds, and it’s added by hand; no specification supplies it. Given it, the theorem says
              every run costs exactly its number of commits, never letting a commit go unbilled,
              never billing one twice and never billing a non-commit, exactly when the charge
              predicate is the commit predicate at unit price. What that tells you is what exact
              pricing would have to look like, in any system. It doesn’t say Ethereum meets it,
              and Ethereum doesn’t: real gas bills computation that commits nothing, which the
              transcription shows in numbers. So the wrapper prices a definition, the transcription
              prices a copied rule, and neither prices the chain.
                  The other four sit on the same three levels, and only one more wrapper is spelled
              out in this section. Casper’s transcription is the accountable-safety proof above; its
              wrapper is a small gadget with the stake at risk handed in. A finalize step that risks
              nothing turns the record on at no charge, which breaks Section 2’s toll, and one
              that risks at least one unit has a floor. It records exposure to loss (no collateral is
              destroyed in it), has no Casper checkpoints in it at all, and is priced by stipulation,
              the same way the gas wrapper is. Attestation, transparency and proof-carrying code
              have transcriptions that prove what their verifiers do and no price theorem, because
              no specification of theirs puts a price on the verdict. Their wrappers, which I don’t
              spell out here, are Section 8’s verifier argument told in each system’s vocabulary,
              with a colliding pair behind each one, and that argument is all they carry here.
                  The book’s rule is that every claim gets a way to break it, so I’ll say how to knock
              down the four claims with the most weight on them. For the gas transcription: a list
              of stores from a fresh slot that ends nonzero with net charge under 22100. For the
              gas wrapper: a schedule in which every run costs exactly its number of commits,
              whose charge predicate still differs from the commit predicate at some step. For
              the finality gadget: a finalizing trace from unfinalized to finalized whose charged
              stake is zero while the gadget claims a floor. For the attestation fragment: a verifier
              reading only the quote’s fields that decides a runtime flag the quote ignores.

From “The pointer thesis”

24.1 Where the shape comes from, and how much of it survived

The shape is from physics, and I want to tell you where exactly, because I borrowed
a word and I didn’t get to keep the whole thing.
   Quantum mechanics has a famous problem: a system can be in a blend of states,
and when you measure it you find one definite state, and nobody agrees on what
“measuring” did. In 1981 Wojciech Zurek asked a sharper question: of all the ways a
measuring device could end up, why does it end up recording one particular kind
of quantity (the position of a pointer on a dial, say)? Why never some blend?
His answer, developed over twenty years and summarised in a 2009 paper called
“Quantum Darwinism,” is that the environment decides. The air, the light, the table,
everything the device touches, is constantly interacting with it, and only certain
states of the device survive that interaction; those are the pointer states. More than
that, the environment selects them, and then it copies them. The light bouncing off
a dial carries the dial’s reading to every corner of the room, redundantly, so that
a thousand observers can each read a different scrap of the environment and all
agree on where the pointer is, without touching the dial. Objectivity, on this view, is
redundancy: a property is objective when many independent fragments of the world
each carry it.
   Zurek isn’t the only one behind it. Fernando Brandão, Marco Piani and Paweł
Horodecki (2015) prove an approximate statement about most small fragments of a
large output: their channels can be approximated by measuring the input in one
common way and preparing fragment states from that result. The error and the
exceptional fraction are part of the theorem. Agreement on outcomes also needs the
fragments to carry enough information about them.
   Xiao-Liang Qi and Daniel Ranard (2021) strengthen the bound on the exceptional
region. Outside a region whose size is controlled by the input size, allowed error and
size of the fragments being examined, locally accessible information is approximately
classical. This does not say that every observer gets an exact record, or any useful
information at all. My definitions below ask for exact Boolean answers from specified



                                                 201
202   24 From “The pointer thesis”


               observers. That is a simpler chosen contract, not the quantum theorem with its
               qualifications removed.
                   What I borrowed is that shape. Certification is to a machine’s ecosystem (the
               machine plus everything around it that reads, stores and relies on its records) what
               the pointer reading is to the room: the thing that gets copied everywhere. It’s a
               good shape and I think it’s the right intuition. I’m going to be exact about what
               survived into the definitions below, because the word “redundant” is doing less than
               it does for Zurek.
                   Zurek’s redundancy is graded (each fragment carries almost all the information,
               with some small loss) and it requires the fragments to be disjoint: different observers
               reading different parts of the environment. The definition below is the zero-loss
               case with no disjointness at all. An ecosystem is a state type, some number of
               observers, and one bit per observer; an observer records an event when its bit is true
               exactly when the event holds; an event proliferates when every observer records
               it. Nothing in that says two observers draw on separate parts of the state. They
               could all be reading the same bit, and the definition permits it. So I kept the word
               and dropped the independence that makes Zurek’s version mean “objective.” That
               leaves a gap between the thesis’s prose and that first formal object, and the prose is
               where the thesis lives. The definitions under the thesis below close part of the gap:
               they define independent carriers, and the definition turns out to force something
               (Theorem 24.1).
                   The independence I dropped is exactly what the thesis’s closest published neigh-
               bour keeps. Jess Riedel, Zurek and Michael Zwolak (2016) took histories of a closed
               quantum system, the story a whole sequence of events tells, and asked which of
               them the world keeps records of in many separate pieces of the environment. As
               I read their paper, redundant records in separate pieces make a history consistent,
               so ordinary yes-or-no reasoning about it works, and they pick out a small set of
               histories from the huge number that are merely consistent: in their words, an
               objective past. Their result is “what proliferates is what counts” in quantum form,
               with the disjointness in it, and it came before this book. Halliwell, in a second 1999
               paper, “Somewhere in the universe,” had already carried the link between records
               and consistent histories from pure starting states to mixed ones, where the records
               still exist but are necessarily imperfect.
                   There’s a deeper mismatch in the borrowing, and it runs against the book, so it
               goes here. In Zurek’s picture the whole thing, system and device and environment
               together, evolves by quantum mechanics’ rule for closed systems, and that rule never
               sends two states to one; every step can be run backwards. So, seen from the whole,
               the environment’s copying is just that, copying: the dial’s reading gets written onto
               fresh light, and no two states anywhere are merged. A simple copying model sends
               each new copy to a previously unused carrier. That is a use of fresh space, like
                                       24.1 Where the shape comes from, and how much of it survived   203


Bennett’s blank page. It is not a proof that every closed quantum evolution visits a
new state forever; finite closed systems can recur.
    Looking only at the dial discards the correlations with the light. In an ideal
complete-dephasing channel, distinct input phases can give exactly the same reduced
output. In gradual decoherence they may only become harder to tell apart; they
need not become exactly equal at any finite time. An exact merge in a reduced
description, approximate loss of accessible coherence, and a heat transfer are three
different claims. To obtain a heat floor one still needs the physical preparation, the
relevant entropy change and the thermal premises. Copying alone supplies none of
those conclusions.
    That is the difficulty for the borrowing. The thesis points at copies being kept
and used. The quantum account explains particular ways information can spread
and become accessible. It does not attach this book’s toll to that act.
    A published no-go cuts closer still. Philippe Allard Guérin, Veronika Baumann,
Flavio Del Santo and Časlav Brukner (2021) gave an argument that, under assump-
tions that look natural in quantum mechanics, an observer’s own perceptions of her
outcome at two different times can’t share one reality: no joint chance for the two,
depending linearly on the starting state, reproduces what unitary quantum theory
predicts for each. A record that persists, for the one holding it, is exactly what gets
into trouble once the whole is treated as one-to-one. It isn’t a theorem about my
machine, which isn’t quantum. It’s still the strongest published reason I know to
hold the word “permanent” loosely when a physicist is in the room.
    Which leaves the question to ask of any borrowed shape: has anybody checked
the original? For a long time quantum Darwinism was a theory without a lab. Then,
in 2018 and 2019, three groups working separately, in Italy, China and Germany,
went looking for its fingerprint, as Philip Ball’s 2019 report in Quanta Magazine
tells it. The German one, at Ulm, with Zurek among the authors, watched the
spin of a single defect inside a diamond and the carbon atoms around it, and
saw the redundancy the theory predicts: the defect’s state turned up copied in its
surroundings, and it didn’t take much of the surroundings before more of them
told you nothing new. The other two did it with photons. Is that the bottom? It
isn’t, not by a long way, and the people involved said so. Zurek himself pointed out
that the photon experiments make their copies artificially and that the diamond’s
environment is small, and others noted that all three are schematic versions of a real
environment and don’t rule out rival accounts. So the shape I borrowed has passed
some first tests in a lab, at the scale of a handful of atoms or photons, with the
caveats written on it. None of that touches the thesis here, which is about machines
and never rested on quantum physics. It changes the picture: what I borrowed is a
lead somebody is checking. It isn’t settled, and I don’t get to lean on it as if it were.
204   24 From “The pointer thesis”


               24.2 What’s proved
               This section has definitions, two toys, a theorem about independence, a survey, and
               two refutations of things that would have been stronger.
                  The definitions are the ones above, plus “unique pointer among rivals”: the event
               proliferates and none of a listed set of rival events does. Uniqueness is relative
               to the list, necessarily, since any event that holds on exactly the same states as a
               proliferating event proliferates whenever it does: the same fact under another name,
               so someone has to say which names count as rivals. And the first criterion is stated
               as a schema: for every ecosystem in some class, its metered event is the unique
               pointer among its rivals. The class and the rivals are inputs. Nothing about them is
               proved. A second set of definitions, after this one, tightens “proliferates” to “copied
               and relied on” and drops the rival list.
                  The formal object is small enough to hold in one hand.

               Definition 24.1 (Ecosystem, records, proliferates, pointer). An ecosystem is a type
               of states, a number N of observers, and for each observer i a Boolean fragment Fi
               on states. Observer i records an event E (a property of states) when, for every state
               s, E(s) holds exactly when Fi (s) is true. E proliferates when every observer i < N
               records it. E is the unique pointer among a list of rival events when E proliferates
               and no rival in the list does. Given a class C of ecosystems, and for each member a
               designated metered event and a list of rivals, the pointer criterion holds for C when
               every member’s metered event is the unique pointer among its rivals.

                   Nothing in the definition makes two fragments read different parts of the state,
               and “metered” is only a label: the definition has no metering in it.
                   One bit per observer can’t even state the objection a replicated system raises.
               Every full node of a blockchain holds the whole shared state, so every fact about
               it, “gas used is at least one” as much as “final,” sits on every node. The tightened
               definitions give each observer whatever it holds, an action it takes on what it holds,
               and a flag for whether it produced the record.

               Definition 24.2 (Copied and relied on, independent carriers, the pointer). An
               ecosystem with reliance is a type of states, a number N of observers, and for each
               observer i a view Vi (a function from states to some type of things an observer can
               hold), an action ai (a Boolean function of the view) and a producer flag. Fix a
               set R of reachable states. An event E is copied on R when for each i < N some
               Boolean function d of the view has E(s) exactly when d(Vi (s)) is true, for every s
               in R. Observer i relies on E when ai (Vi (s)) true gives E(s) for every s in R, and
               ai (Vi (s)) is true for some s in R. E is relied on by parties who didn’t produce it when
               some observer i < N is not a producer and every such observer relies on E; it is
                                                                              24.2 What’s proved   205


copied and relied on when it is both. E is the pointer on R when it is copied and
relied on, and every event that is copied and relied on holds at every state of R
where E holds. The carriers are independent under a family of relations Pi (states
that agree on observer i’s own part of the world) when each view gives equal values
on states related by its Pi , and for any two observers i ̸= j below N and any states s,
t there is a state u with Pi (u, s) and Pj (u, t): the two parts can be set separately.

    Two pointers on the same R agree on every state of R, so the pointer is unique
with no list of rivals. And the definition of independence has a consequence I didn’t
put in on purpose.
    Put one yes/no card in front of each of two readers. If the cards can be set
separately, all four arrangements are allowed: no/no, no/yes, yes/no and yes/yes.
Ask each reader to recover one shared event using only their own card. Suppose
that event is false at no/no and true at yes/yes. At no/yes, the first reader sees
exactly what they saw in the false case, while the second sees exactly what they saw
in the true case. Their answers conflict. That mixed arrangement is exactly what the
independence condition hands the proof.
    Now impose a copying procedure that only leaves no/no or yes/yes. On those
reachable arrangements each reader can report the shared bit correctly. Separate
cards are things that could carry a copy; what makes them copies is that the
arrangements you actually reach have the two agreeing. The theorem below is
about recovering the event exactly on all arrangements, the mixed ones included,
which the copying never leaves behind.


Theorem 24.1 (Independent copies on every state: the event is equivalent at any
two states). In any ecosystem with reliance whose carriers are independent and
which has at least two observers, an event copied on the set of all states holds at
one state exactly when it holds at any other, so it holds at every state or at none.

Proof. Let observers 0 and 1 decide the event by d0 and d1 . Given states s and t,
take u with observer 0’s part from s and observer 1’s from t. Then the event at s is
d0 of observer 0’s view, which is the same at u, so it equals the event at u; and the
event at u equals the event at t the same way through observer 1.
   So separate parts of the world can each carry an exact copy of a real record only
on the states the world actually reaches, where the copying made them agree, and
that’s why the definition asks for copies on a set R of reachable states. Zurek’s copies
are the same: the light carries the dial’s reading because the dial put it there.
206   24 From “The pointer thesis”




              Theorem 24.2 (A replicated header: copying doesn’t separate, reliance does). Let
              the states be a block header (a finalized bit, a gas-used count) and three nodes’
              copies of it, with R the states where every copy equals the header; node i’s view is
              its own copy, its part of the world is that copy, node 0 is the producer, and every
              node acts exactly when its copy says finalized. Then the carriers are independent;
              “finalized” and “gas used is at least one” are both copied on R; “finalized” is the
              pointer on R; and “gas used is at least one” is not relied on by parties who didn’t
              produce it.

               Proof. Independence: to give node i the copy from s and node j the copy from t,
               overwrite node j’s copy in s. On R every copy is the header, so each node reads
               either fact off its copy. Node 1 acts only where its copy, and so the header, says
               finalized, and acts at the finalized header with no gas used; that state shows gas
               used fails node 1’s reliance. Any event relied on by node 1 holds wherever node 1
               acts, which on R is wherever the header is finalized, so “finalized” is the strongest.
                  It’s a sanity check with maps I wrote, and it shows only that the tightened
               definition can tell the two apart where plain copying can’t. Which of the two it picks,
               the actions pick: let every node act when its copy says gas was used, and gas used is
               the pointer. When what the other parties act on is copied, that is the pointer, so here
               the argument lives in the actions, and I wrote those too.
                  The toy has three observers on a replicated ledger, where certification proliferates
               and “work done” doesn’t. It’s an illustration, and that’s all it is; it isn’t evidence.


               Theorem 24.3 (The toy). Let the toy have states (a certified bit, a work count),
               three observers, and every fragment equal to the certified bit. Then “certified” is the
               unique pointer among the one rival “work at least 1”.

               Proof. Every fragment is the certified bit, so every observer records “certified” by
               definition. For the rival, take the state with the bit false and work 1: the event holds
               and observer 0’s fragment is false, so observer 0 does not record it.
                  The survey covers twelve events in one theorem: for each of the five systems in
               Section 17, the designated event (finalized, committed, attested, logged, checked)
               proliferates in the observer map I wrote for that system, and a rival (proposer work,
               a scratch counter, measurement noise, submission latency, prover steps) doesn’t.
               The five together are one statement, an instance of the schema above. The survey
               adds a symmetric message-authentication code, which does not proliferate, and a
                                                                            24.2 What’s proved   207


digital signature, which does, for twelve rows in all. The five maps all use the same
construction: a state has a designated Boolean event and a separate nonnegative
rival count, three observers each return the event bit, and the rival asks whether
its count is at least one. The bit and count can vary independently. The signature
map likewise gives all three observers the authentic-signature bit. In the MAC
map, observers 0 and 1 receive the authentic-tag bit and observer 2 always returns
false. These maps, not a protocol running on a network, are the entire tests here.
And there’s a thirteenth, which I think is the most important one: swap which bit
every observer copies, and the criterion picks the other event. The criterion selects
whatever the observer map copies. The map is where the argument lives, and I
wrote the maps. So the five “confirmations” are five instances of one construction
with the fields renamed. I’m saying that twice on purpose, so it can’t slip past once.


Theorem 24.4 (The survey, and the swap). (1) In each of the five observer models
(proof-of-stake, gas, attestation, transparency, proof-carrying), the designated event
proliferates and the designated rival does not; in the symmetric-MAC model the
authentic-tag event does not proliferate; in the signature model the authentic-
signature event does. (2) Let the states be pairs of bits (first, second), with three
observers whose fragments all equal the second bit. Then “second is true” is the
unique pointer among the one rival “first is true”.

Proof. Each confirmation holds by definition: each observer’s fragment is true
exactly when the event holds. Each refutation exhibits one state where the event
holds and some observer’s bit is down. In (2) that state is (true, false).
    Two stronger claims are both refuted, and I’d ask you not to run them together,
because they fail for different reasons.
    The first stronger claim is that every forgery-resistant commitment proliferates.
If that were true, certification would be the pointer because it’s the commitment.
It’s refuted in three observer models (deniable authentication, where only the
designated verifier’s bit tracks the event; a symmetric MAC, where the key holders
share a secret and nobody else can check; an object capability, an unforgeable token
that grants access to whoever holds it) and confirmed in two (a public log; a digital
signature). Build the first with three observers: give one a copy of the event’s bit
and the other two a constant false. The one records it; it doesn’t proliferate. The
proved half is the map computation. Whether my maps are fair to the real designs is
argued in prose, and you should argue back.
208   24 From “The pointer thesis”




               Theorem 24.5 (One blind observer is enough). (1) In any ecosystem, if some
               observer i < N has Fi (s0 ) false at a state s0 where E holds, then E does not
               proliferate. (2) Let the deniable model have states (authentic, verifier-accepts),
               three observers, observer 0’s fragment the authentic bit and observers 1 and 2
               constantly false. Then observer 0 records “authentic”, and “authentic” does not
               proliferate. The symmetric-MAC and object-capability models also fail to proliferate
               their events; the public-log and signature models proliferate theirs.

               Proof. (1) Recording would require E(s0 ) ⇐⇒ Fi (s0 ), and the left side is true
               while the right is false. (2) Observer 0’s fragment is true exactly when the event
               holds. Apply (1) with observer 1 and the state (true, true). The other two refutations
               apply (1) to a blind observer of their own model (the symmetric-MAC map gives
               observer 2 a constant false; the object-capability map gives the token holder the bit
               and the other two constant false). The two confirmations hold by definition: the
               public-log map gives all three observers the logged bit, and the signature map gives
               all three the authentic-signature bit.
                  The theorem is about the observer maps, and only them. That a third party in
              deniable authentication really cannot be convinced is the modelling claim, and the
              model has no transcript, key or verifier in it to check that against.
                  The second stronger claim is that agreement plus accuracy plus locality imply
              permanence. That is, if all observers agree, each sees the event exactly, and each
              updates by the same local rule with no coordinator, then the recorded event can’t be
              undone. The smallest thing imaginable refutes it: one bit, two observers who both
              see it, and the rule “flip.” All three conditions hold and true becomes false in one
              step. Do it on paper; it takes a minute and it’s humbling. What does give permanence
              is durability, “an observer’s true view stays true,” added as a premise, and then it
              follows in a few lines. So durability is the premise doing the work, and durability
              is Section 3’s permanence wearing a different coat. Agreement doesn’t buy it. Do
              the paper version again with one change, the rule “stay true once true” in place of
              “flip,” and the bit can’t fall, because the rule won’t let it. The observers agreeing has
              nothing to do with it. (The small machine of Section 15 has a reading that’s durable
              in exactly this sense: its flag never comes down, by its step rule (Theorem 15.6).)
              None of this rules out a narrower theorem with real assumptions about the network,
              the adversary or durability; it rules out these two strong forms.
                                          24.3 What isn’t proved, and must stay that way on the page   209


Definition 24.3 (Observation game). A game has states, a number N of observers, a
Boolean event e, a Boolean view Vi for each observer, and a step map. For i, j < N it
has consensus when Vi = Vj on every state; authenticity when Vi (s) = e(s) for every
state and every i < N; coordinator-free update when one function ℓ on Booleans has
Vi (step(s)) = ℓ(Vi (s)) for every state and every i < N; durable views when Vi (s)
true gives Vi (step(s)) true for every i < N; and a permanent event when e(s) true
gives e(step(s)) true.



Theorem 24.6 (What buys permanence). (1) It is false that every game with N > 0,
consensus, authenticity and coordinator-free update has a permanent event. (2)
Every game with N > 0, authenticity and durable views has a permanent event.

Proof. (1) The toggle game: states are Booleans, two observers, event and both
views the identity, step “flip”. Consensus and authenticity hold trivially, the local
rule is “flip”, and the state true steps to false. (2) If e(s) is true, observer 0’s view
is true by authenticity, stays true by durability, and authenticity turns it back into
e(step(s)).


24.3 What isn’t proved, and must stay that way on the page
What isn’t proved is that certification in deployed systems is singled out by being
copied and relied on across independent carriers. The criterion defines independence;
it has no precise definition of “deployed” or “faithfully model,” and no cost law
connecting being copied and relied on to price. Every correspondence between a
mathematical observer map and a real system is a sentence I wrote. The thesis is
a thesis, and anyone who says the formal object proves it is reading more than is
there.


24.4 What the thesis asks of the examples
There are three concrete comparisons to make. First, look at the record a deployed
discipline prices. Do parties who did not produce it copy and rely on it? A priced
record that nobody else relies on would contradict the thesis’s second half.
   Second, compare certification with rival events. If an event not implied by
certification is copied and relied on to the same standard, certification is not the
unique pointer. Multiplication counts or head-turn counts would qualify if they met
that same test in the system being described.
   Third, apply the independence condition of Definition 24.2 to a faithful model of
the ecosystem and ask whether certification itself meets the test. The symmetric-
210   24 From “The pointer thesis”


               MAC example shows why the carriers and their access matter. The two stronger
               forms already refuted above help locate the distinction this thesis needs.
                  These comparisons supply the evidence the worldly claim asks for. The mathe-
               matical criterion supplies a definite question once the model and its observer maps
               are chosen.

From “The counter against
physics”

25.1 The calibration is a unit conversion

Multiply the counter by an energy per unit of cost that you choose, and you get
a theorem: a state with one unit, at the scale kT ln 2 per unit, carries kT ln 2 of
energy, for every k and T. Read that for exactly what it is: a unit conversion, true by
definition, with the scale as an unforced input. Nothing in it picks the scale. The
theorem would be just as true with the scale set to a dollar a unit. What would
connect units of cost to joules is the premise in Section 3, Landauer’s principle,
which is a physical premise. And even as a scale, k B T ln 2 a unit is the floor for one
particular squeeze, a halving. Corollary 3.13’s floor for a flip that brings k states in
beside m already reading yes, starting from the even spread on those m + k states,
is the larger of k B T ln mm+k and m2k
                                    +k k B T ln 2, and for one newcomer among many
yes-states that’s far below a halving, about 1.4 k B T/m. Price every unit at k B T ln 2
and you can overstate the floor of a single narrow flip by a factor that grows with m.
    Remember the constant from Boltzmann’s grave in Section 3, the exchange rate
between a count and heat? It has a newer chapter, and it makes the point sharper
than I can. The k B in k B T ln 2 turns a temperature into an energy, and for most
of its history it was a measured number, with error bars, pinned down by careful
experiments. Since 20 May 2019 it isn’t. That day the international system of units
was redefined so that Boltzmann’s constant is exactly 1.380649 × 10−23 joules per
kelvin, by definition, and the kelvin is whatever makes that true. So the conversion
from temperature to energy has been a convention since then, in the strictest sense:
its numerical value in those units is fixed. Realising the kelvin in a laboratory still
requires measurements, with uncertainty. What’s still physics is everything around
it, whether wiping a bit at a temperature really sheds that much heat, which is what
the Lyon bead was measuring. The calibration theorem is the same kind of move as
that redefinition. It fixes a conversion, and the physics, if there is any, is somewhere
else.



                                                  211
212   25 From “The counter against physics”


               Definition 25.1 (Energy at a scale). For a real number c (joules per unit) and a
               state s, the energy of s at scale c is µ(s) · c. The Landauer calibration at k B and T is
               the statement that every state with µ(s) = 1 has energy k B T ln 2 at scale k B T ln 2.



               Theorem 25.1 (The calibration is a unit conversion). (1) For all real k B and T,
               the Landauer calibration at k B and T holds. (2) For any counter, there are two
               real-valued functions of the state that give 1 and 2 on every state with one unit, so
               the counter alone fixes no energy value.

               Proof. (1) The scale says what one ledger unit is worth. At a ledger reading of
               one, multiplying by that scale returns the scale itself: 1 · k B T ln 2 = k B T ln 2. No
               condition on the signs of k B or T is needed for that multiplication.
                  (2) Choose a scale of one and a scale of two. The same ledger reading of one is
               assigned energy one by the first choice and energy two by the second. The ledger
               has not changed; only the conversion I supplied has. Thus that conversion was not
               determined by the counter.
                   The physics is the premise of Section 3, restated here once in the form the next
               theorems test. In words first: a dissipation hands each instruction a whole number
               of heat units, each unit worth kT ln 2. The floor premise says an instruction that
               takes at least one state where some yes-or-no property is false to a state where it’s
               true, never sends a true one back to false, and sends two different states to one,
               dissipates at least one unit. That’s a different statement from Landauer’s principle as
               Premise 3.5 has it. That one depends on the spread over states, Q ≥ k B T ln 2 · ∆H,
               and gives no floor for a known state (there the distinction was absent from the
               chosen point distribution; that alone says nothing about a prior preparation bill).
               This one gives every such instruction a floor of one whole unit, whatever the spread.
               To get the second from the first you’d need more, at the least a spread with a full bit
               of uncertainty removed at every such step, and the known-state theorem and the
               narrow squeeze of Section 3 show that isn’t automatic. So I give it its own name,
               structural dissipation pricing. The calibration says no instruction dissipates more
               units than the counter charges it. One word about that word: “dissipation” here
               means heat handed to the surroundings, the quantity Section 3 read off Sagawa as
               owed even when no entropy is produced, the heat that pays for where the forgotten
               bit goes. It is not what Section 1 calls dissipating, which is entropy produced, and it
               has nothing to do with waste, or with doing the job badly. There are two premises
               here, nothing is proved about either, and the only question is whether they can hold
               together:
                                                      25.2 The calorimeter protocol, as a specification   213


Premise 25.2 (Structural dissipation pricing). A dissipation is a function δ from
instructions to naturals, counted in units of k B T ln 2. The floor, a hypothesis separate
from Premise 3.5 of Section 3: every instruction that collapses a yes-or-no property
has δ(i ) ≥ 1, where i collapses P when some state with P false is sent to a state with
P true, every state with P true is sent to one with P true, and some two different
states are sent to the same state. The calibration: δ(i ) ≤ cost(i ) for every instruction.
Neither is proved anywhere; the theorems below ask whether they can hold together.

   The last clause of the floor is there because the first two don’t make a step forget
anything on a machine with infinitely many states. INC A sends a state with A = 0
to one with A = 1 and keeps every positive A positive, so it raises “A ≥ 1” one
way, and it is one-to-one. Any one-to-one map that misses some state does the same
to the property “is in my image”. Its input can be read back off its output, so it
loses no entropy, and Landauer’s premise as this book takes it then puts no heat
floor under it at all. It pays in fresh states instead: INC moves every value of A
up one, Hilbert’s front desk, and only a counter with no top can keep doing it. A
thermal implementation can dissipate under the model-specific conditions above;
the premise only sets no floor under it. A floor read without the last clause would
charge a whole unit. On a finite machine the clause asks nothing extra. A step that
sends one false state into the true ones and keeps every true state true puts one
more state into the true set than the set holds, so two of them land on the same
state. And on any machine the property comes along for the ride: an instruction
that sends two different states a and b to one collapses the property “is reached from
a in one or more steps”, since one of a and b lies outside it and lands in it. So the
floor is a price on merging, one unit for every instruction that merges, read as heat.


25.2 The calorimeter protocol, as a specification

What follows is how one would measure this, and it’s here because it makes the
boundary exact. Take a two-state register with energies zero and a gap, a population
p in the excited state, and the simplest possible rate equation for how p changes
in one time step (a rate for climbing into the excited state, a rate for falling out
of it, and the length of the step). For the canonical reset (population one half to
zero, with the rates chosen so the equation closes), the heat delivered to the bath is
exactly half the gap. Choose the gap to be twice kT ln 2 and that heat is exactly kT
ln 2.
    Read that twice, because the temptation is to read it as a derivation of Landauer’s
number and it’s the opposite. The gap was chosen to make the heat come out as
Landauer’s number. The same populations and rates with half that gap give half
the Landauer value, and there’s a theorem exhibiting identical population dynamics
214   25 From “The counter against physics”


               with different heat. The rate equation fixes the shape; the energy scale is a selected
               Hamiltonian (the physicist’s name for the rule that says what energy each state has),
               and selecting it is not deriving it.
                  The canonical reset is a bookkeeping toy, and its rates show it. A register that
               trades heat with a bath at temperature T has rates in detailed balance: the rate up
               over the rate down is e−∆/k B T , which at the gap 2k B T ln 2 is 1/4. The canonical
               rates are 0 and 1, and the factor e−∆/k B T is never 0, so no gap and no temperature
               put them in that ratio. With rates that are in it, a relaxation at a fixed gap moves
               toward the equilibrium population 1/(1 + e∆/k B T ) and, with steps short enough
               not to overshoot, never goes below it. At the gap 2k B T ln 2 that population is 1/5.
               The toy has no work term either; nothing in it pays for the squeeze. Part (4) of the
               theorem below says what follows from that: at the gap k B T ln 2 the toy books less
               heat than Landauer’s bound asks for the same squeeze from one half to zero, so the
               toy has no second law.

               Definition 25.3 (The two-state protocol). For a gap ∆, the register’s mean energy
               at excited population p is ∆ · p. One step of the rate equation is
                                         p′ = p + δt k01 (1 − p) − k10 p ,
                                                                         

               and the heat to the bath over a step from p to p′ is defined as ∆p − ∆p′ . The
               canonical reset takes p = 12 , δt = 1, k01 = 0, k10 = 1, ending at p′ = 0, and counts
               one logical event.



               Theorem 25.2 (The heat is fixed by the chosen gap). (1) The canonical numbers
               satisfy the rate equation. (2) For every ∆, the canonical reset’s heat is ∆/2. (3) For
               all k B , T, the gap ∆ = 2k B T ln 2 gives heat k B T ln 2. (4) If k B > 0 and T > 0, the
               heat is below k B T ln 2 exactly when the gap is below 2k B T ln 2; at the gap k B T ln 2
               it is half of k B T ln 2. (5) There are two different gaps for which the same canonical
               populations satisfy the rate equation and the heats differ.

               Proof. (1) Half the population starts excited. The upward rate is zero, so none enters
               the excited state; the downward rate is one for a step of length one, so the entire
               excited half leaves. Substitution gives 21 + 1 · (0 − 12 ) = 0.
                  (2) The register began with mean energy ∆/2 and ended with zero. The defined
               heat is their difference, ∆/2.
                  (3) Choose ∆ = 2k B T ln 2. Half of that gap is k B T ln 2.
                  (4) Asking whether ∆/2 < k B T ln 2 is the same as multiplying both sides by two
               and asking whether ∆ < 2k B T ln 2. At the particular gap k B T ln 2, part (2) gives
               half that value. With positive k B , T it is strictly smaller because ln 2 is positive.
                                                      25.2 The calorimeter protocol, as a specification   215


   (5) Take gaps one and two. The population equation in part (1) contains neither
gap, so the same populations solve it in both cases. Their heats are one half and one
respectively. The rate equation alone did not select between them.
   A reset that pays its way is driven. Raise the gap a little, let the register settle
with rates in detailed balance, raise it again, and so on, starting from a gap of zero,
where the two states are alike and the population is one half. Raising the gap while
the population holds is work done on the register; settling at the new gap is heat
to the bath. That’s the textbook way to wipe a bit slowly, and here it is with its
numbers worked out.

Definition 25.4 (The driven reset). Write kT for k B T. At gap ∆ the equilibrium
excited population is π (∆) = 1/(1 + e∆/kT ) and the register’s free energy is F (∆) =
−kT ln(1 + e−∆/kT ). Rates satisfy detailed balance at gap ∆ when k01 = k10 e−∆/kT .
A driven reset along a schedule of gaps g0 , g1 , . . . , gn takes, at step m, the gap from
gm to gm+1 with the population held at π ( gm ), which is work π ( gm )( gm+1 − gm ),
                                                                                          
and then lets the register settle to π ( gm+1 ), which is heat gm+1 π ( gm ) − π ( gm+1 )
to the bath. The reset’s work and heat are the sums over its steps. The uniform
schedule from 0 to D in N steps has gm = Dm/N.



Theorem 25.3 (A driven reset pays kT ln 2 in work). (1) With rates in detailed
balance at gap ∆, one step of the rate equation takes p to π (∆) + 1 − δt (k01 +
k10 ) p − π (∆) ; when δt (k01 + k10 ) = 1 this discrete update lands on π (∆) from
                  

any p. (2) For any schedule, work minus heat is gn π ( gn ) − g0 π ( g0 ), the change in
the register’s mean energy. (3) If kT > 0 and the schedule never falls, the work is
at least F ( gn ) − F ( g0 ); if also every step raises the gap by at most h, the work is
                                                   
at most F ( gn ) − F ( g0 ) + h π ( g0 ) − π ( gn ) . (4) If kT > 0, D ≥ 0 and N ≥ 1, the
uniform reset from 0 to D in N steps does work between kT ln 2 − kTe− D/kT and
kT ln 2 + D/(2N ), and leaves an excited population of at most e− D/kT .

Proof. (1) Detailed balance gives k01 = (k01 + k10 )π (∆). In the rate equation, the
upward contribution minus the downward one is k01 − (k01 + k10 ) p. Substitute the
balance equation and this becomes −(k01 + k10 )( p − π (∆)). The update therefore
subtracts the fraction δt(k01 + k10 ) of the distance from equilibrium. What remains
is exactly
                    p′ = π (∆) + 1 − δt(k01 + k10 ) p − π (∆) .
                                                                

When that fraction is one, none of the distance remains: p′ = π (∆).
216   25 From “The counter against physics”


                  (2) Follow one raise from gap a to gap b. Its work is π ( a)(b − a) and its heat is
               b(π ( a) − π (b)). Subtract the heat from the work. The two occurrences of bπ ( a)
               cancel, leaving bπ (b) − aπ ( a), the final mean energy minus the initial mean energy.
               At the next raise, that final energy becomes the next initial energy. Adding over the
               schedule cancels every intermediate energy and leaves only gn π ( gn ) − g0 π ( g0 ).
                  (3) Differentiating the displayed free energy gives slope F ′ (∆) = π (∆). With
               kT > 0, this slope decreases as the gap rises. Over a ≤ b, the increase of F therefore
               lies between the widths times the two endpoint slopes:
                                     π (b)(b − a) ≤ F (b) − F ( a) ≤ π ( a)(b − a).

               The work uses the right-hand quantity, the slope before the raise. It is at least the
               free-energy increase. Its excess is at most (b − a)(π ( a) − π (b)). If the raise is at
               most h, that excess is at most h(π ( a) − π (b)).
                   Add those bounds over the schedule. Intermediate free energies cancel, and
               intermediate populations cancel in the excess bound. I obtain work between F ( gn ) −
               F ( g0 ) and that amount plus h(π ( g0 ) − π ( gn )).
                   (4) For the uniform schedule, h = D/N, π (0) = 1/2, and

                                    F ( D ) − F (0) = kT ln 2 − kT ln(1 + e− D/kT ).
               For x ≥ 0, 0 ≤ ln(1 + x ) ≤ x: it starts at zero and its slope 1/(1 + x ) is between
               zero and one. The free-energy difference is thus at least kT ln 2 − kTe− D/kT and at
               most kT ln 2. The excess from (3) is at most ( D/N )(1/2), giving the work bounds.
               Finally the denominator of π ( D ) = 1/(1 + e D/kT ) is at least e D/kT , so the remaining
               excited share is at most e− D/kT .
                   Try one finite step in units where kT = 1: start with gap zero and half the
               registers excited, then raise the gap to ln 2. The work is 12 ln 2. After settling, the
               excited share is 1/3, so the bath receives ( 12 − 13 ) ln 2 = 16 ln 2 and the registers
               retain 13 ln 2 more energy. Work equals those two amounts added together. A third
               are still excited: one such step has not wiped the bit.
                   The word “step” matters here. Part (1) is a discrete update of the population.
               Its multiplier can be zero. Continuous relaxation at fixed positive rates instead
               multiplies the distance from equilibrium by e−(k01 +k10 )t , which is positive at every
               finite time. Exact settling in the schedule is an idealisation; the algebra does not
               prove that a continuous bath completes it in one finite waiting period.
                   Here nobody picked the gap to make the number come out. Take D large and
               N much larger than D: the work closes in on kT ln 2 from both sides, and the
               population left in the excited state goes to zero. Perfect erasure is the limit, not
               the result at any finite gap. To reuse the original symmetric memory, the gap must
               also return to zero while transitions are suppressed. Lowering it at the final fixed
                                                                          25.3 What this section is   217


population recovers Dπ ( D ) of work and leaves that population unchanged. This
term tends to zero in the same limit. The reset of the population, restoration of
the trap and time needed for settling are separate parts of the physical protocol.
Detailed balance supplies the thermal premise; the displayed sums supply the work
and heat accounting for the idealised schedule.
   What ties it to a machine is that the protocol counts one logical event, by
definition, and one is what the small machine’s CERTIFY charges, the last of the
three paid acts behind a raised flag (Section 15). The tie is two numbers that
happen to match, the protocol’s one by definition and the machine’s the least the
record-layer premise lets CERTIFY cost, and nothing more. No theorem maps the
protocol’s two register states onto any state of the machine. And “heat to the bath”
here means the register’s mean energy before minus after, which quietly assumes the
bath gets exactly what the register loses; nothing here establishes that. So a device
test would have to measure the gap, the populations, the rates, and the bath heat
independently; separately establish that the device’s rates are in detailed balance
(that the up and down rates stand in the ratio the temperature dictates), which
the driven reset assumes and the canonical one breaks; and separately establish
that the device’s physical reset is the machine’s certify event. The driven reset is
an executable experimental specification. The canonical reset is a bookkeeping toy.
Neither is a measured device, and neither is an unconditional conversion of units of
cost to joules, and anyone who tells you otherwise has skipped the conditions.


25.3 What this section is

This section holds a calibration that’s a unit conversion; a pair of premises, consistent
on the steps the toll is about and inconsistent on the whole instruction set; a history
that makes any step injective by paying with a fresh state at every step; and a
measurement protocol that’s a specification with a selected scale. The physics in this
book is in Part I: Landauer’s principle as a named premise with its critics named
(Section 3), and conditional finite-time dissipation bounds, cited, and proved for
one model (Section 1). Here there’s accounting, and I’ve tried to call every line
by its right name. That’s the end of the example machine. What’s left, after one
suspicion I can’t prove, is how you’d break any of this, and where it stops.

26.1 The diagonal and the worked models

The diagonal argument supplies the contradiction inside a substrate that can repre-
sent the candidate’s flip and has the stated recursion property. Its fixed point makes
the proposed decision disagree with itself. An outside calculation of the natural-
number example does not supply an inside program for that substrate. Section 13
and the appendices state the input conventions separately for the natural-number
family, L, the counter programs and the host.
    The protocol examples in Section 17 isolate particular mechanisms: finality, a
charge on commitment, an attestation window, an inclusion proof and a bounds
check. Their statements follow the operations in those fragments. The pointer thesis
then asks why certification is the event to choose. It proposes a connection to records
copied and relied on by independent parties. The counting and window arguments
apply to any record meeting their premises; the thesis supplies the proposed reason
for this choice.
    The game checks in Appendix 32 give another worked construction. Four fixed
bits yield the classical bound by the displayed sign calculation. The matrix arguments
relate contraction, positive semidefiniteness and the score bound. The integer
checker implements its specified test on tallies with every pair sampled. Its earned
chain connects a raised flag to that test. The pinned-matrix test and the completion
test ask different questions: the appendix’s all-ones and PR-box examples make the
difference visible.
    Those connections are what the book has built: a count, a schedule, an ob-
servation and an earned record, with instructions carrying them through actual
runs.
