26.2 Sources and contributions
Appendix 28 sets out the sources and contributions item by item. Here is the short
form: the earlier results I use, the statements proved here, and the constructions
that put them to work.
   Borrowed, with names. As The point warned, I usually had the hunch first and
found these names after, and that order doesn’t make any of it mine. Everything
in Section 6 and the Afterword (every source is cited where it’s used), the category
theory (Eilenberg, Mac Lane) included. Minsky’s two-counter machines and the
constructions that prove their halting undecidable and compile computable functions
into them (Forster, Larchey-Wendling and coauthors). Turing, Church, Kleene,
Rice, Cantor, Gödel, the diagonal pattern, Lawvere’s unification, and later detailed
developments: Michael Norrish’s, Mario Carneiro’s, and Yannick Forster, Fabian
Kunze and Gert Smolka’s. Bell, CHSH, Tsirelson, Stephanie Wehner’s semidefinite
proof of Tsirelson’s bound (2006), NPA, Landau, Masanes, Fine, the elliptope,
and Echenim and Mhalla’s detailed treatment of Tsirelson’s bound. Landauer,
Bennett, Szilard, Maxwell, and their critics. The potential method (Tarjan) and later
treatments (Nipkow, Charguéraud and Pottier), Lamport’s clocks, Floyd and Hoare.
Gettier, Necula, Cook, Hartley, Shannon, Rissanen, Zurek. The guarantee about every
program: the reference monitor (Anderson), execution monitors (Schneider), and
monitors checked at every instruction and proved correct (Azevedo de Amorim and
coauthors). The counting under the permanence results: Poincaré’s recurrence, as
Zermelo used it. The seL4 and CompCert discipline. The facts under the permanence
results are Landauer’s own example and Shannon’s bounds, and “set a bit to one
whatever it held” is my way of saying his RESTORE TO ONE.
   What I proved, each inside its stated interfaces, is listed in the claims section
(Section 21) with what each stands on.
   Interpretation, which is mine and not proved. That structure is what a computation
establishes and the toll is the right thing to price. That certification is the pointer
event. That the record axis “belongs in the step.” Those are proposals, with the
arguments I’ve given, and the proposals are where I’d aim first if I were you.
CHAPTER 27




Things I had to learn first

I came in with none of the formal fields this work turned out to need. By the time
the argument had taken shape I’d needed enough of half a dozen of them that I had
to put down somewhere what those fields had words for. This appendix is that place.
None of it is the argument; it’s what I needed to be able to make it. Everything
below is what I understood after I went and read about it; I’m not claiming to teach
you these subjects, and if you already know a part, skip it.


27.1 Bits, bytes, and what a CPU sees
A bit is one digit that’s either zero or one, the smallest fact a machine can hold.
Everything else is bits stacked up. Eight of them make a byte, which lands you any
number from 0 to 255, and the first thing that genuinely surprised me is that the
same byte is also a letter: a convention called ASCII simply agrees to call 65 the
letter A, 48 the digit 0, 32 a space. Nothing in the byte knows which it is. The
meaning is a convention laid on top.
    You can see the convention’s seams with a pencil. Write 65 in binary: 1000001.
Knock off the first two digits and what’s left, 00001, is 1, and A is the first letter. B is
66, which ends in 00010, two. Now lower case: a is 97, which is 1100001, the same
pattern with one more bit switched on, the one worth 32. Every capital and its small
letter differ in exactly that bit, and that’s on purpose. The first version of the code,
in 1963, had no lower case at all; when lower case arrived, in the 1967 revision,
it was placed so the two cases differ by one bit, which made matching them, and
building keyboards and printers, simpler. Nothing in the byte knows it’s a letter, but
somebody made the letters easy for a circuit to tell apart.
    Everything in this subsection is about real CPUs. None of it belongs to the abstract
model, which has a kind of state and a step rule and nothing about widths or slots,
and the small machine of Section 15 needs very little of it: its two counters have no
width at all, and hold any whole number.
    A word is a chunk of bits the CPU treats as one unit, in 2026 usually 64 bits (0 to
about 18 quintillion) on a desktop or server and 32 (0 to about 4 billion) on many
small devices. The abstract model doesn’t care which width.

                                   220
                                                             27.2 The notation the formal parts use   221


   A register is a named storage slot inside the CPU that holds one word, the fastest
place to read or write. Memory is a longer list of word-sized slots accessed by index,
the address. The program counter is one specific register that tracks where in the
program the machine is; after every instruction it moves on to the next instruction
unless the instruction says jump. On the small machine it’s called the line number.
A stack pointer is a register pointing at the top of a region used for nested calls. A
port is a named channel for talking to the outside world.
   Modular arithmetic means counting wraps around: counting modulo 16, the
numbers run 0 to 15, and 17 wraps to 1. Bitwise operations work on the bits of a
value one position at a time: AND is 1 only when both bits are, OR when either is,
XOR (exclusive or) exactly when they differ. A logical shift moves bits left or right:
for unsigned values it multiplies or divides by a power of two, discarding overflow
on a fixed-width left shift and rounding down on a right shift. Popcount is the count
of one-bits in a value. Hexadecimal is base 16, digits 0 to 9 then A to F, written with
a 0x prefix: 0xFF is 255.
   A trap, on a CPU, transfers control to a handler; the interrupted program may
later resume. It can report an error or request a service. On the small machine a
trap is one bit that goes up when an instruction refuses, and then the core stops
moving. An ISA, instruction set architecture, is the list of every instruction a CPU
recognises, each identified by an opcode, a numeric tag the CPU decodes. The small
machine’s has six. The abstract model has an instruction set and a step rule, but
prescribes no binary opcode encoding.


27.2 The notation the formal parts use
I don’t think in these symbols, and I had to learn to read them. The natural numbers
are 0, 1, 2, and so on, written with a double-struck N; the integers add the negatives;
the reals are the whole number line. A function from a set A to a set B is written
with an arrow. For a finite set, vertical bars around it mean its size; for a number, its
absolute value. A big sigma is a sum over every member of a set. A delta before a
quantity means the change in it. An upside-down A means “for every”; a backwards
E means “there exists.” A double arrow is implication; a double-headed one is “if
and only if.” A small circle between two functions is composition, apply the right one
first. Angle brackets mean one of three things, and the sentence around them says
which: a pair of numbers packed into one, ⟨m, n⟩ (Appendix C.1); a dot product of
two vectors; or, around a random quantity, its long-run average, which I picked up
from the Bell-test papers. The circled times ⊗ in the quantum derivation combines
two systems: A ⊗ B acts as A on the first and B on the second; Appendix 33 gives
its entries. The integer check itself uses neither it nor any quantum system. A
222   27 Things I had to learn first


               superscript T on a matrix is its transpose, rows and columns swapped, and I with a
               subscript n is the identity matrix.


               27.3 Category theory beyond the sketch
               Section 6 sketches objects, arrows, composition, identities, associativity, and the
               identity laws. Beyond that: a functor is a structure-preserving map from one
               category to another, sending objects to objects and arrows to arrows while respecting
               composition and identities; think of it as a translation rule between two worlds
               of structure. An embedding is a functor that throws nothing away, and faithful is
               the precise version for arrows: two distinct arrows between the same two objects
               never land on one. Conservative is the version for inverses: if the image of an arrow
               has an inverse, the arrow already had one, so the functor can’t manufacture an
               isomorphism (an arrow with an inverse) that wasn’t there. The two properties are
               different, and neither gives the other. Read the natural numbers under addition as
               a category with one object and an arrow for each number, and the same for the
               integers. The inclusion of the first into the second is faithful, since distinct naturals
               stay distinct integers. It isn’t conservative: 1 has no inverse among the naturals, and
               its image has one, minus 1. The other way round, the functor from the integers
               onto the trivial one-arrow group is conservative, since every arrow of the integers
               already has an inverse. It isn’t faithful: it sends every integer to the same arrow. An
               embedding needs faithfulness specifically. A monoidal product combines two objects
               or two arrows into one, with a unit and laws that make the combining behave. An
               initial object has exactly one arrow into every object; the counter’s uniqueness in
               this book is a uniqueness-on-reachable-states fact, and no category-theoretic initial
               object is involved. Yoneda’s lemma was the piece that made the categorical engine
               snap into place for me (Section 24), and nothing in the formal development uses it.


               27.4 Bell-test vocabulary

               A correlation between two outcomes is the average of their product; with outcomes
               plus or minus one it lies between minus one and one, plus one meaning always
               match, minus one always differ. A correlator is one such average in a labelled setting,
               E00 for Alice’s setting 0 and Bob’s 0. The marginal of a joint distribution is the
               distribution of one side alone with the other averaged out; zero-marginal means
               each side’s outcome is plus or minus one equally often on average. A local hidden-
               variable model is one where both outcomes are fixed by a shared random variable
               agreed before the game plus each side’s own setting; Bell’s theorem says no such
               model produces certain correlations quantum mechanics does. No-signalling means
               neither side’s marginal depends on the other’s setting. Quantum-realisable means
                                                          27.5 Other terms I use without rebuilding   223


compatible with some quantum state and measurements; the NPA hierarchy gives a
ladder of necessary tests, each a positive-semidefiniteness condition on a moment
matrix. For the four correlators of this game alone, the first rung is already exact;
for the full table, each side’s own odds included, Chaturvedi’s 2026 preprint argues
that no finite rung is, even in this simplest case. The PR box is the no-signalling
correlation that wins the game every time, stronger than quantum mechanics allows.
Cauchy-Schwarz is the inequality that a dot product is bounded by the product of
the lengths. The Tsirelson bound is two root two, the largest game score quantum
mechanics allows; the classical bound is two and the PR bound is four. (If you
go looking for Boris Tsirelson’s 1980 paper, look under Cirel’son, the spelling the
journal printed, or you may not find it.) The elliptope is the set of correlators that
can be filled out into a positive-semidefinite matrix with ones down the diagonal.


27.5 Other terms I use without rebuilding
Provenance is the recorded chain of where an object came from; on the small
machine, a commitment’s provenance is the passing check that came before it
(Section 15). Simulation, in computer science, is one system imitating another’s
input-output behaviour; a Turing machine can simulate a system with effective finite
encodings of its states and moves and a computable step on those encodings, my
small machine included, by representing its state on tape, and the difference this
book is about is what a window on that tape shows. That’s real, and it raises a fair
question: if a tape can stand in for the state, what was the difference? The tape
carries a picture of the state, the way a photograph carries a fire, and a photograph
of a fire keeps you exactly as warm as any other photograph. The counter and the
toll live in the step rule. An encoding copies the bytes, and the step rule isn’t in
them. A Turing machine that bumps a ledger on its tape whenever its table sets its
flag keeps that ledger in its state, and a ledger kept in the state isn’t a price on the
move. The toll prices the machine’s moves: with one move, the next step its table
picks, every step costs at least one once the flag can go up at all, and with a move
for each row of the table, every row that can set the flag costs at least one wherever
it’s taken (Section 15.10). The ledger on the tape only simulates that. What no
machine can do is make a simulated rule binding just by writing it down on tape.
    Projection is a function that drops information on purpose. The window on the
small machine keeps three things, the line number and the two counters, which are
what Minsky’s machine already has, and that’s why projecting lands you back in
ordinary computation. It drops everything else, including the parts doing the new
work: the versions, the table of checked facts, the channel, the trap, the ledger and
the flag. Nothing is wrong with it, and it’s a perfectly good function; it just loses
you what made the state more than a pair of counters. When what’s left runs as
224   27 Things I had to learn first


               the machine underneath runs, reaches what it reaches and still can’t see the record
               or the ledger, the projection is a slice (Definition 30.1): every Thiele-complete
               machine has one onto two counters, its window, and every computer with a move
               for each two-counter instruction is a slice of a certified Thiele-complete machine
               (Theorem 30.4). A quotient is the operation of declaring two things the same when
               they agree on what you chose to look at and working with the classes instead. A
               projection does exactly that. Dropping fields one at a time also glues together states
               that the dropped fields kept apart. The fibre of a function over a value is everything
               the function sends to that value. An injection sends no two inputs to the same
               output; a surjection covers every output. Decidable means an algorithm always halts
               with a yes-or-no answer; keep the claim and the algorithm apart, since the algorithm
               decides the claim only once there’s a proof that its answer matches. Polynomial
               time means running time bounded by a power of the input size; P is the problems
               solvable that way, NP the problems where a candidate solution can be checked that
               way, and whether the two are the same is open.
                  “Kernel” has two uses here. In mathematics, the kernel of a map is what it sends
               to the identity; for a projection the useful version is the relation “these two states
               project to the same thing.” An operating-system kernel is the core of an operating
               system, as in the packet-filter example in Section 17.


               27.6 Matrices
               A matrix is positive semidefinite (PSD) when vT Mv ≥ 0 for every vector v; the game
               check is all about one matrix being PSD. The determinant of a square matrix is a
               single number that is zero exactly when the matrix squashes some direction flat.
               For a two-by-two matrix with rows ( a, b) and (c, d) it’s ad − bc. For a bigger one,
               expand along a row: for each entry, delete its row and column, take the determinant
               of what’s left, multiply by the entry, alternate the signs, and add up; the recursion
               bottoms out at two by two. A principal submatrix is what you get by choosing some
               of the indices and keeping only those rows and the same columns; its determinant
               is a principal minor. A symmetric matrix is PSD exactly when every principal minor
               is at least zero, every one of them, and the step from the game check to Tsirelson’s
               bound uses two of the three-by-three ones (Appendix 33.4). The Schur complement
               turns a question about a block matrix into a question about a smaller one. For a
               symmetric block matrix with corner block P, off-diagonal block Q and other corner
               R, it’s R − QT P−1 Q, and when P is positive definite the whole matrix is PSD exactly
               when that complement is (Lemma 33.6). Positive definite matters; invertible isn’t
               enough. Take P = −1 and every other entry zero: the complement is zero, which is
               PSD, and the whole matrix isn’t.
                                                           27.7 Borrowed ideas the book pushes on   225


27.7 Borrowed ideas the book pushes on
These get their full story in the chapter that uses them, and each gets a one-line
version here for when you’ve forgotten which one Landauer was.
    The pigeonhole principle: put more pigeons than holes and some hole holds two.
Section 3’s room with too few chairs is that, and so is the proof that, when only
finitely many states read yes, a permanent flag can only be raised by a move that
forgets something, two states landing on one. Landauer’s principle (Rolf Landauer,
1961): a step that wipes information, lowering the entropy of what a machine
holds by some number of bits, gives off mean heat at least Boltzmann’s constant
times the temperature times the natural log of two per bit, under a physical model
relating that logical entropy to the memory’s thermodynamic entropy. The bound is
a floor that going slowly doesn’t lower, and it isn’t all the heat: specified thermal
dynamics can require additional entropy production for a finite-time change of
distribution. In this book Landauer’s floor is always a premise with its name on
it. Nothing here derives it (Section 3, Section 19). Entropy, in Claude Shannon’s
sense: for a spread of chances over states, roughly the average number of yes-or-no
questions you’d need to pin the state down, so an even spread over n states has
log2 n bits. A spread, in this book, is just a list of chances that add up to one.
Maxwell’s demon (Section 5): a little creature that watches molecules and sorts fast
from slow, seemingly for nothing; Charles Bennett’s answer was that the looking
can be done without merging anything, so Landauer’s premise sets it no floor (it can
dissipate under the stated thermal-model assumptions, less the slower it goes), and
the floor lands when the demon wipes its notes to look again. The potential method
(Robert Tarjan, 1985): pay for a run with a quantity stored in the state, so a step’s
cost plus the change in the stored quantity is what you count; Section 7 reads the
toll as that method with one particular potential.
    The halting problem (Alan Turing, 1936, who essentially proved it without
stating it in this form; Stephen Kleene stated it in 1952, and Martin Davis seems
to have been the first to name it, in 1958, Section 13): no program decides, for
every program and input, whether it stops. Kleene’s recursion theorem (Stephen
Kleene, 1938): for any transformation of programs that can itself be computed, some
program computes the same partial function as its own transform. Its timing, storage
and records need not agree. Rice’s theorem (Henry Rice, 1953): any property that
depends only on what a program does, and isn’t true of all programs or of none,
can’t be decided by a program. The diagonal is the move under all three: build the
thing that does the opposite of what the table says about it (Section 13). L is a small
lambda calculus, a language made of nothing but functions applied to functions,
which Yannick Forster and Gert Smolka presented and which Section 13 uses as a
tool. A cryptographic hash is a short fingerprint of a longer piece of data, made so
226   27 Things I had to learn first


               that finding two inputs with the same fingerprint is supposed to be hard; a hash
               chain folds each new record into the fingerprint of the last, and a Merkle tree folds
               them pairwise into one root (Section 2, Section 17).
CHAPTER 28




What’s mine, and what isn’t

Every chapter of this book leans on somebody, and I’ve named them where I lean.
This appendix does a different job, and it’s the one where I’d most like to be caught
out, and caught out early. For each thing the book could be accused of claiming as
its own, it says what was already known, what this work actually defines, builds,
proves, or connects, and what kind of contribution that is. Five kinds, and they aren’t
the same claim: a new result (a statement nobody had); a new proof of something
known; an implementation (something built that runs); a synthesis (known pieces
put together under one question); and a conjecture or thesis (a claim stated and not
proved; a thesis ties defined notions to the world, so no proof can settle it). Every
kind can be worth doing. None of them is the others. And if you read down the
list and notice that “new result” hardly shows up, that’s good, and it means you’re
reading it right.
    Two warnings about my own evidence before the list. First, “I didn’t find it” is a
fact about my searches. The literature is bigger than my searches. I searched with
every tool I had, and some tools will cheerfully invent a citation if you let them, so
where it matters I say where I looked, and you should assume it may well be sitting
in a paper I’d need a few more lifetimes of homework to read. Second, nothing
below is inferred from how many theorems there are or from a good story about
who came before. A proof, if it’s right, tells you the statement is true. It doesn’t
tell you it’s new. A thousand correct proofs could be a thousand careful ways of
agreeing with Tarjan.
    After the prior-work map come the items themselves, fifteen of them, numbered
as their subsections are, in the order the book reaches them: what keeping takes
first, certification last.


The prior-work map

What I do and don’t know about prior work. I came to this with no formal training in
the fields it turned out to need, and I’ve read what I could reach, with every tool I
had. The prior-work map below is orientation. It doesn’t pretend to be scholarship.
For each piece of this book it says who did the thing it leans on, and what, if anything,

                                                  227
228   28 What’s mine, and what isn’t


              this development adds. A row that’s wrong gets fixed, and, as the end of Section 21
              says, I’d want to hear of one.
                  There’s a law about rows like these, and it’s a joke that keeps turning out true. In
              1980 the statistician Stephen Stigler proposed that no scientific discovery is named
              after the person who first found it, and then credited the idea to the sociologist
              Robert K. Merton, who had been writing about fights over scientific priority since
              1957. So Stigler’s law is an example of itself, which is either the best joke in the
              history of science or a very tidy accident. Is it a law? No, it’s a pattern, and nobody
              proved it. But this book has already tripped over it twice, in The point at the front
              and in Section 3: the pigeonhole principle wears Dirichlet’s name, and Leurechon
              had it two centuries earlier. And I put my own name on a machine. By Stigler’s law,
              that makes it somebody else’s. The table below is my best attempt at finding out
              whose.
                  What I compare each time is what a result is about. Landauer’s bound is about
              heat, a physical quantity under a thermodynamic model. No Free Lunch (an
              optimisation result, in the list below the table) is about averages over a whole
              spread of problems. The toll is a floor on one kind of step, the step that turns the
              reading on. Rise-only pricing adds a second rule on top: charge nothing on a step
              that doesn’t turn it on. So the comparisons are about the shape of each result. They
              aren’t claims about who got somewhere first, and they don’t show that certification
              is the only event worth pricing.
                  The right-hand column uses words built in the body of the book (the latch, the
              fibre, the universal program, the integer check, the matrix test); the subsections
              after the table say where each one is built.

                   Everything in the right column is stated with its premises in the body of the book,
               and everything in the middle column is cited where it’s used. The table is the short
               form. The long form is the rest of this appendix, which takes each row, says what
               was already known with sources, and says what kind of addition each one is. Very
               little of the right column is a new result. Most of it is things built, new proofs of
               known things, and known pieces put together under one question.
                   A few more labels I went and looked up are neighbours. Some of them also sit in
               the table above, and one, the reference monitor, is the move the small machine’s
               step makes. I list them so you can see why I don’t think the toll is one of them under
               a new name. Each is a claim inviting correction.
               • Linear logic (Girard 1987) treats an assumption as a resource that gets used up.
                 It constrains what a program, or a proof, may consume. The toll constrains the
                 model’s step function instead. It’s the same instinct at a different layer.
               • Foundational proof-carrying code (Appel 2001) checks safety proofs against the
                 smallest trusted base it can: a logic plus the machine’s semantics. Here no proof
                 rides along with the program. The step rule writes the receipts as the run goes.
               • Session types (Honda 1993) constrain how programs talk to each other so the
                 conversation can’t go off script. The toll is about what one computation has paid
                 to establish, which is a different problem.
               • No Free Lunch (Wolpert and Macready 1997): averaged over every problem,
                 whatever an optimiser gains on some problems it gives back on others. That’s an
                                                                                           233


  average over a whole problem space, and the edge is paid for elsewhere. The toll
  charges one step in one run, with no refund anywhere.
• Kolmogorov complexity (Kolmogorov 1965): the description complexity of a
  string is the length of the shortest program that prints it, relative to a fixed
  description language. That’s a property of a finished string, with no steps and no
  moment of paying. The toll is a law about a step.
• Irreversibility cost and information distance (Zurek 1989; Bennett, Gács, Li,
  Vitányi and Zurek 1998; Li and Vitányi 1996): the fewest bits a reversible
  machine must take in and erase irreversibly to turn a string x into a string y,
  about C ( x | y) + C (y | x ), where C ( x | y) is the length of the shortest program
  that prints x given y; and Kolchinsky and Wolpert’s thermodynamic complexity
  of a Turing machine’s outputs (2020). These tie algorithmic information to steps
  and erasures, and they price a transformation between strings, or an output, in
  erased bits. The toll prices the rise of a chosen reading.
• Cook and Levin (Cook 1971; Levin 1973): checking can be cheap while finding
  looks expensive, and whether finding is really harder is the open P-versus-NP
  question, a gap in how difficulty grows with size. The toll puts a fixed floor under
  the one step that commits, however easy or hard the finding was.
• The thermodynamic cost of choosing (Rovelli 2024): settling a binary choice at
  temperature T dissipates at least kT ln 2, with no outside heat bath. That’s the
  closest thing to the toll itself I’ve found, because it charges a commitment. An
  object or an average isn’t what it prices. But it’s physics, indexed to a temperature,
  and its choice is unpredictable, a branching, and stable only for a while, where
  the toll prices a deterministic no-to-yes transition. On my side, finitely many
  yes-states and permanence are what force the raising move to merge states. The
  toll charges the commitment in a model of computation, with no temperature in
  it. It’s the closest neighbour, and it’s still a different thing.
• Persistent copies (Ouldridge and ten Wolde 2017; Ouldridge, Govern and ten
  Wolde 2017): making a copy that stays correlated with its original after the
  two come apart has a thermodynamic floor. The nearest physics I’ve found to
  “learning borrows, committing pays.” It’s work and free energy for a correlation,
  in chemistry; the toll is a count in a model of computation.
• Thermodynamics of finite automata (Ouldridge and Wolpert 2023): if the smallest
  automaton for a language has a letter that sends two states to one, recognising
  that language on a device that repeats one local step produces entropy, and if
  the smallest automaton only reshuffles, recognition carries no such floor. That’s
  Part I’s counting of states with a physical price on it, and one of their worked
  examples is the latch’s language turned inside out. It’s the closest neighbour of
234   28 What’s mine, and what isn’t


                  all to the road the toll is derived on; Section 3 says exactly what’s left over for
                  me.
               • CerCo, the Certified Complexity project of Amadio and coauthors, builds a verified
                 C compiler that carries the object code’s execution cost back up to annotations
                 on the source. There the cost decorates the program and survives compilation.
                 Here it lives in the model’s step rule.
               • Reference monitors and execution monitors (Anderson 1972; Schneider 2000;
                 Azevedo de Amorim and coauthors 2015). A small piece every action passes
                 through, enforcing a rule on every program. Schneider argued that a monitor
                 watching a run can enforce only rules whose breaking shows up at a finite point,
                 and “no certificate without an earlier passing check and commit” is one of those;
                 the micro-policies check a tag rule at every instruction, proved correct. That’s the
                 move the small machine’s step makes, so this one isn’t a neighbour at all: the
                 small machine’s step is a monitor of this kind. What’s added is the edition-bound
                 check-then-commit with a paid, permanent record on top, and the derivation
                 of why the payment can’t be zero. Kevin Hamlen, Greg Morrisett and Fred
                 Schneider (2006) went on to sort which rules monitors, program rewriters and
                 static checkers can each enforce, and they differ, which is one reason this book
                 doesn’t claim that every enforcement mechanism is a Thiele machine underneath.
               • Trusted components in replicated services (Chun and coauthors 2007; Levin and
                 coauthors 2009; Veronese and coauthors 2013; Decouchant and coauthors 2022;
                 Clement, Junqueira, Kate and Rodrigues 2012): a small record inside each replica
                 that a replica taken over still can’t make say two things, with a proof of exactly
                 what that buys. The record is theirs. The toll isn’t in their picture.
               • Ghost state (Owicki and Gries 1976; Filliâtre, Gondelman and Paskevich 2014):
                 variables added for a proof that can be erased without changing what the program
                 computes. The flag and the counter are ghost in that sense. The small machine’s
                 table of checked facts isn’t, because the step reads it and refuses on it, for every
                 program. The slice of Appendix 30 is that erasure on the lift, while its trap is
                 down, with two things on top: the erased layer still earns its record, and what’s
                 left can’t read the record or the ledger (Theorem 30.4).
               • Verifiable computation and proof-carrying data (Parno, Howell, Gentry and
                 Raykova 2013; Chiesa and Tromer 2010): a short proof, checkable by a stranger,
                 that a computation’s output is right. That establishes the claim for the stranger.
                 Producing it may require a witness and substantial computation; it does not
                 identify the original computing device unless that is part of what the proof
                 establishes. Another prover may reproduce it, the copier included. The record
                 here is about one machine’s own history. A proof like that is one way to carry the
                 local record out of the box.
                                                                                         235


• Commitments and entitlements (Brandom 1994): talk as score-keeping, tracking
  who is committed to what and who is entitled to it. Brandom’s entitlements can
  be defeated and can be passed on by testimony. Here entitlement is priced, step
  by step, and a copy carries it only as far as the copy is genuine. The words are
  the same and the machinery is different.
• Correspondence assertions (Woo and Lam 1993; Gordon and Jeffrey 2003). In
  protocol verification, “this end event must be preceded by a begin event for the
  same thing” is a correspondence assertion, and a type system can prove a protocol
  keeps it against any opponent. “No certificate without an earlier commit, and
  no commit without an earlier check of the same claim” has that shape. Theirs is
  proved about programs by typing. Mine is enforced by the step, which is the LCF
  comparison again, at the level of single instructions.
• Monotonic parallel composition (Åman Pohjola and Parrow 2016) and persistence
  in the pi-calculus (Palamidessi, Saraswat, Valencia and Victor 2006). Åman
  Pohjola and Parrow separate models where a neighbouring process can never
  stop a step from models that can solve consensus, and test-and-set, an instruction
  that writes a cell and reports what was there, is on the consensus side. Palamidessi
  and coauthors compare messages that are used up with messages that stay, and
  the end of their study is a reduction from Minsky machines. The small machine,
  put into Åman Pohjola and Parrow’s setting with threads pooled on one shared
  state (two states that have both moved jam), has a consensus process in their
  sense, so it lands on their non-monotonic side; their theorem and that placing
  are argued in a few lines in Section 15. The comparison with Palamidessi and
  coauthors’ persistence stays a comparison.
• Constructor theory (Deutsch and Marletto 2015; Marletto 2016; Marletto, Vedral
  and coauthors 2022; Violaris and Marletto 2022): physics as which tasks a device
  that runs in a cycle can do, with irreversible meaning possible while the inverse
  isn’t. It’s the nearest physics I know to “committing costs” stated for a cycling
  device. It’s quantum and approximate, and Part I’s counting of states is classical,
  finite and exact (Section 3).
• Accountability and fault detection (Haeberlen and Kuznetsov 2009; Küsters,
  Truderung and Vogt 2010; Treitz and Künnemann 2026): when an outside party
  can tell who misbehaved. Their windows are this book’s windows, and the trust
  they leave over, an honest log or a trusted step, is left over here too (Section 8).
• Transferability (Easwaran 2009): a deductive proof lets you learn from someone
  you don’t trust because you can check every step, and a probabilistic proof doesn’t.
  That’s the argument for carrying the object and re-running the check (Section 8).
236   28 What’s mine, and what isn’t


                   And then the silly question that list raises, which I’m going to chase anyway: why
               is an optimisation theorem named after lunch? Wolpert and Macready borrowed
               an old saying, “there ain’t no such thing as a free lunch,” and the saying is older
               than almost everyone who gets credit for it. American saloons in the 1800s put out
               a free lunch for anyone who bought a drink, and the drink paid for the lunch. A
               Washington newspaper was already saying in 1909 that somebody has to pay for a
               free lunch; a newspaper fable by Walter Morrow had the whole line in 1938; a book
               by Pierre Dos Utt already had the squeezed form, TANSTAAFL, as its title in 1949;
               Robert Heinlein’s 1966 novel The Moon Is a Harsh Mistress made that squeezed form
               famous; and the economist Milton Friedman put the saying on the cover of his 1975
               book of essays on public policy. I’d have credited Heinlein or Friedman. As far as
               anything I could find goes, neither coined it. That’s Stigler’s law again, at lunch,
               and it matters here because No Free Insight is named like a cousin of the saying,
               and the family resemblance misleads in one way. The saloon’s lunch was paid for
               somewhere else, in the price of the beer, and that’s Wolpert and Macready’s shape:
               a gain here, handed back over there. The toll isn’t that. On the ledger nothing
               gets paid somewhere else: the step that raises the flag pays, on the spot. A blank
               tape prepared by wiping an uncertain one may have an earlier reset cost under the
               thermal premises. A supplied blank alone does not prove that payment; the toll is
               the step that won’t hide its share that way.


               28.1 Every step is a fresh state, a merge, or a loop

               Known: the step law is the first-return argument every textbook on dynamics makes,
               and under it sits the pigeonhole. A one-to-one move that raises a reading and keeps it
               is Hilbert’s hotel, everybody moving up a room so that a room is left empty (Hilbert’s
               lecture course of 1924–25, made famous by Gamow in 1947), and a collection that
               can be matched one-to-one with a part of itself that leaves something out is exactly
               what Dedekind (1888) called infinite. Recurrence for reversible finite machines is
               Poincaré’s (1890) and Zermelo’s (1896) in its finite form. The noisy steps are the
               fact that a doubly stochastic chain has no closed class it can enter from outside, with
               Gibbs’s inequality for the entropy gap; the unitary steps are that a unitary preserving
               a finite-dimensional subspace preserves its complement; the bilateral shift is the
               standard example of what goes wrong without finite dimension. Heat against time is
               Bennett’s Brownian computer (1982) with Schnakenberg’s formula for the entropy a
               biased walk hands its surroundings; the bounds for finite-time changes of probability
               distributions under specified stochastic dynamics belong to finite-time stochastic
               thermodynamics (Schmiedl and Seifert, 2007; Shiraishi, Funo and Saito, 2018); and
               the trade of space for time by recomputing is Bennett’s (1989). The measurements
               are Bérut and coauthors’ (2012), Jun, Gavrilov and Bechhoefer’s (2014), Hong and
                                                   28.2 The universal floor and the potential method   237


coauthors’ (2016) and Gaudenzi and coauthors’ (2018). Mine: the reading, that
the write of a 0 on a cell is a merge and the toll is the price of something being the
case; putting those old facts side by side as one law with three columns (a fresh
state, a merge, a loop that makes nothing new) and no fourth, for deterministic runs,
beside separate preservation theorems for the stated noisy and quantum models; the
merge-or-fresh-state dichotomy for a reading that stays up, with no finiteness in it;
and the exact count of states for a bounded memory, M ≥ 1 + ( T + 1)( N − 1), with
a building that reaches it (Section 3, Section 1); and that a merge seen in a coarse
description forces, in any finer description it’s exact about, a fine merge, a fine
step out of the set, or infinitely many fine states (Theorem 1.10). Kind: synthesis
and a reading, with the count of states and the every-scale theorem as small exact
lemmas. The mathematics is old. What I’d defend is the arrangement: endless
memory, history-keeping and reversible machines aren’t a way out of what keeping
takes, they’re the fresh-state column, which dodges only the floor a merge adds and
does not settle the implementation’s finite-time dissipation; and finiteness is what
takes that column away.


28.2 The universal floor and the potential method
Known: the potential method of amortized analysis, a function on states whose drop
bounds the cost of a run, is Tarjan’s (1985). Later developments include Nipkow’s
accounts (2015 and 2019) and Charguéraud and Pottier’s time credits (2019). It
has been built into type systems for automatic resource analysis (from Hofmann
and Jost, 2003, and Hoffmann and coauthors, 2011, onward), and recast in cost-
aware type theories (Niu, Sterling, Grodin and Harper, 2022). Mine: choosing the
potential that is one when a designated reading says no and zero when it says yes,
so that “the crossing costs” (the toll) and “every run that crosses paid” (the floor) are
the potential method’s two halves (Section 7), and its count, one unit per crossing
(Observation 2.7), the same sum read crossing by crossing. Kind: a known technique,
used with one particular potential. The choice is the reading, and it’s the point: it
makes “was certified” something the account must carry. The numbers come with it.
A natural-number potential, as defined here, that reads only the reading has to drop
by exactly one from no to yes, or its inequality says something other than the toll
on a machine of two states and one move; so it’s one at no and zero at yes, up to
a constant added to both. The theorem is not new mathematics. If it looks clever,
that’s Tarjan’s cleverness; my part was picking which reading to plug in.
   Go back to Tarjan’s paper and there’s a sentence that sits right on the line this
book draws. He opens with the dictionary (to amortize is to put money aside at
intervals, as in a sinking fund) and then gives two views of the method. In the
banker’s view the computer is coin-operated, and each operation is handed credits
238   28 What’s mine, and what isn’t


               to spend or save; in the physicist’s view, which he credits to Daniel Sleator, every
               state has a potential and you count its changes. Then he stops to say that the credits
               are only an accounting device: the programs that actually run contain no mention
               of credits or debits. That’s exactly right for what he wanted, a bound proved about
               a program from outside. It’s also exactly the gap. In his method the coins live in the
               proof. The one move I chose was to put them in the step, where no program takes
               a step without it being counted. The arithmetic is the same, and only the place is
               different.


               28.3 The toll from finiteness, permanence, and a price on merges

              Known: that steps which merge states (no single-valued inverse) are what Landauer’s
              principle charges for is Landauer’s (1961), sharpened by Bennett (1973, 1982, 2003;
              the words “the erasure of a bit or the merging of two computation paths” are from
              the 2003 abstract); generalizations and the dependence on the input distribution are
              Maroney’s (2009), Sagawa’s (2014), Wolpert’s; the critique is Earman and Norton’s
              and Norton’s. Write-once memory is an old topic in coding theory (Rivest and Shamir,
              1982), where the question is reuse, and what it takes is counted in cells, which in
              this book’s words is paying in fresh states; heat and prices never come up, and Chris
              Heegard (1985) worked out the capacity of permanent memory with noise, again
              a question of how much fits. Statisticians priced permanence on finite memory in
              their own currency: in Hellman and Cover’s analysis of hypothesis testing with finite
              memory (1970), a machine that settles its answer by falling into states it never leaves
              does worse than one that doesn’t, so a decision held for good costs memory states.
              Heat never enters their analysis. And the counting under the theorem is older than
              any of that, twice over. Theorem 3.1 is the finite, discrete cousin of the recurrence
              argument Zermelo, using Poincaré’s theorem of 1890, turned on Boltzmann in 1896,
              here aimed at a reading. Zermelo aimed it at entropy, and Bocchieri and Loinger
              gave the quantum recurrence in 1957. In automata form it is classical: a finite
              machine whose moves are all one-to-one reshuffles recognises only group languages,
              and the inputs that raise a permanent reading form a language that is never one of
              those unless it is empty (Pin’s 1992 paper on reversible automata is the reference
              I’d check; automata theorists treat the step as folklore), and going quantum doesn’t
              change it (Brodsky and Pippenger, 2002: quantum finite automata that measure
              once, at the end, accept with bounded error exactly the group languages). The
              closest of all is recent. Ouldridge and Wolpert (2023) give a proof that if the smallest
              automaton for a language has a step that merges states, every automaton for it
              does, and argue that recognising it on a device that repeats one local step carries an
              entropy floor, while a language whose smallest automaton only reshuffles carries no
              such floor; one of their worked examples is the strings with no b, the complement
                                      28.3 The toll from finiteness, permanence, and a price on merges   239


of the latch’s language. That’s the core with a physical price already on it. And the
logarithm in the heat floor matches the absolute-irreversibility bound of Murashita,
Funo and Ueda (2014), which Manzano, Kardeş, Roldán and Wolpert (2024) carried
over to computation. The logarithms coincide for the stated counts. This does not
supply a physical reverse process: absolute irreversibility needs forward and reverse
path probabilities that the logical map alone does not specify. Constructor theory
states a cousin of the same core for devices that run in a cycle: Marletto, Vedral
and coauthors (2022) and Violaris and Marletto (2022) built an irreversible task
out of the quantum homogeniser and argued that erasure may dissipate more than
Landauer’s bound. Section 3 tells all of it. The price isn’t mine: Li and Vitányi priced
each erased bit (1996), and Demaine and his coauthors priced named instructions
that destroy bits (2016). Mine is what a flat price of at least one on every merging
move does to a reading that stays: it turns “the flip merges” into “the flip costs” on
any machine with finitely many states reading yes, whether or not anybody’s physics
is right, and gives the toll (Observation 3.3); what comes with that form, namely
the characterisation of the moves every merge-pricing schedule must charge (the
merging moves, so the core again in pricing words), the merge-or-revoke dichotomy
for a single move, the squeeze and entropy forms for a permanent flip, and a built
counterexample for each premise (Section 3). The counterexample for finiteness is
the endless strip, the machine that keeps every history, and it says what the premise
is for: the strip raises its reading with no merge by taking a fresh state at every step,
so a price list that charges merges and nothing else lets it certify at a cost of zero.
That’s a price list that charges nothing for the fresh states, not a machine that got out
of paying, and its physical cost remains implementation-dependent. Kind: the core
is classical, and somebody else’s: Zermelo’s in physics, folklore in automata theory,
with Pin’s paper as the place to check it. The pricing form isn’t wholly mine either:
priced in entropy, for automata, it’s Ouldridge and Wolpert’s. They derive the price
from the physics. An assumed price is older than mine too: Li and Vitányi (1996)
took a unit charge per irreversibly erased bit, with reversible steps priced at zero, as
axioms of a model of computation, and Demaine and his coauthors (2016) put that
charge on named instructions of the random-access machine. What’s mine is the toll
derived from a price on merging moves for a reading that stays, its single-move and
counted forms, and the counterexamples. The physics is a named premise and is
Landauer’s. Searches: the core turned up in the places just named, and its physical
price in Ouldridge and Wolpert; I didn’t find the derived toll stated anywhere I
looked, and nobody’s search covers a whole field, mine least of all.
240   28 What’s mine, and what isn’t


               28.4 Learning borrows, committing pays
              Known: that a measurement can be made without merging anything, so that
              Landauer’s premise sets it no floor, and that the floor lands when the demon wipes
              its notes to look again, is Bennett’s resolution of Maxwell’s demon (1982), with the
              quantitative trade-off by Sagawa and Ueda (2009); that the entropy can be split
              between measuring and erasing in more than one way is Fahn’s (1996), and that
              the work an erasure takes depends on what the eraser already knows is del Rio
              and colleagues’ (2011). The closest physics I found to the line between learning
              and committing is Ouldridge and ten Wolde’s fundamental costs of persistent copies
              (2017; with Govern, 2017), copies that stay correlated with their originals after the
              two come apart; theirs is a work and free-energy bound for a correlation in chemistry,
              and Section 5 says how it differs. The sharp chemical form, that a persistent copy
              grown at equilibrium carries no information about its template, is Poulton, ten
              Wolde and Ouldridge’s (2019). Mine: the four-state demon, where copying a hidden
              bit onto a display known to be blank merges nothing and wiping the display merges
              two pairs of states; the second copy that hands the display back blank while the
              original is still there; the three-state ring and the two-state impossibility (Section 5);
              and the certainty bound (Theorem 5.5), which splits the uncertainty about the start
              into the part a run destroyed, paid for in units of cost, and the part the end state
              still holds. That split is Shannon’s chain rule (1948), and bounds of this kind on
              the actual spread are Maroney’s (2009) and Sagawa’s (2014); mine is reading it on
              the ledger, about what an observer saw. Kind: finite models and one bookkeeping
              identity illustrating a known physical point inside my ledger, and not a new physical
              claim. Bennett did the hard part in 1982. My four dots put the line where you can
              work it through yourself: the copy owes no floor and can cost nothing, but it still
              takes something. Its physical cost depends on the dynamics and protocol. And it
              uses up a blank page, which is a fresh state; while the original is still there it can
              hand the page back, so learning borrows. Resetting the uncertain page without
              using its correlations has the stated floor. A supplied blank alone does not prove a
              previous payment. Committing makes the record permanent, so there’s nothing to
              hand back, and with finitely many states it has to merge.


               28.5 The counter as a receipt
              Known: one-way counters that give order and termination are old: Turing’s 1949
              note on checking a large routine, Floyd’s ranking functions (1967), Lamport’s clocks
              (1978). Carrying a run’s cost beside its result is the writer cost of Danner, Licata and
              Ramyaa (2015) and of Danner and Licata (2022), and the graded effect semantics
              of Katsumata (2014), with a working-language version in Orchard, Liepelt and
                                                       28.6 Windows that can’t price what they hide   241


Eades (2019). The receipt side of time credits is Mével, Jourdan and Pottier’s
(2019): in their extension of the Iris logic every step uses up a credit and produces a
receipt, receipts prove lower bounds, and enough of them prove that a counter can’t
overflow in any feasible time, which is the closest published neighbour of a total
that only climbs. Mine: putting three properties side by side (the total only climbs,
it equals the sum of the charges on every path including the failures, and it is the
only such total, on the states a run from the start reaches, for a given price list), the
argument for any system with a counter and a price list, and the sum proved from
the small machine’s own step (Section 7, Section 15). Kind: synthesis; the abstract
statements are proved for any system with a counter and a price list, and on the
small machine the sum is proved too. Against the graded frameworks the answer is
split: the counter is their writer exactly, and their type systems, bounding relations
and before-the-run checking of grades are not embedded, because the grade here
is written by the step during the run (Section 7). For the well-graded, loop-free
programs defined in Section 7, on runs that do not trap, the counter’s rise equals
the fixed grade. The loop built there costs its input value, so no fixed whole number
is its exact grade on every input; an input-dependent grade is exact. The arguments
are given there. Cost-aware type theories such as calf allow grades that depend on
the inputs.


28.6 Windows that can’t price what they hide
Known: a function of an observation is constant on the observation’s fibres; that’s
elementary, and the systems version of it is Moore’s distinguishing experiments
(1956), Kalman’s observability (1960), and the completeness question of abstract
interpretation (Cousot and Cousot, 1977; Giacobazzi, Ranzato and Scozzari, 2000);
in control theory it is diagnosability and opacity of discrete-event systems (Sampath
and coauthors, 1995; Saboori and Hadjicostis, 2007), where a collision is a hidden
event no observer can diagnose without delay; in economics it is the hard-evidence
view of mechanism design (Green and Laffont, 1986; Bull and Watson, 2007), where
what a mechanism can tell apart is limited by the evidence on offer; and its sharp
form for fingerprints is Naor and Rothblum’s lower bound for checking a stored file
(2005); in distributed systems it is Haeberlen and Kuznetsov’s (2009), that only
omission and commission faults can be detected from outside, and in philosophy it
is Easwaran’s transferability (2009). Mine: stating the pricing version (no window
price is exact across a collision), exhibiting a colliding pair for the small machine’s
window, and the theorem that every certified Thiele-complete machine has two runs
from one clean start that end looking the same through the window its base shows,
one certified and one not (Section 8, Section 15). Kind: an elementary observation
about what a window hides, plus specific facts about the machine and the definition.
242   28 What’s mine, and what isn’t


               28.7 Structural entitlement and its bound
               Known: decision-tree counting bounds (a tree that tells N cases apart has depth at
               least log2 N); certifying algorithms that hand over checkable certificates (McConnell,
               Mehlhorn, Näher and Schweitzer, 2011); proof-carrying code (Necula, 1997); the
               gap between justified true belief and knowledge (Gettier, 1963). Mine: the definition
               of entitlement as an object, a checked relation, and a paid event on the record, and
               the count that, given lists, a tree and a covering, a run that paid for the tree’s depth
               pays at least the index bits it commits to (Section 9). Kind: synthesis; the counting
               idea is standard, the assembly and its vocabulary are this work’s, the pricing policy
               is assumed (nothing derives it), and the count is proved in its cost form over any
               Thiele machine, and for the record moves of every certified Thiele-complete machine
               on a run from a clean start, with the earned chain in the run, a floor that takes the
               run’s own checks in place of a tree, and a counterexample showing the covering
               can’t be dropped (Section 22.5); and in a form where the run makes the posterior
               from a prior, every drop is made by a check the run passed, and the index bits are at
               most k times the cost when each passing check keeps a 2−k share, a hypothesis the
               same counterexample shows is needed (Theorems 16.3 and 22.16).


               28.8 The record axis is a latch

              Known: monotone, write-once state is old: the latch (Eccles and Jordan, patent
              filed 1918, paper 1919), sticky bits (Plotkin, 1989), grow-only state in replicated
              data types (Shapiro and coauthors), history variables and stuttering in refinement
              (Abadi and Lamport, 1991), append-only authenticated sets for transparency logs,
              which fit revocation as a once-only event (Tomescu and coauthors, 2019), and, in
              program logic, monotonic state, where a property witnessed once on state that only
              moves forward can be recalled at any later point, with a soundness proof and a
              working home in F⋆ (Ahman, Fournet, Hriţcu, Maillard, Rastogi and Swamy, 2018):
              the type-theory name for a record that stays up. Past the finite case, inductive and
              quasi-inductive definitions (Richter and Aczel, 1974; Burgess, 1986) and the flag
              trick of infinite time Turing machines (Hamkins and Lewis, 2000) are the transfinite
              versions of a record that only grows and a toggle, and Danos and Krivine’s (2004)
              reversible calculus needs an action declared irreversible in order to commit. Mine:
              the exact statement that a record driven by the computation that never switches
              off is, over any base, a latch on one event, with its converse and a counterexample
              for each of the two conditions, and the argument that base-driven records agree
              up to the price list while the stronger uniqueness fails (Section 11). Kind: a small
              structural lemma, I think not stated before in this form (I found no prior statement
                                                                        28.9 The diagonal and L   243


in the searches I ran), built from old notions. The proofs show why the record must
latch and why its price list remains a separate choice.


28.9 The diagonal and L
Known: Kleene’s recursion theorem and Rice’s theorem; Lawvere’s fixed-point form
(1969) and Yanofsky’s retelling (2003); later detailed developments of recursion
theorems and Rice (Norrish, 2011; Carneiro; Forster, Kunze, Smolka and coauthors,
including L itself, 2017, and their library of undecidability results). Mine: the
diagonal stated once over any substrate that supplies a recursion theorem; and Rice
and halting proved again for L from its reduction rules (Section 13); and Rice’s and
Kleene’s theorems proved for the small machine’s host, with Rice’s on every input
of the two-counter machine itself, Kleene’s there with inputs written as powers of
two, and Kleene’s refuted for plain inputs (Appendix 29.2). Kind: new proofs of
known theorems of computability, for particular machines. The theorems hold for
any model with universality and s-m-n, and the work is in building those for each
machine; the refutation on plain inputs is a small fact about two counters, resting
on Schroeppel’s.


28.10 An example machine that meets the definition

Known: two-counter machines and the undecidability of their halting are Minsky’s
(1961, 1967), developed further by Forster, Larchey-Wendling and coauthors (2019,
2020). The guarantee about every program is older than any of this, and it isn’t mine
either. It’s the reference monitor (Anderson, 1972): a check that sits in the path of
every operation, so no program gets around it. Schneider (2000) worked out what
a monitor that watches a run can enforce: only safety properties, the kind a bad run
breaks at some finite moment, and “no CERTIFY without an earlier earned COMMIT”
is one of those. Azevedo de Amorim and coauthors (2015) verified monitors that
check tags at every instruction. The shape of “earned,” a certificate nothing can
make except through the rules, is Milner’s LCF, where a theorem is a value only
the logic’s rules can build (Gordon, Milner and Wadsworth, 1979; Gordon, 2000);
the small machine is that architecture at the level of single instructions. “No certify
without an earlier commit” is also a correspondence assertion in the sense of Woo
and Lam (1993), which Gordon and Jeffrey (2003) check with a type system, and a
store of commitments that the rules of a dialogue keep, each speaker’s updated by
the rules as the moves are made, is Hamblin’s (1970, chapter 8, on formal dialectic);
the difference from all three is again that here the step enforces it. A check that
blesses a value and a write that takes the blessing away is close kin to dynamic
taint tracking, whose labels go back to Denning’s lattice model of information flow
244   28 What’s mine, and what isn’t


              (1976), with the blessing itself, controlled endorsement, given a type system and a
              security theorem by Cecchetti, Myers and Arden (2017), and what a monitor can
              enforce with bounded memory is Talhi, Tawbi and Debbabi’s question (2008). A
              small monotone record inside each replica of a replicated service is A2M (Chun
              and coauthors, 2007), TrInc (Levin and coauthors, 2009), MinBFT (Veronese and
              coauthors, 2013) and Damysus (Decouchant and coauthors, 2022), and Clement,
              Junqueira, Kate and Rodrigues (2012) worked out, with proofs, what such a record
              buys and what it doesn’t. The signature route to the same goal, which needs no
              trusted step, is IA-CCF (Shamis and coauthors, 2021): replicas sign and log their
              protocol messages in the ledger, so blame lands on individual members however
              many of them misbehave. And “check once, issue a capability the system trusts, rely
              on it later” is built and deployed in Garg and Pfenning’s proof-carrying file system
              (2010), whose logic lets a policy’s consequences change with time and state, and
              in Sirer and coauthors’ logical attestation for the Nexus operating system (2011),
              unforgeable statements about programs used as credentials; those are the closest
              systems I’ve found to check, commit, certify. Mine: one worked example, a machine
              of six instruction kinds on two counters, proved to meet the definition so that the
              definition is not empty, with a version for each counter, a bounded fact table and
              a channel; its theorems that, from a clean start, a committed fact was checked
              first about the same edition, that nothing is forged, that nothing is evicted, that
              with the flag up from a clean start the channel names a fact an earlier passing
              COMMIT committed, and a later commit can replace it, as a two-commit run shows,
              and that a run from a clean start to a raised flag adds at least three to the ledger,
              with three reached by a run whose check could have failed (the step doesn’t police
              trivial checks, so one that can’t fail costs three too); the same theorems for any
              property language with an exact equality test whose checker is proved to say yes
              exactly when the claim holds, a sorted list read out of a counter included; and the
              step-for-step match with the two-counter machine, so halting is undecidable for it
              (Section 15). Kind: an instance of monitor enforcement with two things on top that
              I didn’t find put together where I looked: the check is bound to the edition of the
              thing it checked, and a toll is paid for the commitment. The first of those alone is
              old: checking at commit time that nothing you read has changed since is optimistic
              concurrency control (Kung and Robinson, 1981), and each counter’s version here is
              that scheme’s version number. What I didn’t find is that check put together with a
              toll. The universality and the undecidability are borrowed, and the other properties
              are short consequences of the step rule. Whether the design is new I can’t say, and I
              make no claim beyond the proofs.
                             28.11 Thiele-complete and certified Thiele-complete, defined so a clock fails   245


28.11 Thiele-complete and certified Thiele-complete, defined so a clock fails
Known: Turing completeness, and the observation every theorist makes in four
seconds, that a toll alone is cheap: a machine that charges for every step pays
any toll. Enforcement mechanisms have been compared and sorted before, and
they aren’t one notion seen several ways: James Morris (1973) separated access
limitation from authentication in programming languages, and Kevin Hamlen, Greg
Morrisett and Fred Schneider (2006) classified which policies execution monitors,
program rewriters and static analysis can each enforce, and they differ; Wai Wong
(1995), on a neighbouring track, recorded and checked HOL proofs so a theorem
could carry its derivation. Mine: a definition of Thiele-complete with four clauses
that say nothing about what the record is about (computing that runs every program,
costs nothing and can’t touch the record, while every other move costs one; a record
that stays; blank starts; and some run whose record tells two starts apart), with the
proofs that a clock and a silent machine fail it, that each clause is needed, that every
universal base becomes one by adding marks, and that every machine meeting it
hides its record and ledger from its own window (Section 17.2); and a definition
of certified Thiele-complete with four clauses (a universal base that can’t touch
the record and a record that stays up once it’s up, an earned record, exact prices,
nothing for the base and one for each of the three record acts, and some claim whose
check can fail), the proofs that every clock-style machine and a paid latch and a
silent machine fail it while the small machine, the same machine over any property
language with an exact equality test, a checker proved to say yes exactly when the
property holds and a property that holds of one number and fails of another, and
the universal program’s host meet it, that the four clauses are independent, each
failed by some machine with a two-counter machine inside that meets the other
three (Theorem 22.9), and the theorem that every machine meeting it hides its
record and ledger from its own window (Section 15), with the theorem that it is
exactly Thiele-complete with clean starts, the earned chain and a claim that could
have failed added (Theorem 22.3). Kind: definitions with short consequences. The
window theorem is the fibre fact of item 6 plus one observation, that a universal
base prints any window at no cost on the ledger. I’m not claiming the definition is
the only reasonable one, or that LCF kernels, reference monitors and proof-carrying
code are all certified Thiele-complete machines in disguise; Hamlen, Morrisett and
Schneider’s sorting says such mechanisms genuinely differ, and the book doesn’t
have a theorem showing the definition holds up across such differences.
246   28 What’s mine, and what isn’t


               28.12 Five record disciplines, one question
              Known: each system and its guarantee belongs to its designers: Casper (Buterin and
              Griffith, 2017), Ethereum’s storage gas and refunds (EIP-2200, EIP-3529), extend-
              only TPM registers, Certificate Transparency (RFC 9162), proof-carrying code. Mine:
              asking all five whether the write is priced, with a small worked fragment of each
              (Section 17). Kind: synthesis and an organizing reading; the fragments are not
              verifications of those systems.


               28.13 The pointer thesis
               Known: Zurek’s quantum Darwinism (2009), with Ollivier, Poulin and Zurek (2004)
               and Blume-Kohout and Zurek (2006): preferred states are the ones whose records
               proliferate redundantly in the environment. Mine: borrowing that shape for the
               question of which event deserves a record, with definitions (copied and relied on by
               parties who didn’t produce it, independent carriers), two toys, and refutations of
               two stronger forms (Section 18). Kind: thesis. No claim about quantum physics.


               28.14 The universal host for the example
               Known: Turing’s universal machine (1936), which runs any machine given as data,
               and the universal register machines that run two-counter programs. Keeping a guest
               and the code running beside it out of each other’s state is what a virtual machine
               monitor promises (Popek and Goldberg, 1974). Compiling computable functions
               into counter machines is Forster, Larchey-Wendling and coauthors’ work. Mine: one
               fixed program, U, written in the small machine’s own six kinds of instruction on a
               host with a register for every whole number, with no guest step built in, that runs
               every program of the small machine from every clean start start( x, y), halts exactly
               when its guest does, raises its own flag by its own check, commit and certificate
               if and only if the guest’s rises somewhere in its run, and keeps a ledger equal to
               the guest’s at every matching point; its host machine is certified Thiele-complete,
               and whether U halts on a loaded guest is undecidable (Appendix C.1). Two small
               facts force its shape, and they’re stated there: reading a counter above 0 into the
               program’s control flow on this machine writes it, and a finite program can’t keep
               the guest’s facts on a copy of the guest’s counter, by the pigeonhole. Beside it, a
               simpler host with a built-in guest step runs guests by construction and keeps a
               mirror of the guest’s flag, with the mirror, isolation and earned-chain theorems.
               Code that shares unprotected memory with a record can overwrite it, and ordinary
               computers isolate memory and enforce rules with monitors too, so what I claim
               is narrower: the simulation and the record guarantee proved for this one fixed
               program U. Kind: an implementation, with its proofs, of a design whose shape
                                                                            28.15 The game check   247


is Turing’s universal machine, with the record and the toll carried through. The
universality is borrowed from the small machine and from Minsky. And the same
program with one paid move added, UP , runs every computably presented Thiele
machine, with its halting matched, its reading carried as a latch on the host’s own
earned flag whose claim is that reading, and its ledger to within a surcharge of
at most two when the reading starts at no (exactly three when it starts at yes),
which no program on the host, run from a clean start, can avoid when the guest
certifies with its whole ledger below three (Appendix C.2); the counter-compilation
construction is given in Appendix 6. Kind for that part: an implementation with its
proofs again. That a universal machine runs any computable machine is Turing’s;
what’s carried through here is the record, earned again, and the prices, exact up to
the surcharge, and I didn’t find that put together where I looked. In the words of
Definition C.1, U is a universal Thiele machine for the small machine’s programs
with no surcharge, and UP is one for every computably presented Thiele machine
with a surcharge of at most two when the guest’s reading starts at no and three
when it starts at yes.


28.15 The game check
Known: that positive semidefiniteness of a correlation matrix gives the Tsirelson
bound is Tsirelson’s (1980) and Stephanie Wehner’s semidefinite proof (2006), from
a paper on Tsirelson bounds for generalized CHSH inequalities; the moment-matrix
hierarchy, the Schur-complement analysis of the level-one matrix of this scenario
and the level 1 + AB are Navascués, Pironio and Acín’s (2007, 2008), with both four-
body cells unconstrained where my matrix ties them; that every point with a PSD
completion is the correlator table of a quantum strategy, built from anticommuting
observables in a maximally entangled state, is Tsirelson’s too (1980); the arcsine
characterization is Landau’s (1988) and Masanes’s (2003); Echenim and Mhalla
(2023) give a further detailed derivation of the CHSH and Tsirelson bounds. Rational
sum-of-squares certificates are Helfried Peyrl and Pablo A. Parrilo’s (2008), from a
paper on computing sum-of-squares decompositions with rational coefficients. Mine:
a decision procedure in integer arithmetic on outcome counts; a proved chain from
“the counts pass” to “the pinned level-one matrix is positive semidefinite” to “S2 ≤ 8”;
explicit constructions of the representation in finite dimension, on two four-level
systems with real amplitudes and two two-level ones with complex, and a proof
of the arcsine characterization; proofs of the contraction equivalence by explicit
test vectors; and the level-1 + AB cascade (on the slice ⟨{ A0 , A1 } ⊗ { B0 , B1 }⟩ = 0)
and an elliptope gate in whole numbers, with a soundness proof (Appendix 32
and its derivation, Appendix 33). Kind: an integer procedure connecting counts
to these known bounds, with known theorems (Tsirelson’s representation, the
248   28 What’s mine, and what isn’t


              arcsine characterization, the equivalence of contraction and PSD) proved again. I
              found no earlier chain that takes counts as input where I looked. The same check
              earned on the small machine’s host, a raised flag standing on it, is built and proved
              (Theorems 32.4 and 32.5); the idea isn’t new.


               28.16 What that adds up to

              Read straight down the list. The mathematics underneath this book is mostly known,
              and some of it is very old. The question organizing this book is what it costs
              for a distinction to come into being and stay, written into the step of a model of
              computation, with certification, a record that a claim was checked, as its sharpest
              case, and what’s mine in the answer isn’t one result. It’s four, and I name them the
              same way wherever they come up. The toll derived from finiteness, permanence and
              a price on merges (item 3; the counting is classical, the finite cousin of Zermelo’s
              recurrence objection and folklore about permutation automata, and Ouldridge and
              Wolpert already priced it in entropy for automata, so what’s mine is the toll a price on
              merging moves gives for a reading that stays, after Li and Vitányi’s price per erased
              bit and Demaine and his coauthors’ price on instructions, and the price is an abstract
              premise motivated by Landauer’s reset). The pricing form of the window fact (item
              6; the fact itself is elementary). The latch form of a record that never comes down
              (item 8; latches and history variables are old). And a record that’s priced, and on the
              small machine earned, under the reference monitor’s every-program move (items
              10, 11 and 14; the move is Anderson’s, Schneider’s and the micro-policy authors’,
              and what’s added is the check bound to its edition, the toll on top, a definition of
              Thiele-complete that a clock fails, over any base and any event, a certified refinement
              the small machine meets, and universal programs that carry the check and the toll
              to every small-machine program and to every computably presented Thiele machine,
              earning their own flag). Under all four sits item 1, which is old mathematics read
              one way: every step of every machine run by one rule takes a fresh state, merges,
              or goes round a loop, a merge in a coarse description is, in every finer description
              it is exact about, a fine merge, a fine step out of the set, or endlessly many fresh
              states, specified thermal models add their own dissipation bounds, and finiteness is
              what takes the fresh states away. The first is a ledger form of a core that’s classical
              and already priced in physics; the second and third are small exact statements I
              didn’t find stated where I looked; the fourth is built, with its proofs on the page,
