              and its idea isn’t new. The general statements are proved for every machine that
              meets the definition; the small machine and the programs that run on it are the
              worked instance that shows the definition can be met. Around it is my best attempt
              at saying which is which, which you should check as hard as anything else in here.
                                                               28.16 What that adds up to   249


If someone shows me item 3’s ledger form, the pricing form in item 6, or item 8
stated earlier, I’ll move it up the list.
CHAPTER 29




From “The universal machines”

29.1 A simpler host, with the guest step built in

There’s a much simpler host than U, and I keep it in the book because it’s easy to
read and it shows the copy rule in one line. Its universality is by construction: it has
a move that runs one guest step by calling the small machine’s own step. It isn’t a
universal Thiele machine in the sense of Definition C.1, and the reasons are exact.
Its interpreter loop U0 never halts: it keeps returning to its first line even after the
guest stops. The host itself does have an OWN HALT instruction, which another
program can use. The loop also keeps charging one per pass, even when the guest
has stopped or its next step costs nothing, so no fixed surcharge bounds it. And it
isn’t a register host, since its guest step does the computing. A host that fixed the
first two and kept the built-in step would still fail the last (the argument after the
definition). U is the universal Thiele machine of this book. Read it as a picture of
what U does the long way, and as a host that starts from any guest state at all, clean
or not.
    It carries a guest inside its own state: the guest’s program as data, the guest’s
counters, its table, its ledger, its flag. The host has one extra move, run one guest
step. In that one move it looks at the guest’s flag, takes the step, looks again, and if
the flag went from down to up, the host’s own copy of that flag goes up in the same
move, and the host pays for it.

Definition 29.1 (The host). A guest is a program P of the small machine together
with a state g of it (Definition 15.2). A host state h has six parts: its own core core(h),
its ledger µ(h), its own flag own(h), the guest program P(h) held as a list of instruc-
tions, the guest state g(h), and the mirror m(h), a yes/no copy of the guest’s flag.
The host has two kinds of move. OWN i runs the instruction i on the host’s own core,
costs what the small machine charges for i, and raises own exactly when that ma-
chine’s flag would rise. GSTEP b costs b. If the host’s core is trapped it does nothing
else. Otherwise it moves the host to its next line and, if the guest’s next instruction
costs at most b, takes one program step of the guest, which leaves a stopped guest
where it is; in the same move the mirror becomes m(h) ∨ (¬cert( g(h)) ∧ cert( g′ )),


                                   250
                                                     29.1 A simpler host, with the guest step built in   251


where g′ is the new guest state. The host’s reading is own(h) ∨ m(h). Its stored
program is U0 = [GSTEP 1; OWN (INC A); OWN (DEC A 1)]: one guest step, then
two moves that bring the host back to its first line. It loads a program P from ( x, y)
with g(h) = start( x, y) and the mirror and its own flag down.

    Every instruction of the small machine costs at most one, so GSTEP 1 runs every
guest instruction there is. Why charge the budget b? Why not the cost of whatever
instruction got fetched? Because charging the budget keeps the price a function of
the move alone, which is what the definition of Section 2 asks for: you read the
price of a host move off the move, the way you read the small machine’s prices off
its table, without peeking inside the guest. What that costs the host is easy to see. A
host that steps a guest instruction priced 0, an INC say, still pays one for the move.
    So the host’s ledger is closer to a clock than to a receipt for its guests: one unit
per guest step, whatever the step was. It never falls behind the guest’s own ledger
(part (3) of Theorem 29.3 below), and it matches that ledger only when every guest
step happens to cost one. Could a cleverer host have simulation-exact accounting,
a ledger equal to its guest’s? Not with this one fixed-price move: GSTEP 1 is the
same move whether the guest’s next instruction is an INC priced 0 or a paid CHECK,
so one price can’t match both. Two moves can: one that costs nothing, takes the
guest’s step when that step costs nothing and otherwise jumps, and one that costs
one and takes it. A loop that tries the first before the second keeps the host’s ledger
equal to the guest’s at every pass. That host still has its guest step built in. U gets
the match with ordinary instructions: it pays only on the record moves it runs itself,
so its ledger equals its guest’s (Theorem C.4).
    And there’s one thing to say about the definition before the theorems, so you
know which of them carry weight. GSTEP runs the guest’s instruction by calling the
small machine’s own step on the guest’s state: the guest’s step is a primitive of the
host. So “the host runs any guest” (Theorem 29.1) is true by construction. The host
was built to do exactly that, and the proof follows the three moves around the loop.
The theorems with something in them come after it. From a loaded host, or any state
where the two agree, the mirror always equals the guest’s flag (Observation 29.2).
Nothing raises the mirror unless the guest itself passes a CERTIFY inside a guest
step the host paid for (Theorem 29.4). No OWN move, in any host program at all,
touches the guest’s state or the mirror (the second sentence of that same theorem),
and no host move of either kind rewrites the guest’s program. And the mirror is tied
to the guest’s earned chain: after any host run from a loaded host, if the mirror is
up, the guest’s own run from its start contains a passing CHECK, then a COMMIT of
the same claim, then a CERTIFY, since the mirror is the guest’s flag and the guest
started clean (Theorem 15.11). So this host runs any small-machine program by
construction and keeps the guest’s record by theorem, as a copy of the guest’s flag.
252   29 From “The universal machines”


              The mirroring program U0 does not earn the host’s own flag; a different program
              could do so with the host’s OWN CHECK, COMMIT and CERTIFY. U does both the
              long way: it runs the guest with its own instructions, and it earns its own flag.


              Theorem 29.1 (The host runs any guest). (1) If the host’s core is not trapped, then
              after n moves GSTEP 1 the guest state is the guest program’s own run of n steps
              from g(h), the guest program is unchanged, and the host is still not trapped. (2) If
              the host’s core is at line 1 and not trapped, then 3n steps of the program U0 leave
              the guest exactly where n steps of its own program put it. (3) For every two-counter
              program M and inputs a, b: M halts from (1, a, b) exactly when the host program
              made of OWN moves of the compiled M halts, from a host whose core starts at
              ( a, b), whatever guest that host carries.
              Observation 29.2 (The copy is the record). Say a host state h agrees when m(h) =
              cert( g(h)). Every host move takes an agreeing state to an agreeing state, so every host
              trace and every stored host program does too, and a freshly loaded host agrees. In
              particular, if h agrees and is not trapped, the mirror after n moves GSTEP 1 is the
              guest’s flag after n steps of its own program.


              Theorem 29.3 (The host’s step collects the guest’s toll). (1) If h agrees, is not
              trapped, and the guest step that budget b allows takes the guest’s flag from down to
              up, then the mirror is down before GSTEP b and up after it, the host’s ledger rose
              by exactly b, and b ≥ 1. (2) In the run of (1) of Theorem 29.1, from a host that
              agrees, if the guest’s flag goes up between its steps n and n + 1, the mirror goes up
              between the host’s moves n and n + 1, and that move costs 1. (3) For every host
              trace t, µ( g(run(t, h))) + µ(h) ≤ µ(run(t, h)) + µ( g(h)): the host’s ledger grows at
              least as fast as the guest’s.



              Theorem 29.4 (No unpaid host certification). If the mirror is down at h and up
              after a host move i, then i is GSTEP b with b ≥ 1, the host was not trapped, the
              guest’s next instruction was CERTIFY, and the guest’s flag went from down to up in
              that move. A trace made only of OWN moves leaves the mirror and the guest state
              exactly where they were. And the host’s own flag rises only by an OWN CERTIFY
              that passes on the host’s core.
                                                       29.2 The diagonal, on this machine’s programs   253




Theorem 29.5 (The host is a Thiele machine). For every host state and move, if
the host’s reading goes from no to yes, the move costs at least 1; the same holds for
the mirror alone and for the own flag alone. So the host read through own ∨ m, the
host read through m alone, and the guest class are Thiele machines (Definition 2.2),
and any host trace that raises the host’s reading costs at least 1.

   Two statements have been proved here. The designated guest-step runs follow the
guest computation. Separately, every permitted host instruction preserves agreement
with a genuine guest run, so arbitrary host instruction sequences from a loaded
start cannot fabricate the guest’s mirror. The second statement uses the full host
instruction set. It does not follow merely from the first, or from charging for a raise.
The combined reading own-or-mirror remains a different observation: the host may
establish its own record while the guest’s record stays down.

Remark 29.1 (Two guests, run). Load the guest CHECK is zero A; COMMIT is zero
A; CERTIFY; HALT with both counters at 0. After two moves GSTEP 1 the mirror is
down; after three it is up, the guest’s flag is up, the host’s own flag is still down, and
the host has paid 3. Under the program U0 the mirror is down at host step 6 and up
at step 7, with 3 paid. The guest CERTIFY; CERTIFY traps on its first step: after five
moves GSTEP 1 the mirror is down, the guest is trapped, and the host has paid 5 for
nothing.

   That copy can’t be faked. The proof walks every host move there is, and the
copy only rises in a guest step where the guest itself ran CERTIFY and passed. A
host program that never touches the guest leaves the copy exactly where it was.
From a loaded host the copy always equals the guest’s flag, and the host’s ledger
always grows at least as fast as the guest’s, and a three-instruction loop runs any
small-machine program one guest step per pass. Here the mirror, the crossing test
and the charge sit in the host’s step rule, so Theorems 29.3 and 29.4 hold for every
sequence of host moves, including ones that never run a guest. The fixed program
U supplies simulation, an exact ledger at matching points and an earned flag using
ordinary instructions. Its guest simulation theorem concerns that program from its
loaded starts. The host’s native earned-chain rule covers other programs too, but
does not make every such program simulate the same guest.


29.2 The diagonal, on this machine’s programs
Section 13 left the diagonal standing on any substrate, on a little family built to fit
it, and on L, handled on its own terms rather than as a substrate. It also said what a
254   29 From “The universal machines”


              machine has to supply to fill in the same shape, two things: programs that can be
              handed to programs as numbers, and a recursion theorem for the transformations it
              can carry out. This machine’s universal program reads a whole guest program out of
              one register, with a numbering of programs that goes there and back (Definition C.3),
              and halting is undecidable here in the reduction sense of Theorem 15.5. What’s left
              are the two classical results that turn a universal machine into a place where the
              diagonal bites: Rice’s theorem and Kleene’s recursion theorem. They come with a
              wrinkle I should put first, because it decides which machine they’re about.
                   The wrinkle is the one from Section 15, at the end of the subsection on what two
              counters compute. The small machine takes its inputs straight into two counters,
              and Schroeppel’s memo gives a proof that two plain counters can’t even compute
              2n from n. Rice’s reduction has to keep a program’s input safe while a copy of a
              halting instance runs. Kleene’s construction has to run an evaluator while keeping
              its input. Two counters have nowhere to keep it. So the two theorems land where
              they’re true and where I could prove them. The host machine of Definition C.2, the
              one with a counter for every number that U runs on, is a fine programming system:
              its programs are numbers, an input sits in register 1, and there’s room to spare. On
              it both theorems hold in full. On the two-counter machine itself the story splits, and
              I’ll tell it at the end of the subsection: Rice’s theorem holds on every input, by a trick
              for keeping the input that I’ll show you; Kleene’s holds when inputs are written as
              powers of two, the way Minsky packed them; and on plain inputs Kleene’s is false,
              and that’s a theorem too.
                   The host machine’s programs are numbered in the same style as U numbers a
              guest’s. Write ⟨m, n⟩ for (2n + 1) · 2m . INC r is ⟨0, r ⟩, DEC r j is ⟨1, ⟨r, j⟩⟩, HALT is
              ⟨2, 0⟩, CHECK r is ⟨3, r ⟩, COMMIT r is ⟨4, r ⟩, CERTIFY is ⟨5, 0⟩, and a program is the
              list code: the empty program is 0, and an instruction x followed by a program t is
              ⟨ x, code(t)⟩. An instruction number that is none of these codes reads as HALT. Then
              every number decodes to some program, since a positive number is ⟨ x, n⟩ with n
              smaller than it, and decoding a program’s code gives the program back. When I put
              one program after another, the later one’s jumps move down by the length of what
              comes before it, and a jump to a line where it has no instruction, line 0 included,
              goes to the line just after it, so a stop inside it hands on to whatever follows. Say
              a host program outputs y on input x when, started with x in register 1 and every
              other register 0, with a clean table, it halts with y in register 0.
                   Rice comes first, because it’s the shorter road. Henry Rice, whom you met
              in Section 8, proved in his 1951 doctoral thesis, and published in 1953, that no
              program decides any property of what programs do unless every program has it or
              none does. Properties of how a program is written aren’t covered. The road from
              halting is a single detour. Take a property of what programs do, a program y that
              has it, and a program n that doesn’t. Given an instance of two-counter halting, a
                                                      29.2 The diagonal, on this machine’s programs   255


Minsky program H with starting counters a and b, build a host program that first
loads a and b into two fresh registers (not register 1, and named by neither y nor n),
then runs a copy of H moved onto those registers, then empties both, then runs y.
If H halts on ( a, b) the built program behaves like y and has the property. If H runs
forever the built program never stops on any input, and it behaves like the program
that loops, which lacks the property (if the looping program has it instead, swap the
roles of y and n). Everything the built program does before y is INC and DEC, which
add nothing to the ledger and touch no fact, channel, trap latch or flag. So a decider
for the property would be a decider for halting, and by Theorem 15.5 that would let
a program list every single-tape Turing machine that doesn’t halt. Two words carry
the weight, and they’re the ones to check in the statement: what “behaves the same”
sees, and where the program starts.

Definition 29.2 (Behaving the same, on the host). A host program ends in a state
when it halts there, from the start with x in register 1 and the rest clean; a trap
counts as stopping. Two host programs behave the same when for every input x, any
state one ends in has a state the other ends in that agrees with it on every register’s
value, the fact table, the channel, the trap latch, the ledger and the flag, and the
same the other way round. The line number and the versions aren’t compared. A
property of host programs is extensional when behaving the same carries it from one
program to the other.



Theorem 29.6 (Rice’s theorem on the host machine). Let Π be an extensional
property of host programs that some program has and some program lacks. Then Π
is undecidable in the reduction sense of Theorem 15.5.

   A property that depends only on the partial function a program computes,
whether it stops and what it leaves in register 0, is extensional, so it is covered. That
case also follows from the recursion theorem below.
   On the small machine itself the same road works from the clean start, and only
from there. Say two two-counter programs behave the same from the clean start
when, whatever state one halts in from counters (0, 0), the other halts in a state
with the same counters, trap latch, ledger and flag, and the same the other way
round. The fact table and the channel aren’t compared, because the prefix moves
the versions of A and B by fixed amounts and the facts, the channel’s included,
carry versions. There is no input to keep, since the program starts at (0, 0), and H’s
instance is loaded straight into A and B.
256   29 From “The universal machines”




              Theorem 29.7 (Rice’s theorem for the small machine, from the clean start). Let Π
              be a property of two-counter programs that’s carried from a program to any program
              that behaves the same from the clean start, held by some program and lacked by
              some other. Then Π is undecidable in the reduction sense of Theorem 15.5. In
              particular “halts from the clean start” is undecidable.

                  That theorem covers the clean start only. Every input is harder, because the
              standard reduction has to keep the input safe while the halting instance runs, and
              two counters have nowhere obvious to keep it. The way through is at the end of the
              subsection.
                  Kleene’s recursion theorem is the deeper one, and it’s a construction, which is
              Section 13’s point about Kleene’s little gem (Moschovakis’s phrase): the content is
              the building, and the contradiction in Section 13 was just the last line. It says: for
              every transformation F of programs that some program can carry out on numbers,
              there’s a program p that computes the same thing as F ( p). The shape of the building
              is the old one. From the program that carries out F, build an evaluator that reads a
              number, treats it as a program’s code, specialises that program to its own code, runs
              F’s program on the result, and runs whatever program comes back. The specialising
              step is what logicians call s-m-n: fix one input of a program and get a new program
              that needs one input fewer. Then the witness is the evaluator specialised to its own
              code. Run on any input, it builds its own code, hands it to F’s program, gets the
              code of F ( p), and runs that.
                  Each piece on the host machine comes next, with its exact premise.


              Theorem 29.8 (s-m-n, and a universal program for host programs). (1) For a
              number c and a host program V, let spec(c, V ) be c copies of INC 2 followed by V
              with its jumps moved to follow them. Then for all x, y, spec(c, V ) outputs y on input
              x if and only if V, started with x in register 1 and c in register 2, halts with y in
              register 0. (2) There is a host program W made only of INC, DEC and HALT such
              that, for all c, x, y, W started with x in register 1 and c in register 2 halts with y in
              register 0 exactly when the host program numbered c outputs y on input x.

                 One thing to be exact about: U is not that universal program. U runs two-counter
              guests, and a two-counter guest given its input plainly can’t run the evaluator. With
              inputs written as powers of two one can, as Theorem 29.14 near the end of this
              subsection shows. W runs host programs, and it can, because it’s a host program on
              a machine with registers.
                                                      29.2 The diagonal, on this machine’s programs   257




Theorem 29.9 (Kleene’s recursion theorem on the host machine). (1) For every host
program T there is a host program e such that for all x, y: e outputs y on input x if
and only if, for some d, T outputs d on the number of e, and the program numbered
d outputs y on x. (2) So for every transformation F of host programs that some
host program carries out on numbers (on the number of p it outputs the number
of F ( p), for every p), there is a host program e such that for all x, y, e outputs y
on x if and only if F (e) does. (3) The same on numbers: for every function f on
numbers, defined everywhere, that some host program computes, some number e
has the program numbered e and the program numbered f (e) output the same on
every input.
    That fixed point is of the function: e and F (e) stop on the same inputs and leave
the same number in register 0. Their ledgers, flags and fact tables can differ: e is
all INC and DEC, so whatever F (e) pays, e only simulates. I’d like a fixed point that
agrees on the record too, and the next two theorems say how much of that can be
had. The ledger and the flag can be had, but agreement on every register can’t.


Theorem 29.10 (The recursion theorem, up to the record). Let F be a transfor-
mation of host programs that some host program T carries out on numbers. Then
there is a host program e such that, for every input x, any state e ends in has a state
F (e) ends in that agrees with it on register 0, the trap latch, the ledger, the flag, the
number of facts in the table, and whether the channel is empty, and the same the
other way round. So e stops on x exactly when F (e) does, and the ledgers are equal
exactly, with no surcharge. T may itself check, commit and certify.

   Look only at the function computed and you get the usual fixed-point statement
back. There are two limits, and the second one is a theorem. First, e pays after it
has computed everything. It is not the way U runs a guest, mirroring every move: e
pays the counts, CHECKs, then COMMITs, then the CERTIFY, whatever order F (e)
used, so the order of the record’s events isn’t preserved, and for a program F (e) that
never stops, e never stops and pays nothing, though F (e) may pay without bound.
The relation says nothing about states in the middle of a run. Second, no fixed point
agrees on every register, or on what the facts name.


Theorem 29.11 (No fixed point of full behaviour). There is a transformation F of
host programs, carried out by a host program, such that no host program e behaves
the same as F (e) in the sense of Definition 29.2.
258   29 From “The universal machines”


                 A variant of F that also CHECKs register r and commits to it gives F (e) a fact and
              a commitment about r that e can’t have, so no fixed point agrees on the fact table
              alone, or on the channel alone, or on the versions of the registers. What survives is
              what Theorem 29.10 states, plus any register named in advance, or any finite set of
              them: before it pays, the evaluator reads that register’s final value off the simulated
              run of F (e) and writes it into its own copy of the register, keeping its scratch work
              and its two payment registers elsewhere. The register F above writes is chosen by
              looking at e, and that’s the one no fixed point can follow.
                 And with the recursion theorem proved, the wall of Section 13 goes up on this
              machine without assuming that theorem as an extra premise. Call a property of
              host programs functional when it depends only on the partial function a program
              computes.


              Theorem 29.12 (The diagonal on the host machine). Let Π be a functional property
              of host programs that some program has and some program lacks. (1) Read “ f is
              representable” as “some host program carries f out on numbers.” Then no function
              d from programs to Booleans whose flip is representable is right about Π on every
              program: this is the argument of Theorem 13.1, reproved for host programs, with its
              recursion premise a theorem here. (2) No host program decides Π outright: there
              is no program D that, on the number of every program, halts with a number in
              register 0 that’s 1 exactly when the program has Π.
                  Rice and Kleene are the ingredients the diagonal of Section 13 asks a substrate for,
              and the wall they put up doesn’t care about the record: the base does the computing
              and the flag watches. A program’s ledger and flag are among what “behaves the
              same” sees on the host, so a nontrivial property of those is undecidable too, in the
              reduction sense of Theorem 29.6, and the fixed point is up to the record, as far as
              the theorems above say and no further.
                  Now the two-counter machine itself, which I promised at the start of the sub-
              section: Minsky’s two bins of pebbles and nothing else. Rice comes first. Say a
              two-counter program behaves the same as another on a set X of inputs when, on
              every input n in X, started with n pebbles in bin A and none in bin B, one stops
              exactly when the other does, and then they agree on both bins, the trap latch, the
              ledger, the flag, and on which property and which counter each recorded fact and
              the channel are about. The versions are left out: they say which write a fact is about,
              and the trick below moves them.
                  The trick is the whole problem. Rice’s reduction has to keep the program’s input
              safe while a copy of a halting instance H runs, and there’s no third bin to keep it
              in. My first guess was bin B, and that dies at once, because H needs both bins. The
                                                     29.2 The diagonal, on this machine’s programs   259


second guess is Minsky’s own packing: keep several numbers in one bin as powers
of different primes, H’s two counters as the exponents of 2 and 3, and the input
as the exponent of 5. That dies more quietly. To put the input there, the program
would have to turn n pebbles into 5n , an exponential, and Schroeppel’s memo, from
Section 15, gives a proof that two plain counters can’t even build 2n from n. I had
no way to build 5n .
    What works is to stop keeping the input beside the work. Turn n pebbles into
6n + 5, which is only adding, and then multiply in H’s counters as powers of 2 and 3.
H runs by halving the pile and splitting it in thirds and building it back up, and the
6n + 5 rides along in the middle of every one of those piles. It’s odd, and counted
off in threes it leaves two over, so no halving or thirding ever reaches into it.


Theorem 29.13 (Rice’s theorem for the two-counter machine, on any inputs). For
any set X of inputs, a property of two-counter programs that’s carried from a
program to any program that behaves the same on X is undecidable in the reduction
sense of Theorem 15.5, unless every program has it or none does, and then it’s
decidable. X can be every input, the powers of two, or {0}.
    So 6n + 5 is the place to keep the input that two counters seemed not to have.
It isn’t beside the work but inside it, in a factor the work never touches. I like that
one more than I can justify: the input hides in the leftover that every halving and
thirding walks straight past.
    Kleene needs more than keeping an input. It needs a program that runs a
program. So write a number x as 2x pebbles and read a result as the exponent of 2,
the way Minsky did. With inputs written that way, there’s one two-counter program,
call it U2 , that, started on 2x · 3e , stops with 2y exactly when the program numbered
e gives y on x; specialising a program to a number is exact; and the recursion
theorem holds. (U2 is a different program from the universal host program U, which
runs on the machine with a register for every number.)


Theorem 29.14 (Kleene for the two-counter machine, inputs written as powers of
two). (1) For every transformation F of programs that some program carries out
on program numbers, there’s a program e that computes the same function as F (e),
with inputs and outputs written as powers of two. (2) The fixed point is of the
function only: there’s such an F for which no e behaves the same as F (e) once the
properties and counters of the recorded facts count.
    The counterexample in (2) is short. Number the programs the way U numbers
its guests (Appendix 7), so a program’s number is above the code of every property
260   29 From “The universal machines”


              it names. Let F ( p) be the one-line program that checks that bin A holds at least the
              number of p plus one. A program can only record facts about properties it names,
              so no e can record the fact F (e) records, and from a large enough input F (e) does
              record it.
                  Even with Schroeppel in hand, my first guess about Kleene was that the powers
              of two were a convenience, and that plain inputs would only take more work. They
              don’t take more work: with plain inputs, Kleene’s theorem is false. A transformation
              is carried out if some program, started on 2n where n is the number of a program p,
              stops with 2m where m is the number of F ( p).



              Theorem 29.15 (No plain recursion on two counters). (1) No program of n lines
              stops with c · x pebbles in bin A, started on x pebbles in bin A and none in bin B, for
              every x, where c is the least prime above N = 16(n + 1)(4n + 1)16 . (2) So there’s
              a carried-out transformation F such that no program e computes the same plain
              function as F (e).

                  Nothing in that proof used more than this: two bins that move one pebble at a
              time, finitely many controls, a next step fixed by the control and the bins, and bins
              that, above some threshold, are read only through their parity. Any machine like
              that has the same obstruction, and the same computable transformation F with no
              program e equivalent to F (e) on plain inputs.
                  I went looking for Schroeppel’s own memo and found a scan of it. As I read it, his
              route is close in spirit: he trades a two-counter machine that starts with one counter
              empty for a machine with one register that can only add, multiply and divide by
              fixed numbers and take one away, each of those built out of one of the program’s
              loops, and that’s the stages here under another name. I haven’t followed his proof
              line by line, so I compare the two at that level and no further; the argument this
              book stands on is the one above. The c it uses is astronomically bigger than anything
              a real multiplier needs, and finding the best bound is a question I leave alone.
                  So on two bins, Rice holds on every input; Kleene holds with inputs written as
              powers of two, for the function, and fails for the facts; and with plain inputs Kleene
              fails outright. The last one is the answer I didn’t see coming: the powers of two
              weren’t a convenience, and on two bins they’re the difference between a recursion
              theorem and none.
                  That failure has a setting computability theorists know. The mathematician
              Hartley Rogers, in his 1967 textbook, calls a numbering of the computable functions
              acceptable when every other computable numbering of them can be translated into
              it by a computable map, and in every acceptable numbering Kleene’s theorem holds.
              Read on plain inputs, the two-bin programs number something smaller than all
                                                        29.3 The universal machine’s guest interface   261


the computable functions, since none of them computes 2n from n (Schroeppel’s
theorem; compare Theorem 29.15), so they fall outside Rogers’s class, and his route
to the theorem isn’t open there. The computer scientists James Royer and John
Case (1994) studied that territory: programming systems for classes of functions
smaller than all the computable ones, which can’t be acceptable, and what can still
be programmed and proved in them. And the logician and puzzle writer Raymond
Smullyan’s two books on recursion theory and self-reference (1993, 1994) take
the fixed-point theorems apart axiomatically, so you can see which property of a
numbering each one leans on. The plain-input failure is a small instance on the map
they drew.


29.3 The universal machine’s guest interface

I’ll say what the two universal programs cover plainly, a sentence at a time. The
stated class of guests of U is the small machine’s programs, every one of them, from
every clean start start( x, y). The whole-run theorems don’t cover a guest handed
facts in its table, a commitment in its channel or a raised flag at the start; the
one-step simulation, by itself, works from any related pair. It’s the same kind of class
Turing’s universal machine has: a fixed instruction set, and a finite program held
as data, here packed into one number the way Turing packed his into a description
number. Each guest keeps its computation, its halting, its answer, its record and its
prices, exactly: the host halts when it halts, its flag goes up when the guest’s would,
on a chain of its own, and its ledger is the guest’s.
     The guests of UP are every Thiele machine whose driver, step, prices and reading
are computed by programs, with the reading decided on every state. Each keeps its
computation and its halting; its reading becomes the host’s flag as a latch, raised on
an earned chain whose claim is the guest’s own reading; and its prices become the
host’s ledger, exactly, except for a surcharge at the move where the reading first says
yes: at most two when the reading starts at no, exactly three when it starts at yes.
The surcharge is zero when that move costs three or more, and it can’t always be
avoided: when the guest certifies with a ledger below three, no program on that
host, run from a clean start, matches its ledger exactly (Theorems C.7 and C.8).
Picture a clerk who’ll stamp anybody’s receipt. He copies it onto his own forms, bills
at your rates to within two when your reading starts at no and to within three when
it starts at yes, and the stamp at the bottom is one he earned by checking your own
reading, not one he copied.
     What’s outside the class is outside the theorems. A machine whose reading no
program decides (an oracle checker, say, which the definition of certified Thiele-
complete doesn’t rule out) doesn’t come in. Nor does a machine whose next move is
nobody’s computation. The two universal programs have different start conditions:
262   29 From “The universal machines”


              U’s whole-run result starts its guest clean; UP starts its guest where the presentation
              says, even if that state already has a yes reading. The host starts clean and earns a
              claim about that reading. It does not reconstruct or authenticate the guest’s earlier
              history.
                 And insisting on no built-in step is the point, and a plain interpreter shows why. A
              plain interpreter on an ordinary computer runs guests too. If its guest record is freely
              writable data, its meaning depends on the interpreter following the intended update
              rule. An ordinary computer can also protect that data with a monitor or another
              enforced interface. Here the rule is explicit in the host’s instruction semantics: U
              and UP are fixed interpreters, and nothing in them raises the host’s flag except the
              host’s own CERTIFY, and the host’s step won’t let that pass without a COMMIT, or
              the COMMIT without a CHECK on a slot nobody has touched. The guest’s record
              isn’t data the host updates. It’s a record the host earns again, under the same rule,
              on its own registers.
CHAPTER 30




Every model

After the smallest honest machine (Section 15) and the universal programs of the
previous appendix, there was a worry I couldn’t put down. Everything in them was
built on Minsky’s two counters. The definition of certified Thiele-complete names a
two-counter window in clause (a). The universal program runs two-counter guests.
So is all of this a fact about counters? Pick a different machine to start from, a
Turing machine with its tape, a register machine, Alonzo Church’s lambda calculus,
and does the record still go on the same way, or did the definition depend on my
example?
   I chased it with a picture: a box with a panel bolted to its front. The box
computes, with levers for moves and a window showing what clause (a) asks to see,
two numbers and a line number. The panel is the example machine’s earned layer:
version tallies that click when the box writes, a rack for facts, the flag, and the three
buttons CHECK, COMMIT and CERTIFY at a cost of one each. The worry is whether
that panel only bolts onto the one box I built.
   So this appendix goes both ways. First the panel goes onto boxes I didn’t build,
and I say exactly what a box has to supply for it to fit. Then other boxes, panel
and all, get run inside mine. If the record is really about the record and not about
pebbles, both trips should work, and the conditions on the way should say what has
to survive the move.


30.1 From any base up
Start with the direction I care about most: the panel onto a box I didn’t build. The
box can be any machine at all that has a universal base in the sense of clause (a), and
in the picture that means two things: it shows a two-counter window, and for each
two-counter instruction it has a lever that acts on the window as that instruction
does. What else is inside, what its states look like, I don’t get to know.
    My first guess was to keep whatever the box came with. Plenty of machines have
a meter of their own, or a light on the front that means something’s done, so leave
those where they are, bolt the panel on beside them, and see what happens. It can’t
work, and the definition says why. If a lever can cost anything, computing adds to

                                                  263
264   30 Every model


              the ledger, and certified Thiele-complete asks that it add nothing, which is a rule of
              the price list, not a claim about heat. If a lever can switch on the box’s own light, a
              record went up with no CHECK behind it. Certified Thiele-complete rules out both.
              So the meter and the light come off, along with everything else the box already had:
              its costs, its record, all of it, except the states, the levers and what each lever does to
              the state.
                  Then bolt on a panel cut down from the small machine’s, and nothing else: one
              version number for the whole box that every lever raises, which is the tally, a table
              of facts, a channel, the flag, and three new moves, CHECK, COMMIT and CERTIFY,
              each costing one, with every lever costing nothing. The three act as on the small
              machine, with the tally in place of a counter’s version: CHECK files its claim with
              the tally when the checker says yes and the table has room, COMMIT puts the fact
              in the channel when the table holds its claim at the current tally, and CERTIFY raises
              the flag when the channel holds a fact. Call that the lift of the machine. A lever
              moves the box only while the trap is down. The trap works as on the small machine:
              a CHECK, COMMIT or CERTIFY that fails sets it, nothing takes it down, and once
              it’s up every move, a lever included, changes nothing but the ledger.


              Theorem 30.1 (Every universal base lifts). (1) The lift of any box with a universal
              base, over a language of claims about the box’s state (“unchanged” meaning the
              box’s state is the same), with an exact equality test and a checker proved to say
              yes exactly when the claim holds, is certified Thiele-complete, as long as the claims
              its CHECK can ask include one that’s true at one loaded state and false at another,
              and the fact table has room for at least one fact. (2) The claims “this register is at
              least n” and “the program is at line n,” read off the window, always include such a
              claim, so with those claims every universal base lifts. (3) Conversely, every certified
              Thiele-complete machine has an interface meeting the four clauses under which
              its base moves, taken alone, form a machine with a universal base. (4) The panel
              leaves the box alone: from a state whose trap is down, a run of lever pulls in the lift
              moves the box exactly as the box’s own run does, and the trap stays down.

              Proof. (1) The four clauses, one at a time. (a) The lift keeps the box’s states, levers
              and step and adds the tally, the table, the channel, the flag, the trap and the ledger.
              With the trap down, a lever moves the box’s part of the state as the box’s step does,
              raises the tally by one, and touches nothing else. So from a live state, which is
              a state whose box part is live and whose trap is down, the window moves as the
              instruction does and the state stays live; no lever changes the flag; the loaded states
              are the box’s loaded states with an empty panel, so they’re clean; and the flag, once
              up, is lowered by nothing. (b) Start with the panel empty. The only move that
                                                                           30.1 From any base up   265


raises the flag is CERTIFY, and CERTIFY traps unless the channel holds a fact; the
channel is filled only by a COMMIT, which traps unless the table holds its claim at
the current tally; and the table is filled only by a CHECK that passed, which files
the claim with the tally it was checked at. So in front of the raise there’s a passing
CHECK of some claim c, then a COMMIT of c at the same tally, then the CERTIFY.
The trap is down from the CHECK to the COMMIT, since the COMMIT passed and
nothing takes the trap down, and with the trap down the tally rises with every lever
pull, so an unchanged tally means no lever was pulled in between, so the box’s
state is unchanged, and that’s what “the thing c is about is unchanged” says in the
lift’s language, whose claims are about the box’s state. A passing CHECK means the
checker said yes, and the checker was proved to mean what it says. (c) is the price
list: levers at a cost of zero, the three buttons at one each, the ledger the running
sum. (d) Take the claim that’s true at one loaded state and false at another. From
the first, CHECK passes and files it, the table having room, COMMIT finds it at the
same tally, and CERTIFY raises the flag. From the second the CHECK traps, and
a trapped machine raises nothing. (2) “A is at least 1” is true at the loaded state
showing (1, 1, 0) and false at the one showing (1, 0, 0). A window claim is a register
and a number, or a line number, so two claims can be tested equal, and its checker
reads the window, which is its meaning, so the checker is sound by definition. (3)
Take an interface the machine is certified Thiele-complete with and throw away
every move whose kind isn’t base. What’s left has the same states, the base moves
as levers, and the interface’s window, live states and loaded states, and clause (a)
says of exactly these that the window follows every instruction from every live state,
that the state stays live, and that each loaded state shows (1, a, b) and is live. That’s
a universal base. (4) A lever pull with the trap down changes the box’s part by the
box’s step and the tally by one, and nothing else; the trap goes up only on a CHECK
that fails, a COMMIT without its fact, or a CERTIFY with the channel empty, never
on a lever. So along a run of lever pulls the box’s part follows the box’s own run,
pull for pull, and the trap stays down.
   Of the checker’s exactness, (1) uses one half whole and the other half once: (b)
needs the checker to say yes only when the claim holds, and (d) needs it to say yes
on the one claim at the one loaded state where that claim is true. The theorem asks
for both halves whole, the way every theorem about the small machine does, so one
checker serves all of them.
   Clause (b), the small machine’s proof with the bins swapped out for whatever
the box has, is the part I’d braced for, and it turned out to be nothing. The chain
CHECK, COMMIT, CERTIFY never looked at anything but the tally and the claim.
The levers changed only the tally on the panel, making old facts stale; they never
266   30 Every model


              inserted a fact, made a commitment or raised the flag. Neither side of the proof ever
              needed to know it was looking at pebbles.
                 So the earned layer isn’t something two counters do. It sits on top of any box
              with a universal base, and it never needed to know which one. I’d half expected to
              be rewriting the proof once per model.


              30.2 What never lifts
              A theorem that says the panel fits every box is one I don’t trust until I’ve watched
              it fail to fit something. So I went looking for boxes it won’t turn into a certified
              Thiele-complete machine, and the first one I tried was the one I was surest would
              work.
                  It was a Turing machine, with one fixed table, and each lever does one row of
              the table. That looked like the obvious base to me, and it isn’t one at all. Clause
              (a) asks for a lever for every two-counter instruction, and there are infinitely many
              two-counter instructions, since a jump can go to any line. A fixed table has finitely
              many rows, so it has finitely many levers, and it runs out of levers long before it
              runs out of instructions. The first two items below are that failure in two forms, and
              the others fail for other reasons, and each is proved.



              Theorem 30.2 (What doesn’t lift). (1) A box with only finitely many levers has
              no universal base, and no box with a universal base is finitely branching, meaning
              that from every state all its levers together lead to only finitely many different
              states. (2) The lift of a finitely branching box is never certified Thiele-complete,
              whatever claims and fact table it gets. (3) Neither is the lift of a box whose step
              ignores the state it’s in, where each lever sends every state to the same place. (4)
              On a record axis where every place is at or below every other, there is no certified
              Thiele-complete machine at all. (5) With a checker that says yes exactly when the
              claim holds, the lift’s own non-vacuity clause holds exactly when the fact table has
              room and some claim is true at one loaded state and false at another.

              Proof. (1) Take a universal base and its loaded state showing (1, 1, 0). For each j
              the lever for DEC A j must take it, in one pull, to a state showing ( j, 0, 0): A was
              above 0, so one comes off and the line jumps to j. Different j, different window,
              different state. So one state has infinitely many different successors, which is what
              not finitely branching means; and since one lever lands in one place from one state,
              the levers for the DEC A j are all different, so there are infinitely many of them. (2)
              In the lift the moves that cost nothing are the levers, since each button costs one,
              and from any state the levers together lead to finitely many states: with the trap
                                                                             30.2 What never lifts   267


down, the box’s finitely many successors, each with the tally raised by one, and with
it up, the state itself. (The buttons can lead to infinitely many, one CHECK for each
claim, but each costs one.) If the lift were certified Thiele-complete through some
interface, that interface’s base moves would cost nothing by clause (c), so they’d all
be levers, and by Theorem 30.1(3) they’d form a universal base on their own. By
(1) a universal base has a state from which its moves reach infinitely many states,
and levers of a finitely branching box can’t. (3) Suppose each lever lands in one
place whatever the state. The move an interface gives an instruction is a base move,
at a cost of zero by clause (c), while each button costs one, so it’s a lever. Pull the
lever for INC A twice from a loaded state showing (1, 0, 0), and pull the lever for
INC B and then the lever for INC A from the same state. Both runs end with the
lever for INC A, so both end with the box in the one place that lever lands, with the
tally two higher and the panel untouched: the same state of the lift. But the window
would have to show (3, 2, 0) after the first run and (3, 1, 1) after the second, and a
window is a function of the state. The same two runs refute the box on its own. (4)
On a record axis, raising the record means leaving the down-set of where it stood. If
every place is at or below every other, every down-set is the whole axis and no move
ever leaves one; but clause (d) asks for three moves that raise the record from some
loaded state. (5) If the table has no room, every CHECK traps and no CERTIFY ever
fires, so (d) fails. If no claim is true at one loaded state and false at another, (d)’s
second half has no witness. And when both hold, (d) was proved in (1).
    So my first test case failed, and at first I read item (1) as saying a Turing machine
can’t be a base at all. It says something narrower. A Turing machine with one fixed
table can’t be one. A classic model comes in the other way round, with one lever for
each program that carries out one two-counter instruction, run to its end. Pull it
and the box runs that program from start to stop in a single move. That’s how the
register machine below comes in, and it’s the honest reading of “a Turing machine is
a base” too. The levers are the programs, not the rows.
    That still leaves a worry about clause (a) itself. It asks for one lever per two-
counter instruction, which is a fact about how the two-counter machine is presented,
and a box that does each instruction in several pulls looks shut out for a reason
that has nothing to do with computing. It isn’t shut out, and the fix is to let the
pulls be grouped. Call a box a base by simulation when it has a window reading
a two-counter configuration off each state, through any encoding at all, a class of
live states, a finite list of its own levers for each two-counter instruction, and a live
loaded state showing (1, a, b) for each start, such that from every live state the list
acts on the window exactly as the instruction acts on a configuration and the state
stays live. The grouped box has the same states, and its levers are finite lists of the
box’s levers, a list pulled in order.
268   30 Every model




              Theorem 30.3 (The one-lever presentation loses nothing). (1) Every box with a
              universal base is a base by simulation. (2) Every base by simulation is a universal
              base of its grouped box, and a run of the grouped box is the box’s run along the
              joined lists. (3) So for every base by simulation, the lift of its grouped box, with
              the window claims and a fact table with room for at least one fact, is certified
              Thiele-complete. (4) The box with five levers, add one to A and go to the next line,
              the same for B, add one to a jump target (leaving the window alone), and for each
              of A and B “if it’s above 0, take one off and jump to the target, otherwise go to the
              next line, and put the target back to 0 either way,” is a base by simulation, has no
              universal base, and its grouped box lifts to a certified Thiele-complete machine.

              Proof. (1) Each list has one lever. (2) The grouped box’s step is the box’s run along
              the list, so the simulation clause of a base by simulation is clause (a) for the grouped
              box. (3) is (2) with Theorem 30.1. (4) INC r is one pull; DEC r j is j pulls of the
              target lever, then the decrement lever for r, from a live state, where live means the
              target is 0; the decrement lever puts the target back to 0, so the state stays live. The
              box has five levers, so item (1) of Theorem 30.2 says it has no universal base.
                  So clause (a) can be read by simulation, and nothing is lost: a box that carries
              out each two-counter instruction with one fixed finite list of its own moves, through
              any window, is a universal base the moment its moves are grouped, and it lifts. Item
              (1) of Theorem 30.2 is about the presentation, and the five-lever box is the proof
              that a finite lever set is not what fails. What this doesn’t give is a Turing machine’s
              rule table running a tape: the five-lever box is a register box, and the next subsection
              is still how Turing machines come in.
                  Item (3) is a box with no memory. Pull a lever and it lands wherever that
              lever always lands, whatever happened before. So a window on it can’t tell INC A
              followed by INC A from INC B followed by INC A, and a box that can’t tell those
              apart can’t keep two counters in its window.
                  Item (4) is the trouble with bare preorders from Section 4, where the cups
              stopped telling you the place, showing up one level higher. If every place on the axis
              is at or below every other, the flag has no up to go to. No step ever turns a cup over,
              nothing ever has to be paid for, and the earned chain has nothing to earn.


              30.3 Computation is the slice

              The lift says the panel goes onto any box with a universal base. Turn it round and
              it says something about the box: the box is what you see of the lift when you stop
                                                                       30.3 Computation is the slice   269


looking at the panel. That’s where ordinary computation sits in this book, so I want
it said exactly, with a name.

Definition 30.1 (Universal-base slice). Take a machine with a ledger, a whole
number for each state, and a loaded state for each start, and take a box with a
universal base. A universal-base slice, called a slice in this appendix, is different from
the global sliced interface of Definition 2.3. It requires a universal base, a working
domain and reachable hidden witnesses; it does not require all non-computing
moves to fix the view. It is a map from the machine’s states to the box’s states, a
move of the machine for each lever of the box, called its computing move, and a class
of the machine’s states, called working, such that: (i) each loaded state is working,
and the map sends it to the box’s loaded state for the same start; (ii) from a working
state, a computing move lands on a working state, and the map sends where it lands
to where the box’s lever takes the map’s image; (iii) from some loaded state there
are two runs whose ends the map sends to one box state, with the record up at one
end and down at the other, and two runs whose ends it sends to one box state, with
different ledgers.

    When there’s a slice, I’ll say the box is a slice of the machine. The first two clauses
say the slice runs as the box runs. Start with a working state and pull one computing
lever. Clause (ii) says that its picture moves exactly as the box moves, and that
the full state is working again. That last part lets you apply the same rule to the
next lever, then the next. A whole run therefore follows the box’s run, move for
move. Clause (i) puts the two at matching starts, so they finish at matching pictures
too. The third clause says it can’t see what was kept or what it cost: a function of
the box state that gave the record at the end of every run from every loaded state
would have to give both answers at the one box state the clause names, and the
same goes for the ledger. Nothing else is hidden in the definition. Read the box’s
window through the map and take as live the working states whose image is live,
and the first two clauses make the computing moves for the box’s instruction levers a
universal base of the machine. So a machine has a slice onto some box exactly when
it has a universal base whose window meets the third clause, and then the window
itself is a slice onto the two-counter box, whose states are configurations and whose
levers are the instructions. The third clause does work. A Thiele-complete machine’s
computing moves, taken alone, are a box, and the map that forgets nothing meets
the first two clauses onto it and fails the third, because the record and the ledger are
functions of the state; and the window of the first clock of Observation 17.3, whose
record is a question about its counters, fails it too.
270   30 Every model




              Theorem 30.4 (Computation is the slice). (1) Take the lift of a box with a universal
              base, as in Theorem 30.1(1). Forgetting the panel, which keeps the box’s part of
              each state and drops the tally, the table, the channel, the trap, the ledger and the
              flag, is a slice of the lift onto the box, with the levers as its computing moves and
              the states whose trap is down as its working states. Beyond that, CHECK, COMMIT
              and CERTIFY never move the box’s part, from any state; from any box state with
              an empty panel, the lever runs are sent, under forgetting, to exactly the box’s own
              runs from that state, lever list for lever list; and from a loaded state where the claim
              of Theorem 30.1(1) is true, the run of no moves and the run CHECK, COMMIT,
              CERTIFY of that claim end on one box state with the flag down and up and ledgers
              exactly 3 apart, CERTIFY three times ends there too with the flag down and the
              same ledger as the second run, and CERTIFY d times, for any d, ends there with
              the flag down and a ledger d above the first run’s. (2) With no room in the fact
              table, the lift of any box, with any claims, is certified Thiele-complete through no
              interface at all. (3) Let a machine be Thiele-complete through an interface; every
              certified Thiele-complete machine is (Theorem 22.3). Its computing moves are
              exactly its moves that cost nothing, so they’re the same through every interface it’s
              Thiele-complete through; taken alone, with the interface’s window, live states and
              loaded states, they form a machine with a universal base; and the window is a slice
              of the machine onto the two-counter box, with the instructions’ moves as computing
              moves and the live states as working. (4) Let a machine be run as one rule: from
              each state, every move leads to the same next state. It has no universal base, no
              base by simulation and no universal-base slice onto any box. It obeys the toll exactly
              when every move costs at least one or no state reading no steps to one reading yes,
              so it’s a Thiele machine only as a clock or with a reading that never rises.

              Proof. (1) The lift’s loaded states are the box’s loaded states with an empty panel
              and the trap down, so they’re working, and forgetting sends each to the box’s loaded
              state for its start. From a state with the trap down a lever moves the box’s part as the
              box’s step does and leaves the trap down (Theorem 30.1(4)), which is the second
              clause. Start with any box state and an empty panel. The first pull matches the
              box’s move and keeps the trap down; the next pull therefore starts under the same
              conditions. Repeat through the word and every pull matches. A CHECK, COMMIT
              or CERTIFY that passes changes only the table, the channel, the flag and the ledger;
              one that fails sets the trap and adds to the ledger; and with the trap up every move
              changes nothing but the ledger. None of them touches the box’s part. Now take the
              loaded state where the claim is true. The run of no moves ends there with the flag
              down and the ledger at 0. On the other run the CHECK passes and files the claim,
                                                                     30.3 Computation is the slice   271


the table having room, the COMMIT finds it at the same tally, and the CERTIFY
raises the flag: three moves at one each, none of them moving the box, so the same
box state, the flag up and the ledger at 3. CERTIFY from a clean state finds the
channel empty and traps, and every later move changes only the ledger, so CERTIFY
d times ends at the same box state with the flag down and the ledger at d, which at
d = 3 is the chain’s ledger. So the third clause holds, for the flag and for the ledger,
and no function of the box state and the ledger together gives the flag. (2) Take any
interface and the three moves its clause (d) names. Each costs one by clause (c), so
each is a button. Take a loaded state from which they raise the flag; it’s clean by
(a), so its flag is down by (b). If its trap is up, nothing passes and nothing rises. If
its channel holds a fact, CERTIFY alone raises the flag from it: a run of one move
from a clean state that ends with the flag up, where (b) asks for a CHECK and a
COMMIT before the raise. If its channel is empty, some COMMIT has to pass inside
the three moves, before the CERTIFY that raises the flag. Until then no CHECK has
passed, because a CHECK passes only with room in the table, and no button moves
the tally, so that COMMIT found its fact in the loaded state’s own table at the loaded
state’s own tally; that COMMIT and a CERTIFY, run from the loaded state, then raise
the flag in two moves, against (b) again. So the three moves raise the flag from no
loaded state, and clause (d) fails. (3) By (C), computing moves cost 0 and every
other move costs 1, so the computing moves are the moves that cost 0, whichever
interface the machine is read through. Throw the rest away and what’s left has the
same states, the interface’s window, live states and loaded states, and a move for
each instruction that acts on the window as the instruction does from every live
state and keeps it live, with each loaded state live and showing (1, a, b): a universal
base, as in Theorem 30.1(3). The same facts are the first two clauses of a slice onto
the two-counter box, and the third is Theorem 17.5(2): two runs from one loaded
state end on one window, one with the record up and one with it down, and their
ledgers differ. (4) From each state all the moves lead to one state, so the machine is
finitely branching, and by Theorem 30.2(1) it has no universal base. A slice would
give it one, as the remark after Definition 30.1 says. A base by simulation fails too.
A list of p moves does p steps of the rule, so two lists run one after the other end
in the same place in either order. From the loaded state showing (1, 0, 0), INC A
and then DEC A to line 5 shows (2, 1, 0) and then (5, 0, 0), while DEC A to line 5
and then INC A shows (2, 0, 0) and then (3, 1, 0), and one state can’t show both.
For the toll: if a state reading no steps to one reading yes, every move takes that
step, so every move costs at least one, every step does, and the machine is a clock
(Observation 17.3); if none does, the reading never rises and the toll asks nothing.
  Not every part of that carries the same weight, and I’d rather say which before
somebody else does. Some of it holds by construction. That the slice runs as the
272   30 Every model


              box runs, and that CHECK, COMMIT and CERTIFY leave it alone, are how the lift
              was built, and the blindness in (1) follows from the second once the chain raises
              the flag: the chain and the run of no moves end on one box state because no button
              moves the box. In (3), the window meeting the first two clauses of a slice is the
              definition of Thiele-complete read out, and so is the fact that the moves that cost
              nothing are the same through every interface. Three parts could have failed. That
              the lift is certified at all is the earned chain of Theorem 30.1, clause (b). That the
              window of every Thiele-complete machine can’t see what was kept or what it cost is
              Theorem 17.5, and it could have gone the other way: the first clock’s window sees
              its record. That a table with no room certifies through no interface is (2). And (4),
              that a machine run as one rule has no universal-base slice and is a Thiele machine
              only as a clock or with a reading that never rises, is short, and its second half rests
              on one choice in Definition 2.2: the toll puts the price on the move, the same from
              every state. With a price that could read the state, a machine run as one rule could
              charge exactly its raises.
                  What “computation” means in the theorem’s name is a box’s levers acting on its
              states. A model that comes with a move for each two-counter instruction is covered
              as it stands, and one with a fixed list of its own moves for each is covered once its
              moves are grouped (Theorem 30.3). A model run as one rule, a Turing machine
              stepping by its one table, is covered by (4) only: it has no universal-base slice, and
              it cannot supply the appendix’s universal-base target. Its ordinary transition rule
              still lifts to the global sliced machine of Definition 2.3: put the lamp and tally beside
              it. The fixed-list obstruction concerns its instruction interface. A Turing machine
              read the other way, with a lever for each program that carries out one two-counter
              instruction, as in the previous subsection, is a box like any other. Nothing in the
              theorem uses the equivalence of the seven models below (Theorem 30.6(1)), which
              is other people’s work; that equivalence is what says a box with a universal base
              computes whatever the other models compute.
                  A machine run as one rule can still run programs, and its program can keep a
              flag. Whether that flag is a record the machine keeps or a bit it only simulates can
              be said exactly too, once the program is part of the state.
              Observation 30.5 (A record or a simulated bit). Take a machine whose moves are
              operations on configurations, with a reading of the configurations, and put the program
              in the state: a state is a program, which picks an operation for each configuration,
              together with a configuration, and one step applies to the configuration the operation
              the program picks there and keeps the program. Take every program. Then the reading
              never comes down under any operation from any configuration exactly when, read on
              these states, it never comes down from any state.
                                                             30.4 The same thing, in seven models   273


Proof. If no operation brings the reading down anywhere, no step does, since each
step applies an operation. If an operation brings it down from some configuration,
the program that picks that operation at every configuration is one of the programs,
and the step from the state made of that program and that configuration brings it
down.
    So whether a cell is a record or a simulated bit is a fact about the one rule that
steps program and configuration together, and it takes the whole set of states to see
it. A certification cell that some program can clear, like the one a universal machine
keeps as plain data on its tape (Section 15.10), is a simulated bit, though on a run
whose program never clears it the cell goes up and stays, as a record would.

Why the live-state clause stays in the slice
Go back to the blown fuse. A free lever turns the live machine’s gears and leaves
the trapped machine still. Hide the difference between those states and one camera
picture would have to prescribe both outcomes. Theorem 15.1 gives the exact
consequence: hide every native CHECK globally, require autonomous free moves,
and every projected free move becomes identity.
    Keep the camera promise on live states instead. The computing moves act there
and keep the machine live. A failed panel check leaves that domain. That is the
boundary in the slice above. It lets the computational view move while the native
failure rule keeps its power to stop the run. Restricting the promise to reachable
states would not do this: append a failed CHECK to a run and the trapped state
is reachable too. It is the live boundary, which that check crosses, that makes the
difference.
    The certificate and ledger remain passive beside the core. The trap is inside the
core, where the gears read it. Add the publication file and you can keep which
claims were certified, with the native lamp as its nonempty reading. The fuse still
works as before. The file records what was published; the live-state clause says
where the computing promise holds. Both boundaries belong in the description of
the machine. Theorem 15.9 proves that the lamp reports whether the publication
file is empty. Theorem 15.18 counts how many different files can sit above the same
full native state.


30.4 The same thing, in seven models

Now the other direction, the one the worry was really about. Put boxes with different
insides in a row: one with a tape, one rewriting lambda terms, one with bins. Is a
panel riding on one of them the same as a panel riding on another? The honest way
to ask is whether the boxes agree on what they compute.
274   30 Every model


                  My first idea was to wire the boxes to each other myself, two at a time, with a
              relation that says one box’s run keeps pace with another’s, but that’s the wrong tool.
              Section 11’s weak base equivalence is about runs keeping pace, and what I need
              here is coarser: do the boxes compute the same relations on numbers? For that the
              work is already done, and it isn’t mine. The seven models are the weak call-by-value
              lambda calculus L, Turing machines, two Minsky instruction conventions, binary
              stack machines, Conway’s FRACTRAN, and the µ-recursive functions, and that they
              all compute the same things is Minsky’s, Shepherdson and Sturgis’s, and Forster,
              Larchey-Wendling and their coauthors’ work. The translations, with how each one
              writes its inputs and reads its outputs, are theirs (Appendix 6).


              Theorem 30.6 (The base doesn’t matter). (1) A relation on numbers that’s com-
              putable in any one of those seven models is computable in all seven. (2) The box
              that keeps a whole two-counter configuration in one number, with one lever for
              each instruction, has a universal base, its step is computable in all seven models,
              and its lift, with a table of sixteen facts, is certified Thiele-complete. (3) A register
              machine of the kind Cook and Reckhow described, where each two-counter instruc-
              tion compiles to at most five of its own instructions, has a universal base too, and its
              lift is certified Thiele-complete. (4) Any box on numbers with a universal base lifts
              to a certified Thiele-complete machine, and its step, computable in one model, is
              computable in all seven.

              Proof. (1) Compose the translations (Appendix 6). (2) Encode a configuration as
              ⟨ℓ, ⟨ a, b⟩⟩. Decoding, performing one INC or DEC, and encoding the result are the
              total finite arithmetic operations constructed there. So the window follows each
              instruction exactly; loading is the encoding of (1, a, b). (3) Let RAM registers 0, 1
              and 2 hold the simulated line, A and B, and keep register 3 equal to 1. To increment
              counter register r, add register 3 to r and then to register 0. To decrement it with
              target j, use these five lines, numbered 0 to 4: if r > 0 jump to 3; add register 3 to
              register 0; jump to 5; subtract register 3 from r; set register 0 to j. At line 5 the finite
              block has ended. A base move restarts a block at its first line, runs it for its length,
              and keeps the resulting registers; an ended block stays put. The zero and positive
              cases give exactly the required window, and neither changes register 3. Loading
              puts (1, a, b, 1) in registers 0 to 3. (4) Apply the lift theorem to that universal base
              and apply (1) to its step function.
                 So the answer to the worry is that the record doesn’t care. A box with a universal
              base, described in any of the models people have used to say what computing is,
              takes the same panel, becomes the same kind of machine, and pays the same price,
              one each, for the same three acts.
                                                                          30.5 Why two counters   275


    And the trip goes back down. So far the panel went onto other boxes. Now put
another box, panel and all, inside mine. Appendix C.2 proved that the fixed program
UP on the small machine’s host runs every computably presented Thiele machine.
In terms of the seven models, that reads: if the four relations that describe a Thiele
machine (its driver, which picks the next move or says stop, its step, its cost and its
reading, on codes) are computed in any one of the seven by programs that answer
on every input, then the machine is computably presented, and UP runs it, halting
when it halts, with its flag, and with a surcharge of at most two when its reading
starts at no.
    So a Thiele machine described in lambda calculus runs, under one fixed program,
on the small machine’s host, which gives model independence in both directions:
up, by lifting any universal base, and down, by running anything presentable on one
fixed program. When I saw both arrows close I stopped worrying that the definition
depended on my example. A definition that did wouldn’t survive being carried both
ways.


30.5 Why two counters
If the box doesn’t matter, why did I build on two bins? I wanted the smallest box
that holds up, and the obvious smaller one has a single bin. Nothing earlier in this
book says one bin is too few. So before I’d let myself say two is the fewest, I tried
one.
    Watch what a program on one bin can see. While the bin isn’t empty, it can’t
tell how full it is: a stretch of lines that never finds the bin empty does exactly the
same thing from a fuller bin, with the extra pebbles riding along. So the only news a
one-bin program ever gets is the bin running dry. Between two of those moments it’s
just walking its own lines, and among more moments than there are lines, two are
on the same line, which is the pigeonhole again. Put those together and a one-bin
program either stops within a count you can write down in advance, or never stops
at all.


Theorem 30.7 (One counter is too few). (1) A program P of INC, DEC and HALT
instructions on one bin, started with n pebbles in it, halts exactly when it has stopped
within k (n + k + 1) steps, where k is the number of lines in P. So whether it halts is
decidable. (2) Halting for the book’s two-counter machine is undecidable. (3) So no
computable translation takes each two-counter program and start to a one-counter
program and start that halts exactly when the original does.
276   30 Every model


              Proof. (1) If k = 0, the program has already stopped. Otherwise suppose it is
              still running after T = k (n + k + 1) steps, so the T + 1 configurations at times
              0, 1, . . . , T are all live. If the counter has stayed at most n + k up to time T, those
              live configurations have only k (n + k + 1) = T possibilities: k lines and n + k + 1
              counter values. There are T + 1 of them, so one repeats, and the deterministic run
              loops forever.
                  If instead the counter reached n + k + 1 by time T, take the first time it did, and
              look back to the last time before that when it was at most n. In between it stayed
              above n, so above 0. It moves at most one per step, so on the way up it arrived at
