              each of the k + 1 levels n + 1, . . . , n + k + 1; mark the first arrival at each. There
              are k + 1 arrivals and k lines, so two arrivals have the same line ℓ, at levels a < b.
              Every DEC in the stretch between them found the counter positive. Start that same
              stretch at level b: every counter value is larger by b − a, so still positive, every DEC
              goes the same way, and the run comes back to line ℓ at level 2b − a. Repeat forever.
              Either way a run still live at time T never stops, so a run that stops does so within T
              steps, and running P for T steps decides it. (2) The tape simulation, prime packing
              and halting diagonal (Appendix 6), carried over to the book’s instruction set line by
              line. (3) Such a translation, followed by the test in (1), would decide two-counter
              halting, against (2).
                 So two counters are the fewest in exactly this sense: one counter can’t compute
              everything, because its halting can be decided, and two, handed their inputs packed,
              can. I like where the answer came from. The pigeonhole that left no spare chair in
              the closed room is the same one that says why the box has two bins.


              30.6 The lift and its instruction interface

              The lift gives each box with a universal base an earned panel. In the other direction,
              every certified Thiele-complete machine supplies a universal base through its own
              base moves. The obstruction results also identify two kinds of base that never
              lift. Together these describe the coverage proved here. Two further classification
              questions concern bases outside those classes and the possibility of certification
              through an interface other than the lift’s own.
                  The tape-to-counter construction (Appendix 6) connects bases through what they
              compute. The lift then supplies the record operations. Its tally is one edition number
              for the whole box, whereas the small machine keeps an edition per counter. That
              coarser tally is enough for the lift’s arguments. To run the lifted machine inside the
              presented-machine interpreter, supply the four computability relations for its chosen
              presentation.
                                                      30.6 The lift and its instruction interface   277


   The construction lets you follow the box and the panel separately: the base
provides its computing moves, the panel earns its record, and the live projection
recovers the box’s working run.
CHAPTER 31




Running it

Everything so far is definitions and proofs about them. Does any of it run? This
appendix is me finding out. I built the small machine twice, once straight out of the
definitions and once by hand from the same description, ran both, and compared
the whole state after every step. Then I ran the universal programs on guests small
enough to finish, and real programs through the part of the compiler that executes.
    One rule holds for the whole appendix: the runs test the builds, and the proofs
carry the theorems. A run shows what a build did on the inputs it was given, and no
theorem in the book leans on one: every theorem in it stands on its argument, on
the page.
    There are two counts to keep apart as you read. The ledger counts what the price
list charges. The number of interpreter steps counts the work this particular build
does to carry the run out. One guest step can take millions of host steps and add
one to the ledger.
  Stage                     What the next stage must preserve
  Source orders             Variable values and completed procedure calls
  Assignments and jumps     The same branches, loops and final answer
  Counter blocks            Values at block boundaries; spare counters restored
  Host instructions         The same result, with the opposite DEC convention trans-
                            lated
  Packed      two-counter   The result as an exponent in the packed counter
  guest
  Fixed interpreter UP      Guest counters at matching points; decode the exponent
                            to read the answer
This is a path through the compiler, not a claim that one step at each stage takes
the same time. The source compiler inserts no certification chain. Running the
executable uses physical hardware and energy. These runs did not measure heat per
host step, and the ledger is not a heat measurement. The times only tell you which
runs are practical on this build. The table shows what ran and what didn’t.

Theorem 31.1 (The compiler is correct). For every source program with no re-
cursion, and every input: the program computes y exactly when the runner (the
bounded evaluator of the compiled host program) halts with y for some amount
of fuel; exactly when the compiled host program halts with y; exactly when the
two-counter guest halts with y; and exactly when UP running that guest halts with
y, with its first counter at 0, its ledger at 0 and its flag down. So a terminating
compiled program delivers its answer and certifies nothing. To certify a claim about
that answer, a target program must add a CHECK, a COMMIT and a CERTIFY; this
compiler does not insert that chain. The runner also takes exactly as many steps as
the host program does, with the rest of its fuel left over, and a run that doesn’t halt
has used all its fuel.
CHAPTER 32




The CHSH check

The example machine checks whether a number is zero, even, or at least something.
That’s a fine way to show the chain, and a modest thing to certify. This appendix
builds a check worth earning from zero, and then shows the one thing the small
machine’s host does with it, which is smaller and more exact than “it checks for
quantum behaviour,” a sentence that’s tempting and wrong. In brief, so you know
where this is going: the check never computes the famous number and never writes
down the famous bound. It tests seven facts about integers in eight counts. If they
pass, a certain five-by-five table has a property called positive semidefinite, and
that property forces the famous number under the famous bound. The wall is a
consequence of the check, and it never goes into it. Everything else is explaining
those sentences.
   Why put it in the book? Because it pulls four things apart that the small examples
keep bunched together: the counts, the relation the counts satisfy, the act of checking
that relation, and the record kept of the act. Writing a tally down costs nothing on
this machine. The checker decides a real condition about a matrix, using nothing
but integer arithmetic. The step ties the passing check to this edition of the tally,
and the earned chain costs three, the same three it costs for “A is zero.” How deep
the condition is and what the three acts cost are different numbers, and here you
can see them apart.
   And the tally on its own carries no history of where it came from. A tally
somebody made up passes the same arithmetic as one that was played. What’s
claimed here is about the numbers and what the machine does with them, and the
guarantee paragraph below says exactly how far that goes.


32.1 The game
There are two players, Alice and Bob, in separate rooms. A referee flips two
independent fair coins, independent also of the players’ plan, and hands Alice a
question x, zero or one, and Bob a question y, zero or one. Alice answers a, zero
or one; Bob answers b. No talking during the game. They may agree on a plan
beforehand, as detailed as they like. They win a round when their answers differ

                                 280
                                                                                      32.1 The game   281


exactly when both questions were one. In words: if either question is zero, match; if
both are one, differ.
   Try to win every time. A plan is a list: what Alice says to x equals zero, what
she says to x equals one, and the same two for Bob, four bits in all. Now write the
four requirements the rounds impose: a0 equals b0 (both asked zero); a0 equals
b1; a1 equals b0; a1 differs from b1. Chain the first three: a0 equals b0 equals a1,
and a0 equals b1, so b0 equals b1. But the fourth says a1 differs from b1, and a1
equals b0 equals b1, a contradiction, so one of the four must break. So any plan
loses at least one of the four question pairs, and if the referee asks all four equally
often, the best a plan can do is three out of four, seventy-five percent. Do it the
long way too: there are sixteen plans (four bits), and if you write them all out and
count wins you’ll find eight win three rounds and eight win one, and none wins
four (the broken requirements always come in odd numbers, which you can see by
adding up the four equations and keeping only whether the totals come out even
or odd, with “equal” as 0 and “differ” as 1: each of the four answers appears twice
on the left, so the left sides add to an even number, and the right sides add to one).
Sharing a coin before the game to pick a plan at random doesn’t help; a mixture of
numbers no larger than three-quarters is no larger than three-quarters. Arthur Fine,
a philosopher of physics at the University of Washington, much of whose work is on
the physicist Albert Einstein and the quantum theory, proved in 1982 that the whole
“pre-agreed answers, no influence between rooms” story is exactly the mixtures of
those sixteen lists, one scoreboard for all four questions at once; the sixteen are the
whole classical story. That one I don’t have to take on his word: the form of his
theorem that speaks of the four averages is proved in this book, and the end of this
appendix says where.
     Alice’s question   Bob’s question   Both answer 0   Required relation   Result
             0                 0             equal            equal           win
             0                 1             equal            equal           win
             1                 0             equal            equal           win
             1                 1             equal          different         lose
Changing the four planned answers can move the loss to another question pair. It
cannot remove every loss: the first three equality requirements already force the
fourth equality.
   Now the number itself: recode answers zero and one as plus one and minus one.
For each question pair, let E be the average, over many rounds, of Alice’s answer
times Bob’s. The CHSH quantity is S equals E00 plus E01 plus E10 minus E11. For
one fixed plan each win adds one to S and each loss subtracts one, so S is wins
minus losses: a three-win plan has S equal to two, a one-win plan has minus two.
With equal question frequencies, the win rate is one half plus S over eight. So
282   32 The CHSH check


             “seventy-five percent” and “S at most two” are the same fact, and the second is the
             one that got the name.
                The ceiling is easy to reach and costs nothing to write down. Tally one round per
             question pair with both players answering zero every time: after the recoding every
             answer is plus one, so all four correlators are one. That’s the plan that answers zero
             to everything, which matches on every pair and so wins the three pairs that ask for
             a match, S = 1 + 1 + 1 − 1 = 2. On the small machine’s host a tally like that is a
             few INCs on counters, and INC costs nothing. Nothing in it says the rounds were
             played; anybody can tally anything at no charge on the ledger. The other side, that
             no finite mixture of plans beats two, is Theorem 33.1 in Appendix 33, one direction
             of Fine’s result. The full equivalence, in the form that speaks of the four averages,
             appears in the last subsection of this appendix.


              32.2 Where it came from, and why anyone cared
             So who started this, and why would anybody care whether two rooms can win a
             coin game? The names come in a line, and the line didn’t mean much to me until
             I saw that each of them moved the same thing a notch: from an argument about
             what’s real, which you can only agree with or not, toward a pile of counts a referee
             can judge. Each one rests on a different piece of the game above.
                 The trail starts in 1935, with Einstein, by then at the Institute for Advanced Study
             in Princeton, and two younger colleagues there, Boris Podolsky and Nathan Rosen,
             asking whether quantum mechanics describes everything that’s real about a pair of
             particles. Their argument combines certain prediction with a no-disturbance premise:
             if measuring one particle lets you predict the other’s result without disturbing it,
             their criterion treats that result as an element of reality. They argue that the quantum
             description leaves something out. What they rest on is the plan in each player’s
             pocket, a pre-agreed list of answers, the plan above. And the first thing worth
             checking is whether the paper even says what Einstein meant, and by his own
             account it doesn’t quite. Podolsky did the writing, and that June Einstein wrote to
             Erwin Schrödinger, the Austrian physicist whose 1926 wave equation is at the heart
             of quantum mechanics, that the essential thing had been “smothered by formalism.”
             The person who lays that letter out is Arthur Fine, the same Fine whose theorem
             closed the sixteen plans above, in his encyclopedia entry on the argument. So Fine
             stands at both ends of the line: he checked the founding paper against what its
             author meant, and he proved exactly what the plans can do. Even the founding
             paper is a lead to check, not a verdict.
                 For thirty years that was philosophy. In 1964 John Bell turned it into arithmetic:
             if each particle carries pre-agreed answers and nothing travels between the rooms,
             certain averages can’t exceed a fixed number, and quantum theory predicts they do.
                                                    32.2 Where it came from, and why anyone cared   283


What that rests on took me a few reads. For this game the assumptions can be said
without that shorthand: the questions are independent of the shared preparation,
and each room produces its answer using its own question and that preparation,
with any local randomness included. Even stochastic response rules under those
conditions are mixtures of the sixteen plans. Giving up predetermined answers alone
therefore does not evade the bound. What fails is this local explanation under the
stated independence condition. And where does a paper like that get published? In
a brand-new journal called Physics Physique Fizika, which charged authors no page
fees and in fact paid them by the word, ten cents a word, and which folded in 1968
after four volumes, or three. I went looking for that count, and the sources don’t
agree on it: four is the number the American Physical Society’s archive of the journal
gives, and Iulia Georgescu, writing about the journal in Physics World in 2026, says
three. She’s also where the ten cents comes from. As Andrew Whitaker tells it in
his biography of Bell, the journal gave out no free copies, so Bell spent his payment
buying copies of his own paper to mail to other physicists. A result nobody else
holds a copy of hasn’t left the room yet, and Bell paid to send his across. In 1969
four physicists, John Clauser, then a graduate student at Columbia, Michael Horne
and Abner Shimony at Boston University, and Richard Holt at Harvard, rewrote it
as the four-average form above, S at most two, which could be tried in a lab with
imperfect equipment. It’s called the CHSH inequality, after their initials. That notch
rests on what a real lab can deliver. Shimony was in philosophy and in physics at
Boston University both, which is the whole line in one man.
    Then the obvious next question: did anybody actually try it? Stuart Freedman,
a graduate student at Berkeley, and Clauser, by then a postdoc there too, tried
it in 1972. The physicist Alain Aspect’s group at the Institut d’Optique in Orsay,
with two colleagues there, Jean Dalibard and Gérard Roger, did it in 1982 with the
measurement settings switched while the photons were in flight. Their switches
changed the analyzer faster than a light signal could cross between the stations.
They operated at fixed, incommensurate frequencies, however, rather than choosing
fresh independent random settings on every trial. Rapid switching and unpredictable
questions are separate requirements; this experiment did not close every loophole.
Before that, in 1975, Aspect went to see Bell at CERN to tell him what he planned,
and by Aspect’s own account Bell heard him out in silence and then asked, first thing,
whether he had a permanent position. Aspect did. Bell’s reason, as Aspect tells it,
was that most physicists would take the experiment for a waste of time, or take him
for a crackpot. I read that as the toll in its oldest form: a check that could have come
out the other way costs somebody something to run, and Bell wanted to know who’d
pay. And in 2015 three groups of experimental physicists (Bas Hensen’s in Delft,
with electrons 1.3 kilometres apart; Marissa Giustina’s in Vienna; Lynden Shalm’s
at NIST, the National Institute of Standards and Technology, in Boulder) closed the
284   32 The CHSH check


             two main escape routes at once. In 2022 Clauser, Aspect and the Austrian physicist
             Anton Zeilinger shared the Nobel Prize “for experiments with entangled photons,
             establishing the violation of Bell inequalities and pioneering quantum information
             science.” The 2015 tests came out of that line of work, and Zeilinger was one of the
             authors of the Vienna one. So in under fifty years the subject went from a question
             you needed a permanent job to risk to a Nobel. The lab number sits above two. A
             remaining escape, that the referee’s coins were secretly correlated with the particles,
             is a philosophical one and I’m not going to pretend it’s closed.
                 Now put this book’s check on the same line, because that’s where my position
             gives. It sits past the far end, at the counts. Those fifty years went into making the
             counts worth judging, rooms really apart, questions really late, escape routes really
             shut, and my check starts after all of it. It’s handed eight counts and can’t tell a
             lab’s tally from one somebody wrote down at no charge. So the check is a referee of
             counts, and everything that made these counts worth refereeing happened upstream
             of it.
                 What quantum play does: if the players share an entangled pair and choose what
             to measure from their question, the best they can do is S equals two times the square
             root of two, about 2.83, a win rate of about eighty-five percent. Boris Tsirelson,
             a Soviet mathematician who later taught at Tel Aviv University, in a 1980 paper
             called “Quantum generalizations of Bell’s inequality,” proved that that’s a ceiling:
             quantum correlations violate Bell, but only up to there. I prove that ceiling below,
             and show the state that reaches it, with real amplitudes and with complex ones. The
             bound holds in arbitrary dimension, including infinite dimension; the construction
             attaining it needs sufficient room, supplied by the two-qubit example below. The
             first thing proved is the algebra.
                 Then there’s the wall above that, which is the one I find most instructive. In
             1994 the physicists Sandu Popescu and Daniel Rohrlich asked: what if you only
             demand that nothing can be signalled, that Alice’s answers on their own have the
             same odds whatever Bob is asked, and the other way round? Then you can write
             down a “box” (the PR box, after their initials) that wins every round (its answers
             differ exactly when both questions are one, by fiat, each answer alone a fair coin), S
             equals four, and nothing about it lets either room send a message. So no-signalling
             on its own doesn’t explain the quantum ceiling; the PR box meets that condition and
             beats 2.83. (A box isn’t a physical theory, and nobody is claiming one that meets
             everything relativity asks.) The order is: two for plans, 2.83 for quantum, four for
             no-signalling.
                 And the box has the same kind of history as everything else in this book: some-
             body got there first. Popescu and Rohrlich’s initials stuck, but the big review of the
             field by Nicolas Brunner and four coauthors credits boxes like it to Peter Rastall,
             whose 1985 paper on locality and Bell’s theorem appeared in Foundations of Physics,
                                                  32.3 Five sentences that are easy to say by accident   285


and, separately, to Leonid Khalfin, a physicist at the Steklov Institute’s Leningrad
branch, and Tsirelson himself, both in 1985, nine years before. Then the question
that really matters: if relativity isn’t what stops things at 2.83, what is? People have
tried candidates. In 2005 the computer scientist Wim van Dam, who works on the
theory of quantum computing, argued, in a paper on the implausible consequences
of such boxes, that with enough PR boxes, Bob could work out any yes-or-no func-
tion of both their inputs after Alice sends him a single bit, which would make the
whole question of how much two parties need to talk collapse, and that’s a strong
hint the boxes are too good to be real. In 2009 Marcin Pawłowski, a physicist
at the University of Gdańsk, and five coauthors proposed a principle they called
information causality: Bob can’t get more information about Alice’s data than the
bits she actually sends him. They argue that ordinary and quantum physics both
obey it, and that any theory that beats 2.83 breaks it. How much of the rest of the
quantum boundary it reaches is something I haven’t checked. Which principle fixes
the whole quantum boundary, if any one does, this book won’t answer. The check
below is a fence at the middle step.


32.3 Five sentences that are easy to say by accident
Each one comes below with what I can stand behind beside it.
    “Faster than light.” The correlations alone cannot carry a chosen message between
the rooms: each room’s marginal probabilities do not depend on the other question.
That operational statement does not settle every account of what happens between
measurements. “Quantum means random.” The state and the chosen measurements
determine the outcome probabilities and expected score; a particular outcome
can be certain or random. “A Bell violation proves nonlocality.” A statistically
supported violation rejects the local response model under the setting-independence
and experimental assumptions. Calling it a rejection of “realism” does not identify
those assumptions.
    “A tally above two proves nonlocality.” No: S is a population average, and one
finite tally can land above two by luck. Four rounds, one per question pair, with coin-
flip answers, reach S equals four one time in sixteen. That gap between a population
bound and a finite sample is the problem Yanbao Zhang, Scott Glancy and Emanuel
Knill, at NIST in Boulder, work through for real Bell tests (Physical Review A, 2011
and 2013), and the book doesn’t solve it. The way they solve it is worth knowing,
because it has the shape of this book’s record. Turn each round into a small bet
that no local plan can expect to win, even a plan that remembers every earlier
round, multiply the bets as the rounds come in, and the French mathematician Jean
Ville’s inequality, from his 1939 thesis, one of the works that started the theory of
martingales, says the chance the product ever climbs past one over δ is at most δ. So
286   32 The CHSH check


             a flag can go up for good the moment the product crosses, with its error stated up
             front. It needs one thing the small machine doesn’t have: settings drawn underneath
             the program, where the program can’t choose them. And how much can one round
             tell you? Wim van Dam, Richard Gill, a mathematical statistician who later held the
             chair in that subject at Leiden, and Peter Grünwald (2005) measured exactly that,
             in bits of evidence against every local plan. Of the two-party proofs they compare,
             CHSH comes out the strongest, “much stronger” than the others in their words, at
             about 0.046 of a bit per round, a few hundredths, so a convincing record takes
             hundreds of rounds, and four won’t do. About a finite tally, this book proves only the
             narrow thing, that a tally above two, with every question pair asked at least once, is
             not explained by one fixed plan replayed every round, and that narrow thing is what
             the sentence has to stay. The bound for a finite local model is a separate theorem,
             and it says nothing about one tally. And “the PSD test proves the data is quantum.”
             (PSD is short for positive semidefinite, the property of the five-by-five table from
             the opening of this appendix; the next subsection builds both.) It doesn’t, and the
             direction matters. Passing implies the five-by-five table, pinned in a way the next
             subsection explains, is PSD, and that implies S squared is at most eight; both are
             proved. The correlators of a PSD table like that are the correlators of some quantum
             strategy, by Tsirelson’s representation theorem, which is proved here: the strategy is
             built on two systems of four levels each, or two levels each with complex amplitudes.
             The exact edge of the quantum set for this game was found by Lawrence Landau,
             whose 1969 Berkeley thesis was on the quantum theory of local observables, and
             Lluís Masanes, then at the University of Barcelona (next subsection), and that’s
             proved here too. Failing says nothing about whether the play was quantum: the
             check is complete for the pinned matrix, the quantum set is wider than that, and
             quantum behaviour can fail it. Every one of the sixteen plans fails it, and I’ll show
             you why. What it guarantees is in one paragraph, once the check is on the host
             machine.


              32.4 The five-by-five, and what positive semidefinite means
             The mathematics the check rests on is one idea: a table of numbers could be a table
             of dot products. Take a two-by-two symmetric table with a and c on the diagonal
             and b off it. It’s positive semidefinite (PSD) when a and c are at least zero and a
             times c minus b squared is at least zero. Check two by hand: ones on the diagonal
             and one half off, the determinant is three quarters, passes; ones on the diagonal and
             1.2 off, the determinant is negative, fails. The reading: PSD means the table could
             be the dot products of some arrows, and 1.2 is too big a dot product for two arrows
             of length one. Everything else is that idea, generalised to bigger tables.
                                          32.4 The five-by-five, and what positive semidefinite means   287


    Now the table. Rows and columns labelled I, A0, A1, B0, B1 (the identity, Alice’s
two questions, Bob’s two). Diagonal all ones. The I row and column zero off the
diagonal (that’s “zero marginals”: the table takes each answer alone to average to
zero, a fair coin; the counts record no marginals and the check doesn’t test this). A0
with A1 zero, B0 with B1 zero (no round asks Alice both questions, so no tally holds
that average, and the table sets it to zero). Those zeros are choices. They pin the
table to one slice, and I’ll come back to what they cost. And the four Alice-Bob cells
hold the four correlators E, which leaves a five-by-five with four free numbers in it.
It splits into the one in the corner and a four-by-four block made of an identity and
the two-by-two grid E of correlators, and PSD of that block is the same as saying
E shrinks every vector (E is a contraction), which is three inequalities: one minus
E00 squared minus E10 squared at least zero; one minus E01 squared minus E11
squared at least zero; and their product at least the square of (E00 E01 plus E10
E11).
    Run it. All four correlators 0.6 except E11 at minus 0.6, S equals 2.4: the two
diagonal terms are 0.28 and 0.28, the cross term zero, passes; above two, below
2.83. All four at one over root two (E11 negative): diagonals zero, cross term zero,
passes with no slack, S equals two root two exactly, the 2.83; nudge any entry’s
size up and a diagonal goes negative. The all-ones plan (one, one, one, one): first
diagonal is one minus one minus one, negative, fails, though S equals two and it’s a
perfectly legal classical play. The PR box (one, one, one, minus one): fails the same
way. The check is a fence at the middle step, and a fence that keeps out every one
of the sixteen plans: each has all four correlators at plus or minus one, so the first
diagonal is always one minus one minus one. Of the classical plays, only diluted
mixtures of plans get through.
    That point, one over root two in size in every entry, is four real numbers, and no
tally of rounds ever lands on it. A tally’s correlators are ratios of whole numbers,
and the point needs each of them to be one over root two, which would make root
two a fraction, and you killed that fraction with a pencil in Section 6. That point
and its mirror image, every sign flipped, are the only places the bound is reached
(Remark 33.1 in the appendix). So a passing tally always has S squared strictly
below eight. It can get as close as you like: the (17, 3) tally in the five examples
below has S squared equal to 7.84, and tallies of the same shape with a fraction
nearer one over root two get nearer still. Two root two is the least bound the check
can promise, and no tally the check accepts ever reaches it. Hold on to that corner;
it comes back.
    Picture what quantum play can reach as a field, and every test as a fence. In 2007
Miguel Navascués, Stefano Pironio and Antonio Acín, in a paper called “Bounding
the Set of Quantum Correlations” (NPA, after their initials), came at the edge from
outside, resting on necessary conditions. In their terms a table like this one is the
288   32 The CHSH check


             first rung of a ladder: bigger tables of expected products, each PSD, each a necessary
             condition for quantum behaviour when its entries are left free, each rung tighter, and
             (their 2008 paper) converging to the quantum set (strictly, the set from commuting
             operators, which can differ from the tensor-product set in general scenarios). Each
             rung is a fence with the whole field inside it. The check’s table is the first rung
             with the marginals and the two within-party entries pinned to zero. Pinning is a
             stronger demand than the rung makes, so passing puts you inside the first rung, and
             a quantum point can still fail. My fence cuts across the field. Lawrence Landau in
             1988 and Lluís Masanes in 2003 came at the edge itself, for this two-questions-each
             case only. I’d have drawn a flat wall at 2.83. The exact boundary they found is a
             curved surface (the arc-sine form) touching 2.83 at one point. It’s proved here: a
             table has a PSD completion exactly when its entries lie between minus one and one
             and their arcsines pass four inequalities, so those are exactly the quantum tables.
                 So the obvious next question: if every rung is tighter than the last, does some
             rung finally land exactly on the quantum set and stop? My first guess was yes,
             at least here. A 2026 preprint by Anubhav Chaturvedi, a quantum-information
             theorist at the University of Gdańsk, says no: no finite rung of the ladder captures
             the whole quantum set even in this simplest case. And for the four correlators
             alone, a 2008 paper by the same Navascués, Pironio and Acín works out that the
             first rung already lands on Landau and Masanes’s curved edge. That looked like a
             contradiction until I saw what each one fences. Landau and Masanes draw the edge
             for the four correlators alone. Chaturvedi’s is the full behaviours, each room’s own
             odds included. So there’s the corner again from the other side: tallies never reach
             it, and, if his proof holds, rungs never close on the field. I wanted that to be one
             fact, but it’s two. Mine is root two again, about which points a count can hit. His is
             about the fences. His result backs, from outside, the caution this book keeps about
             the ladder, that no finite rung may close on the whole quantum set. It says nothing
             about my own check, though. My check’s caution, that a quantum point can fail it,
             comes from the pinning, and his paper never pins anything.


              32.5 The check, in integers
             There are eight counts: for each of the four question pairs, one counting rounds
             where the answers matched and one where they differed. A round is one bump
             to one count; nothing about the round is tested, and no marginals are recorded.
             Then the check reads the eight and tests seven integer facts. For each pair, let N be
             same plus different and D be same minus different (so E is D over N). The seven:
             the four N are strictly positive (every pair was sampled; a pair with no rounds is
             rejected outright, and never read as a correlator of zero); A, which is N00 squared
             N10 squared minus D00 squared N10 squared minus D10 squared N00 squared, is
                                                                             32.5 The check, in integers   289


at least zero; B, the same with the other column, is at least zero; and with C equal
to D00 D01 N10 N11 plus D10 D11 N00 N01, C squared is at most A times B. Those
are the three contraction inequalities with the denominators cleared, and there’s no
division in them, no reals, no square root and no S. Try it: counts (same, different)
of (4, 1), (4, 1), (4, 1), (1, 4) give E of 0.6, 0.6, 0.6, minus 0.6; N is five and D is
three (minus three for the last); A is 175, B is 175, C is zero; passes. These counts
are the first example above, the S equals 2.4 one, written in integers.
                   Question pair    same    different   total   difference
                        00            4         1         5          3
                        01            4         1         5          3
                        10            4         1         5          3
                        11            1         4         5         −3
First check the four totals: all are positive. Each column’s remaining allowance
is 625 − 225 − 225 = 175. The cross term is 225 − 225 = 0. Thus the final
comparison is 0 ≤ 1752 , and the check passes. These steps verify the arithmetic;
they supply no evidence that rounds were played.
   The theorem chain, in words: a passed check gives PSD of the five-by-five built
from the counts; contraction and PSD are equivalent, both directions; and PSD forces
S squared at most eight. The names are in the statements below. The underlying
algebra is one identity: four times the sum of the four correlators squared, minus S
squared, is a sum of six squares, so if the row sums of squares are at most one, S
squared is at most eight, and the point one over root two reaches it. So Tsirelson’s
bound comes out as algebra, with no Hilbert space (the mathematical home of
quantum states) in it.
   The chain goes below as three statements. The derivations are in Appendix 33,
and the statements say what they prove.

Definition 32.1 (Correlators from the counts; the pinned matrix). For eight counts
and a question pair xy, let Exy = (samexy − diffxy )/(samexy + diffxy ), read as
0 when both are 0. For reals Exy , M ( E) is the symmetric five-by-five matrix on
I, A0 , A1 , B0 , B1 with ones on the diagonal, Exy in the cells ( A x , By ) and ( By , A x ),
and zero everywhere else (Definition 33.5). M is PSD when vT Mv ≥ 0 for every
real vector v, and S( E) = E00 + E01 + E10 − E11 .



Theorem 32.1 (The integer check implies the pinned matrix is PSD). If eight counts
pass the seven integer facts, then M ( E) for their correlators is symmetric and PSD.
290   32 The CHSH check


              Proof. The four positivity facts make every denominator positive, so each Exy is
              Dxy /Nxy , and dividing the other three facts by positive squares turns them into the
              three contraction inequalities (Lemma 33.9). Contraction is equivalent to PSD of
              M ( E) by a Schur complement on the four-by-four block (Corollary 33.7).
                 In the appendix this is Theorem 33.10, and Corollary 33.7 gives the equivalence
              of contraction and PSD in both directions.


             Theorem 32.2 (PSD forces the Tsirelson bound). For all real E00 , E01 , E10 , E11 : if
             M ( E) is symmetric and PSD, then S( E)2 ≤ 8.

             Proof. Call the four entries a, b, c, d, read across the rows. PSD means that no
             choice of five weights makes the matrix’s weighted total negative. Choose weights
             (0, 1, 0, − a, −b) in the order ( I, A0 , A1 , B0 , B1 ). The diagonal contributes 1 + a2 +
             b2 , and the two cross terms contribute −2a2 − 2b2 . The total is 1 − a2 − b2 , so
             a2 + b2 ≤ 1. Weights (0, 0, 1, −c, −d) give c2 + d2 ≤ 1. All four squares therefore
             add to at most two.
                 Now compare four times that sum with the squared score. Their difference is
                 4( a2 + b2 + c2 + d2 ) − ( a + b + c − d )2 = ( a − b )2 + ( a − c )2 + ( a + d )2
                                                                 + ( b − c )2 + ( b + d )2 + ( c + d )2 .
             To check it, each squared letter occurs three times on either expanded side; the cross
             terms are −2ab, −2ac, +2ad, −2bc, +2bd, +2cd on both. The difference cannot be
             negative because it is a sum of squares. Thus the squared score is at most four times
             two, which is eight.
                 This is Theorem 33.8 in the appendix.


             Corollary 32.3 (A passed check means the bound). If eight counts pass the seven
             integer facts, their correlators satisfy S2 ≤ 8. In fact S2 < 8, and passing tallies
             come as close to 8 as you like.

             Proof. Theorem 32.1 makes the pinned matrix of these four correlators PSD, and
             Theorem 33.8 in the appendix, which holds of every such matrix, gives S2 ≤ 8. The
             score is a ratio of whole numbers, and no such ratio squares to 8, so the bound is
                                                                                        2     2
                                                        √ n/q, n/q, n/q, −n/q with 2n ≤ q
             strict. The tallies whose four correlators are
             pass, and their scores 4n/q come as near 2 2 as you like.
                                                       32.6 The check, earned on the host machine   291


Remark 32.1 (Sound, and incomplete). The pinned matrix is stricter than the
quantum question it is usually read against. PSD of M ( E) gives the correlators
a PSD completion, since the zeros are one choice of the two free entries. The
converse fails. The all-ones plan has a PSD completion, and every deterministic
plan is a quantum strategy; yet its minor on rows A0 , B0 , B1 is 1 − 1 − 1 = −1,
so M ( E) is not PSD there, and a tally of one matching round per pair gives A =
1 − 1 − 1 = −1 in the integer check, so the check fails. The second failure is a
named theorem on the machine: the tally of one matching round per pair is refused
forever (Proposition 32.6). Every deterministic plan fails the same way. The tally of
one round per pair fails the check for each of the sixteen plans, and so does any tally
of one plan replayed over any number of rounds (a pair left unsampled fails the
positivity facts outright), because no deterministic plan’s pinned matrix is PSD. So a
pass proves the bound, and a failure proves only that the tally’s pinned matrix isn’t
PSD or a pair went unsampled; it says nothing about whether the play was classical
or quantum. The tally from the start of this appendix, written down at no cost, is
exactly such a tally: it reaches S = 2 and the check throws it out.

   None of that algebra is new, and I don’t want it to read as if it were. Mna-
cho Echenim and Mehdi Mhalla, computer scientists at Grenoble, give a detailed
treatment of the CHSH and Tsirelson bounds (2023). Navascués, Pironio and Acín
already analyse this very matrix by Schur complement and discuss the level above it.
What I think is mine here is smaller: an integer decision procedure on the counts,
and a proved link from “the check passed” to PSD to the bound.
   The picture under the check is older still. Tsirelson showed, in a longer paper
of 1987 after his 1980 note, that the correlations two separated quantum parties
can reach are exactly the tables of inner products of unit vectors, one vector for
each question on each side. With the marginals pinned to zero the pinned matrix is
that picture, and by its Schur complement it is PSD exactly when the two-by-two
table of correlators, read as a matrix, has operator norm at most one. The physicists
Reinhard Werner and Michael Wolf’s survey of Bell inequalities (2001) sets out that
correlation-matrix view for two-party inequalities in general.


32.6 The check, earned on the host machine

Now put it on the machine. A check on the small machine is a property: a checker,
which is a little program that says yes or no, a meaning, which is a statement, and
a proof that the two agree. The version of the small machine with its property
language left open (Section 15, Theorem 22.11) takes any property language with
an exact equality test whose properties come with those three things, and for every
one of them a flag raised from a clean start stands on a passing CHECK of one
292   32 The CHSH check


              claim, a later COMMIT of the same claim with its counter untouched in between,
              and a later CERTIFY, the three acts costing one each. For the game, pack the eight
              counts into one counter, as a list, the way the sorted-list machine packs a list into
              one number (Section 15). Let the checker be the seven integer facts read off the
              unpacked counts. And let the meaning be what the facts are for: every pair was
              sampled, and the pinned five-by-five is positive semidefinite. Then the proof the
              generic machine asks for, that the checker says yes exactly when the meaning holds,
              is the chain above with the denominators cleared in both directions, and a flag
              raised on that machine from a clean start means: these counts, at this edition of
              their counter, passed the check, somebody committed to that with nothing changed
              underneath, and the correlators they give satisfy S2 ≤ 8. The theorems below state
              that exactly, and they’re stronger than the chain above in one way I want to say
              once and plainly. The earlier theorems said that a passed check makes the pinned
              matrix PSD. The check is also complete for it: it passes if and only if every pair was
              sampled and the pinned matrix is PSD, because clearing the denominators works
              in both directions. The statement is over the machine of Definition C.2, the small
              machine with a counter for every number, with PCHSH in place of its one property,
              Slot. A CHECK reads one counter, which is why the eight counts have to travel in
              one.

              Definition 32.2 (The packed tally, and the property PCHSH). Read a whole
              number as a list in the list code (0 is the empty list, and 2x (2y + 1) is x fol-
              lowed by the list y), and take as its tally the first eight entries, in the order
              same00 , diff00 , same01 , diff01 , same10 , diff10 , same11 , diff11 , a missing entry reading
              0. Every number is some tally. The property PCHSH has the checker “the tally passes
              the seven integer facts” and the meaning “every question pair was sampled, and the
              pinned matrix M ( E) of the tally’s correlators is symmetric and PSD.”



             Theorem 32.4 (The check is exact). (1) The seven integer facts hold of a tally if
             and only if every pair was sampled and M ( E) is symmetric and PSD. (2) For every
             whole number v, the checker of PCHSH says yes at v if and only if the meaning
             holds of v’s tally. (3) Reading the number that packs a tally gives that √ tally back. (4)
             If the facts hold, the score of the tally satisfies S 2 ≤ 8 and | S | ≤ 2 2; in fact S2 < 8
                           √
             and |S| < 2 2, and passing tallies come as close to 8 as you like.

             Proof. (1) The forward half is Theorem 32.1. For the backward half, PSD gives the
             three contraction inequalities (Corollary 33.7); with every N positive, multiplying
             them through by the squares of the denominators gives A ≥ 0, B ≥ 0 and C2 ≤ AB,
             which is Lemma 33.9 read the other way. (2) is (1) with the packing, since every
                                                          32.6 The check, earned on the host machine   293


number is a tally. (3) is the code and its decoder being inverse. (4) is (1) forward,
then Theorem 32.2; the strict form and the closeness are Corollary 32.3.



Theorem 32.5 (A raised flag stands on the check). On the machine above, let τ be
a trace from a clean start s0 that ends with the flag up. Then, for some counter c,

  τ = pre · CHECK PCHSH c · mid1 · COMMIT PCHSH c · mid2 · CERTIFY · post,
and with v the value of c after pre: the tally of v passes the seven integer facts; every
pair was sampled and √ the pinned matrix      is symmetric and PSD; its score satisfies
  2                                     2
S ≤ 8 and |S| ≤ 2 2, and in fact S < 8, though passing tallies come as close to 8
as you like; and c still held v when the COMMIT ran. For any stored program run
from a fresh start (counters set, every version 0, line 1, the table and channel empty,
the trap and the flag down, the ledger at 0) that raises the flag, the trace it executed
contains a CHECK PCHSH c that passed, and the tally c held when it ran passes
the seven integer facts, has every pair√sampled and the pinned matrix symmetric
and PSD, and has S2 ≤ 8 and |S| ≤ 2 2. Conversely, from a fresh start in which
a counter c holds the packing of a tally t, the three instructions CHECK PCHSH c,
COMMIT PCHSH c, CERTIFY raise the flag if and only if t passes the check, and then
the ledger reads exactly 3; if t fails, the trap goes up at the CHECK and no number
of further steps raises the flag.

Proof. A raised flag from a clean start has the earned chain (Theorem 15.11, which
holds for this machine as for the small one): a passing CHECK, then a COMMIT of
the same claim with the counter’s edition unchanged, then a CERTIFY. A passing
CHECK of PCHSH at c means the checker says yes at c’s value, which is the meaning
by Theorem 32.4(2), and the bound is (4) of the same theorem. An unchanged
edition means an unchanged value. For the converse, the CHECK passes exactly
when the tally passes; if it does, COMMIT and CERTIFY find their facts and the three
acts cost three; if it fails, the trap latch goes up, and no later move can raise the flag.
A stored program stops at the trap; an explicitly supplied instruction trace may still
incur its defined charges (as in Theorem 15.20).
   So the one thing the machine does with the check, as this appendix’s opening
said, is that a raised flag can stand on it. Five tallies, each a theorem with its counts
written out, show the check isn’t vacuous and where its edges are.
294   32 The CHSH check




             Proposition 32.6 (Five tallies). Counts are given as (same, diff) for the pairs 00, 01,
             10, 11. (4,1), (4,1), (4,1), (1,4) has S = 12/5: the check passes, the flag rises, √ the
             ledger reads 3. (17,3), (17,3), (17,3), (3,17) has S = 14/5 = 2.8, just under 2 2,
             with S2 = 7.84: it passes, the ledger reads 3. (9,1), (9,1), (9,1), (1,9) has S = 16/5
             with S2 > 8: refused forever, whatever follows. (1,0), (1,0), (1,0), (0,1), the PR
             box, has S = 4: refused forever. (1,0), (1,0), (1,0), (1,0), the all-ones classical plan,
             has S = 2: refused forever.

             Proof. A pair’s correlator is (same − diff)/(same + diff), and S adds the first three
             and subtracts the fourth. (4, 1) gives 3/5 and (1, 4) gives −3/5, so S = 12/5; the
             seven facts are the worked tally after Corollary 33.11, with A = B = 175 and C = 0,
             so the check passes, and Theorem 32.5 gives the flag and the ledger at 3. (17, 3) gives
             7/10 and (3, 17) gives −7/10, so S = 14/5 and S2 = 196/25 = 7.84; with N = 20
             in every pair and D = 14, 14, 14, −14, A = B = 400 · 400 − 196 · 400 − 196 · 400 =
             3200 and C = 14 · 14 · 400 − 14 · 14 · 400 = 0, so the seven facts hold and the
             same theorem applies. (9, 1) gives 4/5, so S = 16/5 and S2 = 256/25 > 8; the PR
             box gives 1, 1, 1, −1 and S = 4; both fail the check, because a tally that passes has
             S2 ≤ 8 (Corollary 33.11), and a failed check traps at the CHECK, after which no
             step raises the flag (Theorem 32.5). The all-ones plan gives N = D = 1 in every
             pair, S = 2, and A = 1 − 1 − 1 < 0, the worked tally’s second computation, so it
             fails the check and is refused the same way.
                  The last one is the check being stricter than the bound, as a named theorem: a
              perfectly legal classical play with S = 2 is turned away.
                  What the theorem doesn’t say stays what it was. The tally is placed. Nobody plays
              it on the machine: the theorem starts from a counter that already holds the packing,
              and no instruction plays a round, so a program can put any code in a counter at no
              cost. There are no marginals. The pinned matrix is stricter than the quantum set, so
              failing says nothing about whether the play was quantum, and there is no margin
              for sampling noise at Tsirelson’s point.
                  Writing a tally costs nothing. Each count is a counter, a round is an INC on one
              of them, and INC costs nothing, so a pile of trials costs nothing to write down (what
              it spends is fresh states, in a counter that never runs out of numbers); the packing
              into one counter is a program’s business, and in the theorem the packed counter
              is simply there at the start. What costs is the claim about the pile. On the ledger,
              statistics cost nothing; the claim about them is what costs.
                  So what the check guarantees fits in one paragraph. If it passes, then every
              question pair was sampled, the four ratios from the counts satisfy three polynomial
              inequalities, so the pinned five-by-five is PSD, so S squared is at most eight. It
                                                        32.6 The check, earned on the host machine   295


does not guarantee that the tally came from honest play (a program can INC
whatever counts it likes at no cost, including four rounds that put S at four). It
does not guarantee any marginal is zero (no marginals are recorded and none are
tested). It does not put the players’ state behind the counts. What it does give is
a quantum strategy with the same correlators: a passing tally’s correlators have a
PSD completion, and every point with one is the correlator table of a strategy on
two four-level systems. And it fails the tally of every single plan, honest classical
play included. It has no margin for sampling noise either. Honest play at Tsirelson’s
point aims at correlators of plus or minus one over root two, and there every pair
of squared correlators the check adds up comes to exactly one, the edge of what it
accepts. So an honest tally aimed at that point sits right on the line, and ordinary
luck over a finite number of rounds pushes it over most of the time. I simulated it,
drawing each round’s answers at Tsirelson’s correlators: the check fails 92 percent
of the time at 10 rounds per question pair, 87 percent at 100, and between 85.4
and 85.9 percent from 1,000 to 100,000. In those simulated trials, fair coins passed
every time from 100 rounds per pair on, and failed about 2.5 percent of the time at
10. I saw that in the trials, and it’s no guarantee about any one tally. The limit has a
closed form. At Tsirelson’s point the correlator matrix, read as a two-by-two, turns
arrows without stretching them (it’s a mirror), and every one of its entries wobbles
by the same amount, independently. To first order the largest singular value of the
noisy matrix is one plus the larger eigenvalue of ( G + GT )/2, where G is the wobble
turned back by that mirror, a two-by-two of independent Gaussian entries of equal
size, and the check passes only when that   √ eigenvalue is at most zero. Write that
symmetric matrix’s eigenvalues as u ± v2 + w2 , with u, v, w independent and of
equal spread
         √    (scale them to standard normals). Both are at most zero exactly when
u ≤ − v + w2 . Now v2 + w2 has
           2
                                   √ the exponential
                                                 √     law with mean 2, so the chance
                2
is 12 E 1 − e−u /2 = 21 1 − 1/ √2 = (2 − 2)/4. So honest play at Tsirelson’s
                  

point fails with probability (2 + 2)/4 ≈ 0.854 in the long run. The arithmetic is
on the page; the first-order step is not proved here, and the simulation is what backs
it.
    The check can also allow a tolerance. For ε = p/q it tests the same seven facts
in integers with the 1 in the contraction inequalities replaced by (1 + ε)2 . At ε = 0
it is the check above. A pass means the two-by-two correlator matrix has operator
norm at most 1 + ε, and S2 ≤ 8(1 + ε)2 : divide every correlator by 1 + ε and the
tolerance check becomes the exact one, so the chain above applies to the shrunken
table. A tally of 100 rounds per pair with correlators 0.72, 0.70, 0.72, −0.72 fails
the exact check and passes at ε = 1/10. In the same kind of simulation, with
         √
ε = 3/ n for n rounds per pair, honest Tsirelson play failed it less than once in ten
thousand trials at every size from 100 to 100,000 rounds per pair: under once in
a hundred thousand at 100, five at 1,000, eight at 100,000, close to the long-run
296   32 The CHSH check


             rate the same first-order picture gives, about nine in a hundred thousand.
                                                                                     √     √ In that
             Gaussian calculation the exact expression is 1 − Φ(6) + e−9 Φ(3 2)/ 2, where
             Φ is the standard
                         √         normal cumulative probability. Dropping its tiny tail corrections
             gives e−9 / 2. This is the asymptotic calculation under independent sampling, not
             a finite-sample confidence   √guarantee. What the tolerance gives up shows in the
             bound: a pass caps S at 2 2 (1 + ε), a little outside Tsirelson’s number. The exact
             check remains the default. With p and q fixed for a machine instance, the tolerance
             check also runs as CHECK on the same versioned registers: its CHECK, COMMIT,
             CERTIFY chain certifies exactly when the integer tolerance test passes, costs three
             on success, and traps permanently on failure. From a clean start, every raised
             flag has that earned chain behind it, with the same register value at CHECK and
             COMMIT, and establishes the relaxed bound above: it’s Theorem 32.5 again, with
             the tolerance test as the checker and “every pair sampled and operator norm at most
             1 + ε” as the meaning, the scaling just described being the proof that they agree. The
             check certifies a shape of the tally. What the shape means is a question for whoever
             supplied the rounds.
                 And notice which way the fence faces. The most practical use anyone has found
             for a Bell test, certified randomness, needs the opposite bound. Stefano Pironio and
             colleagues (2010) argued, and backed with an experiment, that a Bell violation, with
             settings the devices can’t choose, certifies that the outputs are random, and a group
             of quantum cryptographers, Rotem Arnon-Friedman, Frédéric Dupuis, Omar Fawzi,
             Renato Renner and Thomas Vidick (2018), made the accounting tight with what they
             call entropy accumulation. Under those assumptions, a predetermined local output
             strategy passes a properly calibrated test only within its stated false-acceptance
             probability, so it’s the cleanest case I know, outside this book, of a check that can fail,
             separating output that was established from output that was printed. That needs S
             bounded from below, with an error probability. This check bounds S from above and
             fails every single plan, so it’s a fence that keeps out the super-quantum and the fixed
             plan. As a nonlocality test it’s no use. The deployed version exists, too: Gautam
             Kavuri and colleagues, physicists at NIST and the University of Colorado (2025),
             run a public randomness beacon whose outputs each trace back through a Bell test
             to a public record, the nearest working system I know to a certified claim written
             down for good. And records cut both ways there. Jonathan Barrett, Roger Colbeck
             and Adrian Kent, working on quantum cryptography (2013), pointed out that a
             device which keeps its own memory of earlier rounds can leak it through outputs it
             makes public later, so reusing a device can undo a guarantee the user thought was
             settled. A permanent record in the wrong box is an attack.
                                                   32.7 A rational stand-in for an irrational number   297


32.7 A rational stand-in for an irrational number
One useful rational bound: 5657 over 2000, which is 2.8285, and two root two is
2.82842... It’s the four-decimal round-up, chosen so that rational arithmetic can test
“above the Tsirelson bound” without square roots, and it’s safe because 5657 squared
is 32,001,649 and eight times 2000 squared is 32,000,000, so the fraction sits above
root eight by about seven hundred-thousandths. It’s used in a few rational theorems.
It is not used in the integer check, and the real-number theorems use root eight.


32.8 Two more tests, as mathematics

The second rung: a nine-by-nine table on I, the four questions, and the four Alice-Bob
products, with the correlators and five extra free numbers for three- and four-body
entries. Its two four-body entries are tied, one the negative of the other. That
tie is a constraint, ⟨{ A0 , A1 } ⊗ { B0 , B1 }⟩ = 0: the zero entries of the first rung
don’t imply it, and a classical strategy breaks it. So the second rung is level 1+AB
on that slice, and that’s what I mean by level 1+AB everywhere in this book. Its
PSD is again equivalent to a contraction-like condition. The check for it proves
something stronger, that the matrix is positive definite, by a cascade of two Schur
steps down to a four-by-four that Sylvester’s criterion closes. Sylvester’s name on a
test about matrices fits in a way I enjoy, because the word matrix is his. In 1850, in
the Philosophical Magazine, the English mathematician James Joseph Sylvester used
it for an “oblong arrangement of terms” out of which you cut square blocks and take
their determinants, and matrix was already an old word for the place something
grows from. The test does exactly that: it cuts the top-left square blocks out of the
big grid, one row deep, two, three, four (the leading minors), and asks each one’s
determinant to be positive. Was he the first to state the test? The name doesn’t settle
that; names stick to people for all kinds of reasons, and I’d treat this one as a lead.
There’s also a fact that’s easy to list without noticing what it means: with all five
extra numbers at zero, the condition certifies a strict subset of the classical set (the
ball where the four correlators’ squares sum to at most one), forcing S at most two;
it can’t certify anything above the classical line. To get a point above two you have
to supply the extras (S equals 2.4 with one extra at minus one half). Appendix 33
has the algebra.
    The two sizes are one test. A single positive-semidefinite predicate takes the size
as a parameter, and the five-by-five and the nine-by-nine are it at two sizes.
298   32 The CHSH check


             Definition 32.3 (The size-generic PSD test). A claim is a top index n and a ma-
             trix M indexed by naturals. It is realizable when Mij = M ji for all i, j ≤ n and
             ∑i≤n ∑ j≤n vi Mij v j ≥ 0 for every real vector v. The CHSH claim of E is top index 4
             with the matrix M( E) of Definition 32.1; the level-1+AB claim uses top index 8 and
             the nine-by-nine matrix.



             Corollary 32.7 (The level-1 test is the size-five case). For all real E00 , E01 , E10 , E11 :
             the three contraction inequalities hold if and only if the CHSH claim of E is realizable.
             The same holds at size nine for the level-1+AB contraction condition and its claim.

             Proof. Top index 4 means the five indices 0, 1, 2, 3, 4. The double sum visits each
             of their twenty-five cells once, multiplies it by its row and column weights, and
             adds the results. That is precisely the weighted-total test for the five-by-five matrix.
             The completed-square calculation in Corollary 33.7 equates that test with the three
             contraction inequalities. Top index 8 similarly visits the eighty-one cells of the nine-
             by-nine matrix. Theorem 33.12 writes its total as three squares plus the residual and
             proves that its test is exactly the residual condition. The same definition therefore
             asks the same question at either size.
                 The third thing is the elliptope, the set of correlators for which some choice of
             the two missing entries (A0 with A1, B0 with B1) makes the five-by-five PSD. Every
             one of the sixteen plans is inside it, mixtures stay inside, every point inside obeys
             S2 ≤ 8, Tsirelson’s point is inside and reaches it, the PR box is out, and it’s strictly
             bigger than the classical set. There’s a certificate checker for it with a soundness
             proof, in two branches: a strict Sylvester check with the completion supplied, and
             a rational factorisation certificate of the completed matrix, checked in integers.
             What’s proved is that a pass puts the correlators inside the set and that no choice
             of inputs gets the PR box through, and, by computation, that some named points
             pass, the all-ones plan and a point that the arc-sine boundary puts exactly on the
             quantum edge among them. And every point with a rational PSD completion has
             such a certificate: peel the completed matrix a row at a time, as in Section 33.8, but
             keep each pivot instead of taking its square root; a zero pivot forces its row to zero,
             and every number that comes out is rational. The certificate checker is sound and,
             on rational completions, complete, as mathematics; none of the small machine’s
             properties is the elliptope check.
                                                                             32.9 The quantum set   299


32.9 The quantum set
Three descriptions of the same set have been running through this appendix: the
tables some quantum strategy can produce, the tables whose five-by-five matrix has
a PSD completion, and the tables inside Landau and Masanes’s curved edge. They
are the same set, for strategies on finitely many levels, and the next subsection takes
away the “finitely”. Each piece is taken in turn below, because the constructions are
the part worth seeing.
    Quantum tables are inside the completion set. A strategy’s correlator is the inner
product of two unit vectors: Alice’s observable applied to the shared state, and Bob’s
applied to it. Four unit vectors always have a PSD table of inner products, so every
quantum table has a completion, and Tsirelson’s bound follows from that: S2 ≤ 8
for every strategy, and its level-one moment matrix passes the PSD test with the
marginals in. Quantum mechanics uses complex amplitudes, and the complex case
goes through by a plain trick: a complex vector of length n is a real vector of length
2n, its real parts followed by its imaginary parts, and the real part of a complex
inner product is the ordinary inner product of those longer real vectors. So the
real argument
          √ runs unchanged. The two-qubit strategy written out in Appendix 33
reaches 2 2 in both readings.
    The completion set is inside the quantum tables. This direction is Tsirelson’s
representation, and the construction is his. Take a table with a PSD completion. A
PSD matrix is a table of inner products of vectors, always: peel off one row at a time,
and a zero on the diagonal forces its whole row to zero. So the completion hands
over four unit vectors, a0 , a1 for Alice and b0 , b1 for Bob, with a x · by equal to the
table’s entries, and they fit in three dimensions: slide one of the two entries the table
leaves open to the end of the range that keeps the completion PSD, and there the
completion is singular, which puts the four vectors in a space of three dimensions.
Section 33.8 has the picture behind the factorization and the measurements. Now
comes the trick. There are three real four-by-four matrices, built from the two-by-
two ones that swap two entries or flip the sign of one, that each square to the identity
and anticommute in pairs: multiply any two of them in either order and the answers
differ only in sign. Use a vector’s three components to mix those three matrices, and
you get an observable that squares to the identity, a legitimate measurement. Give
Alice and Bob a maximally entangled pair of four-level systems, and the correlator
of two such observables comes out as exactly the inner product of their vectors.
So the strategy built from the four vectors produces the table, for both real and
complex amplitudes. Four levels on each side is always enough, and with complex
amplitudes two are.
    The curved edge is the same set again. Each correlator is the cosine of an angle,
the angle between one of Alice’s vectors and one of Bob’s. Walk the loop a0 , b0 , a1 , b1
300   32 The CHSH check


             and back to a0 : the angle across any one leg can’t exceed the sum of the other
             three, because angles on a sphere obey the triangle inequality. Flip one of Alice’s
             vectors to its opposite and walk the loop again, then do the same with her other one,
             and between them you get the other four inequalities, with 2π in them. Written in
             arcsines, those eight inequalities are Landau and Masanes’s four. Going the other
             way, any four angles that pass them can be met by actual vectors: choose an angle
             between Bob’s two that meets both pairs of constraints, build each of Alice’s from the
             two angles it has to make with them, and check that the determinant that says such a
             triangle exists comes out nonnegative. So a table has a PSD completion exactly when
             its arcsines pass the four inequalities, and those are exactly the quantum tables. The
             point (3/5, 4/5, 4/5, −3/5) sits on the edge, because arcsin 53 + arcsin 45 = π2 by
             the 3-4-5 triangle, and so does Tsirelson’s point. The interval construction, including
             both endpoint cases, is pictured in Section 33.9.


              32.10 Any number of levels, and one converse that fails

             Nothing here cares about dimension. Quantum mechanics also allows systems
             with infinitely many levels, and the arguments don’t need finitely many. Whatever
             the dimension, a shared state v gives an expectation on the operators the players
             measure with: x 7→ Re ⟨v, x v⟩. That one line is the whole step. The proofs use only
             what that expectation always has: you can add, multiply, scale and take adjoints,
             the product spreads over sums, and the expectation of x ∗ x is never negative. Each
             measurement is its own adjoint with the expectation of its square at most one,
             which covers unsharp measurements too, and Alice’s commute with Bob’s. That last
             demand is weaker than putting them on two separate systems, so the result covers
             the wider, commuting model as well. On that   √footing Tsirelson’s sum of squares goes
             through, and the CHSH value is at most 2 2. The same nonnegative form, with
             each diagonal entry raised to one, completes the correlator table to a PSD matrix, so
             the four arcsine inequalities hold, and with a unit the full first-level moment matrix,
             marginals and all, is PSD. The other direction needs no help from infinity: every
             point inside the arcsine edge is reached on two four-level systems.
                 Also, the finite-mixture theorem proves only one direction, that a finite local
             model satisfies the bound, and its converse as stated is false: a table with S = 0 that
             no local model produces. Fine’s own theorem, in the form that speaks of correlations,
             is proved separately as an equivalence: a table of four correlators comes from a
             local model exactly when each is between −1 and 1 and all eight CHSH inequalities
             hold. Section 33.10 pictures the finite distribution. And one more thing about tallies,
             which the guarantee paragraph above already used: a tally loaded at no charge is
             not thereby classical (the four rounds with S equal to four). The correlators (one,
             one, one, minus one) are the PR box, which no classical model has as its averages,
                                             32.10 Any number of levels, and one converse that fails   301


though coin-flip play lands on that four-round tally one time in sixteen; the classical
vertex this appendix used, which fails the check like every plan, is (one, one, one,
one).
   That covers the game, the number, the two walls, and the check. The derivations
come in full in the next appendix, Appendix 33; what all this costs against physics
was Section 19.
CHAPTER 33




The CHSH derivation, in full

Appendix 32 tells the story; this appendix does the algebra. Each item gives the
statement with its hypotheses and the steps you need to check it. Nothing here says
that a matrix is a physical state. Where quantum strategies come in, I say exactly
how they stand to the matrices.


33.1 Setting and notation

Start with four piles of tally sheets, one for each pair of settings Alice and Bob can
be given. On each round their answers either agree or disagree. Give agreement a
score of +1 and disagreement a score of −1. In each pile, add the scores and divide
by the number of rounds. That pile’s average is its correlator. It says how strongly
the two answers agree for that pair of settings.

Definition 33.1 (Settings, outcomes, correlators). Alice receives a setting x ∈
{0, 1} and Bob a setting y ∈ {0, 1}. Each answers a bit, recoded as an outcome
a, b ∈ {+1, −1} (bit 0 becomes +1, bit 1 becomes −1). The correlator Exy is the
average of the product ab over the rounds with settings ( x, y). The CHSH quantity is

                             S = E00 + E01 + E10 − E11 .
I collect the four correlators into the 2 × 2 matrix
                                                  !
                                          E00 E01
                                   E=                ,
                                          E10 E11

whose rows are indexed by Alice’s setting and whose columns by Bob’s. The rows of
E are ( E00 , E01 ) and ( E10 , E11 ); the columns are ( E00 , E10 ) and ( E01 , E11 ).


Definition 33.2 (Counters). There are eight counts: for each setting pair, samexy
counts rounds whose two answer bits matched and diffxy counts rounds whose bits




                                  302
                                                                              33.2 The classical bound   303


differed. A round adds one to exactly one of the eight. Write
                                                                               Dxy
        Nxy = samexy + diffxy ,         Dxy = samexy − diffxy ,        Exy =       .
                                                                               Nxy
    A matched round contributes ab = +1 and a differing round ab = −1, so Dxy
is the sum of the products and Dxy /Nxy is their average. Since | Dxy | ≤ Nxy , every
correlator from counters lies in [−1, 1]. When a bucket is empty, its correlator is
defined as 0. The score is E00 + E01 + E10 − E11 .


33.2 The classical bound

Write a fixed plan on a card: Alice’s answer to setting zero, her answer to setting one,
and Bob’s two answers. Each answer is a sign, plus or minus. Before multiplying
anything, look at Bob’s pair. Either the signs agree or they differ. If they agree,
adding them gives plus or minus two and subtracting them gives zero. If they differ,
the sum is zero and the difference is plus or minus two. One of those two quantities
vanishes; the other cannot exceed two in size. The next calculation uses exactly that
split.

Definition 33.3 (Finite factorizable model). Four real correlators E00 , E01 , E10 , E11
are factorizable when there are a natural number λmax , weights p(λ) and re-
sponse signs A( x, λ), B(y, λ) for λ = 0, . . . , λmax such that: 0 ≤ p(λ) ≤ 1;
∑λmax
  λ
    =0 p (λ ) = 1; every A ( x, λ ) and every B (y, λ ) is −1 or +1; and for all x, y,
Exy = ∑λλmax
           =0 p ( λ ) A ( x, λ ) B ( y, λ ). In words: a hidden card λ is drawn with probabil-
ity p(λ), and each player reads an answer off the card using only their own setting.
Only the correlators are modelled.



Theorem 33.1 (The classical bound for a finite local model). If E is factorizable,
then −2 ≤ E00 + E01 + E10 − E11 ≤ 2.


Definition 33.4 (A tally consistent with one local plan). For bits a0 , a1 , b0 , b1 ∈
{0, 1} and a counter record w, w is locally consistent with ( a0 , a1 , b0 , b1 ) when, for
each setting pair ( x, y): if a x = by then diffxy = 0, otherwise samexy = 0; and every
bucket is sampled, N00 , N01 , N10 , N11 > 0.
304   33 The CHSH derivation, in full




               Theorem 33.2 (A tally above 2 is not one replayed strategy). Let w be a counter
               record whose rational score S(w) is greater than 2. Then for all bits a0 , a1 , b0 , b1 ,
               w is not locally consistent with ( a0 , a1 , b0 , b1 ). Every locally consistent record has
               S(w) = 2 or S(w) = −2; the first statement is that fact turned around.


               33.3 Tsirelson’s bound from algebra
               Now put the cards aside and look just at four numbers in the square. I want to
               bound their three-plus-one-minus score from their squared sizes. The useful trick
               is to write the proposed allowance minus the squared score as a pile of squares.
               A square cannot be negative. If the difference really is that pile, the score cannot
               outrun the allowance.


               Lemma 33.3 (The six-square identity). For all real a, b, c, d,
                       4( a2 + b2 + c2 + d2 ) − ( a + b + c − d )2
                          = ( a − b )2 + ( a − c )2 + ( a + d )2 + ( b − c )2 + ( b + d )2 + ( c + d )2 .



               Theorem 33.4 (Row-bound form of Tsirelson’s bound). For all real E00 , E√
                                                                                       01 , E10 , √
                                                                                                  E11
                     2     2           2     2        2
               with E00 + E01 ≤ 1 and E10 + E11 ≤ 1, S ≤ 8. The absolute form |S| ≤ 8 = 2 2
               follows.


                                                                       √            √
               Theorem 33.5 (Tightness). The point√E00 = E01 = E10 = 1/ 2, E11 = −1/ 2
               satisfies both row bounds and has S = 8.

               Remark 33.1 (Where equality can happen). Equality S2 = 8 under the row bounds
               forces equality in both steps of the proof above: the six squares must all vanish,
               which gives a = b = c = −d, and the rows must be tight, which gives 2a2 = 1.
               So the point of Theorem 33.5 and its negative are the only places the bound is
               reached. A tally’s correlators are ratios of whole numbers and 2a2 = 1 has no
               such solution, so no tally reaches the bound; the integer check’s passing tallies stay
               strictly below it and come as near as you like. (Take rational a, b with a2 + b2 = 1,
               from a Pythagorean triple, and the correlators ( a, b, b, − a): they pass
                                                                                       √the check of
               Section 33.5 with no slack, and S = 2( a + b), which gets as close to 2 2 as you like
               as a/b approaches 1.)
