33.4 The zero-marginal 5 × 5 moment matrix
The four averages fill only the Alice–Bob part of a larger table. Give that table five
labels: a constant reading 1, Alice’s two possible readings, and Bob’s two. Each cell
records an average product of its row and column readings. A reading multiplied by
itself is 1, so the diagonal is all ones. The four averages measured fill the cross-player
cells and their reflected partners.

Definition 33.5 (The pinned level-1 matrix). Take rows and columns
I, A0 , A1 , B0 , B1 . Put 1 on the diagonal and the four correlators in the Alice–
Bob cells, and set the four one-party entries (each player’s own average answer) and
the two within-party entries (A0 against A1 , B0 against B1 ) to zero. The resulting
matrix is:

matrix M ( E) is positive semidefinite if and only if the correlators are column
contractive.

               Theorem 33.8 (PSD implies Tsirelson). If the pinned matrix M ( E) √       is symmetric
               and positive semidefinite at E00 , E01 , E10 , E11 , then S2 ≤ 8 and |S| ≤ 8.


               33.5 The integer check

               The machine has whole-number tallies. The three conditions above were written
               using averages, which are fractions. You can test the same conditions without
               ever dividing: first make sure each denominator is positive, then multiply it away.
               Multiplying by a positive number cannot turn a passing inequality into a failing one
               or a failing one into a passing one.


               Lemma 33.9 (The seven facts are the three conditions with denominators cleared).
               Assume N00 , N01 , N10 , N11 > 0 and put Exy = Dxy /Nxy . Then, with p, q, s as in
               Definition 33.6,
                                     A                   B                        C
                             p=     2 N2
                                          ,       q=    2 N2
                                                              ,        s=                   ,
                                   N00 10              N01 11
                                                                            N00 N01 N10 N11
                                                                AB − C2
                                              pq − s2 =                        .
                                                          ( N00 N01 N10 N11 )2
               Because every denominator is positive, 0 ≤ A ⇐⇒ p ≥ 0, 0 ≤ B ⇐⇒ q ≥ 0 and
               C2 ≤ AB ⇐⇒ s2 ≤ pq.



               Theorem 33.10 (Soundness of the integer check). If a tally w passes the seven-
               integer check, then its four correlators Exy = Dxy /Nxy are column contractive.
               Consequently its pinned matrix is positive semidefinite.



               Corollary 33.11 (A passed check implies the Tsirelson bound). If a tally w passes
               the seven-integer check, the correlators of w satisfy S2 ≤ 8. It is the composition of
               Theorem 33.10, Corollary 33.7 and Theorem 33.8.

                  Two of the seven facts carry the bound by themselves. With every pair sampled,
               0 ≤ A and 0 ≤ B are p ≥ 0 and q ≥ 0, so the four squared correlators add to
               at most 2 and Lemma 33.3 gives S2 ≤ 8. The fact C · C ≤ A · B is what positive
               semidefiniteness adds, and the bound doesn’t use it. For the form this takes on the
               small machine, where a raised flag has to stand on a passing check, see Appendix 32.

33.6 Level 1+AB
The first table had one place for each player’s answer. Enlarge it by giving each
Alice–Bob product a place too. There are four such products, so the five places
become nine. A cell still asks for the average product of its row and column labels.
Some of those products reduce to entries I already have; five more numbers are
needed for the particular filling below. Call them γ1 through γ5 . The longer labels
say which products they stand for.

Definition 33.7 (The level-1+AB matrix). The 9 × 9 real matrix on rows and

               33.7 The elliptope completion
               Go back to the two within-player cells I filled with zero. Leave them blank this time.
               Keep all four measured cross-player averages fixed, and ask whether there is any
               way to fill those two blanks that makes the table pass. A failure with zeros no longer
               settles the question; another filling may work.

               Definition 33.9 (Completion and membership). Complete the matrix of Sec-
               tion 33.4 by setting the within-party entries to free reals: x in the ( A0 , A1 ) cells and
               y in the ( B0 , B1 ) cells, marginals still zero. The correlators are elliptope realizable
               when some x, y make the completed matrix symmetric and PSD.

                                                                                 
                                                         1 0      0      0     0
                                                       0 1       x     E00   E01 

                                        M ( E; x, y) = 0 x       1     E10   E11 

                                                                                 

                  Its quadratic form at v = (v0 , . . . , v4 ) is ∑k v2k + 2xv1 v2 + 2yv3 v4 + 2E00 v1 v3 +
               2E01 v1 v4 + 2E10 v2 v3 + 2E11 v2 v4 . The pinned matrix is the case x = y = 0, so PSD
               of M ( E) implies membership.

               Definition 33.10 (The two branches). The positive-minor branch: with the com-
               pletion supplied as buckets and all entries scaled to integers by a positive common
               factor K, the four leading minors of the 4 × 4 block on A0 , A1 , B0 , B1 are strictly
               positive. The factorization branch: with the completion and a rational certificate
               supplied (six subdiagonal entries over a common positive denominator, four pivots
               dk ≥ 0 over another), the ten entry equations of M = LDLT hold in cross-multiplied
               integer arithmetic. The full gate is their disjunction.
                                                               33.8 The four-vector construction   309




Theorem 33.13 (Soundness of the gate). If the full gate returns yes on a counter
record, any completion buckets and any certificate, then the four correlators of the
record are elliptope realizable. On the PR box tally the gate returns no for every
choice of the remaining arguments.


33.8 The four-vector construction
Put two equal-length pencils down with their ends together, and draw a line along
one of them. Keep that pencil still and turn the other. Use the square corner of a
sheet of paper to bring the moving pencil’s tip straight across to the line, and mark
where it lands. The mark moves from a full pencil-length forward, through the
joined ends when the pencils make a square corner, to a full pencil-length backward.
Call those readings 1, 0 and −1. That is the signed shadow I want. Between
those positions the reading is the cosine of the angle. A dot product of unit vectors
measures that shadow.


33.9 The arcsine boundary, in both directions

Keep Bob’s two directions hinged at one end. Alice’s first direction has to make a
specified angle with each. How far can Bob’s hinge open while that is possible? Hold
Alice’s direction upright and sweep each of Bob’s around it at its required angle.
Point them towards the same side to close the hinge as far as it will go; point them
towards opposite sides to open it as far as it will go. There is a whole interval of
openings between those limits. Alice’s second direction asks for another interval.


33.10 The finite local model, in both directions
Make sixteen cards, each with four boxes labelled A0 , A1 , B0 , B1 , and put every
possible arrangement of plus and minus signs on them, one arrangement per card.
Draw a card before choosing which setting to ask each player about. Alice reads her
chosen box; Bob reads his. Neither needs the other’s setting. How often each card is
drawn is the only choice left in this model.


33.11 Scope of the constructions

The constructions above pin down the correlators. What follows is where they stop:
they aren’t a completeness claim for the integer check, they say nothing about full
behaviours with marginals, and they say nothing about data from a lab.
310   33 The CHSH derivation, in full


                   The arcsine boundary. For two settings and two outcomes per side, Tsirelson,
               Landau (1988) and Masanes (2003) give theorems characterizing the correla-
               tors reachable by quantum measurements: | arcsin E00 + arcsin E01 + arcsin E10 −
               arcsin E11 | ≤ π and its sign variants. The characterization holds as an if and only if
               with the completion set and, through Tsirelson’s representation, with the quantum
               set. The point (3/5, 4/5, 4/5, −3/5) is on the boundary: arcsin 53 + arcsin 45 = π2
               by the 3-4-5 triangle, so the sum is π; Tsirelson’s point is on it too. The necessary
               direction holds in any dimension, for any expectation on an algebra of observables
               in which Alice’s commute with Bob’s.
                   Equality with the quantum set. That the completion set of Section 33.7 is exactly
               the set of quantum correlators is Tsirelson’s representation theorem, and it is proved
               in finite dimension: for both real and complex amplitudes, with two four-level
               systems enough for every point, and two two-level ones with complex amplitudes.
               That PSD of the level-1 moment matrix is necessary for quantum behaviour is
               proved for strategies in finite dimension, with real amplitudes and with complex
               ones, and in any dimension, marginals included, for any expectation on an algebra
               of observables.
                   Achievability. Theorem 33.5 is a point of R4 . A state      √ and measurements that
               reach it are explicit:
                                 √     the two-qubit state (|
                                                           √  00 ⟩ + | 11 ⟩) /  2 with√A0 = √
                                                                                            Z, A1 =√X,
               B0 =√( Z + X )/ 2√and B1 = ( Z − X )/ 2 has correlators 1/ 2, 1/ 2, 1/ 2,
               −1/ 2 and S = 2 2, and every strategy in finite dimension has S2 ≤ 8, with real
               amplitudes and with complex ones. Both are arithmetic about the model. How close
               a real laboratory comes, and what that costs in loopholes closed, is the story told in
               Appendix 32.
                   Completeness of the checks. The 1+AB cascade and the elliptope gate are proved
               sound. The cascade has no proved converse; the gate’s factorization branch accepts
               every rational PSD completion, by the row-peeling of Section 33.8 with each pivot
               kept. The level-1 integer check is the exception: on the small machine the same
               seven integer facts hold exactly when every pair was sampled and the pinned matrix
               is positive semidefinite (Theorem 32.4), so that check is complete for what it tests.
               The 1+AB cascade is strict and misses boundary points. The two 1+AB checks
               don’t sit inside one another. The full check accepts the 12/5 tally above, which the
               zero-moment check refuses, and it refuses the tally with E00 = 1 and the other three
               correlators 0, where H11 = 0, which the zero-moment check accepts.
                   Finite levels. For full behaviours, marginals included, the NPA levels are relax-
               ations. Chaturvedi (arXiv 2607.14569, 2026) argues, in a preprint, that no finite
               NPA level characterizes the complete quantum set even in this simplest Bell scenario.
               I haven’t gone through that proof, and nothing here rests on it.
                                                                               33.12 Prior work   311


  Statistics and physics. Nothing here says how many rounds make a tally infor-
mative, and nothing says the counters came from honest play; a program can INC
whatever counts it likes.


33.12 Prior work
                                              √
Tsirelson’s 1980 paper gives the bound 2 2 for quantum correlations, and his
theorem says that quantum two-outcome correlators are inner products of real unit
vectors. Landau (1988) and Masanes (2003, arXiv quant-ph/0309137) each give a
condition that, by their proofs, characterizes the quantum correlators exactly for two
settings and two outcomes. Wehner (2006, arXiv quant-ph/0510076) gets Tsirelson
bounds, CHSH among them, from a semidefinite program on the 4 × 4 Gram matrix
of those vectors. Navascués, Pironio and Acín (2007, arXiv quant-ph/0607119;
2008, arXiv 0803.4290) built the hierarchy of moment-matrix tests; their 2008
paper applies a Schur complement to the level-1 matrix of this scenario, recovers
the arcsine condition, and discusses the 1+AB level. I went back to each of those
papers for these points, and that is what I found in them. The results are theirs.
Two of them are proved again here, in finite dimension: Tsirelson’s representation
and the arcsine characterization; the rest of their proofs are not redone. Echenim
and Mhalla give a detailed derivation of Tsirelson’s bound (arXiv 2306.12535), and
exact rational sum-of-squares certificates
                                   √       go back at least to Peyrl and Parrilo (2008).
So the moment-matrix route to 2 2 is standard. What is specific here is narrower:
an integer certificate computed from outcome counts, and the argument from “the
counts pass” to PSD of the pinned matrix and on to S2 ≤ 8. I did not find that chain
elsewhere, and I make no claim beyond it. On the host machine, the same chain
is earned: a raised flag from a clean start stands on a passing check of the packed
counts, and the check passes exactly when the pinned matrix is positive semidefinite
and every pair was sampled (Appendix 32, Theorems 32.4 and 32.5). The converse
direction of the integer check, integers from PSD, is proved there too, and there too
I make no claim beyond the chain.
CHAPTER 34




Methodology note

Everything this book claims as its own stands or falls on the arguments written in
it; what it borrows is cited to the people who proved it, and the physics enters as a
named premise. If a proof on the page is wrong, the result is wrong, and nothing
else I could tell you would rescue it.
    Language models were my hands, as I said in Section 24: they did symbol-
pushing, drafted code and helped work through derivations. I have to answer
for what stayed. Start with the definitions and follow how each step leads to the
conclusion. If I counted a finite construction, its cases belong on the page. If I
claimed something for every run, the argument has to explain why the next step
preserves it. A successful example cannot carry that claim, and a name attached to
a proof cannot fill a missing step.
    The same demand applies to the picture around the mathematics. A definition
has to say what I meant, and a model of a device has to fit that device. Those
questions belong beside the premises and the argument, where you can examine
the fit.

From the plain-text statement

These passages were taken out of THIELE_MACHINE.txt and are kept here as they
stood.
BUILD THE BOX, THEN FORGET WHAT THE TASK DOES NOT ASK FOR

The smallest machine above an ordinary computing box has a lamp beside it
and a tally under it. Computing buttons follow the old rule, cost nothing,
and leave the lamp and tally alone. A mark leaves the computing picture
alone, adds one to the tally, and turns the lamp on if its named event holds.
Once on, it stays on. Begin with the lamp down and the tally at zero.

Run only computing buttons and the old machine runs exactly as before.
Forget the lamp and tally afterward and you recover its computation.
The larger description contains it. An event that holds somewhere gives
a visible difference between before and after marking even when the
computing picture stays the same. An ordinary computer can store the
picture, lamp and tally when their rules are effectively given.

Now let two builders implement those instructions differently. One keeps
every button press on a tape. The other uses a few extra counters. Ask
each for the computing picture, lamp and exact tally. Require computing
to follow the base rule, marks to latch exactly the named event without
changing the picture, and each move to add its zero-or-one charge to the
tally. The reading must obey the toll and permanence.

Read those three things from either implementation. After a computing
move, the picture follows the old rule; the free move cannot change the
permanent lamp; adding zero leaves the tally alone. After a mark, the
picture stays put, the lamp latches the event and the tally rises by one.
Those are precisely the simple box’s rules. Repeat this for every button
in a list and the readings agree for the whole run. Any list reaching a
state of the simple box reaches an implementation state with those same
readings. Starting together gives all the same reachable triples.

That makes three stages: write a button list, run it on the builder’s


                                            329
330   36 From the plain-text statement


               box, then read the picture, lamp and tally. The last step forgets whatever
               extra history the builder kept. There is only one way to do it while
               preserving those three readings: report their actual values.

               Try running that last step backward. Turn the simple box halfway around,
               then turn it again. The picture is back, and the lamp and tally never
               changed. Give the other box a separate counter that counts free turns.
               Its counter is now two larger. Which counter value should the simple
               box’s one state turn into? Preserving the turns would require both.
               There is no such map. Forgetting the extra counter works; recovering it
               from the three readings does not.

               To see what has to be kept, give two states the same future button list.
               If a required answer differs, those states cannot share one full state
               in a faithful implementation. That one state would have to give two
               answers to the same continuation. Include the empty list: reading now
               counts as a test. Group states only when every such test agrees. Once
               grouped, pressing a common button keeps them grouped, because that button
               can be put in front of every remaining test. Every grouping that preserves
               answers and steps must respect these tests. This is the coarsest one.

               For the picture, lamp and tally, equal current readings already give
               identical future readings: their next values follow exactly the rules
               above. Different triples are distinguished by reading them now. Remove
               states that differ only in history these questions can never reveal,
               and every reachable triple has exactly one remaining state. Any two
               implementations with no such redundancy differ only in the names of
               those states. A button list reaching a state fixes its partner in the
               other implementation, so even that renaming is forced.

               The questions do work here. Ask for the extra turn count and it has to
               survive too. Stop asking for the tally and repeated failed marks can
               become indistinguishable. If no mark event ever holds, a clean run never
               reaches a lamp-up state. The minimum concerns what can be reached and
               what the chosen questions can distinguish. It does not select the event.
               I develop this familiar idea from minimal automata for
               the actual lift, with the observations and accounting kept explicit.


               Put a hidden switch behind a dark lamp. Pressing the button copies the
               switch to the lamp. Both boxes can look dark now and give different next
               answers. Keep only the lamp and the next step cannot be predicted from
               that picture. Keep the switch too and it can. The exact test is proved: equal pictures must
               pictures under every permitted use of the same button, with explicit
               representatives and successor choices for the converse construction.
                                                                                      331



The arrows do not always choose which question matters. Nor does a finite
watch prove that a lamp stays lit forever: a reset can sit just past the
last tick anyone watched. These boundaries have checked counterexamples.
Showing that real equipment follows the chosen model needs evidence
about that equipment as well as a proof about the model.




The first two counts concern tasks with their own stated operations and
recovery requirements. The third concerns a publication-file extension of
the counter example. They count fibres of different projections.

First ask which checks passed on the current page. At working value b, let
m(b) named predicates be true. Allow each successful check to be requested
independently. Require an exact answer for every name since the last WRITE.
One check gives two choices: run it or leave it alone. A second independent
check doubles the choices, and each further check doubles them again.
With m(b) available checks there are 2^m(b) possible selections. Realize any subset
by writing b and running exactly those checks. Two different subsets cannot
end at the same state because one named query would need opposite answers.
The fibre above b therefore needs at least 2^m(b) states.

That bound is attainable. Use states (b,A), where A is a subset of the true
check names at b. WRITE replaces b and clears A. A passing CHECK inserts
its name. A failed CHECK and IDLE preserve the state. The empty start is
correct; every command preserves the intended history reading. These are
all the command cases, so the claim holds for every finite program. Every
legal state is reachable by the realizing word already given. For finite B,
the exact total is the sum of 2^m(b) over its working values.

Next keep the answers across wiped editions. On each edition, publish or leave it alone. The
two histories, the second four, the third eight. If n independent publication
choices must remain readable when the workspace returns to one picture b,
there are 2^n distinguishable histories above that picture. For one machine
that must do this for every n, no finite fibre suffices: given any proposed
size K, choose n with 2^n>K. That is an infinite reachable fibre forced by
unlimited, recoverable edition history.
